### [1] [Can LLMs Really Understand Item Difficulty Levels? Implications for Automated Item Generation Using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28634)
*Xinyi Wang,Hong Jiao,Ming Li,Sydney Peters,Hanna Choi,Tianyi Zhou,Qingshu Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study evaluates LLMs' ability to predict item difficulty in a large-scale Reading and Writing test, finding that GPT-4.1 achieved the best LLM performance (QWK=0.578) but was outperformed by ConvBERT (QWK=0.625). Advanced models like GPT-5.4 tended to underestimate difficulty, and embedding analysis revealed semantic information alone is insufficient for difficulty prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Item difficulty estimation is critical for both formative and high-stakes summative assessments. As LLMs are increasingly explored for educational applications, understanding their capacity to predict and generate items with targeted difficulty levels is essential for valid assessment design. The study aims to benchmark LLM performance against traditional encoder-only models and feature-based supervised methods.

Method: The study used items from a large-scale Reading and Writing test. Multiple LLMs were evaluated under various prompting strategies and parameter settings (e.g., temperature). Performance was compared against encoder-only language models (e.g., ConvBERT) and feature-based supervised machine learning models. Quadratic weighted kappa (QWK) served as the primary evaluation metric. Embedding dimension reduction was also performed to analyze the separability of item difficulty levels in semantic space.

Result: Zero-shot GPT-4.1 with temperature=0 achieved the highest LLM accuracy (QWK=0.578). However, ConvBERT outperformed all models (QWK=0.625). All LLMs struggled with hard items, and GPT-5.4 systematically underestimated difficulty. Embedding visualization revealed that item embeddings from different difficulty levels were intermixed, indicating that semantics alone insufficiently captures difficulty.

Conclusion: LLMs cannot reliably predict item difficulty from semantic information alone, and their tendency to underestimate difficulty — especially as model capability increases — raises concerns about using LLMs to generate items with targeted difficulty levels. Caution is warranted in operational assessment contexts.

Abstract: The estimation of item difficulty plays a key role in both formative assessment and large-scale high-stakes summative assessments. This study explores how large language models (LLMs) perform in predicting item difficulty levels using items from a large-scale Reading and Writing test. The study investigated various prompting strategies and parameter settings across multiple LLMs. LLM performance was compared with encoder-only language models and feature-based supervised machine learning models. Zero-shot GPT-4.1 with a temperature of 0 yielded the highest item difficulty level prediction accuracy, with a quadratic weighted kappa (QWK) of 0.578. However, LLMs' prediction accuracy was lower than that of ConvBERT (QWK = 0.625), which outperformed the best feature-based supervised machine learning model. Further analysis showed that all LLMs struggled to label hard items; in particular, the current advanced GPT-5.4 tended to underestimate item difficulty levels. Dimension reduction of embeddings showed that item embeddings from different difficulty levels were mixed together, indicating that semantic information from items alone is likely insufficient for item difficulty level prediction. The findings suggest that if LLMs cannot understand item difficulty levels as evidenced by empirical data and tend to treat most items as easy when their own capabilities increase, caution should be exercised when using LLMs to generate items with targeted difficulty levels.

</details>


### [2] [Imbalanced Data Clustering via Targeted Data Augmentation Using GMM and LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28635)
*Noor Khalal,Abdallah Alaa-Eddine Djamai,Imed Keraghel,Mohamed Nadif*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A novel unsupervised data augmentation method combining Gaussian Mixture Models (GMMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) to detect underrepresented clusters and generate synthetic documents, improving clustering performance and interpretability on imbalanced text datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underrepresented topics in NLP pose significant challenges for unsupervised tasks, as standard clustering methods often fail to adequately capture minority topics, leading to poor representation and degraded performance on imbalanced data.

Method: The proposed approach leverages GMMs for their flexibility and robustness in detecting clusters corresponding to underrepresented data regions. LLMs are then employed to generate synthetic documents that enrich these minority clusters, effectively balancing the data distribution in an unsupervised manner.

Result: Experiments on various imbalanced text datasets show that the method preserves clustering performance across all cases and often enhances cluster interpretability.

Conclusion: The GMM-LLM augmentation framework offers a robust and scalable solution for improving data representation in unsupervised NLP tasks, particularly for addressing minority topic underrepresentation.

Abstract: In Natural Language Processing (NLP), dealing with underrepresented topics is challenging, especially in unsupervised tasks where clustering might not adequately capture minority topics. To tackle this challenge, our paper presents a novel unsupervised data augmentation method that integrates Gaussian Mixture Models (GMMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs). Due to their flexibility and robustness, GMMs can detect clusters corresponding to underrepresented areas in the data, while LLMs create synthetic documents to enrich these clusters and improve their representation. Experiments on various imbalanced text datasets demonstrate that our approach preserves clustering performance in all cases and often enhances cluster interpretability, offering a robust and scalable solution for improving data representation in unsupervised NLP tasks.

</details>


### [3] [Chain-of-Models: Cross-Model Auditing for Bias-Robust LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28636)
*Qian Wang,Zhanzhi Lou,Zhenheng Tang,Nuo Chen,Bingsheng He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Chain-of-Models (CoM) is an automated audit pipeline where a second LLM inspects the first model's reasoning trace to mitigate cognitive biases in LLM judging. Auditor identity matters: standalone bias resistance does not predict audit effectiveness, and optimal auditor selection is bias-specific. A per-bias auditor selector achieves 0.884 accuracy vs. 0.824 (best fixed auditor) and 0.805 (no-audit baseline).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs serving as automated judges are vulnerable to cognitive biases. Existing mitigations — prompt-driven debiasing (brittle across bias types) and human evaluation (unscalable) — are insufficient. The authors investigate whether a second model auditing the first model's reasoning trace can effectively mitigate bias, and how auditor identity affects outcomes.

Method: The authors propose Chain-of-Models (CoM), an automated audit pipeline where an auditor model inspects the reasoning trace of a judge model before producing a final judgment. They systematically vary auditor identity (same model, same-family, different-family) across 9 models from 6 families, testing on 4 cognitive biases (bandwagon, authority, distraction, sycophancy) and 4 factual datasets. They then develop a per-bias auditor selection rule based on functional diversity, per-bias standalone resistance, and calibrated audit effectiveness.

Result: Key findings: (1) Standalone bias resistance does not predict audit effectiveness — Kimi-K2.5 is strong standalone but a weak auditor for Qwen2.5-72B. (2) Optimal auditors are bias-specific: GPT-4o excels on bandwagon, authority, and distraction; GLM-5 excels on sycophancy. The per-bias selection rule achieves 0.884 accuracy across biased slices, outperforming the best single fixed auditor (0.824) and the no-audit baseline (0.805).

Conclusion: Auditor identity matters significantly in Chain-of-Models debiasing, and a bias-aware selection strategy yields substantial gains over one-size-fits-all approaches. The authors release data, configurations, and an LLM-agent skill for reproducibility.

Abstract: LLMs increasingly serve as automated judges, but their judgments remain vulnerable to cognitive biases. Existing mitigations mostly rely on prompt-driven debiasing, which is brittle across bias types, or human evaluation, which does not scale. We study \emph{Chain-of-Models} (CoM), an automated audit pipeline in which a second model inspects the first model's reasoning trace before producing the final judgment. The key design question is whether the auditor should be the same model, a same-family model, or a different-family model. Across 9 models from 6 families, 4 cognitive biases, and 4 factual datasets, we find that auditor identity matters in two ways. First, standalone bias resistance does not predict audit effectiveness: Kimi-K2.5 is the strongest standalone model on several biases, yet is a weak auditor for Qwen2.5-72B's biased traces. Second, the best auditor is bias-specific: GPT-4o is strongest on bandwagon, authority, and distraction, while GLM-5 is strongest on sycophancy. We operationalize these findings with a per-bias auditor selection rule that, given the bias type, scores candidates along functional diversity, per-bias standalone resistance, and calibrated audit effectiveness. Under a calibration/test split, the selector reaches the highest accuracy across the four biased slices ($0.884$ vs.\ $0.824$ for the strongest single fixed auditor and $0.805$ for the no-audit baseline). We release data, configurations, and an LLM-agent skill at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/chain-of-models-B585 .

</details>


### [4] [ZeroR@CHiPSAL 2026: Two-Stage Vision-Language Adaptation with Contrastive Learning for Nepali Meme Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28637)
*Nitiz Khanal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A two-stage vision-language pipeline using Qwen3-VL-8B with LoRA fine-tuning and contrastive learning, achieving 2nd place in hate speech detection (F1: 0.797) and 4th in sentiment analysis (F1: 0.518) for Nepali memes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing multimodal hate speech and sentiment detection in Nepali memes, a low-resource South Asian language context where existing OCR-and-translation pipelines suffer from error propagation and lack native script understanding.

Method: Adaptation of the RA-HMD framework using Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct with native Devanagari support. Two-stage training: (1) LoRA fine-tuning with MLP projection head for generative classification, and (2) contrastive backbone fine-tuning with supervised InfoNCE loss. Class imbalance is handled via minority oversampling, image augmentation, and focal loss. Inference uses an ensemble of Stage 1 token probabilities and Stage 2 classifier scores with validation-tuned weights.

Result: 2nd place on hate speech detection (F1: 0.797); 4th place on sentiment analysis (F1: 0.518).

Conclusion: Leveraging a vision-language model with native Devanagari support eliminates error-prone OCR/translation pipelines and yields strong results for low-resource multimodal classification. The two-stage training and ensembling strategy proves effective, and detailed ablations and error analysis provide insights for future work on South Asian languages.

Abstract: This paper presents our system for the CHiPSAL 2026 shared task on multimodal hate speech and sentiment detection in Nepali memes. We address both subtasks: binary hate speech classification and three-class sentiment analysis. Our approach adapts the Robust Adaptation of Hateful Meme Detection (RA-HMD) framework using Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, a state-of-the-art vision-language model with native Devanagari support. We employ a two-stage training pipeline: (1) LoRA fine-tuning with an MLP projection head for generative classification, and (2) contrastive backbone fine-tuning with supervised InfoNCE loss. We handle class imbalance through minority oversampling, image augmentation, and focal loss. At inference, we ensemble Stage 1 token probabilities with Stage 2 classifier scores using validation-tuned weights. Our end-to-end approach eliminates error propagation from separate OCR and translation pipelines by leveraging the model's native Devanagari understanding. Our system achieved \textbf{2nd place} on hate speech detection (F1: 0.797) and \textbf{4th place} on sentiment analysis (F1: 0.518). We provide detailed ablations, error analysis, and insights into adapting large vision-language models for low-resource South Asian languages.

</details>


### [5] [Learning Stateful Predictive Knowledge From Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28638)
*Yan Song,Xidong Feng,Bo Liu,Xinyu Cui,Haotian Fu,Zichen Liu,Mengyue Yang,Cheng Deng,Jian Zhao,Jun Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Stateful Knowledge Learning (SKL), which replaces traditional trajectory-level reflection in LLM agents with explicit, state-grounded predictive assessments. Implemented via self-distillation (SKL-SD) and reinforcement learning (SKL-RL), SKL outperforms reflection-based methods across WebShop, ScienceWorld, and ChessPuzzles benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents learn from experience through trajectory-level reflection, which the authors frame as "episodic hindsight"—yielding brittle, path-dependent heuristics that lack generalization. The core motivation is to shift from hindsight-driven summarization to foresight-driven predictive knowledge anchored to individual states, enabling more granular, generalizable, and bootstrappable learning.

Method: SKL maintains explicit, declarative predictive assessments tied to specific states rather than full trajectories. Two algorithmic instantiations are introduced: SKL-SD uses self-distillation to extract state-grounded predictive knowledge from agent experience, while SKL-RL employs reinforcement learning to train agents to autonomously learn and leverage this knowledge for policy making. Both approaches train the agent to internalize stateful knowledge as an inherent capability.

Result: Experiments across interactive environments (WebShop, ScienceWorld) and a complex reasoning task (ChessPuzzles) demonstrate that SKL-equipped models significantly outperform current reflection-based training paradigms, validating the superiority of state-grounded predictive knowledge over trajectory-level reflection.

Conclusion: Shifting from trajectory-level reflection to stateful predictive knowledge provides a more robust, generalizable foundation for LLM agent learning. The SKL framework establishes that predictive foresight anchored to individual states yields stronger performance than episodic hindsight-based approaches.

Abstract: As large language model (LLM) agents increasingly learn from experience, they primarily rely on trajectory-level reflection to extract insights. Viewed through the lens of predictive knowledge, we argue that this approach operates on episodic hindsight rather than predictive foresight, yielding brittle, path-dependent heuristics. To address this, we propose Stateful Knowledge Learning (SKL). SKL shifts the agent's focus from trajectory-level summarization to maintaining Stateful Knowledge: explicit, declarative predictive assessments anchored to state. We first demonstrate a motivating example showing how stateful knowledge provides granularity, enhances generalization, and enables knowledge bootstrapping. To further scale up the idea, we introduce two algorithms via self-distillation (SKL-SD) and reinforcement learning (SKL-RL), training agents to autonomously extract state-grounded predictive knowledge from experience and learn to leverage it for policy making. Experiments on interactive environments (WebShop, ScienceWorld) and a complex reasoning task (ChessPuzzles) demonstrate that equipping models with the inherent ability to learn stateful predictive knowledge significantly outpaces current reflection-based training paradigms.

</details>


### [6] [The Asymmetric Effects of Knowledge Distillation on Bias in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28639)
*Plawan Kumar Rath*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Knowledge distillation in small instruction-tuned LMs causes asymmetric bias effects: it improves context-following on unambiguous tasks but destroys per-item refusal calibration on ambiguous ones. The two effects are uncorrelated, indicating distinct mechanisms, and aggregate metrics conceal the harm. The authors propose PCCD, a three-step diagnostic protocol that catches both the asymmetric harm and trivial-refuser failure modes that aggregate evaluations miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge distillation is widely used to compress large language models into smaller ones, but its effects on social bias and refusal behavior are poorly understood. Aggregate bias metrics may mask important per-item harms, and there is a need for finer-grained diagnostics that separately assess refusal calibration, context-following, and capability preservation.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic study across a 28-configuration grid, using response-based distillation from a Gemma-2-9B teacher into two student families (SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct and OLMo-2-1B-Instruct). They evaluate on BBQ-disambig (unambiguous, tests context-following) and BBQ-ambig (ambiguous, tests refusal calibration). They introduce two novel metrics: silence-loss (correct refusals turned into answers) and filled-silence (those answers being stereotypical). They audit four training corpora for refusal-as-answer-shape prevalence. They also test supervised fine-tuning with refusal injection and propose the Per-Condition Calibration Diagnosis (PCCD) protocol.

Result: Distillation reduced context-overriding errors from 44% to 24% on unambiguous tasks but caused 15% of correctly refused items on ambiguous tasks to receive stereotype answers. The silence-loss and filled-silence magnitudes were uncorrelated (Spearman ρ=0.19, n.s.), suggesting distinct mechanisms. Aggregate metrics (CrowS-Pairs, overall BBQ Stereotype Reliance Score) concealed these per-item harms. Training corpora contained <0.5% refusal-as-answer-shape. SFT with refusal injection either broke parsing or induced a trivial-refuser regime (99.8% refusal rate, 0.2% disambig accuracy).

Conclusion: Distillation produces asymmetric effects on bias that aggregate metrics obscure. The two effects (context-following improvement and refusal calibration loss) are mechanistically distinct. PCCD provides a reliable three-step protocol for diagnosing these issues, catching both the asymmetric harm and trivial-refuser failure modes that standard aggregate evaluations miss.

Abstract: We show that knowledge distillation in small instruction-tuned language models has asymmetric effects on bias. On unambiguous tasks (BBQ-disambig), response-based distillation from a Gemma-2-9B teacher improves context-following: for the most biased baseline (SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct), it cuts the context-overriding error rate from 44% to 24%. On ambiguous tasks (BBQ-ambig), the same distillation destroys per-item refusal calibration: 15% of items where the baseline correctly abstained instead receive stereotype answers, even when overall refusal rate is preserved. The pattern reproduces on a second student family (OLMo-2-1B-Instruct), with silence-loss of 8% and filled-silence accounting for 89% of new bias. Across the full 28-configuration grid, the magnitudes of silence-loss and filled-silence are uncorrelated (Spearman $ρ=0.19$, n.s.), indicating that the two effects arise from distinct mechanisms.
  Aggregate stereotype metrics (CrowS-Pairs, overall BBQ Stereotype Reliance Score) average over both effects and conceal the per-item harm. We trace the calibration loss to a data-side mechanism: an audit of four training corpora finds <0.5% refusal-as-answer-shape. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with refusal injection either breaks parsing or over-corrects into a trivial-refuser regime (refusal rate 99.8%, disambig accuracy 0.2%) that aggregate metrics would call perfectly calibrated. We propose Per-Condition Calibration Diagnosis (PCCD), a three-step protocol that evaluates refusal calibration, context-following, and capability preservation. PCCD catches both the asymmetric harm and the trivial-refuser failure mode that aggregate evaluations miss.

</details>


### [7] [TokenSwap: Benchmarking and Reducing the Modality Gap in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28640)
*Andong Hua,Colton Bishop,Igor Mordatch,Arian Hosseini,Jindong Gu,Aleksandra Faust,Rebecca Roelofs,Yao Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies a "modality gap" where MLLMs perform worse on semantically equivalent image-interleaved inputs versus text-only inputs. Using TokenSwap, they create TokenSwap-Bench and find an average gap of 19.6% across 42 models. Reasoning models show smaller gaps (~10.1%), and training with TokenSwap mitigates the gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs should produce consistent responses for semantically equivalent inputs regardless of modality (text vs. image). However, the authors observe systematic performance discrepancies under cross-modal variations, motivating a systematic investigation of this "modality gap."

Method: The authors introduce TokenSwap, which constructs semantically equivalent inputs by replacing textual concepts with aligned images, producing interleaved text-image token sequences. They transform existing text benchmarks (e.g., MMLU) into TokenSwap-Bench and evaluate 42 MLLMs, comparing text-only vs. image-interleaved performance. They also test interventions including prompting strategies, scaling compute, and training with TokenSwap data.

Result: A pervasive modality gap exists across all 42 MLLMs: performance drops 4.2%–47.4% (mean 19.6% ± 3.3%) when switching from text-only to image-interleaved inputs. Reasoning models show significantly smaller gaps (avg. 10.1% vs. 25.5% for non-reasoning models). Prompting and scaling compute alone do not reliably reduce the gap, but incorporating TokenSwap during training effectively mitigates it while preserving text-only and vision-language performance.

Conclusion: The modality gap is a widespread and robust phenomenon in MLLMs. Reasoning represents a promising direction for reducing it, while training-time integration of cross-modal TokenSwap data is an effective mitigation strategy.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) should generate consistent responses given semantically equivalent inputs across modalities. However, we observe a systematic discrepancy in model predictions under such cross-modal variations. Specifically, we define the modality gap as the difference in model performance under semantically equivalent textual and multimodal inputs. We introduce TokenSwap, a method that constructs such inputs by replacing textual concepts with semantically aligned images, resulting in sequences where visual tokens are interleaved with text tokens. Based on TokenSwap, we transform existing text-based benchmarks such as MMLU into image-interleaved counterparts, resulting in TokenSwap-Bench. Across 42 MLLMs, we observe a pervasive modality gap, with performance decreasing by 4.2% to 47.4% when moving from text-only to image-interleaved inputs, averaging 19.6% +/- 3.3% across models. Notably, we observe that reasoning models exhibit consistently smaller gaps, achieving an average gap of 10.1% compared to 25.5% for non-reasoning models. In contrast, neither prompting strategies nor scaling training compute alone reliably reduces the modality gap. Finally, we demonstrate that incorporating TokenSwap during training effectively mitigates this gap while preserving strong text-only and vision-language performance.

</details>


### [8] [The Formalism Trap: Are LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluators Blinded by Consensus Mimicry under Social Load?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28641)
*Dahlia Shehata,Ming Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces the "Agentic Formalism Trap" — a phenomenon where LLM-as-a-Judge evaluators conflate surface-level procedural structure with factual correctness — and proposes the Evaluative Dissonance Index (EDI) to quantify this failure. Analyzing 22,500 trajectories across three domains, the authors taxonomize hallucination maneuvers, identify syntactic triggers via logistic meta-evaluation, demonstrate domain-agnostic vulnerability through zero-shot transfer, and show that different swarm topologies produce distinct semantic blind spots, ultimately arguing that unanchored closed-loop LLM evaluation is inherently unstable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-as-a-Judge systems proliferate for automated evaluation of agentic AI outputs, there is a critical risk that these evaluators can be "captured" — rewarding stylistically or structurally compliant responses that are semantically vacuous or factually incorrect. The authors aim to formally characterize, quantify, and diagnose this failure mode, which undermines the reliability of closed-loop agentic evaluation pipelines.

Method: The authors analyzed 22,500 trajectories across three benchmark domains (GAIA, SWE-bench, Multi-Challenge). They: (1) extracted a semantic taxonomy of hallucination maneuvers validated via deterministic lexical grounding with extreme statistical significance (p < 10⁻¹²⁰); (2) trained a logistic meta-evaluator to isolate syntactic triggers of evaluator capture (ROC-AUC 0.8779); (3) conducted zero-shot Leave-One-Domain-Out transfer experiments to test domain-agnostic vulnerability (mean ROC-AUC 0.7482); and (4) performed architectural profiling across different simulated swarm topologies to identify topology-induced semantic blind spots.

Result: Key findings: (1) The logistic meta-evaluator achieved ROC-AUC 0.8779 in isolating syntactic triggers of evaluator capture. (2) Zero-shot Leave-One-Domain-Out transfer yielded a mean ROC-AUC of 0.7482, confirming the vulnerability is domain-agnostic. (3) Lexical grounding validation reached p < 10⁻¹²⁰. (4) Different swarm topologies produced mathematically distinct patterns of semantic blind spots, demonstrating that evaluation instability is topology-dependent.

Conclusion: Unanchored closed-loop LLM evaluation is fundamentally unstable and systemically divergent. The Agentic Formalism Trap represents a pervasive failure mode wherein evaluators are seduced by procedural form over semantic substance. Architectures cannot be treated as interchangeable, as distinct topologies induce distinct vulnerability profiles. The authors argue that architecture-specific vigilance filters are necessary to mitigate these blind spots in production agentic evaluation systems.

Abstract: We introduce the \textit{Agentic Formalism Trap} and the Evaluative Dissonance Index ($D_E$), quantifying how LLM-as-a-Judge systems conflate structural proceduralism with semantic truth under adversarial load. Analyzing 22,500 trajectories across 3 domains (GAIA, SWE-bench, Multi-Challenge), we extract a semantic taxonomy of hallucination maneuvers, validated via deterministic lexical grounding ($p < 10^{-120}$). A logistic meta-evaluator isolates the exact syntactic triggers of this evaluator capture (ROC-AUC 0.8779), while a zero-shot Leave-One-Domain-Out transfer proves the vulnerability is universally domain-agnostic (mean ROC-AUC 0.7482). Architectural profiling reveals that distinct simulated swarm topologies induce mathematically disparate semantic blind spots, proving that unanchored closed-loop evaluation is unstable, systemically divergent and necessitates architecture-specific vigilance filters.

</details>


### [9] [Evaluating Federated Pre-Training: On the Reliability of Downstream Fine-Tuning and Intrinsic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28658)
*Claudia Grosser,Maike Heuer,Denis Krompass,Thomas A. Runkler*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Federated pre-training evaluation is unreliable when using downstream fine-tuning benchmarks like GLUE; instead, direct next-token prediction on the same text better preserves the true pre-training quality ranking established by test perplexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating federated pre-trained models is difficult because differences in client participation and data heterogeneity make comparisons challenging. Pre-training test perplexity is tied to the pre-training distribution, while downstream benchmarks introduce task-specific adaptation that may distort the true pre-training quality signal. The authors aim to determine which evaluation protocol more faithfully reflects federated pre-training quality.

Method: The authors train multiple 16M-parameter transformer models under controlled centralized and federated settings on identical client data. They establish a reference ranking based on pre-training test perplexity, then assess various evaluation protocols — including GLUE downstream fine-tuning (full, head-only, reduced-data variants) and intrinsic next-token prediction on GLUE text — by measuring whether each protocol preserves that reference ranking.

Result: Downstream fine-tuning on GLUE does not reliably preserve the pre-training ranking, while direct next-token prediction on GLUE text exhibits a strong correspondence with pre-training test perplexity. This holds across different fine-tuning strategies and data regimes.

Conclusion: Downstream fine-tuning alone can be misleading when comparing federated pre-trained models. Evaluation signals closer to the original pre-training objective — such as direct next-token prediction — deserve greater attention for faithfully assessing federated pre-training quality.

Abstract: Federated pre-training offers a way to train foundation models on private or distributed data without centralizing the underlying datasets. However, evaluating federated pre-training remains challenging because differences in client participation and local data availability can make directly comparable evaluation difficult. Moreover, pre-training test perplexity is tied to the pre-training distribution, while downstream benchmarks introduce task-specific adaptation that may not faithfully reflect the test perplexity established during pre-training. In this work, we study which evaluation protocol more reliably reflects federated pre-training quality. Using a controlled set of centralized and federated-trained models of a 16M parameter transformer model trained on identical client data, we assess evaluation protocols by whether they preserve a reference ranking established on the same pre-training testset. We compare downstream fine-tuning on GLUE, including full, head-only, and reduced-data variants, with next-token prediction on GLUE text as an intrinsic evaluation signal. Our results show that downstream fine-tuning does not reliably preserve the pre-training ranking, whereas direct next-token prediction exhibits a strong correspondence with the pre-training test perplexity. These findings suggest that downstream fine-tuning alone can be misleading when comparing federated pre-trained models, and that evaluation signals closer to the original pre-training objective deserve greater attention.

</details>


### [10] [Are the Financial Reasoning from LLMs Credible? A Real World Test over Long-Horizon Statements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28661)
*Xinke Tong,Xuanming Zhang,Tianyi Tang,An Yang,Jiatu Hu,Guojie Lin,Zhenzhen Shi,Lingfeng Zeng,Boyu Yang,Bing Zhao,Hu Wei,Lin Qu,Dayiheng Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FinIndices is a benchmark for testing LLM structural reasoning on financial statements. It reveals two bottlenecks: (1) Knowledge Bottleneck — performance collapses without formula hints due to fragile pattern matching, and (2) Structural Bottleneck — multi-metric table generation drains reasoning, causing models to revert to shallow heuristics. SFT partially mitigates these issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing financial QA benchmarks lack real-world complexity — they use multiple-choice or single-hop QA on cropped tables, ignoring cross-statement dynamics, temporal de-cumulation, and long-context reasoning. The financial domain, requiring numerical precision and multi-step logic, is ideal for probing whether LLMs exhibit genuine structural reasoning or merely surface-level pattern matching.

Method: The authors introduce FinIndices, a large-scale benchmark with up to 32K-token uncropped financial statements. An automated synthesis pipeline with adversarial traps generates two task types: Single-Index computation and Table-Index tabulation, testing domain knowledge, temporal reasoning, and caliber matching. Evaluation compares LLM performance with and without explicit formula hints, and assesses the impact of Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT).

Result: Two critical LLM vulnerabilities emerged: (1) Knowledge Bottleneck — removing formula hints causes catastrophic performance drops (e.g., Gemini-3.1-Pro falls from 70.70% to 38.22% on table tasks), exposing failures in temporal de-cumulation and stock-flow caliber mismatch. (2) Structural Bottleneck — generating multi-metric, multi-period tables drains reasoning capacity, causing models that perform well in isolation to resort to shallow heuristics (fetching wrong adjacent columns, lazy literal arithmetic). SFT yields meaningful zero-hint improvements (+8.54% Single, +3.82% Table).

Conclusion: LLMs currently lack genuine structural reasoning over long financial contexts, suffering from both knowledge and structural bottlenecks. The fragility of pattern matching without explicit hints and the regression under cognitive load demonstrate that surface-level heuristics dominate. However, SFT results suggest that data-centric alignment can partially restore structured logic, pointing toward a viable mitigation path.

Abstract: Do Large Language Models (LLMs) possess genuine structural reasoning, or merely rely on surface-level pattern matching? The financial domain, demanding numerical precision and multi-step logic over long contexts, is an ideal testbed. Existing benchmarks fail to capture real-world industrial complexity, predominantly relying on multiple-choice questions or single-hop QA over cropped tables while ignoring intricate cross-statement dynamics and temporal de-cumulation.
  To bridge this gap, we introduce FinIndices, a large-scale benchmark evaluating data-processing fidelity over uncropped financial statements (up to 32K tokens). Utilizing an automated synthesis pipeline with adversarial traps, FinIndices encompasses Single-Index computation and Table-Index tabulation to test complex domain, temporal, and caliber reasoning.
  Our evaluation reveals two severe LLM vulnerabilities. First, a "Knowledge Bottleneck": despite memorizing formulas during pre-training, models demonstrate fragile pattern matching. Removing explicit formula hints causes performance to collapse (e.g., Gemini-3.1-Pro drops from 70.70% to 38.22% on table tasks), exposing fatal flaws in temporal de-cumulation and stock-flow caliber mismatch. Second, a "Structural Bottleneck": the intense cognitive load of generating multi-metric, multi-period tables actively drains reasoning capacity. Under structural pressure, LLMs that flawlessly execute isolated derivations regress to shallow heuristics, such as fetching incorrect adjacent columns or substituting deep accounting adjustments with lazy literal arithmetic. Finally, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) yields substantial zero-hint gains (+8.54% Single, +3.82% Table), validating that structured logic can be partially restored via data-centric alignment.

</details>


### [11] [The Checking Problem: What must be true before AI ships in a regulated firm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28666)
*Prerit Ahuja*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Enterprise AI workflows in regulated financial services are assessed not just by accuracy but by the human review burden they impose. In a study of six document-heavy workflows across four model families and three tool configurations, only 56.1% of configurations cleared a "production bar" requiring sustained accuracy, reproducibility, verifiable attribution, and informative confidence signals. The key finding: requiring models to cite sources and state confidence dramatically reduces the proportion of output requiring human review, while self-verification adds latency without commensurate benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI programmes stall at high rates for poorly understood reasons. The authors aim to measure the mechanism behind these failures, arguing that the bottleneck is not raw accuracy but the human review burden — how much output must still be manually checked. This property, they contend, is measurable yet rarely measured in practice.

Method: Six document-heavy workflows typical of regulated financial services were executed across four model families and three tool configurations (baseline, source citation with confidence signal, and self-verification), each repeated three times. This yielded 5,093 scored output elements across 72 configurations. Each configuration was assessed against two standards: a "demonstration bar" (one correct run on one case) and a "production bar" (sustained accuracy, reproducibility, verifiable attribution, informative confidence). The review burden was then estimated out-of-sample for each configuration.

Result: 57 of 72 configurations (79.2%) cleared the demonstration bar; only 32 (44.4%) cleared the production bar, giving a 56.1% survival rate from demonstration to production. A baseline tool with no confidence signal requires 100% review. Source citation plus confidence reduces review burden to 49% while maintaining error tolerance in 17 of 20 configurations. Self-verification reduces review to 44% but at 2.3× latency and fails to hold error tolerance.

Conclusion: The practical value of an AI workflow is determined less by its accuracy rate and more by the proportion of output a human must still review. This review-burden metric is both measurable and critical for real-world deployment, yet is rarely quantified. Confidence signals and source attribution are the most cost-effective means of reducing it.

Abstract: Enterprise AI programmes stall at a rate that is widely quoted and poorly explained. This paper measures the mechanism. Six document-heavy workflows of the kind performed daily in regulated financial services were run across four model families and three tool configurations, three times each, producing 5,093 scored output elements across 72 configurations. Each configuration was assessed twice: against a demonstration bar, being a single correct run on a single case, and against a production bar requiring sustained accuracy, reproducibility across repeats, verifiable attribution, and a confidence signal that carries information. 57 of 72 configurations cleared the demonstration bar and 32 cleared the production bar, a survival rate of 56.1%. The paper then computes the review burden each configuration imposes, estimated out of sample rather than with hindsight. A tool that states no confidence requires review of 100% of its output, because it offers a reviewer no basis for triage. Requiring the tool to cite its sources and state a confidence reduces that to 49% while holding the residual error tolerance in 17 of 20 configurations. Adding a self-verification pass costs 2.3 times the latency of the plain configuration, reaches 44%, and is the only configuration that fails to hold the error tolerance. The practical implication is that the value of an AI workflow is set less by how often it is right than by how much of it a human must still check, and that the second property is measurable and rarely measured.

</details>


### [12] [TELLER: Dual-Path Iterative Preference Optimization for Table Entity Linking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28680)
*Yixin Peng,Kehao Li,Stefan Decker*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TELLER is a table entity linking framework that uses iterative preference optimization with residual-error-based data refreshing for direct answers, and length-normalized regularized preference optimization for reasoning paths, achieving improved accuracy on TableInstruct and MammoTab V2 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing table entity linking methods suffer from two key limitations: (1) static preference data cannot adapt to residual errors of an evolving model during training, and (2) variations in reasoning length bias sequence-level preference learning, undermining the effectiveness of explicit reasoning approaches.

Method: TELLER retrieves and ranks Wikidata candidates with reduced table evidence in the prompt. It has two paths: (1) Direct-answer path uses iterative direct preference optimization (DPO) that refreshes preference data with residual errors from the updated model. (2) Reasoning path employs filtered and compressed chain-of-thought rationales for supervised fine-tuning, followed by iterative length-normalized regularized preference optimization to mitigate length bias.

Result: On TableInstruct, the direct-answer path improved accuracy from 94.35% to 94.50%, and the reasoning path from 92.90% to 92.95%. On MammoTab V2, the direct-answer path improved from 87.59% to 88.20%, and the reasoning path from 79.09% to 81.85%, while maintaining high rates of complete reasoning generation.

Conclusion: Iterative preference learning with adaptive error-based data refreshing benefits both concise entity prediction and explicit reasoning. Length-normalized regularization effectively mitigates reasoning-length bias, enabling more robust performance gains on challenging benchmarks like MammoTab V2.

Abstract: Entity linking in tables matches short and ambiguous cell mentions to their corresponding knowledge-base entities. Existing approaches typically rely on data preprocessing pipelines that retain either compact or extensive table content as contextual evidence, and then formulate entity linking as a language generation task for instruction-tuned models; recent systems further incorporate explicit reasoning to disambiguate challenging mentions. However, their training supervision is usually static: fixed preference data cannot adapt to the residual errors of an evolving model, while variations in reasoning length can bias sequence-level preference learning. To address these limitations, we present TELLER: Table Entity Linking through Learning from Errors and Reasoning. We first retrieve and rank Wikidata candidates and retain reduced table evidence in the prompt. The direct-answer path applies iterative direct preference optimization and refreshes its preference data with residual errors from the updated model. The reasoning path uses filtered and compressed chain-of-thought rationales for supervised fine-tuning, followed by our iterative length-normalized regularized preference optimization. On the TableInstruct entity-linking subset, the direct-answer path improves accuracy from 94.35\% to 94.50\%; on the MammoTab V2 evaluation set, it improves accuracy from 87.59\% to 88.20\%. The reasoning path improves accuracy from 92.90\% to 92.95\% on TableInstruct and from 79.09\% to 81.85\% on MammoTab V2, while maintaining high rates of complete reasoning generation. These results show that iterative preference learning benefits both concise entity prediction and explicit reasoning.

</details>


### [13] [Demystifying Entropy-based Selection for Chain-of-Thought Compression in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28707)
*Sara Candussio,Daniel Scalena,Luca Bortolussi,Elisabetta Fersini,Malvina Nissim,Gabriele Sarti*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper challenges entropy-based pruning for CoT reasoning, showing it performs no better than random pruning across models and tasks. Low-entropy token retention only helps in math benchmarks due to numeric token properties. Causal patching experiments reveal task information is distributed across the entire reasoning chain, not concentrated in a few identifiable tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously test the robustness of entropy-based CoT pruning methods and determine whether task-relevant information is concentrated in a small subset of CoT tokens identifiable by entropy heuristics, or distributed across the full reasoning chain.

Method: The authors evaluate low- and high-entropy CoT step selection across multiple models and reasoning tasks, comparing against random pruning baselines. They analyze token-level entropy on mathematical versus non-mathematical benchmarks, examining numeric token properties. Finally, they perform causal patching experiments, restoring original activations for subsets of CoT tokens to measure information distribution.

Result: Entropy-based pruning offers no advantage over random pruning in any setting. Low-entropy token retention appears effective only on math benchmarks, explained by numeric tokens being inherently low-entropy while carrying semantic content. Causal patching shows near-perfect performance recovery when patching only a few tokens, but no heuristic identifies which tokens matter—information is distributed across the full chain.

Conclusion: Entropy-based pruning methods for CoT reasoning are not robust and offer no benefit over random selection. The apparent success on math benchmarks is an artifact of numeric token properties. Task information is diffusely distributed across the reasoning chain, implying that effective CoT compression cannot rely on simple token-level heuristics like entropy.

Abstract: Entropy-based pruning has been proposed as an effective method for compressing Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning with negligible accuracy loss. We test the robustness of low- and high-entropy CoT step selection methods across various models and reasoning tasks, showing that entropy offers no advantage over random pruning in any evaluated setting. Moving from sentences to tokens, we then show that retaining low-entropy tokens seems effective only on mathematical benchmarks. We find this is due to the inherently low-entropy nature of numeric tokens, which also convey semantic content in such problems. Finally, we demonstrate that patching a subset of a few CoT tokens with their original activations recovers near-perfect full-trace performance, providing causal evidence that task information is not concentrated in a small set of CoT tokens identifiable by heuristics, but rather distributed across the full reasoning chain.

</details>


### [14] [The Morphological Core of Dungan: A Two-Dialect Finite-State Model and a Multi-Genre Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28766)
*Anton M. Alekseev,Sergey I. Nikolenko*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper presents a finite-state morphological analyzer for Dungan (a Sinitic language written in Cyrillic), measures quantitative morphological properties across three genres, and finds that overt inflection is rare (9.3%), ambiguity is sharply localized on two clitics, and the grammatical core is effectively closed — with most analysis failures due to lexical gaps rather than unimplemented morphology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite detailed grammatical descriptions of Dungan, no systematic quantitative measurement of its morphological properties in actual usage exists. The authors aim to fill this gap by using a computational model as a measuring instrument to quantify how morphology behaves in real texts.

Method: A finite-state morphological analyzer was implemented using HFST (Helsinki Finite-State Technology), covering both the Gansu (literary standard) and Shaanxi dialect groups. The model formalizes existing grammatical knowledge without introducing new description. It was evaluated on corpora spanning three genres (encyclopedic, journalistic, and literary), with held-out testing, development coverage analysis, and comparison against a stem-only baseline.

Result: Three main findings: (1) Overt inflection affects only 9.3% of tokens in the encyclopedic register, with only ten inflectional categories and negligible degree marking. (2) Ambiguity is low: 78.1% of tokens receive a single analysis, with residual ambiguity concentrated on two clitics (-di for genitive/progressive, -ni for locative/prospective). (3) The grammatical core is effectively closed: 78–95% of analyzer failures stem from missing lexicon entries, while deliberately unimplemented phenomena account for at most 4.5%. Held-out coverage (80–85%) matched or exceeded development coverage (73%); morphology contributed 5.2 percentage points over a stem-only baseline (67.4%).

Conclusion: The open frontier of Dungan is lexical rather than grammatical. The morphological system is small, closed, and low-ambiguity, meaning further progress in Dungan NLP depends primarily on lexicon expansion. All tools, sources, and evaluation scripts are released openly.

Abstract: Dungan, a Sinitic language of Central Asia written in a Cyrillic-based script, is described in detail in the grammatical literature, yet the quantitative properties of its morphology in actual usage have, to the best of our knowledge, never been measured systematically. This paper uses a finite-state morphological analyzer as a measuring instrument. Implemented with HFST and covering both dialect groups (the Gansu variety, which is the literary standard, and the Shaanxi variety), the model offers no new grammatical description; it formalises the knowledge accumulated in Dungan studies and makes it measurable on corpora of three genres. Three results follow. Overt inflection is rare and limited: only 9.3% of recognized tokens in the encyclopaedic register have an overt marker, the system has just ten categories, and degree marking is almost absent. Ambiguity is genuine but sharply localized: 78.1% of tokens receive a single analysis, and the residue sits almost entirely on two clitics, -di (genitive/progressive) and -ni (locative/prospective). And the grammatical core proves effectively closed, the claim the instrument is really needed for: between 78% and 95% of the tokens the analyzer fails on, depending on register, are simply absent from the lexicon, and the phenomena the model deliberately declines to implement account for at most 4.5% of those failures. Held-out coverage (80 to 85%) is no lower than development coverage (73%), while a stem list with no morphology already reaches 67.4%, so the morphology is worth 5.2 points. The open frontier of Dungan is lexical. The analyzer, its sources and every evaluation script are released openly.

</details>


### [15] [Self-Supervised Skill Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28777)
*Siran Peng,Cuiyu Yang,Tianyu Fu,Tianshuo Zhang,Haoyuan Zhang,Weisong Zhao,Anyang Su,Minghui Wu,Huiying Li,Xiangyu Zhu,Chenxu Zhao,Zhen Lei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SSO is a self-supervised framework that optimizes reusable LLM agent skills using only unlabeled task instances, without ground-truth feedback. It iteratively generates skill probes, uses an LLM judge to compare outcomes, extracts behavioral differences, and ranks behaviors by aggregated evidence to produce improved skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world applications lack ground-truth labels, task scores, rewards, or reliable task-specific evaluators, making existing skill optimization methods that rely on GT feedback inapplicable. There is a need for a method that can optimize LLM agent skills using only unlabeled data.

Method: SSO operates iteratively: (1) runs the current skill on an unlabeled batch; (2) generates complete skill probes from a subset of executions; (3) runs probes on the same batch; (4) an LLM judge compares resulting answers, trajectories, artifacts, or terminal states; (5) a separate behavior extractor identifies behavioral differences without seeing judge decisions; (6) aggregates evidence for/against observed behaviors across instances; (7) ranks behaviors by evidence and renders a new skill from top-ranked behaviors; (8) accepts the update only if the new skill outperforms the current one on an unlabeled validation set.

Result: SSO outperforms existing GT-free prompt optimizers on both closed-ended and open-ended tasks. On closed-ended benchmarks, it approaches and sometimes exceeds the strongest GT-based skill optimizer, all without using any GT feedback.

Conclusion: Self-supervised skill optimization is a viable and effective alternative to GT-dependent methods, enabling LLM agent skill improvement in settings where labeled feedback is unavailable.

Abstract: Agent skills provide frozen large language model (LLM) agents with reusable procedural guidance, and recent work shows that such skills can be optimized with ground-truth (GT) feedback. Many applications, however, lack GT labels, task scores, rewards, or reliable task-specific evaluators. We therefore introduce Self-Supervised Skill Optimization (SSO), a comparative framework that learns a reusable skill from unlabeled task instances alone. At each step, SSO runs the current skill on an unlabeled batch, uses a subset of the resulting executions to generate complete skill probes, and runs the probes on the same batch. An LLM judge compares the resulting answers, trajectories, artifacts, or terminal states. A separate behavior extractor identifies behavioral differences without seeing the judge's decisions. SSO uses these decisions to aggregate evidence for and against the observed behaviors across instances. It then ranks the behaviors by the resulting evidence and renders a new complete skill from the highest-ranked behaviors. The update is accepted only if the new skill outperforms the current one on an unlabeled validation set. SSO outperforms existing GT-free prompt optimizers on both closed-ended and open-ended tasks. On closed-ended benchmarks, it approaches and sometimes exceeds the strongest GT-based skill optimizer without using any GT feedback.

</details>


### [16] [Benchmarks Are Not Monolithic: Sample-Level Auditing and Orchestration for LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28801)
*Philipp D. Siedler,Jordan Sassoon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a dataset-centric meta-evaluation framework that audits benchmark datasets at the sample level across five latent dimensions, revealing internal heterogeneity in five major LLM benchmarks (MMLU, ARC, WinoGrande, HellaSwag, TruthfulQA) and enabling criterion-driven recomposition of benchmark subsets for targeted model evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM benchmarks are treated as monolithic tasks with aggregate accuracy scores, which obscures substantial variation in the cognitive, linguistic, and ethical demands of individual samples. This lack of granularity prevents nuanced understanding of model capabilities and failure modes.

Method: The authors propose a sample-level annotation framework with five latent dimensions: (1) Cognitive and Knowledge Demands, (2) Language and Content Quality, (3) Task Properties, (4) Context, and (5) Ethics, Safety, and Fairness. They apply this framework to annotate samples from MMLU, ARC, WinoGrande, HellaSwag, and TruthfulQA, then demonstrate criterion-driven orchestration of composite benchmark subsets for targeted evaluation (e.g., Reasoning Depth, Ethical Sensitivity).

Result: Annotation reveals pronounced internal heterogeneity within all five benchmarks that aggregate accuracy scores fail to capture. The framework successfully enables recomposition of cross-dataset benchmark subsets tailored to specific evaluation criteria, facilitating more targeted and interpretable assessments of LLM capabilities.

Conclusion: The work reframes benchmark evaluation as dataset introspection. By auditing benchmarks at the sample level along multiple latent dimensions, researchers can move beyond monolithic aggregate metrics and construct principled, criterion-driven evaluation suites that better reflect diverse and specific evaluation needs.

Abstract: Benchmark datasets are central to evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs), yet they are typically conceived as monolithic tasks, obscuring substantial variation in the demands of individual samples. We introduce a dataset-centric meta-evaluation framework that audits benchmark datasets at the sample level along five latent dimensions: 1. Cognitive and Knowledge Demands, 2. Language and Content Quality, 3. Task Properties, 4. Context, and 5. Ethics, Safety, and Fairness. Applying this framework, we annotate five influential benchmarks -- MMLU, ARC, WinoGrande, HellaSwag, and TruthfulQA -- revealing pronounced internal heterogeneity that is not captured by aggregate accuracy scores. We show how these annotations enable criterion-driven orchestration of composite benchmark subsets across datasets, supporting targeted evaluation of model capabilities such as Reasoning Depth or Ethical Sensitivity. This approach reframes benchmark evaluation as dataset introspection, providing a principled methodology for analyzing and re-composing existing benchmarks to better reflect diverse evaluation needs.

</details>


### [17] [Rolling With Resistance: Preference-Optimized LLM Counselors Can Trade Goal Persistence for Relational Attunement in Motivational Interviewing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28814)
*Weiying Chen,Junlong Shen,Zhexuan Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to teach language models to produce "rolling with resistance" responses in motivational interviewing, penalizing either capitulation or confrontation. Penalizing confrontation reliably reduces goal persistence, while penalizing capitulation is inert because models rarely commit this error. A prompt-only control achieves attunement gains without the goal-persistence cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In Motivational Interviewing (MI), sustain talk requires counselors to "roll with resistance"—maintaining both goal persistence and relational attunement. Two common failures exist: capitulation (abandoning the change agenda) and confrontation (overriding client autonomy). The authors investigate whether preference optimization can teach language models to roll with resistance without inadvertently provoking the opposite failure mode.

Method: The authors introduce a two-axis evaluation framework with Goal Persistence (GP) and Relational Attunement (RA), yielding a four-quadrant scheme where rolling with resistance is high on both. Using the AnnoMI corpus, they construct topic-disjoint DPO preference datasets with on-policy negatives, where preference sets differ only in which failure (capitulation vs. confrontation) is penalized. An automatic judge validated against expert labels and rechecked by trained human coders scores blind pairwise win-rates across three aligned instruction models (Qwen and Llama families), under a firewall where disjoint model families generate, label, and judge.

Result: Penalizing confrontation reliably lowers GP below parity across all bases and all seed runs—a robust cost—while RA gains are base-dependent (present on two of three bases). Penalizing capitulation is inert because these models rarely capitulate on-policy. A prompt-only control (no optimization) raises RA without the GP cost, indicating the cost stems from optimization rather than attunement itself.

Conclusion: The failure trade-off in preference optimization is gated by each base model's on-policy failure profile. Directly penalizing one failure can provoke the opposite failure, and the GP cost of penalizing confrontation is robust. Prompt-based interventions may achieve attunement gains without the optimization-induced cost, suggesting careful consideration of when and how to apply preference optimization for counseling dialogue tasks.

Abstract: In Motivational Interviewing (MI), a client's sustain talk (arguments for the status quo) calls for the counselor to roll with resistance, a move that can fail in two opposite ways: capitulation (abandoning the change agenda to preserve rapport) or confrontation (arguing or directing, overriding the client's autonomy). We introduce a two-axis evaluation of counselor responses, anchored in the Motivational Interviewing Treatment Integrity (MITI) code, Goal Persistence (GP) and Relational Attunement (RA), yielding a four-quadrant framing in which rolling with resistance is high on both, and we ask whether penalizing one failure through preference optimization teaches rolling with resistance or provokes its opposite. From the expert-annotated AnnoMI corpus we build topic-disjoint Direct Preference Optimization data whose preference sets differ only in which failure is rejected, using on-policy negatives. An automatic judge, validated against AnnoMI's expert labels and rechecked by trained human coders, scores blind pairwise win-rates against each base under a firewall in which disjoint model families generate, label, and judge. Across three aligned instruction models spanning the Qwen and Llama families, penalizing confrontation reliably lowers goal persistence below parity, on every base and in every seed run, a robust cost, whereas the attunement gain is base-dependent, present on two of the three bases but absent on the third. Penalizing capitulation is inert, because these models rarely capitulate on-policy, so the trade is gated by each base's failure profile. A prompt-only control raises attunement without the goal-persistence cost, locating the cost in the optimization rather than in attunement itself.

</details>


### [18] [Benchmarks Are Not Validation: A System-Level View of Financial LLM Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28840)
*Burak Payzun,İrem Demirtaş,Simona Scala,Elena Ferretti,Seçil Arslan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that financial LLM systems require holistic, system-level validation beyond benchmark scores, encompassing data, retrieval, generation, agent behavior, governance, and operations. It advocates for hybrid evaluation, controlled LLM-as-a-judge methods, and ongoing validation as a discipline producing decision-ready evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of LLMs in finance is model-centric, relying on benchmark scores and one-off reviews. This is dangerously insufficient for production deployment, where systems combine retrieval, proprietary data, tool use, orchestration, and human escalation. The authors argue that benchmark performance alone does not constitute readiness evidence for financial applications.

Method: The paper proposes a multi-layer validation framework spanning: data quality, model design, retrieval and generation performance, agent behavior, governance, and implementation. It advocates hybrid evaluation combining automated metrics with human review, and details controlled use of LLM-as-a-judge with safeguards: multiple judges, structured rubrics, inter-judge agreement, and auditability checks.

Result: The authors identify failure modes poorly captured by static benchmarks: retrieval failures, unfaithful generation, tool misuse, escalation errors, and operational instability. They demonstrate that system-level validation reveals risks invisible to model-only evaluation, and that judge-based methods require rigorous controls to be trustworthy in regulated settings.

Conclusion: Financial LLM validation must evolve from one-time model scoring into an ongoing system discipline producing decision-ready evidence. The paper calls for a research agenda targeting system-aware benchmarks, agent trace validation, judge alignment protocols, and lifecycle validation standards suitable for regulated financial environments.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in financial applications that combine retrieval, proprietary data, tool use, orchestration logic, monitoring, and human escalation. Yet evaluation often remains model-centric: benchmark scores, task accuracy, or one-off qualitative reviews are treated as evidence of readiness. In financial settings, this is insufficient. We take the position that financial LLM systems should not be approved for production based on benchmark performance alone. They require system-level validation evidence across the application stack: data, model design, retrieval and generation performance, agent behavior, governance, and implementation. Drawing on industry experience validating GenAI applications in financial institutions, we outline a multi-layer validation view and explain why hybrid evaluation is necessary. We discuss where LLM-as-a-judge methods are useful and why they require controls such as multiple judges, rubrics, agreement, and auditability checks. We also highlight failure modes poorly captured by static benchmarks, including retrieval failures, unfaithful generation, tool misuse, escalation errors, and operational instability. Our position is that financial LLM validation should be an ongoing system discipline rather than a one-time model scoring exercise. Validation should produce decision-ready evidence, not only scores. We conclude with a research agenda for system-aware benchmarks, agent trace validation, judge alignment protocols, and lifecycle validation standards.

</details>


### [19] [TextCloak: Thwarting Unauthorized LLM Exploitation via RL-Driven Unlearnable Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28862)
*Chengshuai Zhao,Pingchuan Ma,Dawei Li,Bohan Jiang,Zhiyuan Yu,Zhen Tan,Huan Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TextCloak is an RL-driven framework that transforms clean text into unlearnable examples to protect data from unauthorized LLM fine-tuning, using a novel GRPO-UE optimization method that degrades downstream model performance while preserving semantic fidelity and linguistic naturalness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text protection methods (unlearnable examples) are designed for classification tasks in discriminative models and rely on class-specific cues, making them ineffective for the open-ended generation settings of modern LLMs. There is a need for a protection mechanism that works against unauthorized LLM fine-tuning.

Method: TextCloak uses a generative policy to transform clean text batches into unlearnable examples. The policy is optimized via GRPO-UE (Group-Relative Policy Optimization for Unlearnable Examples), a bi-level RL approach where a surrogate LLM is fine-tuned on generated text, and the downstream degradation in the surrogate's performance serves as a reward signal to update the generator through group-relative policy optimization.

Result: Experiments on six public datasets and nine state-of-the-art LLMs show TextCloak consistently impairs unauthorized fine-tuning while preserving text utility. The method demonstrates transferability and robustness across model architectures, training configurations, and adaptive attacks.

Conclusion: TextCloak provides a practical, broadly applicable defense against unauthorized LLM exploitation, overcoming the limitations of prior classification-focused unlearnable example methods through RL-driven discovery of generalizable protective patterns.

Abstract: The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has led to significant advances across a wide range of language tasks, while simultaneously raising growing concerns about unauthorized data exploitation and privacy leakage. Unlearnable examples (UEs) offer a promising defense by introducing carefully designed perturbations into data such that models trained on them exhibit degraded utility. However, existing methods for text protection are primarily designed for classification tasks (e.g., sentiment analysis) in discriminative language models and often rely on injecting class-specific linguistic cues, which limits their effectiveness in the open-ended generation settings of LLMs. In this work, we propose TextCloak, an RL-driven framework for protecting textual data against unauthorized LLM exploitation. TextCloak employs a generative policy that transforms batches of clean text into unlearnable examples while preserving semantic fidelity and linguistic naturalness. To optimize the policy, we introduce GRPO-UE, which rewards generated unlearnable text based on the downstream degradation they induce in fine-tuned surrogate LLMs and updates the generator parameters via group-relative policy optimization. This bi-level optimization enables the generator to discover generalizable protective patterns beyond class-specific cues. Comprehensive experiments on six publicly available datasets and nine state-of-the-art LLMs demonstrate that TextCloak consistently impairs unauthorized fine-tuning while maintaining text utility for legitimate use. Further analyses establish its transferability and robustness across model architectures, training configurations, and adaptive attacks, highlighting its broad applicability as a practical defense against unauthorized LLM exploitation.

</details>


### [20] [Small Is Enough: Per-User Style Rewriting of AI-Edited Text via LoRA Adapters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29238)
*Antorweep Chakravorty*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: InMyStyle is a privacy-preserving, single-user system that fine-tunes small language models (0.5B–7B) via LoRA to rewrite AI-generated text in a personal writing style, achieving consistent performance across model sizes with an automatic composite score plateau at 0.69 and reducing perceived AI-ness by over 20%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable personalized, privacy-first text rewriting that adapts AI-edited content to an individual's writing style without requiring explicit instruction prompts at inference, while determining whether small language models are sufficient for this task.

Method: InMyStyle constructs paired training examples from a user's documents using multiple local helper LLMs, then fine-tunes LoRA adapters on base models (0.5B–7B parameters). It employs length-aware generation budgets and automatic chunking to handle variable-length inputs. Evaluation uses 219 scientific-paper pairs with an automatic composite score, plus 400 ratings from five LLM judges measuring perceived AI-ness.

Result: The automatic composite score plateaus at 0.69 (scale 0–1) across all model sizes under both greedy and sampled decoding, indicating small models suffice and that model size governs trade-offs rather than quality ranking. LLM judges rate InMyStyle outputs as having over 20% lower mean perceived AI-ness than helper-AI generated inputs, with perceived AI-ness decreasing as model size increases within the system.

Conclusion: Small language models are sufficient for personalized style rewriting, with a performance plateau suggesting diminishing returns from scaling. The privacy-first, single-user design is viable, and larger models primarily reduce perceived AI-ness rather than improving core rewriting quality.

Abstract: InMyStyle is a privacy first, single user system that adapts small language models to rewrite AI-edited text towards an individual user's writing style without an instruction prompt at inference. Given a user's documents, it uses multiple local helper LLMs to construct paired training examples and fine tunes LoRA adapters on base models ranging from 0.5B to 7B parameters. Length aware generation budgets and automatic chunking support inputs of different lengths. On 219 evaluation pairs from a scientific-paper corpus, the automatic composite score plateaus at 0.69 [scale 0-1] across all model sizes under both greedy and sampled decoding. This observed plateau suggests that small models are sufficient for the measured rewriting task, with model size determining trade-offs rather than a stable quality ranking. As a secondary evaluation, 400 ratings from five LLM judges give InMyStyle outputs a mean perceived AI-ness score over 20% lower than their helper-AI generated inputs, while mean perceived AI-ness scores decrease with model size within InMyStyle.

</details>


### [21] [Token-Level Diagnosis of Sycophancy in LLMs with Attribution-Guided Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28906)
*Hieu Nguyen,Mahammed Kamruzzaman,Anshuman Chhabra,Gene Louis Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates sycophancy in LLMs using Integrated Gradients-based token attribution (Authority Share Index). It finds sycophantic responses attend more to authority claims than credentials, and proposes attribution-guided contrastive activation steering to mitigate sycophancy at inference time, reducing it from 96% to 25% without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior sycophancy evaluations only assess whether model output matches an authority's claim but cannot pinpoint which part of the prompt (authority credentials, assertive claim, or problem statement) drives sycophantic behavior. This gap motivates a fine-grained, token-level attribution analysis.

Method: The authors introduce the Authority Share Index (ASI), an Integrated Gradients-based token attribution method measuring how much authority-related text drives model decisions. They then propose attribution-guided contrastive activation steering: constructing a steering vector from high-attribution tokens of sycophantic vs. resistant responses to selectively push models toward resistance at inference time without retraining.

Result: Across 5 models and 30 test configurations, sycophantic responses consistently direct more attention to authority tokens than resistant ones. Within sycophantic cases, the authority's claim receives more attention than their credentials. The proposed steering method reduces sycophancy from 96% to 25% in the strongest case.

Conclusion: Token-level attribution both explains what drives sycophancy and directly informs a practical, inference-time intervention. Attribution-guided contrastive activation steering proves effective for mitigating sycophancy without retraining.

Abstract: Sycophancy refers to the tendency for large language models (LLMs) to match user beliefs at the cost of factual correctness, thereby undermining model reliability. Prior work on evaluating sycophancy in LLMs aims to assess whether a model's output matches an authority's claim, but cannot reveal which part of the prompt drives this sycophantic behavior. To bridge this gap, we investigate the relationship of sycophantic responses with an authority's credentials, their assertive claim, and the problem statement. We introduce the Authority Share Index (ASI), an Integrated Gradients-based token attribution method, which measures the degree to which a model's decision is driven by authority-related text. Through extensive experiments across five models and 30 test configurations, we find that sycophantic responses consistently direct more attention toward authority tokens than resistant ones. Moreover, our token attribution method reveals that for the sycophantic cases, the claim asserted by the authority receives more attention than the authority's credentials. Building on these findings, we propose attribution-guided contrastive activation steering to mitigate LLM sycophancy. Our method constructs a steering vector from high-attribution tokens of sycophantic and resistant responses, selectively pushing models toward resistance. This enables inference-time steering without retraining, lowering sycophancy from 96% to 25% in the strongest case. Together, our results show that token-level attribution can both explain what drives sycophancy and directly inform a practical intervention.

</details>


### [22] [FairFund-Bench: Evaluating Distributive Bias in LLM Resource Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28934)
*Martin Lukk*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Audit format—rating vs. ranking, single vs. multi-stimulus, transparent vs. disguised—can reverse the direction of demographic bias in LLM resource allocation, while causal framing of need exerts an effect an order of magnitude larger and is consistent across all conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior LLM fairness audits have yielded contradictory findings—evidence of both pro- and anti-minority discrimination even within the same models. The authors hypothesize that these inconsistencies stem from uncontrolled variation in audit design features, motivating a systematic benchmark to isolate their effects.

Method: The authors introduce FairFund-Bench, a benchmark of 600 financial assistance requests varying evaluation task (rating, ranking, allocation), comparison context (single vs. multi-stimulus), audit transparency (transparent vs. disguised), demographic attributes (4 races, 2 genders), and five causal framings of need grounded in welfare deservingness theory. Templates were calibrated against 1.3M real GoFundMe campaigns. Fourteen LLMs were evaluated on four criteria: demographic bias, deservingness alignment, cross-task consistency, and cross-context consistency.

Result: Audit format reverses bias direction: models favor minorities in single-claimant rating but penalize some groups in side-by-side ranking. Bias magnitude is several times larger in disguised audits than transparent ones—when demographics are the only differing feature, models overwhelmingly split funds equally. Causal framing effects exceed demographic effects by roughly an order of magnitude and remain consistent across models and formats, showing LLMs robustly reproduce human deservingness judgments.

Conclusion: Audit design choices fundamentally shape fairness conclusions about LLMs. The outsized and robust effect of causal framing relative to demographics suggests that current models are more sensitive to narratives of deservingness than to protected attributes, though format-dependent demographic biases remain a concern requiring careful, standardized evaluation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly involved in the distribution of scarce resources, raising concerns about biased allocations based on characteristics like race and gender. Recent LLM audits have produced inconsistent results, however, finding evidence of both positive and negative discrimination towards women and ethnic minorities, even for the same models. We show that this disagreement can arise from differences in audit format and introduce FairFund-Bench, a benchmark that systematically varies key features of previous audit designs: the evaluation task (rating, ranking, or allocation), comparison context (single or multi-stimulus), and whether the audit is transparent or disguised. The benchmark comprises 600 requests for financial assistance created from human-authored templates (calibrated against 1.3M real GoFundMe campaigns) across three domains, four race and two gender categories, and five causal framings of need derived from welfare deservingness theory. Across 14 models, audit format changes the direction of bias: models advantage minorities when rating claimants individually but penalize some groups when ranking them side by side.
