Bias magnitude, though small overall, is several times greater in disguised audits than in transparent ones, where, faced with appeals differing only in claimants' names, models overwhelmingly split funds equally. Causal framing effects, by contrast, exceed demographic effects by roughly an order of magnitude and are consistent across models and audit formats, indicating that current LLMs robustly reproduce human deservingness evaluations. The benchmark scores models on four criteria (demographic bias, deservingness alignment, cross-task consistency, and cross-context consistency), is publicly available, and can be readily adapted to other substantive domains.

</details>


### [23] [FriendBench: Benchmarking Dyadic Familiarity Inference in Humans and Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29602)
*Jeffrey M. Girard,Jason Z. Zheng,Jacqueline R. Vertino,Antony D'Avirro,Benjamin Peloquin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FriendBench is a benchmark using 20-second dyadic ice-breaker clips to test whether AI models can infer familiarity (friends vs. strangers) from social interaction cues. Across text, audio, and video modalities, the best models match human accuracy but exhibit a different response bias (leaning toward "stranger"), and only humans benefit from visible behavior beyond speech.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Social understanding requires reading behavior beyond mere words. Existing benchmarks rarely test the ability to infer relational context (familiarity) from brief, naturalistic interactions where all pairs answer identical prompts — making the manner of interaction the only diagnostic signal.

Method: The authors constructed FriendBench: 96 balanced dyads (friends vs. strangers) recorded in 20-second ice-breaker conversations. They compared 26 models from 7 companies against matched human panels across text (transcripts), audio, and video modalities. Statistical comparisons of accuracy and response bias were performed between models and human crowds.

Result: The best model and the human crowd are statistically indistinguishable on accuracy across all modalities. However, humans maintain balanced predictions across "friend" and "stranger," while top models systematically favor "stranger" — reflecting a difference in effective prior, not discriminatory ability. Richer modalities help both unequally, and only humans gain additional accuracy from visible behavior on top of speech.

Conclusion: Current AI models can reach human-level accuracy in social inference from brief dyadic interactions, but they achieve this through a different decision strategy (biased prior toward "stranger"). Visual behavioral cues uniquely benefit human perception, highlighting a gap in how models integrate multimodal social signals.

Abstract: Reading a social situation often depends on behavior, not words alone. We introduce FriendBench, a benchmark for inferring whether two people are already familiar or are meeting as strangers, from a 20-second clip of a dyadic ice-breaker conversation. Every pair answers the same type of prompt, so only the manner of interaction can reveal the answer. Across text, audio, and video, we compare 26 models from seven companies against matched human panels over 96 balanced dyads. The best model and the human crowd are statistically indistinguishable on accuracy in every modality, but reach it differently: humans stay balanced across the two answers, while the strongest models lean toward "stranger"---a difference in effective prior, not discrimination. Richer channels help both unequally, and only humans gain from visible behavior on top of speech. We release the stimuli, human ratings, and model predictions.

</details>


### [24] [BLADE: Boundary-Expanded and Layer-Adaptive Dynamic Exit for Efficient LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28966)
*Keshu Fu,Keqin Peng,Jun Bai,Shuhan Qin,Chen Li,Junzhu Liang,Yefei Chen,Jiaqi Li,Yuanxin Ouyang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BLADE introduces a lightweight dynamic early-exit framework for LLM reasoning that uses multi-granular checkpoints (sentence, self-doubt, paragraph boundaries) and automatic probe layer selection to terminate reasoning early, reducing token generation by 15.8–24.8% while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing early-exit methods for LLM reasoning rely on detecting explicit self-doubt expressions, missing many earlier termination opportunities. Expanding to ordinary reasoning boundaries increases coverage but introduces highly diverse intermediate states whose predictive signals are scattered across different hidden layers, making fixed-layer probing suboptimal.

Method: BLADE constructs checkpoints at three granularities: sentence boundaries, self-doubt expressions, and paragraph boundaries. Robust training labels are derived via repeated answer completions to estimate sufficiency of prefixes. A compact subset of informative probe layers is automatically learned per checkpoint type, avoiding fixed or all-layer representations. At inference, calibrated predictions are combined with checkpoint-specific confirmation rules to balance early-exit responsiveness against premature termination risk.

Result: On five benchmarks using Qwen3 reasoning models, BLADE preserves near-baseline accuracy while reducing generated tokens by 24.8% on Qwen3-8B and 15.8% on Qwen3-4B. Ablations confirm the benefits of diverse checkpoint types and automatic layer selection.

Conclusion: Multi-granular boundary detection combined with learned layer-adaptive probing provides an effective strategy for reducing redundant computation in LLM reasoning without sacrificing task performance.

Abstract: Large language models often improve task performance by generating long reasoning traces, but the resulting computation is frequently wasted on redundant verification and revision. Existing probe-based early-exit approaches mainly inspect explicit self-doubt expressions, leaving many earlier termination opportunities undetected. Expanding inspection to ordinary reasoning boundaries improves coverage, but also exposes highly diverse intermediate states whose predictive information may reside in different hidden layers. We present Boundary-Expanded and Layer-Adaptive Dynamic Exit for Efficient LLM Reasoning (BLADE), a lightweight framework that dynamically terminates reasoning by estimating whether the generated prefix is sufficient for correct answering. BLADE constructs multi-granular checkpoints from sentence, self-doubt, and paragraph boundaries, and derives robust training labels through repeated answer completions. It further learns a compact subset of informative probe layers instead of relying on fixed choices or expensive representations from all layers. At inference time, calibrated predictions are combined with checkpoint-specific confirmation rules to balance responsiveness and premature-exit risk. Experiments on five benchmarks and two Qwen3 reasoning models show that BLADE preserves near-baseline accuracy while reducing generated tokens by 24.8% on Qwen3-8B and 15.8% on Qwen3-4B. Ablation studies further confirm the benefits of diverse checkpoints and automatic layer selection, demonstrating an effective approach to more efficient LLM reasoning.

</details>


### [25] [Mixture-of-Translators: Translating KV Caches Across Heterogeneous Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28979)
*Jin-woo Lee,Minkyung Song,Junghyun Oh,Seunghoon Han,Soyoung Park,Gwangseon Jang,Sungsu Lim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MoT proposes a framework to translate KV caches between heterogeneous LLMs using multiple translator modules and a Context Correction Loss, enabling cross-architecture cache reuse for multi-agent reasoning and long-context generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous LLM systems waste computation by redundantly prefilling or storing separate KV caches for identical contexts, since existing caches are model-specific and non-transferable across architectures. This limits scalability in multi-model reasoning and long-context scenarios.

Method: MoT employs multiple translator modules (mixture-of-translators) to map source LLM KV caches into a target LLM's cache space, capturing diverse source-target mappings. A Context Correction Loss aligns the replayed target trajectory with the native target trajectory. The framework addresses two failure modes: propagated translation shift (early injection) and last-state shift (late injection) through translator mixtures and target-side correction.

Result: Across Qwen2.5, GPT-2, and OPT translations, MoT achieves 51.0% average closed-set QA accuracy and 0.43 average extractive QA F1 for Qwen2.5-7B-scale translation. In case studies, it retains 96.3% of direct-context quality in long-context cache-augmented generation.

Conclusion: MoT enables scalable, quality-preserving KV cache reuse across heterogeneous LLMs, reducing redundant computation in multi-model and long-context workflows.

Abstract: Heterogeneous Large Language Model (LLM) systems increasingly rely on shared contexts, retrieved evidence, and multi-agent dialogue histories, yet their internal key-value (KV) caches remain model-specific and cannot be reused across architectures. Consequently, each model must repeatedly prefill or store caches for the same context, limiting the scalability of multi-model reasoning and long-context generation. We propose Mixture-of-Translators(MoT), a cache translation framework that maps context KV caches from a source LLM into the cache space of a target LLM. Unlike prior approaches that depend on a single projection path or global shared latent space, MoT uses multiple translator modules to capture diverse source--target mappings. To further reduce residual translation error, we introduce a Context Correction Loss that aligns the replayed target trajectory with the native target trajectory. We reveal two competing failure modes in cache translation: propagated translation shift from early injection and last-state shift from late injection. MoT addresses them through translator mixtures and target-side correction. Across homogeneous and heterogeneous translations among Qwen2.5, GPT-2, and OPT models, MoT preserves downstream QA performance, including Qwen2.5-7B-scale translation with 51.0% average closed-set QA accuracy and 0.43 average extractive QA F1. In practical case studies, MoT enables quality-preserving memory reuse for multi-agent reasoning and retains 96.3% of direct-context quality in long-context cache-augmented generation, demonstrating scalable KV cache reuse across heterogeneous LLMs.

</details>


### [26] [PARALLEL: A Prefrontal-Aligned Reinforcement inspired Approach for Language-Model Learning under Explicit Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28982)
*Namkyung Yoon,Sanghong Kim,Hwangnam Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PARALLEL introduces a dual-controller mechanism — combining goal-related and uncertainty-related signals — that assigns sample-dependent update intensities using reinforcement-inspired utility-cost feedback, enabling more efficient, stable, and selective language model adaptation while retaining 94–99% of full-adaptation performance across reasoning and summarization tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional language model adaptation applies uniform updates to all training samples, ignoring that different samples provide varying local update benefits. This wastes computational resources on low-benefit updates and can lead to unnecessary parameter changes, motivating a more sample-aware, efficient adaptation strategy.

Method: PARALLEL (Prefrontal-Aligned Reinforcement Inspired Approach for Language-model LEarning) models two control signals — goal-related and uncertainty-related — as separate controllers, fusing them with the model's current representation. A reinforcement-inspired controller then determines sample-dependent update intensity based on immediate utility-cost feedback, effectively learning when and how strongly to adapt for each sample.

Result: PARALLEL retains 94.1–99.2% of full-adaptation performance while using updates more efficiently than selective baselines. On XSum and CNN/DailyMail, it preserves 96.9–98.6% of ROUGE-1/ROUGE-2 and 98.8–98.9% of ROUGE-L scores. Under matched cumulative adaptation time or GPU energy, PARALLEL achieves higher ARC accuracy and exhibits more stable late-stage adaptation trajectories than full adaptation.

Conclusion: Learning when and how strongly to update each sample supports stable, efficient post-deployment stream adaptation while avoiding unnecessary parameter updates, demonstrating that sample-adaptive update strategies are both practical and performant for real-world deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Recent language models achieve strong performance across a variety of tasks, but conventional adaptation applies updates uniformly across training samples regardless of their local update benefit. We propose PARALLEL, a prefrontal-aligned reinforcement inspired approach for language-model learning. Inspired by the complementary roles of goal-related and uncertainty-related control, PARALLEL represents these forms of information as separate controller signals and combines them with the current model representation. A reinforcement-inspired controller assigns sample-dependent update intensity using immediate utility-cost feedback. PARALLEL therefore learns when and how strongly to adapt to each sample, prioritizing beneficial updates while limiting unnecessary parameter changes. PARALLEL uses available updates more efficiently than selective baselines while retaining 94.1--99.2\% of Full-adaptation performance. Beyond multiple-choice reasoning, experiments on XSum and CNN/DailyMail show that PARALLEL retains 96.9--98.6\% of the ROUGE-1 and ROUGE-2 scores achieved by Full adaptation and 98.8--98.9\% of the corresponding ROUGE-L scores. When compared at the same cumulative adaptation time or GPU energy, PARALLEL achieves higher ARC accuracy and exhibits a more stable late-stage adaptation trajectory than Full adaptation in the representative run. These results show that learning when and how strongly to update each sample supports stable and efficient post-deployment stream adaptation while avoiding unnecessary updates.

</details>


### [27] [From Inline Notes to Collected Commentaries: Toward Context-Preserving Organization of Exegetical Knowledge in Classical Chinese Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29044)
*Ke Liang,Qi Su,Churen Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper frames collected commentary compilation as an NLP task, proposing a framework combining prompt chaining and cross-source mention clustering to automatically organize Confucian annotations with their associated main-text segments, achieving >97% CoNLL F1 on the Classic of Mountains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inline notes and collected commentaries are vital to the Confucian exegetical tradition but have been largely neglected by computational methods. There is a need to computationally model and organize these annotations while preserving their contextual dependency on the main text.

Method: The framework uses (1) a two-step prompt chaining approach: first identifying which main-text segment an annotation is associated with, then classifying its exegetical function; and (2) cross-source mention clustering to integrate commentary across different editions by grouping equivalent annotations.

Result: The proposed method achieves a CoNLL F1 score above 97% in a case study on the Classic of Mountains (Shan Hai Jing).

Conclusion: The framework provides a foundation for large-scale organization of historical exegetical knowledge and supports a broad range of downstream philological and NLP tasks.

Abstract: Inline notes and collected commentaries are important forms of scholarly communication that evolved within the Confucian exegetical tradition, yet have received little computational attention. Drawing on traditional Chinese exegetics and philology, this paper formulates collected commentary compilation as an NLP task and proposes a computational framework that preserves the contextual dependency of inline notes while enabling their automatic compilation and exegetical knowledge organization. It combines two-step prompt chaining for identifying the associated main-text segments and exegetical functions of annotations with cross-source mention clustering for integrating commentary across editions, achieving a CoNLL F1 score above 97% in a case study on the Classic of Mountains. Our framework lays the foundation for the large-scale organization of historical exegetical knowledge, thereby supporting a broad range of downstream philological and NLP tasks.

</details>


### [28] [Tokenizer-Agnostic Engram Module](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29065)
*Jia Peng Lim,Hai Leong Chieu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes replacing XOR-based N-gram hashing in Deepseek's Engram memory module with polynomial hashing over byte sequences, enabling tokenizer-agnostic compatibility so that Engram embeddings can be reused across models with different tokenizers without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deepseek's Engram module ties N-gram embedding lookup to a specific tokenizer via token-level hashing. This means a model using a different tokenizer must train its own Engram embeddings from scratch, severely limiting reusability. The authors aim to decouple the hashing mechanism from the tokenizer to enable cross-tokenizer embedding reuse.

Method: The authors replace the original XOR-based N-gram hashing with general polynomial hashing operating over byte sequences (not token IDs). Instead of partitioning hash space into disjoint N-gram-specific tables, they use a joint embedding space across all N-gram orders. This ensures that byte-equivalent token sequences produce identical hash values regardless of the tokenizer used, achieving hash equivalence.

Result: The simple substitution of polynomial hashing over byte sequences produces performance comparable to the original tokenizer-dependent XOR hashing, while successfully achieving tokenizer-agnosticism.

Conclusion: Polynomial hashing over byte sequences is a practical, low-cost modification that makes Engram embeddings reusable across tokenizers, removing a key barrier to wider adoption of conditional memory modules.

Abstract: Deepseek's Engram, a conditional memory module, was introduced to trade-off storage versus reasoning in large language models. However, the module relies on token-level $N$-gram hashing for Engram embedding lookup, introducing a tight coupling to the tokenizer used: a model with a different tokenizer would have to train its own Engram embeddings from scratch. To improve the reusability of Engram embeddings, we propose a change to the hashing routine, enabling compatibility between Engram models using different tokenizers. Instead of modelling disjoint $N$-gram spaces, we treat $N$-gram as a method to sample potentially useful byte sequences, from all possible byte sequences across tokens. We replace the XOR-based hashing with the general polynomial hashing with a joint embedding space across $N$. This work investigates the possible trade-offs and shows that this simple substitution produces comparable performance and achieves tokenizer-agnosticism: hash equivalence for byte-equivalent token sequences.

</details>


### [29] [Semantics of Subterfuge: Benchmarking Legal Deception Detection Against General-domain State-of-the-Art](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29066)
*Theekshana Samaradiwakara,Nisansa de Silva,George C. Lobb*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A survey and empirical comparison of NLP-based automatic deception detection, evaluating fine-tuned transformers and LLMs across legal and general domains. Fine-tuned models excel in data-rich settings, while few-shot LLMs remain competitive in low-resource legal contexts. Chain-of-Thought prompting often underperforms direct classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deception detection is critical for legal proceedings, law enforcement, and online security, yet human judgment suffers from limited accuracy and scalability. NLP offers a data-driven alternative, but the landscape—spanning feature-based ML to LLMs—lacks unified empirical benchmarking, particularly regarding domain sensitivity across legal vs. general-domain settings.

Method: The authors survey the evolution of NLP-based ADD from feature-based machine learning to LLM approaches, then conduct a unified empirical evaluation across seven datasets (two legal, five general-domain). They compare six fine-tuned transformer models and seven LLMs under four prompting strategies, including direct classification and Chain-of-Thought prompting.

Result: Results reveal strong domain sensitivity: fine-tuned transformer models perform best in data-rich general domains, while few-shot LLMs remain competitive in low-resource legal settings. Notably, Chain-of-Thought prompting frequently underperforms direct classification, challenging assumptions about its universal benefit in deception detection tasks.

Conclusion: The findings underscore the necessity of domain adaptation and interpretable systems for high-stakes legal applications. No single approach dominates all settings; instead, practitioners should select methods based on domain characteristics and resource availability, with careful consideration of prompting strategy effectiveness.

Abstract: Deception detection has critical implications for legal proceedings, law enforcement, and online security. Although human judgment is limited in accuracy and scalability, Natural Language Processing (NLP) offers a data-driven alternative. We present a survey and comparative analysis of NLP-based Automatic Deception Detection (ADD) focusing on the legal domain, reviewing the evolution from feature-based machine learning to Large Language Model (LLM) approaches. We conduct a unified empirical evaluation across seven datasets (two legal, five general-domain), comparing six fine-tuned transformer models and seven LLMs under four prompting strategies. The results show strong domain sensitivity, with fine-tuned models excelling in data-rich general domains and few-shot LLMs remaining competitive in low-resource legal settings. Chain-of-Thought prompting often underperforms direct classification. These findings highlight the need for domain adaptation and interpretable systems in high-stakes legal contexts.

</details>


### [30] [Faster but Different: Diagnosing and Controlling Content Drift in Accelerated Multimodal Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29079)
*Yaoxuan Dou,Yang Shu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Training-free acceleration of diffusion-based MLLMs can silently alter generated content. This paper diagnoses the root causes (stale visual/generated-text states) and shows that shortening KV-cache refresh intervals provides a tunable speed–agreement frontier, achieving near-exact consistency at 1.3× speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training-free acceleration methods for diffusion-based multimodal LLMs (dMLLMs) promise easier deployment, but their impact on output consistency is unstudied. The authors investigate whether such acceleration silently changes generated content, a critical reliability concern for serving-time deployments.

Method: A paired diagnostic study on 300 real images comparing Fast-dLLM accelerated outputs against unaccelerated baselines. Methods include: (1) confidence-threshold tuning analysis, (2) state-refresh ablation and image-swap interventions to identify drift sources (stale visual and generated-text states), (3) KV-cache refresh interval manipulation to establish speed–agreement frontiers, (4) replication on dLLM-Cache and LaViDa, (5) targeted content-substitution audit on 50 low-agreement pairs, (6) blinded two-annotator factual-error evaluation, and (7) testing of adaptive and smoothed-refresh variants against fixed-interval refresh.

Result: Confidence-threshold tuning changes decoding behavior but not baseline agreement. Shortening the KV-cache refresh interval yields a monotonic speed–agreement frontier with near-exact agreement at 1.3× speedup. Stale visual and generated-text states are identified as primary drift contributors. dLLM-Cache recovers agreement only when both caches are tightened, eliminating its speed advantage. The threshold-insensitivity and refresh recovery replicate across independent prompts and images. The factual-error difference between accelerated and baseline outputs is 0.00 (95% CI [-0.17, +0.17]), detecting no difference but not establishing equivalence. No adaptive or smoothed-refresh variant beats the fixed interval at matched compute.

Conclusion: The work provides a paired diagnostic methodology and an implementation-scoped consistency control (KV-cache refresh interval tuning), explicitly not an accuracy or safety guarantee. The key practical insight is that a fixed short refresh interval offers the best speed–agreement trade-off among tested approaches.

Abstract: Training-free acceleration makes diffusion-based multimodal large language models (dMLLMs) more deployable, but it may silently change generated content. We study this serving-time consistency problem on 300 real images, comparing Fast-dLLM outputs with the same model's unaccelerated outputs. Across the mild parallelism induced in our long-form setting (1.05--1.25 committed tokens per step), confidence-threshold tuning changes decoding behavior but not baseline agreement. State-refresh ablations and an image-swap intervention instead identify stale visual and generated-text states as contributors to drift. For the tested Fast-dLLM implementation, shortening the KV-cache refresh interval yields a monotonic speed--agreement frontier and near-exact agreement at a measured 1.3x speedup. The initial diagnosis also appears with dLLM-Cache and LaViDa, although dLLM-Cache recovers agreement only after both caches are tightened, which removes its speed advantage. Independent prompts and images reproduce the threshold-insensitivity and refresh recovery. A targeted audit finds genuine content substitution in half of 50 low-agreement pairs. In a separate blinded two-annotator evaluation, the pooled accelerated-minus-baseline factual-error difference is 0.00 (95% CI [-0.17,+0.17]); this sample detects no difference but does not establish factual equivalence. Finally, none of the tested adaptive or smoothed-refresh variants beats the fixed interval at matched compute. Our contribution is a paired diagnostic and an implementation-scoped consistency control, not an accuracy or safety guarantee.

</details>


### [31] [Can Zero-Shot LLMs Predict Child Malnutrition? A Fairness and Temporal Robustness Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29082)
*Muhammad Ashad Kabir,Md Ahshanul Haque*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study evaluates GPT-4o-mini in a zero-shot setting for predicting child stunting from Bangladesh DHS survey data (2007–2022). The LLM matched a random forest baseline in balanced accuracy, showed higher sensitivity for detecting stunting cases, and demonstrated temporal robustness, but exhibited fairness disparities across residence and wealth groups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early identification of children vulnerable to malnutrition is critical in low- and middle-income countries, particularly South Asia, for timely intervention. The authors explore whether pretrained LLMs can offer a low-barrier, semantically flexible alternative to traditional supervised models without requiring task-specific training data.

Method: Maternal, child, healthcare, and household characteristics from BDHS (2007–2022) were converted into semantically interpretable prompt-based representations. GPT-4o-mini performed zero-shot stunting prediction. Performance was benchmarked against a supervised random forest classifier, with fairness evaluated across demographic/socioeconomic subgroups and temporal robustness assessed across survey waves.

Result: Zero-shot GPT-4o-mini achieved comparable balanced accuracy to the random forest baseline, substantially higher sensitivity for stunting case detection, consistent performance across child sex groups, and stable predictions across BDHS waves. However, notable fairness disparities emerged across residence (urban/rural) and household wealth categories.

Conclusion: Foundation models show promise for public health prediction tasks with competitive zero-shot performance, but observed disparities across socioeconomic dimensions warrant caution and further fairness-focused investigation before real-world deployment in public health settings.

Abstract: Child malnutrition remains a major public health challenge in low- and middle-income countries, particularly in South Asia, where early identification of vulnerable children is critical for timely intervention and resource allocation. This study aims to evaluate the feasibility, fairness, and temporal robustness of using a pretrained large language model (LLM) in a zero-shot setting for child stunting prediction using population health survey data. Using Bangladesh Demographic and Health Survey (BDHS) data collected between 2007 and 2022, we transformed maternal, child, healthcare, and household characteristics into semantically interpretable prompt-based representations and evaluated GPT-4o-mini for zero-shot stunting prediction, comparing its performance against a random forest baseline and assessing fairness across demographic and socioeconomic groups as well as temporal robustness across survey waves. The results demonstrate that zero-shot inference using GPT-4o-mini achieved comparable balanced accuracy to the supervised baseline while exhibiting substantially higher sensitivity for identifying stunting cases, relatively consistent performance across child sex groups, and stable predictive behaviour across BDHS waves; however, important fairness disparities were observed across residence and household wealth categories, highlighting the need for further investigation before deployment of foundation models in public health prediction settings.

</details>


### [32] [M3-DuplexBench: A Multi-Turn, Multilingual, Multidomain Benchmark for Full-Duplex Spoken Dialogue Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29125)
*Ryo Fukuda,Atsushi Ando,Hiroki Kanagawa,Takatomo Kano,Marc Delcroix,Naohiro Tawara,Yuya Chiba*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: M3-DuplexBench is a multi-turn, multilingual, multidomain benchmark for evaluating full-duplex spoken dialogue systems, supporting English and Japanese across casual conversation and QA tasks, with analysis of dialogue context effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fair comparisons of full-duplex spoken dialogue systems in multi-turn conversations are challenging, and existing benchmarks lack coverage of languages and dialogue domains.

Method: The authors introduce M3-DuplexBench, a benchmark supporting English and Japanese, covering casual conversation and multi-turn QA. Evaluation is conducted under multiple dialogue context settings: single-turn, user-only, and teacher-forced full-context.

Result: Experiments reveal model-specific turn-taking characteristics, clear performance gaps across languages and domains, and mixed effects of dialogue context on model behavior.

Conclusion: M3-DuplexBench enables more comprehensive and fair evaluation of FDSDSs, highlighting the influence of language, domain, and dialogue history on system performance.

Abstract: Full-duplex spoken dialogue systems (FDSDSs) can listen while speaking, enabling natural behaviors such as smooth turn-taking, backchannel handling, and user barge-in handling. However, fair comparisons in multi-turn conversations remain a challenge. In addition, existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of languages and dialogue domains. We propose M3-DuplexBench, a multi-turn, multilingual, multidomain benchmark for FDSDSs. M3-DuplexBench supports English and Japanese and covers both casual conversation and multi-turn question answering. In addition, we evaluate models under multiple dialogue context settings, including single-turn, user-only, and teacher-forced full-context settings, to analyze how dialogue history affects model behavior. Experiments with recent FDSDSs reveal model-specific turn-taking characteristics, clear performance gaps across languages and domains, and mixed effects of dialogue context.

</details>


### [33] [Authorship Verification of Transcribed German-Language Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29168)
*Oren Halvani,Sophie Titze*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper extends Authorship Verification (AV) from written text to spoken language transcripts (German YouTube videos). Evaluating ten AV methods on 300 videos from 150 speakers, traditional character- and token n-gram approaches achieve up to 88% accuracy and 90% AUC, significantly outperforming modern transformer-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two underexplored gaps motivate this work: (1) AV research has focused almost exclusively on written text, ignoring spoken language despite its prevalence in videos; (2) AV studies predominantly target English, with German and other languages receiving little attention.

Method: The authors compiled three corpora of German-language video transcripts (300 videos, 150 speakers). They applied ten AV methods spanning traditional stylometric approaches (character n-grams, token n-grams) and modern transformer-based models, evaluating each method's ability to verify whether two video transcripts originate from the same speaker.

Result: Traditional n-gram-based AV methods achieved the best results (up to 88% accuracy, 90% AUC). Transformer-based approaches performed significantly worse across all three corpora, indicating that modern neural methods do not yet surpass simple stylometric techniques for this task.

Conclusion: Traditional AV methods remain both competitive and relevant, even when applied to spoken-language transcripts in German. The findings caution against prematurely discarding established stylometric techniques in favor of more complex neural architectures.

Abstract: Authorship Verification (AV) represents an important subfield of digital text forensics and addresses the fundamental question of whether two texts were written by the same author. Although the field has made substantial progress over the past two decades, several important challenges remain unresolved or underexplored. For instance, most AV research has focused on written texts, despite the fact that language is expressed not only in written but also in spoken form, such as in videos. Moreover, existing AV studies have predominantly concentrated on English, while other languages, including German, have received comparatively little attention. To address these research gaps, we apply AV to spoken language in the form of transcripts of German-language videos and examine the effectiveness of established AV methods in verifying a speaker's identity across video pairs. Our experimental evaluation, based on a total of ten AV methods applied to three self-compiled corpora comprising 300 videos from 150 speakers, shows that the best performance (up to 88% accuracy and 90% AUC) is achieved by traditional AV approaches based on simple character- and token n-gram representations. In contrast, more modern transformer-based approaches perform significantly worse on all evaluated corpora. Our results therefore suggest that traditional methods in the field of AV remain both competitive and relevant.

</details>


### [34] [Learning Latent Reasoning Traces for Scalar Reward Models End-to-End](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29185)
*Sanwoo Lee,Clive Bai,Hsiu-Yuan Huang,Kun Liang,Weijie Liu,Yunfang Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LatentRM introduces a reward modeling framework that treats reasoning traces as discrete latent variables, optimized end-to-end via on-policy learning to explicitly maximize downstream scalar reward likelihood, tightly coupling generative reasoning with precise scoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalar reward models are efficient and probabilistic but rely on superficial cues and fail on complex/OOD tasks. Generative reward models offer robust reasoning but lack numerical flexibility and probabilistic interpretability. Existing hybrid approaches use off-policy multi-task learning, which does not ensure that generated reasoning traces actively benefit scalar reward prediction, creating a mismatch between reasoning and scoring.

Method: LatentRM models intermediate reasoning traces as discrete latent variables and performs on-policy end-to-end optimization to maximize the likelihood of downstream scalar rewards. This tightly couples the reasoning-based evaluation with precise numerical scoring, unlike prior off-policy multi-task hybrids.

Result: LatentRM outperforms scalar, generative, and hybrid reward models on both preference modeling and policy alignment across in-distribution and OOD datasets, as well as in RLHF settings spanning open-ended conversation to complex reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: LatentRM effectively bridges the gap between scalar and generative reward modeling by ensuring that latent reasoning traces directly contribute to reward prediction through on-policy optimization, yielding superior generalization and alignment performance.

Abstract: Reward models (RMs) are central to aligning large language models with human preferences via reinforcement learning. Although traditional scalar RMs enable efficient and probabilistic reward modeling, they rely on superficial cues that fail to generalize to complex or out-of-distribution (OOD) tasks. Conversely, generative RMs leverage extensive reasoning to improve robustness on challenging tasks, but their natural language-based scores lack the numerical flexibility and probabilistic interpretability that scalar RMs offer. While recent approaches combine both paradigms through off-policy multi-task learning, such parallel optimization does not guarantee that generated reasoning traces actively align with or benefit downstream scalar reward prediction. To address this mismatch, we propose LatentRM, a reward modeling framework that learns intermediate reasoning traces as discrete latent variables to explicitly maximize the likelihood of downstream scalar rewards. Through on-policy optimization of the latent reasoning space end-to-end, LatentRM tightly couples deep reasoning-based evaluation with precise scoring. Extensive validations on in-distribution and OOD datasets and RLHF show that LatentRM outperforms scalar, generative, and hybrid RMs on preference modeling and policy alignment across tasks ranging from open-ended conversation to complex reasoning.

</details>


### [35] [Detecting Experiential Intertextuality Across Migration Routes: Beyond Surface Similarity in French Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29188)
*Sakayo Toadoum Sari,Nelly Robin,Michelle Auzanneau,Lakhdar Sais,Veronique Petit,Marie Veniard,Said Jabbour,Fabien Delorme*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces "experiential intertextuality detection" — automatically identifying shared lived experiences across migration narratives — and evaluates annotation-free methods against expert judgments, finding that hybrid supervised models (r=0.45) outperform all zero-shot approaches, including LLMs (best r=0.38).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Migrants traveling geographically distinct routes (Trans-Saharan, Balkan) report strikingly parallel experiences (police violence, smuggler exploitation, dangerous crossings, family separation), yet no computational method exists to automatically detect these shared experiential echoes across narratives. The task is motivated by the need to analyze large-scale migration testimonies without requiring costly annotated training data.

Method: From 108 French migration narratives spanning both corridors, the authors automatically generate sentence pairs and score them using six annotation-free approaches: (1) lexical baselines, (2) sentence embeddings, (3) POS-based structural features, (4) a migration-specific theme lexicon, (5) context-aware narrative features (e.g., narrative position), and (6) zero-shot LLM scoring with Qwen2.5-7B and Mistral-7B under three prompting strategies (zero-shot, few-shot, chain-of-thought). All methods are validated against 816 expert-annotated intertextuality judgments (Krippendorff's α=0.27). A supervised hybrid combines all 31 features.

Result: All surface, structural, and embedding methods correlate only weakly with expert judgments (r ≤ 0.30). Qwen2.5-7B zero-shot achieves the best single-method correlation (r=0.38). Few-shot examples degrade Qwen but dramatically improve Mistral. Narrative position significantly predicts intertextuality, with departure-phase pairs showing the highest experiential echoes. The supervised hybrid combining all 31 features achieves r=0.45, a 21% improvement over the best individual method.

Conclusion: Experiential intertextuality detection is a challenging task evidenced by low inter-annotator agreement (α=0.27). While zero-shot LLMs show promise, they fall short of supervised hybrids. Narrative structure — particularly departure-phase positioning — is a strong signal for detecting shared experiences, suggesting future work should further leverage narrative arc features alongside stronger models.

Abstract: Migrants traversing geographically distinct routes such as the Trans-Saharan and Balkan corridors often recount strikingly parallel lived experiences: police violence, smuggler exploitation, dangerous crossings, and family separation. We introduce the task of experiential intertextuality detection: automatically identifying shared experiential echoes across migration narratives without requiring annotated training data. From 108 French migration narratives spanning both corridors, we automatically generate sentence pairs and score them using annotation-free methods: lexical baselines, sentence embeddings, POS-based structural features, a migration-specific theme lexicon, context-aware narrative features, and zero-shot LLM scoring with Qwen2.5-7B and Mistral-7B under three prompting strategies. We validate all methods against 816 expert-annotated intertextuality judgments (inter-annotator Krippendorff's $α= 0.27$). Our results reveal that all surface, structural, and embedding methods correlate only weakly with expert judgments ($r \leq 0.30$); Qwen2.5-7B zero-shot achieves the best single-method correlation ($r = 0.38$); few-shot examples degrade Qwen but dramatically improve Mistral; narrative position significantly predicts intertextuality, with departure-phase pairs showing the highest experiential echoes; and a supervised hybrid combining all 31 features achieves $r = 0.45$, a 21% improvement over the best individual method.

</details>


### [36] [Hy-MultiTurn: A Six-Dimensional Benchmark for Deep Multi-Turn Dialogue Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29196)
*Eileen Ye,Jiawen Tao,Yaoming Li,Chenxu Liu,Wenhan Yu,Yaxin Fan,Xiaokun Yuan,Mengzhou Wu,Yanbing Jiang,Maxm Pan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Hy-MultiTurn is a Chinese benchmark featuring 209 controlled tasks across 12-76 turns, organized into six evaluation modes derived from real chatbot failure mechanisms. Evaluating 22 frontier models shows even the strongest (GPT-5.5) meets all requirements in only 41.1% of responses, with no single model excelling across all six modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-turn benchmarks focus on short exchanges and fail to evaluate deep multi-turn understanding in Chinese, particularly: remembering earlier details, tracking revisions, identifying referents, and withholding inappropriate actions. They also offer limited diagnostic insight into how and why models fail.

Method: The authors analyzed real chatbot failures to identify six recurring failure mechanisms, then translated these into six controlled evaluation modes: constraint memory, precise execution, constraint synthesis, object localization, action suppression, and reference resolution. They constructed 209 tasks with 12-76 turns, incorporating difficulty factors like dialogue length, irrelevant-topic distraction, and colloquial phrasing. Twenty-two frontier model configurations were evaluated against these tasks.

Result: Hy-MultiTurn proves broadly challenging. GPT-5.5, the strongest overall configuration, satisfies all requirements in only 41.1% of responses. No single model performs best across all six modes, indicating that different architectures/configurations have distinct failure profiles and relative strengths.

Conclusion: Long multi-turn dialogue understanding in Chinese remains a significant challenge for frontier models. The six-mode diagnostic framework provides fine-grained insight into model failure patterns, and the benchmark reveals that substantial progress is needed before models can reliably handle extended, constraint-rich conversational interactions.

Abstract: Long-running multi-turn interactions with chatbots and agents are now common, and a correct response often depends on remembering earlier details, tracking later revisions, identifying intended objects or referents, and withholding action when required conditions are unmet. Existing multi-turn benchmarks typically cover short exchanges and do not fully evaluate these capabilities in long multi-turn interactions, particularly in Chinese, while offering limited insight into how and why models fail. To address these limitations, we analyze real chatbot failures to identify six recurring mechanisms and use them to define six controlled evaluation modes in Hy-MultiTurn, a Chinese benchmark for deep multi-turn dialogue understanding. The six modes evaluate constraint memory, precise execution, constraint synthesis, object localization, action suppression, and reference resolution. Across the six modes, we construct 209 controlled tasks spanning 12-76 turns, with dialogue length, irrelevant-topic distraction, and colloquial phrasing adding further difficulty. Evaluation of 22 frontier model configurations shows that Hy-MultiTurn is broadly challenging, as even GPT-5.5, the strongest overall configuration, satisfies all requirements in only 41.1 percent of responses and no model performs best in all six modes.

</details>


### [37] [Knowing When to Quit: Diagnosing and Training LLMs to Abort Futile Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29211)
*Xinyan Guan,Jiali Zeng,Chunlei Xin,Yaojie Lu,Hongyu Lin,Xianpei Han,Le Sun,Fandong Meng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs produce plausible but incorrect reasoning on tasks beyond their capabilities. The authors characterize this "futile reasoning" and propose CaRL, which uses reward shaping and hindsight refusal augmentation to align model behavior with actual capability boundaries, reducing specious reasoning while preserving utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs generate semantically void yet computationally expensive reasoning on beyond-capability tasks, producing plausible-sounding but incorrect derivations that mislead users. There is a systematic miscalibration between model capability and behavior that needs to be addressed.

Method: The authors first characterize futile reasoning through systematic analysis, identifying specious reasoning as the dominant failure mode. They then introduce CaRL (Capability-aligned Reinforcement Learning), which uses two key techniques: (1) reward shaping that incentivizes refusal over futile reasoning, and (2) hindsight refusal augmentation that converts past failures into refusal supervision signals.

Result: CaRL achieves substantial reduction in futile reasoning while preserving performance across task difficulties, effectively realizing capability-aligned behavior without sacrificing utility.

Conclusion: Aligning model behavior with actual capability boundaries via CaRL effectively mitigates the futile reasoning problem, enabling models to appropriately refuse beyond-capability tasks rather than generating misleading reasoning chains.

Abstract: Large language models generate computationally expensive yet semantically void reasoning on beyond-capability tasks, creating risks where plausible-sounding but incorrect derivations mislead users. We characterize this \textit{futile reasoning} phenomenon through systematic analysis, revealing universal capability overreach and systematic miscalibration between capability and behavior. The dominant failure mode is specious reasoning, which outputs look superficially valid but contain subtle errors, escalating with task difficulty. To address this, we introduce \textbf{CaRL} (\textbf{Ca}pability-\textbf{a}ligned \textbf{R}einforcement \textbf{L}earning), which aligns model behavior with capability boundaries through reward shaping that incentivizes refusal over futile reasoning and hindsight refusal augmentation that converts failures into refusal supervision. Experiments demonstrate a substantial reduction in futile reasoning while preserving performance across task difficulties, effectively achieving capability-aligned behavior without sacrificing utility. \footnote{https://github.com/icip-cas/Knowing-When-to-Quit}

</details>


### [38] [Data Turnstile: A Scalable Open Framework for Function-Calling Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29250)
*Goutham Ramakrishnan,Megha Sharma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Data Turnstile is an open-source framework that generates high-quality synthetic training data for function-calling in small language models (SLMs). It uses constrained, stepwise generation with validation and error-feedback loops, enabling SLMs fine-tuned on its data to rival or surpass models 3×–53× larger on tool-use benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SLMs are appealing for agentic deployment due to low latency, cost, and on-device privacy, but they perform poorly on tool-use tasks because training data is scarce and noisy. Unlike large models, SLMs cannot overcome low-quality supervision through capacity alone, making data quality the critical bottleneck.

Method: Data Turnstile takes user-defined API specifications and generates synthetic training data through a constrained, stepwise generation process. It decomposes multi-turn tool-use interactions and incorporates validation and error-feedback loops, providing fine-grained control over API diversity, conversation complexity, and output correctness.

Result: On BFCL (single-turn), a Turnstile-fine-tuned Qwen3-0.6B achieves 75.9% accuracy (vs. 67.4% base with thinking), approaching Qwen3-1.7B (78.4%) and Qwen3-4B (79.9%) despite being 3× and 7× smaller. On τ²-bench (multi-turn), Turnstile-trained Qwen3-1.7B scores 31.1% pass¹ on Telecom (4.7× over 6.6% base), surpassing Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct (27.4%). Qwen3-0.6B reaches 24.6% (7× over 3.5% base), approaching the 53× larger 32B model.

Conclusion: High-quality synthetic data from Data Turnstile enables SLMs to dramatically close the gap with much larger models on function-calling tasks. The framework and a dataset of 1,000+ APIs and 100K+ multi-turn interactions are released publicly.

Abstract: Small language models (SLMs) are attractive for agentic deployment due to low latency, reduced cost, and on-device privacy, yet they struggle with tool-use tasks where training data is scarce and noisy. Unlike larger models, SLMs cannot compensate for low-quality supervision through sheer capacity, making data quality the critical bottleneck. We present Data Turnstile, an open-source framework that takes user-defined API specifications and generates high-quality synthetic training data for function calling. Turnstile decomposes multi-turn tool-use interactions into constrained, stepwise generation with validation and error-feedback loops, providing fine-grained control over API diversity, conversation complexity, and output correctness. We demonstrate effectiveness of domain adaptation with Turnstile data on two challenging function calling benchmarks. On the BFCL single-turn benchmark, a Qwen3-0.6B fine-tuned on Turnstile data without chain-of-thought achieves 75.9% overall accuracy (versus 67.4% for the base model with thinking enabled), closing the gap with thinking-enabled Qwen3-1.7B (78.4%) and Qwen3-4B (79.9%) despite being 3$\times$ and 7$\times$ smaller respectively. On $τ^2$-bench, a multi-turn agentic benchmark, Turnstile-trained Qwen3-1.7B achieves 31.1% pass^1 on the Telecom domain, improving 4.7$\times$ over its 6.6% base and surpassing Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct (27.4%), a model 19$\times$ larger. Turnstile-trained Qwen3-0.6B achieves 24.6%, improving 7$\times$ over its 3.5% base and approaching the 32B model (53$\times$ larger). We release Data Turnstile along with a dataset spanning 1,000+ APIs and 100K+ multi-turn interactions.

</details>


### [39] [CalibratedRubric: Task-Adaptive Rubric Banks for Open-Ended LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29252)
*Mengting Chen,Yanshu Sun,Wanting Liang,Beidi Luan,Rui Sun,Dezhi Chen,Jing Li,Zuo Bai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CalibratedRubric introduces a task-adaptive framework for automated LLM evaluation that uses Bayesian measurability filtering and IRT-based bank assembly to construct compact, reliable rubric sets, significantly improving human-gold agreement and rank fidelity across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated rubric generation pipelines rely on strict judge unanimity and binary variance filters, which conflate measurable rubrics with merely informative ones and fail to scale expert curation. There is a need for a principled, uncertainty-aware method to distinguish and select rubrics that are both reliable and discriminating for open-ended LLM output evaluation.

Method: CalibratedRubric operates in three stages: (1) type-specific scoring tailored to rubric categories; (2) Bayesian rubric-measurability filtering using a Beta-Bernoulli agreement posterior to estimate each rubric's measurability; and (3) item response theory (IRT)-based bank assembly using a submodular information-coverage objective for compact rubric selection. Task-label perturbations are also employed to validate task-adaptive scoring.

Result: Measurability filtering improved human-gold agreement on JudgmentBench from κ=0.604 to κ=0.743. IRT-based greedy selection outperformed random selection in cross-fitted rank fidelity across six response blocks. On FinResearchBench, only 49 rubrics (vs. 131) were needed to reach the target correlation. Task-label perturbations reduced system separation, confirming the value of task-adaptive scoring.

Conclusion: CalibratedRubric provides an efficient, uncertainty-aware approach to open-ended LLM evaluation, with calibration gains contingent on sufficient judge redundancy. The framework's measurability filtering and IRT-based selection offer a scalable alternative to expert curation.

Abstract: Reliable evaluation of open-ended LLM outputs requires fine-grained rubrics, yet expert curation is costly and difficult to scale. Existing automated pipelines rely on strict judge unanimity and binary variance filters, which cannot distinguish measurable rubrics from informative ones. We introduce CalibratedRubric, a task-adaptive framework that combines type-specific scoring, Bayesian rubric-measurability filtering, and item response theory (IRT)-based bank assembly. CalibratedRubric estimates each rubric's measurability with a Beta--Bernoulli agreement posterior and uses a submodular information-coverage objective to construct compact rubric banks over the observed capability range. Across financial, healthcare, general, and legal benchmarks, measurability filtering improves human-gold agreement on JudgmentBench from $κ=0.604$ to $0.743$. IRT-based greedy selection improves cross-fitted rank fidelity over random selection across all six evaluated response blocks and requires only 49 rather than 131 rubrics to reach the target correlation on FinResearchBench decision-support tasks. Task-label perturbations further reduce system separation, confirming the practical relevance of task-adaptive scoring. These results support CalibratedRubric as an efficient, uncertainty-aware approach to open-ended LLM evaluation, with calibration gains depending on sufficient judge redundancy.

</details>


### [40] [Translation with Thought: Difficulty-Adaptive Reasoning via Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Domain Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29287)
*Yongshi Ye,Biao Fu,Chongxuan Huang,Yidong Chen,Xiaodong Shi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TwT (Translation with Thought) is a resource-rational framework for multi-domain machine translation that learns to adapt reasoning effort based on input difficulty. It achieves superior translation quality over much larger SOTA reasoning models while reducing token usage by 32–60%, demonstrating that cognitive-inspired modulation between intuitive and deliberate reasoning yields robust generalization and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-domain machine translation (MDMT) faces varying linguistic complexity across domains. The authors draw inspiration from human translators, who naturally modulate cognitive effort — using intuitive processing for simple inputs and deliberate reasoning for complex ones. Existing LLM-based translation models lack this adaptive reasoning economy, often expending excessive or insufficient inference compute regardless of input difficulty.

Method: TwT employs a two-stage training pipeline: (1) Supervised fine-tuning on difficulty-aware long chain-of-thought (CoT) traces, originally distilled from DeepSeek-R1 and subsequently rewritten by GPT-4o to exhibit human-like reasoning economy (concise for easy inputs, elaborate for hard ones). (2) Reinforcement learning with a hybrid reward function that jointly optimizes translation quality and reasoning efficiency. This trains the model to modulate between intuitive and deliberate reasoning based on input difficulty. The framework is evaluated across three backbone model scales (including 7B and 14B variants).

Result: TwT-7B and TwT-14B outperform significantly larger SOTA reasoning models in translation quality across 15 benchmarks (both in-domain and out-of-domain) and 62 languages (3 seen, 59 unseen), while simultaneously reducing token usage by 32–60%. Ablations across three backbone models confirm the robustness of the approach.

Conclusion: Aligning translation model behavior with cognitive principles — specifically, resource-rational modulation of reasoning effort — enables strong generalization, high translation quality, and efficient inference in multi-domain machine translation. The TwT framework demonstrates that reasoning economy can be learned and optimized rather than relying on uniformly expensive CoT decoding.

Abstract: Multi-domain machine translation (MDMT) poses a unique challenge due to varying levels of linguistic complexity across domains. Inspired by human translators' ability to adapt reasoning effort based on difficulty, we propose TwT (Translation with Thought), a resource-rational framework that learns to modulate inference between intuitive and deliberate reasoning. TwT is trained in two stages: (1) supervised fine-tuning on difficulty-aware long chain-of-thought traces distilled from DeepSeek-R1 and rewritten by GPT-4o to reflect human-like reasoning economy, and (2) reinforcement learning with a hybrid reward to optimize translation quality and reasoning efficiency. Evaluated on 15 benchmarks spanning in-domain and out-of-domain settings, as well as 3 seen and 59 unseen languages, with ablations across three backbone models, TwT-7B and TwT-14B outperform much larger SOTA reasoning models in translation quality, while reducing token usage by 32--60\%. These results confirm that aligning translation behavior with cognitive principles enables robust generalization, high translation quality, and efficient reasoning in MDMT.

</details>


### [41] [Cross-Lingual Transfer for Machine Translation in Turkic Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29355)
*Omer Burak Cinar,Mehmet Mert Dalkilic,Cagri Toraman*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates cross-lingual transfer patterns among five Turkic languages (Turkish, Azerbaijani, Uzbek, Kazakh, Kyrgyz) using pairwise transfer matrices with mT5 models. Key findings: transfer is strongest between closely related pairs (Turkish-Azerbaijani, Kazakh-Kyrgyz), transfer direction matters, translation target language affects transfer behavior, and Latinization improves performance in script-mismatched settings though inconsistently across metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-lingual transfer is critical for low-resource machine translation, yet its behavior within closely related language families—such as Turkic languages—remains poorly characterized. The authors aim to systematically map transfer dynamics in this understudied setting.

Method: The authors construct pairwise transfer matrices among five Turkic languages using mT5 models. Each model is fine-tuned on one transfer source language and evaluated on a different transfer target language, while keeping the translation target constant. They also test the effect of Latinization (script conversion) in script-mismatched pairs and assess the stability of transfer sources across different datasets and model configurations.

Result: Transfer is strongest between closely related pairs (Turkish-Azerbaijani and Kazakh-Kyrgyz). Transfer direction is asymmetric—the choice of source and target matters. The same source-target pair can behave differently depending on the translation target language. Latinization improves BLEU and chrF scores in several script-mismatched settings, but gains are not uniform across metrics. Transfer source rankings remain mostly stable across datasets and model settings.

Conclusion: Cross-lingual transfer among Turkic languages is governed by linguistic proximity, directional asymmetry, and script compatibility. These findings provide practical guidance for selecting transfer sources in low-resource Turkic MT and highlight the need for metric-specific evaluation when applying script normalization techniques like Latinization.

Abstract: Cross-lingual transfer is central to low-resource machine translation, but its behavior within closely related language families remains insufficiently characterized. We study transfer among five Turkic languages; Turkish, Azerbaijani, Uzbek, Kazakh, and Kyrgyz; using pairwise transfer matrices. In this setting, each model is fine-tuned with one transfer source and evaluated on a different transfer target while the translation target remains the same. Across mT5 experiments, we find that transfer is strongest between closely related Turkic pairs, especially Turkish-Azerbaijani and Kazakh-Kyrgyz. We also show that transfer direction matters, and that the same transfer source-transfer target pair can behave differently when the translation target changes. Latinization improves BLEU and chrF in several script-mismatched settings, but its effect is not uniform across metrics. Additional analyses show that transfer sources are mostly stable across different datasets and model settings.

</details>


### [42] [Zero-Mem: Zero-Token Memory Operations for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29377)
*Yilin Xiao,Zhehan Zhu,Yujing Zhang,Jin Chen,Zijin Hong,Luyao Zhuang,Qinggang Zhang,Shengyuan Chen,Xiaocao Ouyang,Lingfei Ren,Xiao Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Zero-Mem proposes zero-token memory operations for LLM agents, using an entity-context graph and temporal hierarchy to retrieve past interaction traces without any intermediate LLM calls, achieving competitive QA performance while reducing memory-operation time by 57.6%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent memory systems incur recurring token and time costs by using additional LLM calls for generating intermediate records and mediating retrieval. These processes can also obscure original evidence through omission or merging of details. The authors ask whether structured memory access can function without any generation at all.

Method: Zero-Mem preserves original interaction traces and organizes them via two complementary structures: (1) an entity-context graph that exposes cross-interaction connections, and (2) a temporal hierarchy that maintains conversational locality and session state. For each query, Zero-Mem weights both views, retrieves from both, and traces their structure to recover supporting relations or context. A deterministic calibration step discards conflicting evidence before the retrieved traces are fed to a final-QA reader — the sole LLM call in the pipeline. No step outside final question answering invokes an LLM or consumes LLM tokens.

Result: On long-memory and long-context QA benchmarks, Zero-Mem achieves competitive performance while eliminating all LLM calls and LLM-token consumption from memory operations. With the same final-QA reader and context budget, it reduces memory-operation time cost by 57.6% relative to the fastest compared baseline. Ablations confirm the contribution of both structural views and their query-dependent coordination.

Conclusion: Structured agent memory does not require generating intermediate representations of the past. Zero-Mem demonstrates that zero-token memory operations — relying solely on deterministic retrieval from organized original traces — can match the performance of generation-based memory while being significantly faster and cheaper.

Abstract: LLM agents need memory to act consistently over long interactions, yet many systems use additional LLM calls to operate that memory. Generating intermediate records and mediating their retrieval adds recurring token and time costs, while omitted or merged details can obscure the original evidence. We ask whether structured memory access requires generation at all. Zero-Mem introduces \emph{zero-token memory operations}: no step outside final question answering invokes an LLM or consumes LLM input or output tokens; encoder computation is accounted for separately. Zero-Mem preserves original interaction traces as its source of record. It organizes the traces in two complementary ways. An entity--context graph exposes connections across interactions, while a temporal hierarchy preserves conversational locality and session state. For each query, Zero-Mem weighs the two views, retrieves from both, and follows their structure to recover supporting relations or surrounding context. Deterministic calibration first discards conflicting evidence and then keeps the reader's answer grounded in the retrieved traces. Only the final-QA reader invokes an LLM. Across long-memory and long-context question-answering benchmarks, Zero-Mem achieves competitive performance while eliminating LLM calls and LLM-token consumption from memory operations. With the same final-QA reader and context budget, it reduces memory-operation time cost by 57.6\% relative to the fastest compared baseline. Ablations support the contribution of the two views and their query-dependent coordination. Overall, the results show that structured agent memory need not generate an intermediate representation of the past. After peer review, the code and implementation details will be available at \textcolor{blue}{https://github.com/TheMoon0815/Zero-mem}.

</details>


### [43] [PTP: Previous-Token Prediction based LLM Inversion for Near-Exact Prompt Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29378)
*Pirzada Suhail,Nagasai Saketh Naidu,Atanu R Sinha,Amit Sethi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a black-box method for inverting LLMs to recover prompts from outputs by training an "inverse language model" from scratch using synthetic data from the target LLM, based on previous-token prediction, outperforming prior semantic reconstruction approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM generation involves a many-to-many mapping between prompts and responses, making prompt recovery from outputs challenging. Prior work requires access to model weights/logits and fine-tuning on external datasets. The authors aim to achieve LLM inversion in a purely black-box setting without auxiliary aids, using only the target LLM's own outputs.

Method: The authors train an explicit inverse language model from scratch using data synthetically generated by the target LLM. The inverse model is trained via previous-token prediction (predicting the preceding token given subsequent context), establishing a generative symmetry with forward next-token prediction. This functional approach operates in a black-box setting, requires no external datasets or model internals, and supports diverse prompt reconstruction through sampling, where all reconstructed prompts induce similar responses under the forward LLM.

Result: The approach generalizes across datasets and exhibits transferability in reconstructing prompts from responses generated by different LLMs. Across token-based evaluation metrics for both prompt and response reconstructions, the proposed method outperforms prior work.

Conclusion: A functional, black-box approach to LLM inversion using a previous-token prediction paradigm is both feasible and superior to prior semantic reconstruction methods that rely on model internals and external datasets, offering better generalization and transferability.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) generate text by auto-regressively sampling the next token. This inherently leads to a many-to-many mapping between prompts and responses, complicating the task of inferring prompts from observed outputs. Prior work on LLM inversion frames prompt recovery as a semantic reconstruction task. They rely on fine-tuning pretrained sequence-to-sequence models on large external datasets--and requiring access to model weights or logits--to generate semantically plausible prompts. In contrast, we present a functional approach to inverting a given LLM in a black-box setting, without auxiliary aids. We train an explicit inverse language model entirely from scratch on data synthetically generated from the target LLM itself. Analogous to forward next-token prediction, our inverse model is trained using previous-token prediction, establishing a generative link between the forward and inverse processes that enables faithful prompt reconstruction. Moreover, it naturally supports diverse prompt reconstructions through sampling, whereby all such prompts induce similar responses under the forward, target LLM. Our approach generalises across datasets and exhibits transferability in reconstructing prompts from responses generated by different LLMs. Further, across the set of token based evaluation metrics for prompt and response reconstructions, our approach outperforms prior work.

</details>


### [44] [Studying quantization trade-offs for efficient inference deployment in machine translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29397)
*Jim Zhao,Sohir Maskey,Koen Oostermeijer,Douglas Orr,Teryn Jones*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates the trade-offs between quantization and inference efficiency for LLM-based machine translation in server environments, finding that combining document-chunking with W4A8/W8A8 quantization improves latency-throughput Pareto curves, while also revealing that standard segment-level MT benchmarks fail to capture how quantization interacts with long-context document translation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantization is widely used to reduce memory footprint and improve LLM inference efficiency, but its impact on latency and throughput is rarely evaluated under realistic, orchestration-level workloads. Furthermore, standard MT benchmarks (sentence-level) fail to capture long-context dynamics, leaving a gap in understanding how quantization and document-chunking strategies interact in production translation systems.

Method: The authors study quantization trade-offs on two translation model families (EuroLLM and Hy-MT2) across five model sizes (1.7B to 22B) deployed on single A100 or H100 GPUs. They combine document-chunking strategies with W4A8 and W8A8 quantization formats and evaluate inference efficiency under varied workloads. They also introduce a document-level evaluation from WMT24++ to assess translation quality under quantization with different text chunking strategies.

Result: W4A8/W8A8 quantization combined with document-chunking improves the latency-throughput Pareto frontier. Hy-MT2 proves robust under quantization, whereas EuroLLM exhibits severe sensitivity with translation quality collapsing rapidly across all quantization formats. Standard segment-level evaluation fails to predict the interaction between quantization and long-context document translation.

Conclusion: The trade-off between inference efficiency and translation quality depends jointly on both the quantization format and the text chunking strategy. Model-specific robustness to quantization varies significantly, and document-level evaluation is essential for accurately assessing these trade-offs in realistic deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Deploying large language models in realistic server environments poses challenges, as the system needs to provide high-quality responses with low latency. Quantization is a common approach to reduce the memory footprint and improve inference efficiency, yet its impact on latency and throughput is rarely evaluated under controlled, orchestration-level workloads. In this work we study the quantization trade-offs of two translation model families, EuroLLM \citep{martins2025eurollm} and Hy-MT2 \citep{zheng2026hy} across five models ranging from 1.7B to 22B for efficient deployment on a single A100 or H100 GPU. We demonstrate that combining a document-chunking strategy with W4A8 or W8A8 quantization improves the latency-throughput Pareto-curve under a wide range of workloads. Furthermore, since standard machine translation (MT) benchmarks rely on isolated sentences and fail to capture long-context dynamics, we introduce a document-level evaluation from WMT24++ to assess how text chunking strategies affect translation quality under quantization. Our results reveal that standard segment-level evaluation can fail to predict the interaction between quantization and long-context document translation. While Hy-MT2 remains robust under quantization, EuroLLM shows strong sensitivity and translation quality collapses rapidly for all considered quantization formats. Overall, our experiments show that the trade-off between inference efficiency and translation quality depends not only on the quantization format, but also on the choice of text chunking strategy.

</details>


### [45] [Know It, Act on It: Investigating Memory Utilization in LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29433)
*Zhaoxin Feng,Jianfei Ma,Emmanuele Chersoni*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper identifies a "knowledge utilization gap" in LLM agents: models often correctly recall user preferences (Know) yet fail to act on them in behavioral scenarios (Act). Using a decoupled evaluation paradigm across 16 systems and 1,000 preferences, the authors show this gap is pervasive, partially mitigated by memory architectures, but dangerously pronounced for health/therapy preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents increasingly serve as personalized companions where memory of user preferences is critical. Prior work conflates two distinct failure modes: failing to remember a preference versus remembering it but failing to deploy it. Without decoupling these, it is impossible to diagnose whether improvements should target memory encoding/retrieval or downstream behavioral integration. The paper aims to isolate and systematically measure this latter "utilization" breakdown.

Method: The authors propose a decoupled evaluation paradigm with paired Know and Act tests for the same user preference. The Know test assesses whether the model can explicitly recall/recognize the preference; the Act test presents a behavioral scenario where the preference should shape the agent's response. They evaluate 1,000 user preferences across 16 LLM systems and five memory architectures, varying preference expression strength across three levels (explicit, implicit, weak). The gap between Know and Act performance quantifies the utilization failure.

Result: A substantial gap exists between Know and Act outcomes: agents frequently pass recall tests but fail to reflect the same preference in behavior. Memory architectures reduce but do not close this gap. Utilization failures are especially severe for health and therapy-related preferences — precisely the domain where failures to act carry the highest real-world stakes.

Conclusion: The knowledge utilization problem is a distinct and critical bottleneck in LLM agent design that cannot be addressed solely by improving memory retrieval. Evaluating and optimizing the Know→Act pipeline requires dedicated benchmarks and architectural innovations, particularly for high-stakes domains like health. The decoupled evaluation framework offers a reusable methodology for future work on agent personalization.

Abstract: As large language model (LLM) agents evolve into personalized companions, memory has emerged as a core capability. However, LLMs face a knowledge utilization problem: they may fail to act on relevant user preferences even when they are fully present in context. When an agent fails to tailor its response in a context where previously shared user preferences should matter, it is unclear whether the model failed to remember that information or remembered it but failed to use it. To isolate this breakdown, we introduce a decoupled evaluation paradigm that administers paired Know and Act tests to the same user preference. We conduct large-scale experiments across 16 systems and five memory architectures, evaluating 1,000 preferences embedded at three levels of expression strength. Our results show a large gap between Know and Act outcomes: agents often pass the recall test for a user preference but fail to reflect that same preference in the paired behavioral scenario. While memory architectures reduce this gap, utilization remains especially weak for health and therapy-related preferences, where failures to act carry the greatest real-world stakes.

</details>


### [46] [Evidence-Type Competition: When Can Interventional Data Teach Language Models Causal Direction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29484)
*Xining Xun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: In controlled synthetic environments with Simpson's paradox, interventional pretraining data fails to teach language models correct causal direction. Models learn the causal capability but apply it based on inference-time context — observational context suppresses correct causal reasoning, while removing it releases the ability. The phenomenon is robust across seeds, scales, and architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
