Motivation: To test the assumption that interventional data is the gold standard for teaching models causal reasoning, by systematically examining whether models actually learn to distinguish correlation from causation when the two conflict.

Method: Synthetic Simpson's-paradox worlds where observational correlation and causal effect have opposite signs. Authors vary the fraction of interventional samples during pretraining, then probe models with different inference-time contexts (purely observational, mixed, purely interventional). Activation patching localizes the mechanism, and findings are validated on CLadder and across model scales (up to 0.93B parameters) and seeds. An evidence-averaging protocol is proposed to reduce sign errors.

Result: (1) Increasing interventional pretraining fraction does not improve causal direction — magnitude grows but sign copies from observational context. (2) Inference-time context governs behavior: observational context causes systematic sign reversal in most worlds; purely interventional context yields 41/50 correct. (3) Erasing observational evidence releases causal ability (ratio_true = +0.56). (4) Stable across 11/11 seeds and robust at 0.93B parameters (31.8% vs. 6% reversals). (5) Activation patching localizes the "switch" to middle layers' observational rows. (6) Evidence-averaging protocol reduces sign errors from 26% to 9%.

Conclusion: The capability for causal reasoning lives in the weights, but the behavioral switch lives in the context. Interventional data alone is insufficient — inference-time context determines whether the model uses its causal knowledge or defaults to observational correlations. This has implications for how causal reasoning should be evaluated and deployed in LLMs.

Abstract: Interventional data is widely regarded as the gold standard for teaching models causal reasoning. We test this assumption in a fully controlled synthetic environment pitting observational correlation against causal effect, and find it fails instructively. In Simpson's-paradox worlds, where the two have systematically opposite signs, increasing the fraction of interventional samples in pretraining does not improve causal direction: the magnitude of the model's do()-response grows monotonically, yet its sign is copied from the observational context. What governs whether interventional evidence is used is not the training mixture but the evidence type present in the context at inference time. Under an identical training recipe, a purely observational context induces systematic sign reversal in 29/50 worlds, a mixed context in 19/50, while aligned interventional probes alone yield 41/50 correct. Erasing observational evidence from the context immediately releases the suppressed causal interpolation ability (ratio_true = +0.56); a four-state content manipulation shows the switch is content-mediated and graded. The suppression is stable across training seeds (11/11 strong reversals persist on a matched-protocol second seed) and robust as a rate at 0.93B parameters (31.8% vs. 6% reversals in the matched probe-only arm), even as absolute gains shrink four-fold. An external audit on CLadder exposes a learned positive-effect prior with a two-layer structure: sign-randomized retraining removes it in-distribution but not out-of-distribution. We summarize: the capability lives in the weights; the switch lives in the context, and activation patching localizes the switch to the middle layers' observational rows. We further quantify the sampling noise floor of probe-based causal evaluation and an evidence-averaging protocol that cuts sign errors from 26% to 9%.

</details>


### [47] [ARB: A Matched Authorship-Rewriting Benchmark Dataset for AI-Text Detector Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29539)
*Gaetano Perrone,Simon Pietro Romano*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Standard AI-text detection benchmarks fail to predict detector performance when human-authored text is rewritten by an LLM. While detectors catch ~90% of direct LLM text, recall drops to 15-30% on LLM-rewritten human text, even though LLM-rewritten LLM text remains largely detectable (78-83%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work focused on paraphrasing/rewriting of LLM-generated text, but the realistic threat scenario — where human-authored content is polished or revised by an LLM — remains unexamined. The authors aim to determine whether conventional human-vs-LLM benchmarks predict detector behavior in this under-explored regime.

Method: The Authorship-Rewriting Benchmark (ARB) consists of 1,800 human source texts from XSum, WritingPrompts, and OpenWebText. Each source produces four matched variants: HUMAN, Free-LLM (direct generation), H2L (LLM-rewritten human text), and LLM2L (LLM-rewritten LLM text), using four open-weight generators (Llama-3.2-3B, Qwen2.5-7B, Mistral-7B, Gemma-2-9B). Five detectors were evaluated at TPR@1%FPR.

Result: FastDetectGPT and Binoculars detected 91.2% and 93.5% of direct LLM text but only 30.8% and 15.1% of H2L text — a drop of 60-78 pp. On LLM2L, they retained 78.3% and 83.0% recall (only 10-13 pp decline). RADAR showed similar patterns (66.8% → 12.2%). BERT-Defense and RoBERTa-Defense remained below 3% across all regimes.

Conclusion: Conventional benchmark performance does not transfer to the H2L scenario, revealing a critical blind spot in current detection evaluation. Detectors remain robust to same-model LLM rewriting but fail dramatically when human text is LLM-revised.

Abstract: Standard AI-text detection benchmarks compare human-written text against text generated directly by large language models (LLMs). While prior work has shown that rewriting and paraphrasing can degrade detector performance, it remains unclear whether performance measured on this conventional benchmark predicts detector behavior when human-authored content is rewritten by an LLM. To address this gap, we introduce Authorship-Rewriting Benchmark (ARB), built from 1,800 human source texts (600 each from XSum, WritingPrompts, and OpenWebText) and four open-weight generators (Llama-3.2-3B, Qwen2.5-7B, Mistral-7B, Gemma-2-9B). Each source item yields four matched variants: human-written (HUMAN), direct LLM generation (Free-LLM), LLM-rewritten human text (H2L), and same-generator LLM-rewritten LLM text (LLM2L). We evaluated five detectors (FastDetectGPT, Binoculars-falcon-7b, RADAR, BERT-Defense, RoBERTa-Defense) at a strict 1%-false-positive operating point (TPR@1%FPR). FastDetectGPT and Binoculars-falcon-7b detected 91.2% and 93.5\% of direct LLM text, but only 30.8% and 15.1% of human text an LLM had rewritten, a drop of 60-78 percentage points. The same detectors retained 78.3% and 83.0% recall when LLM text was rewritten by the same model, a much smaller decline of 10-13 points. RADAR followed the same pattern (66.8% to 12.2%), while BERT-Defense and RoBERTa-Defense stayed below 3% recall across all regimes. These results show that detector performance measured on the conventional human-vs-LLM benchmark does not transfer to human-authored text revised by an LLM, even though the same detectors remain largely robust to LLM-only rewriting.

</details>


### [48] [Sycophancy Undermines Epistemic Vigilance in Cooperative Vision-Language Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29585)
*Rupak Sarkar,Neha Srikanth,Saloni Gupta,Claire Bonial,Philip Resnik,Rachel Rudinger*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces an information-asymmetric dialog task where two vision-language models must spot differences between privately viewed images through conversation. Models display poor epistemic vigilance—frequently agreeing with partners despite contrary private evidence—a form of sycophancy. Task-agnostic sycophancy-reduction steering vectors improve fidelity to private evidence and enhance cooperative reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans maintain common ground by detecting and repairing conflicts between new information and prior beliefs (epistemic vigilance). For AI systems to be reliable cooperative partners, they need the same capacity to weigh incoming information against private evidence and surface inconsistencies—yet current vision-language models appear to lack this capability.

Method: The authors design a dialog-based "spot-the-difference" task under information asymmetry: two models each privately view one image and must converse to determine if images are identical or identify differences. They evaluate vision-language models' epistemic vigilance by measuring whether models appropriately rely on private visual evidence versus blindly agreeing with conversational partners. They also apply a task-agnostic steering vector learned from general sycophancy examples to reduce over-accommodation.

Result: Models routinely fail at epistemic vigilance—they overlook key evidence in their private image and agree with partners even when unwarranted. These failures are linked to sycophancy manifesting as over-accommodation and weak evidential grounding. Steering models with a sycophancy-reduction vector reduces epistemic vigilance errors and produces more faithful reporting of private evidence.

Conclusion: Epistemic vigilance is a critical but underdeveloped capability in vision-language models for cooperative tasks. Sycophancy underlies these failures, and task-agnostic steering interventions offer a promising direction for making models more reliable, evidence-faithful partners in information-asymmetric settings.

Abstract: To maintain common ground in cooperative conversation, humans iteratively update their beliefs as conversation participants share new information; participants who are epistemically vigilant detect when new information conflicts with prior beliefs and take steps to repair these conflicts. In order for AI systems to serve as reliable partners in complex cooperative tasks, they must similarly weigh incoming information against their own private evidence and shared context and appropriately surface inconsistencies when they arise. To measure the epistemic vigilance of vision-language models in cooperative settings, we present an information-asymmetric, dialog-based "spot-the-difference" task. Two models are privately shown one image each, and must determine through conversation whether the images are identical or, if not, identify the difference. Models routinely fail at this: they frequently overlook key evidence in their private image in favor of agreeing with their conversational partner, even when their agreement is unwarranted. We relate these violations of epistemic vigilance to the broader behavior of sycophancy, which manifests itself in cooperative goal-oriented dialog as over-accommodation and weak evidential grounding. Our results show that model steering to reduce sycophancy with a vector learned from task-agnostic sycophancy examples can reduce epistemic vigilance-related errors, making models more faithful reporters of their evidence, and in turn, more reliable partners in information-asymmetric cooperative tasks.

</details>


### [49] [ResKV: Reconstructing Omitted Attention Contributions for Fixed-Budget KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29591)
*Yuhang Zhan,Lisi Chen,Shuo Shang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ResKV splits the KV cache budget into an exact main cache and a compact residual cache that jointly participate in softmax normalization, restoring both numerator and denominator contributions of omitted tokens rather than applying post-hoc corrections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KV cache compression methods either permanently discard tokens (eviction) losing aggregate attention contributions, or merge tokens at the cost of perturbing retained keys/values. The authors identify that omitted information can be formulated as residual statistics in softmax attention, motivating a method that preserves exactness for critical tokens while efficiently capturing the contribution of evicted ones.

Method: ResKV allocates a fixed KV budget into: (1) an exact main cache retaining important tokens unchanged, and (2) a compact residual cache storing aggregated residual statistics (numerator and denominator residuals from softmax). Both caches participate in the same softmax normalization. A construction-time validation proxy determines residual allocation per layer and KV head, and a decode-time dynamic gate modulates residual contributions per query.

Result: Comprehensive evaluations on LongBench and RULER across query-aware and query-agnostic settings, multiple backbones, varying cache budgets, and representative baselines show broad improvements under the same retained KV budget while maintaining practical efficiency in peak memory and long-context decode throughput.

Conclusion: ResKV effectively bridges the gap between eviction-based and merging-based KV cache compression by restoring omitted token contributions through joint softmax participation, achieving superior performance without sacrificing decoding efficiency.

Abstract: KV cache compression is essential for efficient long-context inference. Existing eviction methods permanently discard unselected tokens and consequently remove their aggregate contribution to attention. Merging-based alternatives preserve more information but can perturb retained keys and values that should remain exact. We observe that the information omitted by cache eviction can be formulated as residual statistics in both the numerator and denominator of softmax attention. Based on this observation, we propose ResKV, which divides a fixed KV budget into an exact main cache and a compact residual cache that reconstructs the contribution of omitted tokens. ResKV lets main-cache tokens and residual entries participate in the same softmax normalization, so residual entries restore both attention numerator and denominator mass rather than acting as a post-hoc correction. A construction-time validation proxy determines residual allocation for each layer and KV head, while a decode-time dynamic gate adjusts residual contributions for individual queries. Comprehensive evaluations on LongBench and RULER, covering query-aware and query-agnostic settings, multiple backbones, cache budgets, and representative compression baselines, demonstrate broad improvements under the same retained KV budget while preserving the practical efficiency of compressed decoding, including peak memory usage and long-context decode throughput.

</details>


### [50] [Evolving language compositionality in a frequency-structured meaning space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29642)
*Fabio De Ponte,Eloise Gaines-White,Conor Houghton,Seth Bullock*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Investigating how meaning frequency shapes compositional structure in the iterated learning model, this paper finds that high-frequency meanings can become grammatical exceptions (mirroring natural language), but when frequency is assigned to sub-meaning parts rather than whole meaning vectors, language transmission collapses — despite reliable learning of frequent elements — because compositional generalization requires holistic form-meaning units for frequency to scaffold emergent structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The iterated learning model has established that a transmission bottleneck drives the emergence of linguistic compositionality. However, natural languages exhibit frequency effects where high-frequency items often resist regular grammatical patterns (e.g., irregular verbs). The authors aim to investigate how skewed meaning-frequency distributions interact with the learning bottleneck to shape emergent linguistic structure, specifically testing whether frequency effects observed in natural languages can be replicated and what happens when frequency is defined at different levels of granularity (whole meanings vs. sub-meaning parts).

Method: The authors extend the standard iterated learning framework, where agents learn a language from a previous generation's output and then produce data for the next generation through a bottleneck. They manipulate the frequency distribution of meanings in two conditions: (1) frequency skewed over whole meaning vectors, where some complete meanings appear much more frequently than others; and (2) frequency skewed over parts/components of meaning vectors, where certain semantic primitives recur more often. Transmission success, compositional structure, and learning fidelity are measured across generations under both conditions.

Result: Under whole-meaning frequency skew, high-frequency meanings can escape the pressure to conform to the general compositional grammar, mirroring natural language irregulars. However, when frequency is distributed over meaning parts rather than whole meaning vectors, the language fails to transmit stably across generations, even though the most frequent sub-elements are reliably learned. The relational compositional structure necessary for generalization does not emerge, preventing stable linguistic transmission.

Conclusion: Frequency can shape emergent linguistic structure only when defined over holistic form-meaning pairings that learners can acquire as units. Fragmenting frequency across smaller sub-meaning components undermines the relational scaffolding needed for compositional generalization, causing transmission failure. This suggests a fundamental constraint on how statistical regularities in the input interact with the learning bottleneck to produce structured, transmissible languages.

Abstract: The iterated learning model was introduced to investigate language evolution: the way in which the characteristic properties of human languages have been shaped, at least partly, by repeated transmission from one language user to another. The key finding is that language compositionality can arise spontaneously as a consequence of language being passed repeatedly through a language learning bottleneck. Here we explore how changing the frequency of different meanings, so that some meanings occur much more frequently than others, affects the character of its compositionality. We find that, as observed in natural languages, high-frequency meanings can escape the pressure to conform to the grammar that characterizes lower-frequency meanings. However, when the frequency structure is instead imposed on parts rather than on whole meaning vectors, the language fails to transmit across generations. This occurs despite the fact that the most frequent elements are reliably learned. These results suggest that frequency can shape emergent linguistic structure only when the frequency distribution is defined over form-meaning units that learners can acquire holistically. When frequency is instead distributed over smaller units, it fails to support the relational structure required for compositional generalisation, thereby preventing stable language transmission.

</details>


### [51] [TokTier: Exact Stateful Tokenization for Agentic LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29678)
*Zhenyu Zhang,Zhichao Cao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TokTier is a stateful tokenization service for LLM serving systems that eliminates redundant re-tokenization of cached prompt KV states. It offers incremental repair (0.5–1.1 ms for 100K–3M characters, up to 437× faster than HuggingFace) and GPU-accelerated full tokenization (0.87 ms for 1M characters, up to 491× faster than HF). Under vLLM, median TTFT drops 16–34% and P99 drops 23%, sustaining 1,821 req/s versus 40 req/s for a stateless 16-core front end.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM serving systems cache prompt KV states, but front ends still fully re-tokenize request text on every call—even when most of the prompt is reused. This is especially costly for coding agents that resubmit long transcripts after small tool results. Tokenization can consume up to 64% of time-to-first-token at a 94.1% prompt-cache hit rate. The core challenge is that even a short append can shift token boundaries near the end of the prior sequence, making naive prefix reuse unsafe.

Method: TokTier provides a stateful tokenization service guaranteeing emitted token IDs are identical to full reference tokenization. It uses two strategies: (1) Incremental repair for session continuations—re-tokenizes a small window around the append, splices only after a stable-boundary check, and widens the window or falls back to full tokenization on failure. (2) GPU full tokenization for calls without a reusable prefix—decomposes GPT-family regex pre-tokenization into run-local rules, then runs exact pre-tokenization and BPE on a GPU. A sampled shadow verifier re-checks live traffic for correctness.

Result: Differential campaigns across 17 tokenizer families covered 1.5×10¹⁰ split checks, a 12.4 TB real-text corpus, and 93,000+ replayed agent steps with zero divergence from reference. Incremental repair achieves 0.5–1.1 ms latency from 100K to 3M characters (437× faster than HF, 2.1× faster at 1M than prewarmed Gigatoken). GPU full tokenization encodes 1M characters in 0.87 ms (491× below HF, 23.4× below fastest published CPU method). With vLLM, median TTFT drops 16–34%, P99 drops 23% under recorded bursts. At a 50 ms P99 target, four repair cores plus one GPU sustain 1,821 req/s versus 40 req/s for a 16-core stateless front end.

Conclusion: TokTier demonstrates that stateful, correctness-guaranteed tokenization—combining incremental repair and GPU acceleration—can eliminate tokenization as a bottleneck in LLM serving, particularly for agentic workloads with long, incrementally growing contexts. The approach is production-validated with zero correctness divergence across massive-scale testing.

Abstract: LLM serving systems cache prompt KV state, yet most front ends still re-tokenize the full request text on every call. The cost lands on coding agents, which resubmit a long transcript after each small tool result, and reuse is hard because even a short append can change token boundaries near the end of the previous sequence. Across 153,951 calls from two agent ecosystems, the median call appends about 1.4K characters, and only 1.0-3.6% of calls start or rebuild a session with contexts of millions of characters. At a 94.1% fleet prompt-cache hit rate, tokenization reaches up to 64% of time to first token.
  TokTier is a stateful tokenization service with one contract: emitted token IDs are always identical to full reference tokenization of the request text. For a session continuation, it re-tokenizes a small window around the append and splices only after a per-request stable-boundary check, widening the window or falling back to full tokenization on failure. For a call without a reusable prefix, it decomposes GPT-family regex pre-tokenization into run-local rules and runs exact pre-tokenization and BPE on a GPU. A sampled shadow verifier re-checks live traffic.
  Across 17 tokenizer families, differential campaigns cover 1.5x10^10 split checks, a 12.4 TB real-text corpus, and 93,000+ replayed agent steps, with zero divergence. Incremental repair takes 0.5-1.1 ms from 100K to 3M characters, up to 437x faster than HF tokenization and 2.1x faster at 1M than the strongest cache-based baseline (Gigatoken) fully prewarmed. GPU full tokenization encodes a 1M-character request in 0.87 ms, up to 491x below HF and 23.4x below the fastest published CPU method. With vLLM, median time to first token drops 16-34% and P99 drops 23% under recorded bursts. Under a 50 ms P99 objective, four repair cores plus one GPU sustain 1,821 requests/s where a 16-core stateless front end saturates at 40.

</details>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [52] [OpenClaw and Ollama in Agentic AI: Toward Fully Autonomous and Scalable AI Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28629)
*Konstantinos I. Roumeliotis,Ranjan Sapkota*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a layered architecture for Agentic AI that separates inference (Ollama), orchestration, and execution (OpenClaw) layers, demonstrating that persistent memory, tool use, and adaptive decision-making emerge from system-level integration rather than standalone LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid shift from reactive LLMs to persistent, action-capable AI agents has exposed critical gaps in architectural understanding, particularly in separating inference, orchestration, and execution layers. Unified frameworks for designing and evaluating full-stack agentic systems remain scarce.

Method: Proposes a layered architecture for Agentic AI and implements a prototype using OpenClaw (agent runtime orchestration with reasoning, tool use, and action execution) integrated with Ollama (LLM inference layer). The system is experimentally validated to assess emergent capabilities such as persistent memory, tool utilization, and adaptive decision-making as architectural complexity increases.

Result: Capabilities like persistent memory, tool utilization, and adaptive decision-making emerge from system-level integration rather than standalone models, with performance improving consistently as architectural complexity increases. Challenges in scalability, security, privacy, governance, and evaluation are also identified.

Conclusion: The work establishes a unified architectural foundation for Agentic AI, validates full-stack autonomous agent effectiveness, and provides a roadmap for scalable, secure, and trustworthy agentic systems. All artifacts are publicly released for reproducibility.

Abstract: The rapid transition from reactive large language models (LLMs) to persistent, action-capable systems has exposed critical gaps in the architectural understanding of Agentic AI, particularly in separating inference, orchestration, and execution layers for autonomous AI agents. Despite recent advances, unified frameworks for designing and evaluating full-stack agentic systems remain limited. This paper presents a comprehensive, layered architecture for Agentic AI, outlining the evolution from reactive LLM interfaces to persistent, goal-driven autonomous AI agents with memory, planning, and continuous execution. We analyze OpenClaw and Ollama as a full-stack Agentic AI system, where Ollama serves as the LLM inference layer and OpenClaw enables agent runtime orchestration, integrating reasoning, tool use, and action execution. A prototype experimental validation of the OpenClaw-Ollama architecture demonstrates that capabilities such as persistent memory, tool utilization, and adaptive decision-making emerge from system-level integration rather than standalone models, with performance improving consistently as architectural complexity increases. The study further examines challenges in scalability, security, privacy, governance, and evaluation of agentic systems, highlighting the need for robust benchmarking and system-level design. Future directions include scalable multi-agent architectures, distributed autonomous systems, and human-aware Agentic AI frameworks for responsible deployment. Overall, this work establishes a unified architectural foundation for Agentic AI, validates the effectiveness of full-stack autonomous AI agents, and provides a roadmap for building scalable, secure, and trustworthy agentic systems. All models, code, and datasets are publicly released to support reproducibility and benchmarking.

</details>


### [53] [Can AI Evaluate AI Scientists? A Benchmarking Study of Autonomous Research Generation Systems Using Automated Multi-Model Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28631)
*Vaibhava Lakshmi Ravideshik,Mayank Kejriwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a rigorous benchmarking protocol using automated LLM peer review to evaluate AI Scientist systems across originality, rigor, clarity, and significance. Four frameworks (Sakana AI v1 & v2, CycleResearcher, Data-to-Paper) generated 60 papers alongside 15 FARS benchmark papers. Three independent LLM reviewers found FARS significantly outperforms competitors, with strong inter-reviewer agreement (Gemini-Claude ρ=0.907) validating automated evaluation reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating and comparing the quality of AI-generated scientific papers remains an open challenge. As AI Scientist systems capable of autonomous research proliferate, there is no established quantitative benchmark or standardized protocol for assessing their output quality, hindering progress in the field.

Method: The authors propose an automated peer-review benchmarking protocol using three frontier LLM reviewers (GPT-5.4, Gemini, Claude) that assess papers across four dimensions: originality, scientific rigor, clarity, and significance on a 1–5 scale. Four AI Scientist frameworks (Sakana AI v1 & v2, CycleResearcher, Data-to-Paper) each generated papers from 15 consistent research proposals published by FARS, producing 60 papers evaluated alongside 15 FARS benchmark papers. Inter-reviewer agreement was measured using Spearman's ρ.

Result: FARS benchmark papers significantly outperformed all competing frameworks, achieving mean scores of 2.14–2.47 versus 1.00–1.87 for other systems — more than 2× higher on Gemini and Claude evaluations. Gemini and Claude showed strong agreement (ρ=0.907, p<0.001), both correlating extremely strongly with the synthesis score (ρ=0.961, p<0.001). GPT-5.4 exhibited weaker agreement (ρ≈0.32), suggesting divergent evaluation criteria.

Conclusion: The paper establishes the first quantitative benchmark for AI Scientist systems and demonstrates that multi-model LLM evaluation provides a scalable, consistent framework for assessing autonomous research quality, though reviewer model selection matters due to criterion divergence.

Abstract: AI Scientist systems capable of autonomous research have the potential to significantly accelerate scientific discovery. However, evaluating and comparing the quality of AI-generated papers remains an open challenge. We propose and implement a rigorous benchmarking protocol using an automated peer-review system that harnesses frontier large language models to assess scientific papers across four core dimensions: originality, scientific rigor, clarity, and significance. We evaluate four leading AI Scientist frameworks: \textit{Sakana AI (v1 & v2)}, \textit{CycleResearcher}, and \textit{Data-to-Paper}. Each framework was run on a consistent set of 15 research proposals published by a commercial autonomous AI scientist company (FARS), generating 60 papers that we evaluate alongside 15 FARS benchmark papers. Using three independent LLM reviewers (GPT-5.4, Gemini, and Claude), we find that FARS benchmark papers significantly outperform all competing frameworks, achieving mean scores of 2.14--2.47 on a 1--5 scale compared to 1.00--1.87 for other systems. Notably, FARS scores are more than 2$\times$ higher than the next-best systems on Gemini and Claude evaluations. We find strong agreement among Gemini and Claude ($ρ$ = 0.907, $p < 0.001$), and both correlate extremely strongly with the synthesis score ($ρ$ = 0.961, $p < 0.001$), validating the reliability of automated evaluation. However, GPT-5.4 exhibits weaker agreement ($ρ\approx 0.32$), suggesting it evaluates papers using different criteria. These results establish the first quantitative benchmark for AI Scientist systems and demonstrate that multi-model LLM evaluation provides a scalable, consistent framework for assessing autonomous research quality.

</details>


### [54] [LLM Framework for Discovering Major Mathematical Conjectures: AI's Quest for the Next Riemann Hypothesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28632)
*Alizer Wong,Zixin Zeng,Yi Tan,Wenyuan Li,Xuhang Chen,Xingru Lai,Yang Shi,Liangsi Lu,Yanhui Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a three-stage pipeline for automated mathematical conjecture discovery: region search from local evidence, reflective validation for quality assessment, and formal verification in Lean 4/Mathlib. All 20 test candidates passed formal parsing/type-checking while proving non-trivial (not solvable by exact? or aesop) and free of duplicates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Major mathematical conjectures still rely heavily on expert intuition, and no unified method exists for systematically generating and validating conjectures with genuine mathematical potential. The authors aim to automate the discovery of "high problem taste" conjectures—problems whose proofs could reorganize research areas and provide durable assistance to human mathematicians.

Method: A three-stage pipeline: (1) region search using explicit local evidence modules to generate candidate conjectures; (2) reflective validation assessing foundationality, novelty, and potential significance; (3) formal validation in Lean 4 and Mathlib, including parsing, type-checking, and automated proof attempts via `exact?` and `aesop` to confirm non-triviality.

Result: All 20 candidate conjectures passed Lean parsing and type-checking. None were directly absorbed by `exact?`, none were automatically discharged by `aesop`, and no explicit or near duplicates were found, demonstrating stable transition from natural language to formal verification with non-triviality confirmed.

Conclusion: The three-stage pipeline successfully produces formally verifiable, non-trivial, and original mathematical conjectures, establishing a viable automated approach to conjecture generation with potential to aid human mathematical research.

Abstract: Major mathematical conjectures still depend heavily on expert intuition, so a unified method for the systematic generation and validation of conjectures with substantial mathematical potential remains unavailable. We present a three stage pipeline for major conjecture discovery, with region search from explicit local evidence modules, reflective validation for foundationality, novelty, and potential significance, and formal validation in Lean 4 and Mathlib. The objective is the discovery of mathematical problems with high problem taste, namely problems whose proofs could reorganize the language of a research area and provide durable help to human mathematical research. Experiments on twenty candidates showstable passage from natural language to formal checks, with twenty out of twenty candidates passing Lean parsing and type checking, twenty out of twenty candidates not directly absorbed by exact?,twenty out of twenty candidates not automatically discharged by aesop, and no explicit duplicates or near duplicates.

</details>


### [55] [Multi-Agent Planning with Spatio-Temporal and Topological Constraints using STL-GO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28679)
*Sheryl Paul,Vidisha Kudalkar,Anand Balakrishnan,Lars Lindemann,Alberto Speranzon,Jyotirmoy V. Deshmukh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses multi-agent path planning under spatio-temporal logic with graph operators (STL-GO), a formalism that captures temporal, spatial, and topological constraints (e.g., sensing, communication graphs). The authors propose two encodings—mixed-integer programming (MIP) and satisfiability modulo theory (SMT)—both with soundness guarantees, and provide a unified interface for specifying constraints, topologies, and STL-GO formulas. Evaluations on a multi-UAV search-and-rescue benchmark demonstrate expressiveness under dynamic multi-graph interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent planning in applications like wildfire fighting and factory inspection must satisfy complex spatio-temporal and topological constraints (which agents interact, when, and where). While spatio-temporal logics exist to formalize such requirements, planning paths that satisfy STL-GO specifications—especially with multiple time-varying graphs (sensing, communication, task topologies)—remains challenging. Existing approaches lack unified, sound encodings that handle the interplay of dynamic graphs and temporal logic constraints for multi-agent systems.

Method: The authors develop two sound encodings for multi-agent path planning under STL-GO specifications: (1) a mixed-integer programming (MIP) formulation and (2) a satisfiability modulo theory (SMT) formulation. They also provide a unified specification interface that allows users to define agent constraints, graph topologies (potentially time-varying), and STL-GO formulas, enabling direct comparison between the two solving approaches. The encodings handle STL-GO's graph operators for reasoning about sensing, communication, and task topologies across multiple agents.

Result: Both encodings are evaluated on a multi-UAV search-and-rescue benchmark, with ablation studies over team size and graph complexity. The results highlight the expressiveness of the proposed encodings under dynamic multi-graph interactions, demonstrating their ability to generate plans that satisfy complex spatio-temporal and topological constraints.

Conclusion: The paper successfully presents MIP and SMT encodings with soundness guarantees for multi-agent planning under STL-GO, along with a unified interface. The approach effectively handles dynamic, time-varying graph topologies and spatio-temporal constraints, as demonstrated in UAV search-and-rescue scenarios. This work advances the state of the art in formal multi-agent planning with rich topological and temporal specifications.

Abstract: Multi-agent planning problems arise in a variety of engineering applications, such as multi-robot wildfire fighting and unmanned aerial inspection in factories. A particular challenge is the existence of spatio-temporal (i.e., when and/or where an agent should do what) and topological constraints (i.e., how agents should interact), as typically formalized via the notion of graphs. Over the last years, various frameworks have been proposed that can capture such constraints via spatio-temporal logics. We focus here on spatio-temporal logic with graph operators (STL-GO), a recent formalism that supports reasoning about multiple agents and their topologies, such as sensing, communication, and task topologies. In this paper, we consider the problem of planning multi-agent paths that satisfy constraints written in STL-GO. This problem is particularly challenging due to the need of encoding multiple, potentially time-varying graphs via the graph operators inherent to STL-GO. We present two encodings of this problem, one based on mixed-integer programming (MIP) and another based on satisfiability modulo theory (SMT), with soundness guarantees. We provide a unified interface for specifying agent constraints, their graph topologies, and the STL-GO specification, enabling seamless use of both methods and facilitating direct comparison between them. We evaluate both encodings on a multi-UAV search-and-rescue benchmark, ablating over team size and graph complexity, highlighting the expressiveness of the proposed encodings under dynamic multi- graph interactions.

</details>


### [56] [ThinkReset: Learnable Intermediate Interface Construction for Bounded-Context Long-Horizon Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28642)
*Fei Ding,Yongkang Zhang,Runhao Liu,Yuhao Liao,Zijian Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ThinkReset addresses the context-window bottleneck in long chain-of-thought reasoning by introducing reusable intermediate interfaces with explicit writeback/reset operations, optimizing for post-reset continuation success rather than final-answer rewards alone, thereby reducing premature guessing and improving success rates under fixed context budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long chain-of-thought reasoning suffers from three problems under bounded context windows: redundancy accumulation, context overflow, and error anchoring. The authors argue the core bottleneck is the lack of a reusable intermediate interface that can replace discarded history and support continued reasoning. Furthermore, outcome-reward-driven RL encourages premature guessing when the context window is nearly exhausted and the task remains unsolved, rather than promoting continued careful reasoning.

Method: ThinkReset introduces a text-space mechanism with two key components: (1) interface writeback and reset — the model explicitly constructs a reusable intermediate representation (a compressed state summary) and performs a context reset, discarding prior redundant reasoning while preserving essential intermediate state; (2) direct optimization for post-reset continuation success, training the model to effectively resume and complete reasoning after the reset operation, rather than relying solely on final-answer outcome rewards.

Result: ThinkReset consistently improves success rates across multiple long-horizon reasoning benchmarks under fixed context window constraints, demonstrating the effectiveness of reusable intermediate interfaces and post-reset continuation optimization.

Conclusion: The core bottleneck in bounded-context long-chain reasoning is not trajectory compression or test-time control, but the absence of a reusable intermediate interface. ThinkReset provides an effective text-space solution by combining interface writeback/reset with post-reset continuation training, mitigating premature guessing and enabling sustained careful reasoning under fixed context budgets.

Abstract: Long chain-of-thought reasoning improves performance on complex problems, but it also introduces redundancy accumulation, context overflow, and error anchoring. We argue that under bounded context windows, the core bottleneck is not trajectory compression or test-time control, but the absence of a reusable intermediate interface that can replace discarded history and support continued solving. We further identify a key failure mode of outcome-reward-driven long-chain reinforcement learning: when the model has not solved the task before the window is nearly exhausted, the final-answer reward encourages premature guessing rather than continued careful reasoning. We propose ThinkReset, a text-space instantiation of this view. ThinkReset explicitly constructs reusable intermediate interfaces through interface writeback and reset, and directly optimizes post-reset continuation success. Across multiple long-horizon reasoning benchmarks, this perspective consistently improves success rates under fixed context windows.

</details>


### [57] [TAPR: Enhancing LLM Performance with a Task-Aware Prompt Rewriter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28657)
*Oliver Savolainen,Emanuele Bastianelli,Hosein Azarbonyad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TAPR is a Task-Aware Prompt Rewriter fine-tuned via GRPO reinforcement learning that reformulates suboptimal user prompts into clearer, task-optimized versions, yielding consistent accuracy gains across QA, summarization, and arithmetic reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are highly sensitive to prompt quality, yet non-expert users struggle to craft effective prompts, creating a performance gap. Automating prompt optimization lowers this barrier and unlocks LLM potential without requiring users to master prompt engineering.

Method: TAPR is built by fine-tuning Phi-4-mini-instruct using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a reinforcement learning approach. Rewards are computed via LLM-as-judge evaluations that assess both the quality of the reformulated prompt and the correctness/quality of the downstream task output generated from that prompt.

Result: TAPR consistently outperforms base models in prompt rewriting across diverse tasks — question answering (Natural Questions), summarization, and arithmetic reasoning (GSM8K). Reformulated prompts exhibit clearer, more instructive language and lead to measurably higher task accuracy.

Conclusion: Reinforcement learning with GRPO and LLM-as-judge rewards is an effective strategy for training prompt rewriters. TAPR demonstrates that automated prompt reformulation can meaningfully improve downstream LLM performance, making strong LLM results more accessible to non-expert users.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) often require carefully crafted prompts to unlock their full potential, which can be a barrier for non-expert users. This work addresses the challenge by introducing a Task-Aware Prompt Rewriter (TAPR), a model that reformulates user prompts into task-optimized prompts with the explicit goal of improving downstream LLM performance. We train TAPR using reinforcement learning with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), where rewards are derived from LLM-as-judge evaluations of both the reformulated prompt and the corresponding task output. Experimental results on diverse tasks, such as question answering, summarization, and arithmetic reasoning, show that our method yields consistent gains over base models in prompt rewriting ability. Fine-tuning Phi-4-mini-instruct (as the base model for TAPR) produces prompts that contain clearer and more instructive language, leading to higher accuracy on established benchmarks such as Natural Questions and GSM8K. Our code is available at: https://github.com/OliverSavolainen/task-specific-prompt-rewriter

</details>


### [58] [Beyond Component Testing: Validating Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29405)
*Fabio Orazio Mirto,Luca D'Agati,Giuseppe Tricomi,Stefano Silvestri,Francesco Longo,Antonio Puliafito,Giovanni Merlino*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic AI systems require validation of multi-step trajectories rather than isolated component testing. This survey of 257 papers identifies five key validation dimensions—behavioral, safety, temporal, regulatory, and multi-agent—and finds that temporal validity, runtime evidence, regulatory legibility, and multi-agent assurance remain under-developed. The paper proposes a lifecycle research agenda centered on trajectory-based validation in context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional validation methods—component testing and one-shot input-output evaluation—are insufficient for agentic AI systems, whose acceptable behavior depends on how decisions unfold over time and under changing environmental conditions. There is a need to systematically characterize the validation problem and identify coverage gaps in current approaches.

Method: A systematic survey synthesizing 257 papers across agent evaluation, software assurance, cyber-physical systems, runtime monitoring, and regulatory guidance. The review constructs a five-dimension taxonomy (behavioral, safety, temporal, regulatory, multi-agent) to map current approaches, expose coverage gaps, and validate findings through three cross-domain case studies (medical care, industrial operations, smart-mobility).

Result: Behavioral evaluation is comparatively mature. However, four areas remain significantly under-developed: temporal validity, runtime evidence maintenance, regulatory legibility, and open-ended multi-agent systems assurance. The three case studies confirm that all five taxonomy dimensions recur in safety-critical settings, grounded in documented failure patterns.

Conclusion: Trustworthy deployment of agentic AI depends on validating trajectories in context rather than assessing isolated components alone. The paper proposes a lifecycle-oriented research agenda focused on bounded-autonomy specifications, adversarial trajectory generation, runtime monitoring, and audit-ready evidence structures.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems act through multi-step trajectories that combine planning, tool use, memory, interaction, and adaptation. This behavior stretches validation practice beyond component testing and one-shot input--output evaluation, because acceptable system behavior now depends on how decisions unfold over time and under changing environmental conditions. This survey synthesizes 257 papers spanning agent evaluation, software assurance, cyber-physical systems, runtime monitoring, and regulatory guidance in order to characterize the validation problem for agentic systems. The review is organized around a five-dimension taxonomy covering behavioral, safety, temporal, regulatory, and multi-agent concerns, and uses that taxonomy to map current approaches and expose recurrent coverage gaps. The analysis shows that behavioral evaluation is comparatively mature, while temporal validity, runtime evidence maintenance, regulatory legibility, and open-ended multi-agent systems assurance remain under-developed. Three cross-domain case studies (medical care, industrial operations, smart-mobility systems) provide operational illustrations of how the five taxonomy dimensions recur in safety-critical settings, grounded in the failure patterns documented in the reviewed literature. The paper concludes with a lifecycle-oriented research agenda centered on bounded-autonomy specifications, adversarial trajectory generation, runtime monitoring, and audit-ready evidence structures. The central claim is that trustworthy deployment of agentic AI depends on validating trajectories in context rather than assessing isolated components alone.

</details>


### [59] [Empowering Cross-Domain Sequential Recommendation with Hybrid Tokenization and Serial-Parallel Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28659)
*Yuxuan Hu,Yuhao Wang,Tianbo Huang,Chao Zhang,Ziwei Liu,Lihua Zhang,Xiangyu Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GenCDSR is a generative cross-domain sequential recommendation framework that introduces a hybrid tokenization mechanism with multi-tower codebooks and a serial-parallel decoding strategy, achieving 1.5% accuracy gains and 85.1% inference latency reduction over state-of-the-art baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative CDSR methods suffer from two key shortcomings: (1) tokenization ignores cross-domain collaborative correlations, weakening the learned semantic identifiers, and (2) inefficient decoding strategies like beam search hinder real-time deployment, creating an accuracy-efficiency trade-off.

Method: GenCDSR proposes two core components: (1) a cross-domain hybrid tokenization mechanism using a multi-tower architecture with hierarchical shared-specific and fine-grained codebooks to capture both cross-domain commonalities and domain-specific distinctions; and (2) a cross-domain serial-parallel decoding strategy that exploits the hierarchical SID structure to partially parallelize generation, reducing inference latency while maintaining generation consistency.

Result: On three public datasets, GenCDSR achieves an average accuracy improvement of 1.5% and an average inference latency reduction of 85.1% compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: GenCDSR effectively addresses the tokenization and decoding inefficiencies in generative CDSR, delivering both superior recommendation accuracy and significantly faster inference, making it suitable for real-time cross-domain recommendation scenarios.

Abstract: Cross-domain sequential recommendation (CDSR) aims to model users' dynamic interest transitions and sequential patterns across multiple domains. Recently, generative recommendation (GR) has emerged. It first learns semantic identifiers (SIDs) from item semantics and formulates recommendation as autoregressive generation. However, existing methods face two critical issues: (1) they ignore collaborative correlations across domains during tokenization, and (2) they adopt inefficient decoding strategies, such as beam search, during generation, which hinders real-time deployment. To address these limitations, we propose GenCDSR, an effective and efficient generative framework for CDSR. Specifically, we design a cross-domain hybrid tokenization mechanism with a multi-tower architecture to jointly capture cross-domain commonalities and domain-specific distinctions through hierarchical shared-specific and fine-grained codebooks. Furthermore, we develop a cross-domain serial-parallel decoding strategy that leverages the hierarchical SID structure to partially parallelize generation, significantly reducing inference latency while preserving generation consistency. Experiments on three public datasets show that GenCDSR achieves an average accuracy improvement of 1.5 percent and an average inference latency reduction of 85.1 percent compared with state-of-the-art baselines. The implementation code and datasets are available online: https://github.com/Applied-Machine-Learning-Lab/RecSys2026_GenCDSR.

</details>


### [60] [An Ontology-Guided, Deduplication-Aware Extraction Layer for Knowledge Graph Construction from Heterogeneous Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28662)
*Vaibhav Dangaich,Kevin Lewis,Kundeshwar Pundalik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A production knowledge graph extraction system that uses ontology-guided prompting with a fine-tuned LLM and a five-stage refinement pipeline to achieve 95% search recall with zero false merges, correcting seven classes of quality defects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs extract entities and relationships from unstructured documents fluently but inconsistently: type vocabularies fracture, name variants proliferate, relationships duplicate, and distinct individuals sharing a name risk silent conflation. A production-grade solution is needed that aligns extracted knowledge to a formal ontology with high fidelity.

Method: A streaming pipeline consumes documents from Kafka, routes them through format-specific handlers (PDF, spreadsheets, Office, images), and performs two-pass extraction using a locally hosted Qwen3.5-9B model fine-tuned on the target ontology. The core innovation is ontology-guided extraction: relevant ontology slices are retrieved live from a graph database via embedding similarity and injected into the prompt, reducing catalog overhead by ~94% versus static slices. Extracted results pass through a five-stage refinement pipeline: (1) deterministic cleaning, (2) cross-chunk merging, (3) a second relationship extraction pass, (4) six model-free deduplication algorithms, and (5) an embedding resolution subsystem with a conflict guard that no similarity score can override.

Result: Search recall improved from ~70% to 95% with zero false merges on intelligence corpora. Seven classes of silent quality defect were corrected, including a single-character source-text truncation bug and systematic duplication of entities carrying title prefixes.

Conclusion: Ontology-guided extraction with dynamic retrieval significantly reduces prompt overhead while maintaining alignment. A multi-stage, model-free refinement pipeline effectively resolves entity inconsistencies without introducing false merges, making the system suitable for production intelligence applications.

Abstract: Large language models extract entities and relationships from unstructured documents fluently but inconsistently: type vocabularies fracture across documents, the same person surfaces under several name variants, relationships duplicate, and distinct individuals who share a name risk silent conflation. This paper presents the design, implementation, and empirical refinement of a production extraction layer that converts a live document stream into a validated knowledge graph aligned to a formal ontology. The system consumes document metadata from Kafka, routes PDF, spreadsheet, Office, and image content through handlers built for each format, and extracts entities and relationships in two passes using a locally hosted Qwen3.5-9B model tuned on the ontology. Its distinguishing component is ontology-guided extraction: the relevant slice of a curated ontology is retrieved live from a graph database by embedding similarity and injected into the extraction prompt, reducing catalog overhead by about 94 percent relative to static domain slices. Extracted results then pass through a refinement pipeline of five stages: deterministic cleaning, merging across chunks, a second pass for relationships, six deduplication algorithms that require no model inference, and an embedding resolution subsystem whose conflict guard no similarity score can override. Evaluation on intelligence corpora improved search recall from roughly 70 to 95 percent with no false merges, and corrected seven classes of silent quality defect, ranging from a bug that truncated source text by a single character to the systematic duplication of entities that carried title prefixes.

</details>


### [61] [How Hard Does It Think? Analyzing Step-Aware Reasoning Energy in LLM Chain-of-Thought Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28674)
*Hui Wei,Junda Wu,Sheldon Yu,Sizhe Zhou,Yizhu Jiao,Ming Zhong,Bowen Jin,Tong Yu,Shijia Pan,Jiawei Han,Julian McAuley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SARE quantifies per-step reasoning effort in Chain-of-Thought via CKA-based geometric analysis of transformer hidden states, revealing non-uniform energy distributions, phase transitions, and predictive signals for answer correctness beyond surface-level confidence metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing interpretability methods for Chain-of-Thought reasoning either rely on output-level signals (post-hoc) or aggregate processing depth into a single trajectory-level scalar, rendering step-wise computational effort opaque. The authors aim to fill this gap by enabling fine-grained, step-level analysis of how models allocate reasoning effort.

Method: SARE employs Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) between Gram matrices of token hidden states across adjacent transformer layers to measure per-step reasoning energy, capturing inter-token relational structure without requiring eigenvector alignment. It then contextualizes this energy within a semantic progression model that treats CoT trajectories as transitions among latent semantic states.

Result: Across 6 reasoning benchmarks and 3 open-weight LLMs: (1) reasoning energy is highly non-uniform across step types with phase-like transitions; (2) incorrect trajectories exhibit systematically lower energy at critical reasoning junctions; (3) SARE-based features match or surpass output-based confidence baselines for predicting answer correctness.

Conclusion: Internal geometric dynamics captured by SARE encode predictive information about reasoning quality that goes beyond surface-level output signals, offering a new lens for interpreting and evaluating chain-of-thought reasoning at step-level granularity.

Abstract: Understanding how computational effort is allocated across individual chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning steps remains an open challenge: existing interpretability methods rely on output-level signals or collapse processing depth into a single trajectory-level scalar, leaving step-wise effort opaque. We propose Step-Aware Reasoning Energy (SARE), a geometric framework that quantifies effort at the granularity of individual CoT steps via Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) between Gram matrices of token hidden states across adjacent transformer layers, capturing inter-token relational structure without requiring eigenvector alignment or cluster correspondence. SARE further contextualizes this energy within reasoning's semantic progression by modeling CoT trajectories as transitions among latent semantic states. Across six reasoning benchmarks and three open-weight LLMs, we find that reasoning energy is highly non-uniform across step types, exhibiting phase-like transitions invisible to trajectory-level metrics; incorrect trajectories show systematically lower energy at critical reasoning junctions; and SARE-based features match or outperform output-based confidence baselines in most settings, indicating that internal geometric dynamics encode predictive information beyond surface-level signals.

</details>


### [62] [Reasoning in Real World Clinical Care: Why Large Language Models Are Not Yet Safe for Autonomous Clinical Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28677)
*Shayndhan Sivanathan,Shravan Nageswaran,Mehdi Zadem,Ryaan Sultan,Nicolas von Mallinckrodt,Max Solovyev,Alexey Matyushkin,Sumon Sadhu,Gabriele C DeLuca,Sanjeeva Jeyaretna,James Hillis,Manoj Ramachandran,Prakash Jayakumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs lack proven safety for autonomous patient triage due to fundamental deficits in information gathering under uncertainty, where missing a catastrophic diagnosis far outweighs the cost of false alarms — a task misaligned with models optimized for probable text continuation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed for clinical decision support and triage, yet the evidence for their safety in autonomous triage of undifferentiated patients — where no clinician is in the loop — has not been established. The concern is not about medical knowledge deficits but about the fidelity of clinical evaluation under uncertainty and asymmetric risk.

Method: This is a Perspective article. It synthesizes clinical reasoning principles, triage logic, and known LLM failure modes to argue that safe triage requires sequential decision-making under asymmetric cost, active information gathering, and behaviors (broadening differentials, seeking red flags, lowering escalation thresholds) that current LLM systems are not optimized to perform.

Result: The paper identifies specific LLM failure modes in triage: failing to broaden the differential diagnosis, not seeking missing red flags, insufficient escalation threshold lowering, premature judgment without adequate information, and failure to exclude high-harm diagnoses. These are compounded by assistant-like positive biases (credulity, agreeableness, miscalibration) and are difficult to detect in evaluations using complete, curated, confidence-gated simulations.

Conclusion: Safe autonomous triage is a fundamentally different task from diagnostic reasoning. Until LLMs can reliably perform information-gathering under uncertainty — actively seeking unvolunteered signals and escalating when catastrophic diagnoses remain unexcluded — deploying them without clinician oversight is premature and potentially dangerous.

Abstract: LLM now pass medical licensing examinations and, in curated cases, can rival physicians at diagnostic reasoning. These developments have accelerated the use of LLMs for symptom assessment and clinical decision support in diagnostic and treatment guidance, administrative documentation, and rules-based alert enhancement. This Perspective concerns the most consequential of these applications: the autonomous triage of self-presenting, undifferentiated patients, with little or no clinician in the loop. For that task, the evidence of safety does not yet exist. The gap is not in medical knowledge but in the fidelity of clinical evaluation: a model optimized to continue the most probable text is not optimized to act safely when the safe answer is the improbable must-not-miss diagnosis. Safe triage is not the selection of the most likely diagnosis; it is a sequential decision under asymmetric cost, in which the single catastrophic miss outweighs many false alarms, and the decisive signal may be one the patient has not volunteered - and that the model has not been trained to seek. The core deficit is therefore one of information gathering under uncertainty. Under incomplete histories, LLM systems may fail to show the behaviors safe triage requires: broadening the differential; seeking the missing red flag; lowering the threshold for escalation; deferring judgement until sufficient information is obtained; and escalating concern where high-harm diagnoses remain unexcluded. These modes of failure for LLMs can be difficult to detect considering that evaluations to date often use complete, well-curated, confidence-gated simulations. The application of LLMs under these conditions may be amplified by assistant-like behaviors and positive bias, including credulity, agreeableness, and miscalibration - when these are not constrained by clinical triage logic.

</details>


### [63] [ViSAGE: Constructing Self-Correcting Memories for Long-Form Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28678)
*Xinkui Zhao,Enbo Chen,Yifan Zhang,Chang Liu,Guanjie Cheng,Naibo Wang,Yueshen Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ViSAGE is a multimodal agentic memory framework that builds entity-centric, self-correcting memories using cross-modal identity binding, bidirectional memory refinement, and multi-agent cross-verification, achieving 5.9% higher accuracy than the strongest baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal agent memory systems suffer from two key failures: (1) aggressive compression and segment-wise processing discard fine-grained identity cues, and (2) over-reliance on vector similarity retrieval surfaces semantically related but identity-mismatched evidence, causing entity confusion, error propagation, and hallucinated answers in long-horizon environments.

Method: ViSAGE introduces three core mechanisms: (1) cross-modal binding that anchors entity identity over long temporal ranges by linking multimodal cues; (2) bidirectional memory refinement that propagates delayed identity evidence both retroactively (to correct historical records) and forward (to improve future reasoning); and (3) multi-agent cross-verification that evaluates retrieved evidence under an identity-evidence alignment constraint, enabling abstention when evidence is insufficient rather than generating unsupported answers.

Result: ViSAGE consistently outperforms the strongest baseline across extensive evaluations, achieving a 5.9% improvement in accuracy.

Conclusion: Entity-centric, self-correcting memory with cross-modal identity binding and cross-verification effectively mitigates entity confusion and hallucination in long-horizon multimodal agents, with abstention serving as a safer alternative to unsupported answering.

Abstract: Multimodal agents operating in long-horizon environments must build and continually update multimedia memories to support entity-consistent, temporally grounded reasoning. However, existing agentic memory approaches often discard fine-grained dentity cues under aggressive compression and segment-wise processing. They also rely heavily on vector similarity retrieval, which can surface semantically related yet identity-mismatched evidence, leading to entity confusion, error propagation, and hallucinated answers.
  We propose ViSAGE, a multimodal agentic memory framework that constructs self-correcting, entity-centric memories. Specifically, ViSAGE anchors entity identity via cross-modal binding over long temporal ranges. It then applies bidirectional memory refinement to propagate delayed identity evidence, retroactively unifying historical records and improving future reasoning. We also introduce multi-agent cross-verification to assess retrieved evidence under an identity-evidence alignment onstraint, enabling abstention instead of unsupported answers when evidence is missing. Extensive results demonstrate that ViSAGE consistently outperforms the strongest baseline, achieving 5.9% higher accuracy.

</details>


### [64] [Library Reachability in LSR-Synth: How Anti-Memorization Design Changes the Measurement of Symbolic Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28684)
*Zhan'ao Yao,Liang Yin,Zhihao Gao,Boxuan Zhang,Xiaoyu Wu,Linjing Li,Rongyan Wang,Tingwei Chen,Youwei Wang,Xiaolin Zhao,Jiahui Shi,Jianjun Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LSR-Synth benchmarks mitigate memorization by injecting novel synthetic terms into known equations. This paper shows that a semantics-free fixed-vocabulary baseline already solves most tasks, and language model priors only help when vocabulary coverage is artificially disrupted—suggesting current tasks evaluate expression recombination rather than genuine prior-driven discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing scientific equation discovery benchmarks risk conflating genuine discovery from data with memorization of publicly available equations. While LSR-Synth addresses this by introducing novel synthetic terms, the authors probe a narrower question: do these tasks actually distinguish between scientific priors supplied by language models and conventional semantics-free operator search? The goal is to isolate whether language model priors contribute meaningfully beyond a fixed search vocabulary.

Method: The authors construct a semantics-free baseline using a fixed vocabulary with publicly documented provenance. They then systematically assess candidate coverage through three interventions: (1) semantic blinding—removing access to task semantics, (2) library weakening—reducing the operator vocabulary, and (3) matched operator-family knockouts—selectively removing operator families. They evaluate under both standard and strict out-of-distribution settings, measuring whether language-model-generated candidates expand the set of solvable instances beyond the fixed vocabulary.

Result: Under the current task snapshot, search budget, and scoring protocol, the fixed vocabulary already covers most tasks. Language-model-generated candidates rarely expand the set of solvable instances. Their marginal contribution becomes substantial only when vocabulary coverage is selectively disrupted. Strict out-of-distribution evaluation lowers absolute success rates but does not alter this relationship between fixed vocabulary and LM priors.

Conclusion: The findings do not invalidate LSR-Synth's memorization controls nor imply language model priors are useless. Rather, they support a limited conclusion: current LSR-Synth tasks are sufficient for evaluating fitting and recombination of novel expressions, but are insufficient alone to identify contributions from priors beyond a fixed search space.

Abstract: Existing benchmarks for scientific equation discovery are largely composed of well-known equations available in the public domain, making it difficult to determine whether a model is discovering laws from data or merely recalling answers from its training corpus. LSR-Synth mitigates this problem by introducing novel synthetic terms into established scientific mechanisms and filtering the resulting tasks for novelty, solvability, and scientific plausibility. This paper examines a narrower measurement question: can these tasks further distinguish scientific priors supplied by language models from conventional operator search that does not access task semantics? We construct a semantics-free baseline using a fixed vocabulary with publicly documented provenance, and assess the role of candidate coverage through semantic blinding, library weakening, and matched operator-family knockouts. Under the current task snapshot, search budget, and scoring protocol, the fixed vocabulary already covers most tasks, while language-model-generated candidates rarely expand the set of solvable instances. Their marginal contribution becomes substantial only when vocabulary coverage is selectively disrupted. Strict out-of-distribution evaluation lowers the absolute success rates of all methods but does not alter this relationship. These findings neither invalidate LSR-Synth's controls against memorization of complete formulas nor imply that language-model priors are generally unhelpful. Rather, they support a more limited conclusion: most current tasks remain suitable for evaluating the fitting and recombination of previously unseen expressions, but are insufficient on their own to identify contributions from priors beyond a fixed search space.

</details>


### [65] [Safety, or Just Capability? A Validity Audit of Agent-Safety Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28685)
*Youting Wang,Xiao Han,Dingyan Shang,Yuan Tang,Bowen Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper audits four agent-safety benchmarks (R-Judge, InjecAgent, AgentHarm, AgentDojo) as measurement instruments, revealing that their scoring metrics are flawed, their rankings of models diverge due to small-panel artifacts, and capability predicts task success but correlates negatively with misalignment safety. The authors argue that safety claims require specifying the benchmark, metric, target behavior, and model panel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent-safety benchmark scores are routinely quoted interchangeably as if they measure a unified construct of "agent safety," yet no systematic validation of these instruments exists. The authors aim to subject these benchmarks to measurement validation — treating them not as ground truth but as instruments whose reliability, convergent validity, and predictive validity must be empirically established.

Method: Four benchmarks (R-Judge, InjecAgent, AgentHarm, AgentDojo) are run under their official implementations and author-provided scorers on up to 22 models, with MMLU and GPQA measured under a single protocol as a capability composite. Analyses include: (1) analytic derivation of the F₁ baseline for binary trace-judgment benchmarks, (2) rank-correlation comparisons across benchmarks, (3) small-panel subsampling to quantify ranking instability, (4) held-out validity correlations between benchmark scores and external safety/capability outcomes, and (5) leave-one-organization-out and organization-clustered bootstrap robustness checks.

Result: An "always positive" policy attains F₁ = 0.690 on R-Judge, outperforming 5 of 21 discriminating models. The three broad-coverage benchmarks rank the same 18 models differently; R-Judge specificity vs. AgentHarm safety correlates ρ = −0.64 at n=7 but ρ = +0.02 at n=18, with 25% of random size-7 subsets reaching |ρ| ≥ 0.5. Capability correlates ρ = +0.60 with task success but ρ = −0.44 with misalignment safety (n=21). On paired n=20, the capability–safety contrast is Δ = −1.00 (95% CI [−1.48, −0.49], p < 0.001). AgentHarm shows the strongest held-out validity (ρ = +0.72 with jailbreak safety after controlling capability), but this reflects convergent validity among instruments measuring harmful compliance, not general safety.

Conclusion: Agent-safety benchmarks suffer from metric flaws (trivial F₁ baselines), severe ranking instability under small panels, and construct confusion — what they measure is often harmful compliance rather than general safety. Capability is positively associated with task completion but negatively associated with misalignment safety, a robust finding. The minimum required for any safety claim is to name the benchmark, metric, target behavior, and model panel.

Abstract: Agent-safety benchmarks measure different behaviors, and their scores get quoted interchangeably as an agent's safety. We treat four of them (R-Judge, InjecAgent, AgentHarm, AgentDojo) as measurements to be validated, running each under its official implementation and author-provided scorer on up to 22 models, with MMLU and GPQA measured by us under one protocol as a capability composite. The metric is the first problem. On any binary trace-judgment benchmark scored by $F_1$, an ``always positive'' policy attains $F_1 = 2π/(1+π)$; on R-Judge that is $0.690$, above five of the 21 models that actually discriminate. The three broad-coverage benchmarks then rank the same 18 models differently, and the trade-off behind that disagreement is a small-panel artifact: R-Judge specificity against AgentHarm safety correlates $-0.64$ at $n{=}7$ and $+0.02$ at $n{=}18$, and a quarter of random size-7 subsets reach $|ρ| \geq 0.5$ around that near-zero value. Held-out validity turns on which outcome you pick. Capability predicts task success ($ρ{=}{+}0.60$) but correlates negatively with misalignment safety ($ρ{=}{-}0.44$, $n{=}21$). On their paired $n{=}20$ panel, the corresponding contrast is $Δ{=}{-}1.00$ (95% CI $[-1.48, -0.49]$, $p<0.001$), and it survives leave-one-organization-out and organization-clustered bootstrap analyses. On an expanded 41-model panel, the misalignment correlation weakens to $-0.16$ (95% CI $[-0.54, +0.22]$) and jailbreak strengthens to $+0.34$, though neither change is significant. \mbox{AgentHarm} shows the strongest held-out association, $ρ{=}{+}0.72$ with three-template jailbreak safety after controlling capability. But both instruments score harmful compliance, so this is evidence of convergent validity rather than general safety. Naming the benchmark, metric, target behavior, and model panel is the minimum a safety claim needs.

</details>


### [66] [SciToolAgent-Evo: An Ontology-Aware Self-Evolving Agent for Open-World Scientific Tool Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28692)
*Yuqi Tang,Chenyi Zhou,Libin Wang,Keyan Ding,Qiang Zhang,Huajun Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciToolAgent-Evo is an ontology-aware, self-evolving LLM agent that dynamically acquires and integrates novel scientific tools using an evolving memory, contrastive trajectory learning, and a LinUCB-based exploration-exploitation gate, achieving state-of-the-art performance on the newly introduced OpenSciToolBench benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents rely on predefined tool spaces with static semantics, which limits their applicability in open-world scientific workflows where tool requirements, capabilities, and boundaries change dynamically. There is a need for agents that can autonomously discover, acquire, and integrate novel tools without manual reconfiguration.

Method: SciToolAgent-Evo features: (1) an evolving memory of skills, experiences, and an ontologized tool graph that distills generalizable knowledge from contrastive trajectories during accumulation; (2) during inference, it formulates active tool requests and uses a LinUCB-based bandit gate to dynamically balance exploration vs. exploitation; (3) when a novel tool is acquired, its scientific ontology is completed online for seamless integration into the known graph. The method is evaluated on OpenSciToolBench, a new benchmark with 900 tasks across four difficulty levels.

Result: SciToolAgent-Evo achieves state-of-the-art performance on OpenSciToolBench, demonstrating robust generalization across diverse and dynamically evolving scientific tool-use scenarios.

Conclusion: The self-evolving, ontology-aware design enables LLM agents to break free from static tool spaces and operate effectively in open-world scientific environments, with strong empirical validation on a comprehensive benchmark.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents have been increasingly adopted in scientific research for organizing and invoking specialized computational tools. However, their reliance on predefined tool spaces with static semantics limits their applicability to open-world scientific workflows, where tool requirements, capabilities, and boundaries evolve dynamically. To this end, we propose SciToolAgent-Evo, an ontology-aware self-evolving agent for open-world scientific tool acquisition. Driven by an evolving memory of skills, experiences, and an ontologized tool graph, it distills generalizable knowledge from contrastive trajectories during accumulation, whereas during inference, it formulates active requests and utilizes a LinUCB-based bandit gate to dynamically balance exploration and exploitation. Once a novel tool is acquired, its scientific ontology is completed online for seamless integration into the known graph. Moreover, we introduce OpenSciToolBench, a benchmark containing 900 realistic tasks across four difficulty levels. Extensive evaluations show that SciToolAgent-Evo achieves state-of-the-art performance, validating its robustness and generalization.

</details>


### [67] [EarlyDx: An Admission-Anchored Benchmark for Open-Ended Generation of Evidence-Supported ED-Encounter Diagnoses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28788)
