*Jiahui Li,Ruili Fang,Zishuai Liu,Yutong Guo,Nan Yang,Wenzhan Song,Jin Lu,Fei Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EarlyDx is a benchmark for open-ended early diagnosis using 154,834 MIMIC-IV ED encounters, restricted to admission-time data and supervised by ED diagnoses (not discharge codes). An LLM auditor verifies label support. No system reliably synthesizes admission evidence; post-training improves inference-dependent recall to 56% but still falls short of clinician-level performance on time-critical conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing clinical diagnosis benchmarks are poorly suited for the real-world emergency department setting: they use closed diagnosis code sets, exclude free-text notes, and supervise with discharge diagnoses that incorporate information unavailable at admission. This creates a mismatch between benchmark design and the clinical task of making rapid, evidence-incomplete diagnoses at hospital admission.

Method: The authors construct EarlyDx from 154,834 MIMIC-IV ED encounters. Each encounter is restricted to records available at admission time (t₀) and supervised by diagnoses recorded during the ED encounter rather than at discharge. An LLM auditor verifies every free-text diagnosis label as "supported," "partially supported," or "unsupported" by the admission evidence; primary evaluation uses only fully supported labels. Systems are evaluated under a semantic LLM-as-judge protocol, benchmarking frontier general models, medical-specialized models, and in-domain post-trained models on both extraction-based and inference-dependent diagnoses.

Result: Zero-shot models predominantly extract rather than infer, recovering only 3–31% of inference-dependent diagnoses. Post-training raises inference-dependent recall to 56%, but a substantial margin remains. On time-critical conditions, no system matches a clinician's balance of sensitivity and precision.

Conclusion: EarlyDx reveals a significant gap in LLMs' ability to perform reliable inferential diagnosis from admission-time evidence, particularly for time-critical conditions. The benchmark and its construction pipeline are released openly to spur further research on this clinically important task.

Abstract: Clinical diagnosis at hospital admission must be made rapidly from limited, incomplete evidence. Existing diagnosis-prediction benchmarks are poorly suited to this setting: they restrict prediction to closed code sets, exclude free-text notes, and supervise with discharge diagnoses that incorporate the full inpatient course. We introduce EarlyDx, a large-scale benchmark for open-ended early diagnosis, built from 154,834 emergency department encounters in MIMIC-IV. Each encounter is restricted to records available at admission time $t_0$ and supervised by the diagnoses recorded during the ED encounter rather than at discharge. An LLM auditor further verifies every free-text label as supported, partially supported, or unsupported by that evidence; the primary evaluation scores only fully supported labels. Under a semantic LLM-as-judge protocol, no evaluated system --- frontier general, medical-specialized, or in-domain post-trained --- synthesizes admission-time evidence reliably. Zero-shot models score largely by extraction, recovering only 3-31% of diagnoses that must be inferred rather than read from the record; post-training raises inference-dependent recall to 56%, but a sizeable margin remains, and on time-critical conditions no system attains a clinician's balance of sensitivity and precision. We release the full construction and evaluation pipeline at here.

</details>


### [68] [Model or Harness? An Interaction-Centric Taxonomy for Localizing Agent Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28802)
*Harsh Raj,Vipul Gupta,Anas Mahmoud,Razvan-Gabriel Dumitru,Darvin Yi,Aakash Sabharwal,Yunzhong He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an interaction-centric failure taxonomy for AI agents that localizes failures to specific component interactions rather than system-level outcomes, enabling targeted repairs across model training, harness engineering, and environment redesign.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation practices reduce agent failures to system-level outcomes, obscuring the source of faults and creating a "repair-assignment problem" where the same visible failure could require different interventions depending on its origin. Existing failure taxonomies are benchmark-specific and lack shared structure, making them insufficient for guiding improvements.

Method: The authors introduce an interaction-centric taxonomy that organizes 41 failure modes by assigning each to an edge between two components (e.g., model–tool, agent–environment) and a fault side indicating where repair belongs. Failures are categorized as model-side (targeting post-training), harness-side (scaffolding/tool-integration fixes), or environment/grader failures (evaluation redesign). The taxonomy is grounded in worked examples from public benchmarks, system cards, published reports, and logged agent trajectories, and validated using independent reasoning agents as judges against human category labels.

Result: Across four frontier models, the strongest judge achieves Cohen's κ=0.76 against human category labels, indicating substantial inter-rater agreement and suggesting the taxonomy captures shared structural patterns rather than annotator-specific preferences.

Conclusion: The interaction-centric taxonomy provides a generalizable, actionable framework for diagnosing agent failures across diverse architectures and evaluation settings. By localizing failures to specific component interactions, it directly guides repair strategies and moves beyond outcome-level evaluation toward diagnostic assessment that can improve agent systems systematically.

Abstract: Existing evaluations often reduce agent failures to system-level outcomes, obscuring where the fault originated and which intervention would improve the agent system. This creates a repair-assignment problem: the same visible failure may call for model post-training, harness engineering, environment redesign, or benchmark repair depending on its source. Because agent behavior emerges from interactions among models, harnesses, users, tools, memory, and environments, outcome-level labels are often insufficient for improvement. Most failure taxonomies do little to resolve this problem because they are benchmark-specific and lack a shared structure. We introduce an interaction-centric taxonomy that localizes failures to the interactions in which they originate and identifies the responsible component. It organizes 41 failure modes by assigning each to an edge between two components and a fault side indicating where the repair belongs. This makes the taxonomy actionable: model-side failures identify targets for post-training, harness-side failures point to scaffolding and tool-integration fixes, and environment or grader failures reveal evaluation conditions requiring redesign. The schema applies across agent architectures, from coding assistants to long-horizon personal assistants and multi-agent systems. We ground the taxonomy in worked examples from public benchmarks, model system cards, published reports, and logged agent trajectories, and evaluate its reproducibility using independent reasoning agents as judges. Across four frontier models, the strongest judge reaches Cohen's $κ=0.76$ against human category labels, suggesting that the categories capture shared structure rather than annotator-specific preferences.

</details>


### [69] [Best Friends, Not Forever: Evaluating Long-Horizon Persona Collapse and Behavioral Drift in AI Companions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28818)
*Pranav Narayanan Venkit,Akshara Prabhakar,Yu Li,Daniel Lee,Chien-Sheng Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current AI companions fail to maintain stable personas and recall interaction history over extended conversations. An audit framework called ANCHOR, testing 2,008 conversations across 27 personas and four models, reveals that no model reliably preserves persona consistency (trajectory accuracy ~44.4%) or user-state recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI companions engage in repeated, long-horizon social interactions, two understudied failure modes emerge: persona collapse (loss of role, boundaries, values, or style) and behavioral drift (gradual erosion of those properties). The authors argue that locally acceptable replies do not guarantee longitudinal stability, and existing evaluation paradigms collapse distinct dimensions into overly simplistic scores, obscuring these failures.

Method: The authors introduce ANCHOR, a controlled synthetic audit framework with two measurement probes: (1) the Identity Probe, combining a sealed 102-item questionnaire with turn-level behavioral judgments to assess persona enactment; and (2) the Trajectory Probe, scoring 110 calibrated counterfactual questions drawn from 35 conversation banks to measure trajectory recall. The study covers 2,008 conversations spanning 27 personas, nine interaction schedules, three generated memory configurations, and four evaluated models.

Result: No model or configuration reliably preserves either persona enactment or trajectory recall. Trajectory accuracy averages only 44.4%; user-state recall remains near four-option random chance. No tested context condition or memory mechanism consistently resolves these failures. Questionnaire retention varies by model and persona facet, disagrees with turn-level behavioral judgments, and is sensitive to evaluator choice.

Conclusion: Current systems do not yet reliably support long-horizon companion continuity. Auditing frameworks must disentangle persona enactment, trajectory recall, evaluator provenance, and deployment context rather than collapsing them into a single trust or stability score.

Abstract: As AI companions increasingly mediate repeated social interaction, users may rely on a stable role and shared history, yet locally acceptable replies do not ensure that either persists. We study two observable long-horizon failures: 'persona collapse', the loss of a deployed role, boundaries, values, or style, and 'behavioral drift', the gradual or recurrent erosion of those properties. We introduce ANCHOR, a controlled synthetic audit that separately measures persona enactment and trajectory recall. The study contains 2,008 conversations spanning 27 personas, nine interaction schedules, three generated memory settings, and four evaluated models. The Identity Probe combines a sealed 102-item questionnaire with turn-level judgments, while the Trajectory Probe scores 110 calibrated counterfactual questions from 35 conversation banks. Our results show that no evaluated model and configuration reliably preserves either dimensions: trajectory accuracy averages only 44.4%, user-state recall remains near four-option chance, and no tested context condition or memory consistently resolves these failures. Questionnaire retention also varies by model and persona facet, disagrees with turn-level behavior, and is sensitive to evaluator choice. These results indicate that current systems do not yet reliably support long-horizon companion continuity and that audits must distinguish persona enactment, trajectory recall, evaluator provenance, and deployment context rather than collapse them into a single trust or stability score.

</details>


### [70] [Fragility of Value under Imperfect Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28881)
*Winter Cross*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes a model of AI alignment training where an agent's value function satisfies a proxy condition before deployment. It identifies conditions under which deployment of an η-catastrophic agent — one that drives expected human value below a threshold η under optimization — becomes possible, highlighting the dangers of overoptimization and advocating for designs like quantilizers that limit optimization pressure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the "fragility of human value" concern in AI safety: that optimizing too aggressively for an imperfect proxy of human values can lead to catastrophic outcomes. It aims to formally characterize when alignment training with proxy conditions can still result in catastrophically misaligned agents.

Method: The authors construct a theoretical model of idealized alignment training where an agent's value function is constrained to satisfy a proxy condition before it optimizes the world. They then derive conditions on the human value function and proxy accuracy under which an η-catastrophic value function — one that pushes expected human value below η in the optimization limit — would be deployed.

Result: The primary results identify specific conditions under which deployment of η-catastrophic agents occurs despite proxy-satisfying training, demonstrating that even rigorous pre-deployment alignment checks can fail under sufficient optimization pressure when proxies are imperfect.

Conclusion: Overoptimization poses a fundamental risk that cannot be eliminated solely by pre-deployment training with proxy conditions. The authors recommend AI architectures that inherently limit optimization pressure, such as quantilizers, as a complementary safety strategy.

Abstract: As more responsibility is placed upon AI systems, it becomes increasingly important to guarantee that these systems are aligned with humanity. A common fear in AI safety is that human value is fragile -- that is, optimizing too heavily for an imperfect proxy to human values will lead to a catastrophic outcome. In this paper, we present a model of the alignment problem where an agent undergoes idealized alignment training that guarantees its value function satisfies a proxy condition before optimizing the world. Our primary results identify conditions on the human value function and the accuracy of several proxy conditions under which an agent with an $η$-catastrophic value function, one that is guaranteed to take the expectation of human value below $η$ in the limit of optimizing power, would be deployed. Our results highlight the danger of overoptimization and motivate AI designs that limit optimization pressure, such as quantilizers, rather than relying solely on pre-deployment training.

</details>


### [71] [Identifying Informative Environments for Cognition Parameter Inference via Bayesian Experimental Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28894)
*Manisha Dubey,Rimvydas Rubavicius,N. Siddharth,Subramanian Ramamoorthy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formulates cognitive experiment design as a Bayesian Experimental Design (BED) problem, treating the experimental environment as the design variable. It introduces exact Monte Carlo BED and amortized BED frameworks, demonstrating that amortized BED matches exact rankings at lower cost, and shows no single environment is uniformly optimal across inference objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Bayesian inverse planning approaches treat experimental environments as fixed, leaving open the question of which cognitive experiments are most informative for inferring cognition parameters. The authors aim to provide a principled framework for optimizing experimental design to maximize information about latent cognitive mechanisms.

Method: The authors formulate cognitive planning experiment design as a Bayesian Experimental Design (BED) problem, with the environment as the design variable. They establish two frameworks: (1) an exact Monte Carlo BED benchmark for ground-truth design evaluation, and (2) an amortized BED framework that learns to perform efficient posterior inference and design evaluation. Both are tested on the Mouselab-MDP process-tracing paradigm.

Result: Amortized BED closely matches the environment rankings produced by exact Monte Carlo BED while substantially reducing computational cost. No single environment is uniformly optimal across all cognitive inference objectives; trade-offs exist between expected information gain, posterior recoverability, and information efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed BED framework provides a principled approach for designing informative cognitive experiments tailored to Bayesian parameter inference, enabling researchers to optimize experimental environments based on specific inference objectives rather than relying on fixed, potentially suboptimal designs.

Abstract: Computational cognitive modeling seeks to infer latent cognitive mechanisms underlying observed behavior. Bayesian inverse planning provides a principled framework for such inference, but its success depends critically on the experimental environment. Existing approaches typically treat environments as fixed, leaving open the question of which cognitive experiments are most informative for cognition parameter inference. We formulate the design of cognitive planning experiments as a Bayesian Experimental Design (BED) problem, treating the experimental environment as the design variable. We establish an exact Monte Carlo BED benchmark and introduce an amortized Bayesian experimental design framework for efficient posterior inference and design evaluation. Experiments on the Mouselab-MDP process-tracing paradigm show that amortized BED closely matches the environment rankings of exact Monte Carlo BED while substantially reducing computational cost. We further show that no single environment is uniformly optimal across cognitive inference objectives, revealing trade-offs between expected information gain, posterior recoverability, and information efficiency. These results provide a principled framework for designing informative cognitive experiments for Bayesian parameter inference.

</details>


### [72] [NeSyFS: A Neuro-symbolic Fast-Slow Thinking Framework for LLM Agent under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28942)
*Duo Xu,Faramarz Fekri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes NeSyFS, a neuro-symbolic fast-slow thinking framework for LLM agents under partial observability, using a knowledge graph for belief state representation, a reactive fast-thinking module, an uncertainty-aware slow-thinking planner based on twisted sequential Monte Carlo, and a reflection module to switch between them—achieving significant gains on ALFWorld, Webshop, and ScienceWorld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents operating under partial observability face three key challenges: (1) belief state inference from limited observations, (2) task objective misalignment, and (3) planning under uncertainty. Prior methods condition actions on full or summarized action-observation histories, but redundant/irrelevant information in these histories can mislead decision-making. A unified framework is needed to address all three challenges.

Method: NeSyFS employs a knowledge graph (KG) to represent the belief state as triplets, providing structured context to all modules. It integrates: (1) a fast-thinking module for reactive action generation; (2) a slow-thinking module that performs uncertainty-aware planning following a twisted sequential Monte Carlo (TSMC) algorithm; and (3) a reflection module that evaluates fast-thinking actions and triggers a switch to slow-thinking when reactive actions repeatedly fail, thereby mitigating task objective misalignment.

Result: Experiments on three representative benchmarks—ALFWorld, Webshop, and ScienceWorld—demonstrate significant advantages over previous methods.

Conclusion: The neuro-symbolic fast-slow thinking framework effectively unifies belief state inference, misalignment correction, and uncertainty-aware planning for LLM agents under partial observability, outperforming prior approaches across diverse embodied and web-based environments.

Abstract: Recently Large Language Models (LLMs) have been increasingly deployed as autonomous agents in applications such as self-reflection, retrieval-augmented generation, and scientific discovery. In these settings, agents must act based on limited observations rather than full environmental states, leading to partial observability. This introduces several key challenges: belief state inference, task objective misalignment, and planning under uncertainty. Prior approaches typically condition actions on full or summarized action-observation histories whose redundant and irrelevant information can mislead the decision making of LLM agent. Inspired by human cognition, we propose a novel neuro-symbolic fast-slow thinking (NeSyFS) framework for LLM agent, addressing the challenges introduced by partial observability in a unified approach. We use a knowledge graph (KG) to represent the belief state, providing triplets as context for every module of NeSyFS. The fast-thinking module performs reactive action, while slow-thinking conducts a new uncertainty-aware planning by following the high-level structure of twisted sequential Monte Carlo (TSMC) algorithm. To mitigate the misalignment of task objective, a reflection module is used to reflect fast-thinking actions, and also switches to the slow-thinking module whenever reactive actions repeatedly fail. Experiments on three representative benchmarks, i.e. ALFWorld, Webshop, and ScienceWorld, demonstrate significant advantages over previous methods.

</details>


### [73] [MerchantBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Long-Term Coherence in E-Commerce Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28956)
*Qiming Shi,Yulong Tao,Linbo Jin,Zhaolu Kang,Yibo Dou,Jiawen Zhu,Tianjun Pan,Shaokang Fu,Chengyu Wang,Siyue Li,Yaping Cheng,Di Weng,Chengfu Huo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MerchantBench is a 365-day e-commerce simulation benchmark for evaluating LLM agents' Long-Term Coherence—their ability to maintain purposeful, adaptive behavior over extended horizons with delayed feedback. The best LLM achieved only 27.3% of human participants' mean final net assets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent benchmarks focus on bounded, short-horizon tasks with immediate success criteria. Real-world deployment demands Long-Term Coherence: sustaining goal-directed behavior across long horizons while adapting to accumulated, delayed evidence. No current benchmark adequately measures this capacity in a setting where actions constrain future choices and incoherence yields measurable cumulative effects.

Method: The authors introduce MerchantBench, a 365-day order-level simulation grounded in 98,843 real e-commerce product records with 26 interaction tools. The environment spans four interdependent decision domains: Product Sourcing, Listing and Pricing Control, Cash-Flow Management, and Mixed-Latency Feedback Adaptation. It couples promptly observable Upstream Supplier Events with delayed Downstream Order Outcomes, forcing agents to track individual order lifecycles and revisit prior decisions. Eight LLMs were evaluated under two agent frameworks across 48 runs of 365 simulated days each, with human participants serving as a baseline.

Result: A substantial performance gap emerged between LLM agents and humans. The best-performing LLM configuration reached only 27.3% of the mean final net assets achieved by human participants, highlighting significant deficiencies in long-term planning, delayed-feedback integration, and coherent multi-step decision-making.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents lack the Long-Term Coherence required for sustained autonomous operation in environments with interdependent decisions, heterogeneous feedback delays, and cumulative consequences. MerchantBench provides a rigorous testbed for advancing this capability, and the large human-LLM gap underscores the need for fundamental progress in long-horizon agent reasoning.

Abstract: Large language model agents are increasingly evaluated as autonomous tool users, yet most benchmarks focus on bounded tasks with immediate success criteria. Real-world deployments often require Long-Term Coherence, the capacity to preserve purposeful behavior across extended horizons while adapting decisions to accumulated evidence. Evaluating this capacity requires a persistent environment in which actions constrain future choices, feedback arrives at heterogeneous delays, and incoherent behavior produces measurable cumulative effects. Seller-side e-commerce provides a suitable setting for this evaluation through recurrent and interdependent decisions over Product Sourcing, Listing and Pricing Control, Cash-Flow Management, and Mixed-Latency Feedback Adaptation. We introduce MerchantBench, a 365-day order-level simulation grounded in 98,843 real e-commerce product records and equipped with 26 tools for agent interaction. MerchantBench couples promptly observable Upstream Supplier Events with delayed Downstream Order Outcomes, requiring agents to follow individual order lifecycles and revisit earlier decisions. We evaluate eight LLMs under two agent frameworks in 48 runs, each spanning 365 simulated days. Our results reveal a substantial gap between even the latest LLMs and human participants, with the best LLM configuration attaining only 27.3\% of the mean final net assets achieved by human participants.

</details>


### [74] [Scaling Scientific Discovery Environments for Turn-Level Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28990)
*Yucheng Xu,Keyi Zhang,Yuyang Yu,Min Zhang,Shiyuan Meng,Pei Chu,Zhongying Tu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciDisco introduces a scalable framework for training scientific discovery agents using process-verifiable environments, combining verifier-filtered trajectory synthesis (DAG-grounded) with turn-level credit assignment (DiscoPO) to achieve SOTA on hypothesis-driven scientific data analysis benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon scientific analysis with LLM agents is constrained by the lack of process-supervised environments over real-world scientific data. Existing approaches lack mechanisms to verify intermediate analytical progress during multi-turn interactions.

Method: SciDisco consists of three components: (1) SciThèque — a compilation of hypotheses, datasets, hidden evidence graphs, and verifiers into task environments where analytical progress can be checked step-by-step; (2) DAG-grounded trajectory synthesis — constructs verifier-filtered multi-turn demonstrations using directed acyclic graph structures; (3) DiscoPO — a training algorithm that uses the environment as a training signal source, assigning turn-level credit to actions that produce verifiable analytical evidence.

Result: SciDisco-14B achieves state-of-the-art performance on hypothesis-driven scientific data analysis benchmarks.

Conclusion: Process-verifiable environments combined with turn-level credit assignment provide an effective training paradigm for scientific discovery agents, enabling scalable and verifiable long-horizon scientific reasoning over real-world data.

Abstract: Large language model agents have shown promising capabilities in data-driven scientific discovery tasks, where an agent interacts with an execution environment and produces a statistical claim. Long-horizon scientific analysis remains constrained by the lack of process supervised environments over real-world scientific data. This paper introduces SciDisco, a scalable framework for training Scientific Discovery agents in process-verifiable environments. SciThèque compiles hypotheses, datasets, hidden evidence graphs, and verifiers into task environments where analytical progress can be checked during interaction. DAG-grounded trajectory synthesis uses these environments to construct verifier-filtered multi-turn demonstrations. DiscoPO then uses the environment as the source of training signal, assigning turn-level credit to actions that produce verifiable analytical evidence. Experiments show that SciDisco-14B reaches state-of-the-art on hypothesis-driven scientific data analysis benchmarks.

</details>


### [75] [MMShopBench: A Real-Log Benchmark for Multimodal, Multi-Turn Shopping Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29002)
*Zeying Hao,Hao Guo,Mengtao Xu,Yimin Hu,Yuheng Song,Zesheng Zhou,Jinsong Lan,Xiaoyong Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MMShopBench is the first benchmark built from real shopping logs for evaluating multimodal, multi-turn AI shopping agents. It requires agents to infer purchase intent and product requirements from images and dialogue, retrieve candidates, and verify compliance. An offline sandbox and training set help narrow the gap between open-source and proprietary models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for AI shopping assistants rely on text-only or synthetic requests, failing to capture the complexity of real-world shopping where users express needs jointly through images and multi-turn dialogue. This creates a gap in evaluating agents' ability to handle realistic, multimodal shopping scenarios.

Method: The authors construct MMShopBench from carefully cleaned, manually annotated real shopping logs. Each request is annotated with ground-truth purchase intent and mandatory product requirements. They design an evidence-grounded multimodal evaluation protocol where agents must: (1) infer requirements from user images and multi-turn dialogue, (2) retrieve candidates via image/text search, and (3) verify candidates against all requirements using product images and structured attributes. They also build an offline shopping sandbox for reproducible experimentation and a companion training set for fine-tuning open-source models.

Result: Evaluation of representative open-source and proprietary models reveals a substantial performance gap. Fine-tuning an open-source model on the companion training set within the offline sandbox significantly narrows this gap, demonstrating the effectiveness of the training data.

Conclusion: MMShopBench provides a realistic, reproducible benchmark for multimodal multi-turn shopping agents. Real-log data with ground-truth annotations, combined with an offline sandbox, enables meaningful progress in this domain, and fine-tuning on curated training data proves effective at closing the gap with proprietary systems.

Abstract: Online shoppers increasingly turn to AI shopping assistants, using images and multi-turn dialogue to express and refine product needs that are difficult to articulate in text alone. However, existing benchmarks largely rely on text-only or synthetic requests, underrepresenting complex real-world shopping requirements jointly expressed through images and language. We introduce MMShopBench, the first real-log benchmark for multimodal, multi-turn shopping agents. Built from carefully cleaned and manually annotated shopping logs, MMShopBench provides ground-truth annotations of each request's purchase intent and mandatory product requirements. Agents must infer these requirements jointly from user images and multi-turn dialogue, retrieve candidate products through image and text search, and verify that each candidate satisfies all requirements using its product images and structured attributes. We evaluate representative open-source and proprietary models using an evidence-grounded multimodal protocol and construct a companion training set for fine-tuning an open-source model. To ensure reproducible experimentation, we build an offline shopping sandbox, where fine-tuning substantially narrows the performance gap between our open-source model and leading proprietary models, demonstrating the effectiveness of our training data.

</details>


### [76] [Evidence-Grounded Constraint Checking in Construction Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29058)
*Rashid Mushkani,Hugo Berard,Shin Koseki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A pipeline for constraint-checking professional documents using rules over extracted facts is evaluated on PDF evidence allocation strategies. A region-focused (tiled) approach improves accuracy over page-level retrieval in a repeated within-family test, but the advantage disappears in a broader cross-family evaluation, revealing a resolution-breadth trade-off rather than a universal benefit for region-based evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Professional document review involves constraint checking where decisions depend on relations across text, geometry, pages, and revisions. Current retrieval-augmented approaches lack systematic evaluation of how evidence granularity (page-level vs. region-level) affects decision accuracy, particularly under fixed evidence budgets.

Method: An evidence-grounded pipeline normalizes extracted facts, applies deterministic four-state rules, retains source spans, and escalates unresolved cases. A PDF evidence allocator is tested under two designs: (1) a repeated four-system test on 160 reference-based tasks from 29 construction projects, comparing four-image budgets of page overviews vs. one overview plus three overlapping tiles; (2) a disjoint two-system breadth extension to assess generalizability. Statistical inference uses project-cluster bootstrap CIs and exact p-values.

Result: In the repeated test, reallocating from four page overviews to one overview plus three tiles improved standardized decision accuracy by 10.6 percentage points (95% CI: 4.3–18.0; p=0.031). This effect did not persist in the broader block: Region-RAG changed accuracy by −4.1 points (95% CI: −10.2 to 1.9; p=0.209). Exact finding-set recovery remained low, false passes common, and repeated-run agreement poorly calibrated.

Conclusion: There is no universal advantage for region-focused evidence over page-level retrieval; instead, a resolution-breadth trade-off exists. This motivates rule-aware evidence routing and the retention of expert human review, rather than full automation.

Abstract: Professional-document review is a constraint-checking problem in which decisions depend on relations among text, geometry, pages, and document revisions. We present an evidence-grounded pipeline that normalizes extracted facts, executes four-state rules deterministically, retains source spans, and escalates unresolved cases. We evaluate its PDF evidence allocator on 160 reference-based tasks from 29 construction projects using a repeated four-system test and a disjoint two-system breadth extension. In the repeated test, reallocating a four-image budget from retrieved page overviews to one overview and three overlapping tiles improves project-family standardized decision accuracy by 10.6 percentage points (95% project-cluster bootstrap CI: 4.3 to 18.0; exact p = 0.031). This effect does not persist in the broader block: Region-RAG changes accuracy by -4.1 points (95% CI: -10.2 to 1.9; exact p = 0.209), while an equal-image sensitivity favors page breadth. Exact finding-set recovery remains low, false passes remain common, and repeated-run agreement is poorly calibrated. The results identify a resolution-breadth trade-off rather than a universal advantage for region-focused evidence, motivating rule-aware evidence routing and expert review.

</details>


### [77] [On the Generalization of Steering Vectors for Chain-of-Thought Faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29062)
*Matthew Nguyen,Kyle Cox,Austin Meek,Iván Arcuschin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether activation steering can improve the faithfulness of chain-of-thought reasoning—specifically, whether models can be made to verbalize cues that influence their answers. Steering generalizes well across cue types and datasets when effective, but only works reliably for the largest model tested (Gemma-3 12B), and it reduces hidden cue use rather than increasing overall cue reliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning is valuable for AI safety because it allows monitoring of a model's reasoning process. However, models sometimes fail to verbalize instrumental reasoning steps—for instance, ignoring a cue that influenced their answer—making the CoT unfaithful. The authors aim to extend prior work on activation steering for improving CoT faithfulness by systematically testing how well such steering generalizes.

Method: The authors test three models (Gemma-3 4B, Qwen-3.5 9B, Gemma-3 12B) in a cued question-answering setting. They apply activation steering using four different vector construction methods and evaluate faithfulness by measuring cue acknowledgment. Generalization is assessed through cross-cue and cross-dataset analyses, and they also test whether steering increases cue salience/use versus merely promoting verbalization of already-used cues.

Result: Steering reliably increases cue acknowledgment only for the largest model (Gemma-3 12B). When steering is effective, its effects generalize broadly across cue types and datasets—effect size depends primarily on the evaluation setting, not the training setting of the steering vector. All four vector construction methods yield similar effect sizes, even one that does not mention a specific cue. Steering does not increase overall cue use; rather, it reduces hidden cue use (unacknowledged cue reliance).

Conclusion: Activation steering can improve CoT faithfulness primarily by reducing hidden cue use rather than inflating cue reliance. The approach generalizes well across settings when it works, and the method of vector construction matters little. However, effectiveness is model-scale-dependent, working reliably only for the largest model tested.

Abstract: Model capabilities have improved in large part due to scaling chain of thought. This has been a promising development for AI safety--where models verbalize their reasoning, it is possible to monitor it. However, in some cases, models do not verbalize important steps in their reasoning process. For example, models prompted with a cue suggesting the incorrect answer may fail to acknowledge that cue, even when it appears instrumental to their conclusion. When chain of thought (CoT) fails to disclose instrumental reasoning steps, we describe it as unfaithful. Prior work has shown that activation steering can be a useful method to improve faithfulness in CoT. We extend this line of work by studying how well steering for faithfulness generalizes across cue types, datasets, and methods of constructing the steering vector for three models (Gemma-3 4B, Qwen-3.5 9B, Gemma-3 12B) in a cued question-answering setting. While steering reliably increases cue acknowledgment for only the largest model (Gemma-3 12B), we find that when steering is effective, its effect generalizes broadly across cue types and datasets--in cross-cue and cross-dataset analyses, effect size is determined primarily by the evaluation setting, rather than the vector's train setting. How the vector is built also matters little--four construction methods, including one whose optimization target mentions no specific cue, yield similar effect sizes. Finally, we consider the possibility that steering promotes the salience of the cue and causes greater cue use, rather than targeting verbalization behaviors. However, we find no evidence for this--steering leaves the rate of cue use roughly unchanged while reducing hidden cue use, i.e., cue use that is not acknowledged.

</details>


### [78] [A Generalized-Bayes Perspective on Counterfactual Explanations: Posterior-Based Decision-Making and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29077)
*Keita Kinjo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Counterfactual explanations are reformulated as posterior inference in a generalized Bayes framework, where distance-minimization corresponds to MAP estimation. The authors propose alternative decision rules (Bayes, CVaR), multi-model mixing via Bayesian model weights, and novel evaluation metrics, validated on simulated and Google Trends data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The predominant distance-minimization formulation of counterfactual explanations lacks a rigorous theoretical foundation. The authors aim to provide this by connecting CEs to generalized Bayesian inference, and to expand the framework to support risk-aware decision rules and model multiplicity.

Method: The authors show that distance-minimization CE equals the MAP estimate of a Gibbs posterior with a distance-based prior (DP-GBCE). They then propose: (1) a Bayes decision rule minimizing expected decision loss, (2) a risk-averse CVaR-CE rule, and (3) Bayesian model averaging to mix posteriors across multiple models. Novel metrics evaluate individual CEs and the full posterior.

Result: Experiments on simulated data and Google Trends data demonstrate trade-offs among the decision rules, showing that the posterior perspective enables flexible, risk-aware counterfactual generation and principled handling of model multiplicity.

Conclusion: The generalized Bayes framework provides a unified theoretical foundation for counterfactual explanations, extending beyond standard MAP/distance-minimization to support diverse decision rules and model averaging, with practical benefits demonstrated empirically.

Abstract: Counterfactual explanations (CEs) enhance the interpretability of machine learning models by identifying the smallest change to an input required to obtain a desired output. Although CEs are conventionally formulated as a distance-minimization problem, the theoretical basis of this formulation has received limited attention. We show that a distance-minimization-based CE is mathematically equivalent to the maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimate of a Gibbs posterior within the generalized Bayes framework, specifically when a distance-based prior is used. We call this formulation the Distance-Prior Generalized Bayes CE (DP-GBCE). Building on this posterior perspective, we introduce two decision rules beyond MAP within a unified framework: a Bayes decision that minimizes expected decision loss and CVaR-CE, a risk-averse decision rule. We also propose an extension that uses Bayesian model weights to mix the posterior distributions of multiple models, thereby accounting for model multiplicity, where several models have comparable predictive performance. Finally, we define metrics for evaluating both individual CEs and the posterior distribution as a whole, and use experiments on simulated data and Google Trends data to quantify the trade-offs among the decision rules.

</details>


### [79] [Harnessing the Wisdom of LLM Crowds through Complementarity-Driven Iterative Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29087)
*Yanbin Fang,Xuan Wei,Wei Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WILC proposes a sequential, relay-style LLM coordination framework where successor models are dynamically selected based on complementarity to address bottlenecks in prior outputs, outperforming static ensembling and single-model refinement while matching GPT-5.2 performance at ~7× lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Individual LLMs have heterogeneous capability boundaries that limit enterprise deployment. Existing multi-LLM coordination approaches use fixed combination strategies, failing to exploit dynamic, state-dependent complementarity. The authors see an opportunity to unlock collective intelligence by strategically sequencing models based on the specific bottlenecks in evolving solutions, extending the wisdom-of-crowds paradigm beyond static aggregation.

Method: WILC operates on two design principles: (1) iterative reflection-and-refinement, creating a state-preserving workflow where models diagnose and refine predecessor outputs; and (2) complementarity-driven model selection via a dual-gate mechanism — Prospective Complementarity Fit (PCF) selects the most suitable worker for the current bottleneck, while Posterior Complementarity Gain (PCG) evaluates whether the transition improved the solution. Experiments span four diverse benchmarks comparing against self-refinement, ensemble, and query-routing baselines.

Result: WILC outperforms all baselines — single-model self-refinement, ensemble methods, and query-routing methods — across four diverse benchmarks. Under standardized pricing, WILC matches GPT-5.2's average benchmark performance at approximately 7× lower estimated per-query cost, while enabling data sovereignty via self-hosted deployment.

Conclusion: The study extends wisdom-of-crowds theory from static aggregation to sequential AI complementarity and provides transferable design principles for multi-AI coordination. The dual-gate mechanism offers a principled approach to dynamic model selection that captures both prospective fit and posterior gain, advancing the state of the art in collective LLM intelligence.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in enterprise settings, yet individual models remain bounded by model-specific capability limitations. These heterogeneous boundaries pose a deployment challenge, but also create an opportunity: strategically coordinating multiple LLMs may unlock collective intelligence exceeding any single model. Existing approaches fix how models are combined in advance, overlooking the dynamic, state-dependent role of complementarity in complex problem solving. Drawing on the wisdom-of-crowds paradigm, we reconceptualize collective LLM intelligence as relay-style complementarity: a sequential process in which each successor model is selected to address the specific bottleneck identified in its predecessor's output. To operationalize this, we propose WILC (Wisdom Integration of LLM Crowds), a framework grounded in two design principles. First, iterative reflection-and-refinement establishes a state-preserving workflow through which models diagnose and refine prior outputs. Second, complementarity-driven model selection governs transitions via a dual-gate mechanism: prospective complementarity fit (PCF) identifies the worker most suited to the current bottleneck, while posterior complementarity gain (PCG) evaluates whether the selected transition improves the evolving solution. Experiments across four diverse benchmarks show that WILC outperforms existing approaches, including single-model self-refinement, ensemble methods, and query-routing methods. Under standardized pricing assumptions, WILC matches the average benchmark performance of GPT-5.2 at roughly 7 times lower estimated per-query cost, while facilitating data sovereignty through self-hosted deployment. This study extends wisdom-of-crowds theory from static aggregation to sequential AI complementarity and provides transferable design principles for multi-AI coordination.

</details>


### [80] [CAGE: Certified Authorization under Typed-Return Uncertainty for Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29190)
*Blaise Delattre,Cong Wang,Yang Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAGE is a certification framework for LLM agent tool-use decisions that guarantees safety over a declared neighborhood of plausible binding errors—one categorical misbinding plus bounded numerical drift. It directly certifies the joint categorical–numerical space, proving that separate channel certification does not compose safely.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Runtime permission gates in tool-using LLM agents authorize actions based on tool returns, but these gates only check the observed return. They remain vulnerable to small errors in how the return was bound to its source (e.g., parsing errors, field misalignment). The authors ask whether a candidate action remains authorized over a neighborhood of plausibly misbound returns, capturing both categorical binding faults and numerical drift simultaneously.

Method: CAGE enumerates discrete categorical branches exactly and certifies continuous numerical perturbations within each branch. Two variants are proposed: CAGE-Exact, which certifies the policy directly when the policy function is executable and analyzable; and CAGE-Lip / CAGE-RS, which certify a learned gate under an explicit, measured fidelity assumption when the policy is not directly executable.

Result: Across synthetic, policy-as-code, regulatory, and real-transaction settings, CAGE eliminates in-budget false-allow decisions that accurate pointwise gates would admit, while preserving a useful fraction of autonomous decisions. The authors also prove formally that certifying categorical and numerical channels independently does not compose—perturbations safe on each channel alone can jointly render the same action unsafe.

Conclusion: Joint certification over categorical and numerical perturbation spaces is necessary and feasible. CAGE provides a practical mechanism to close the binding-error vulnerability in LLM agent tool-use, enabling safer autonomy without fully sacrificing decision automation.

Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents act on typed tool returns, records pairing provenance and categorical fields with numerical values. Runtime permission gates generally authorize the observed return and action, leaving the decision unprotected against small errors in how the return was bound to its source. We ask whether a candidate action stays authorized over a declared neighborhood of plausible correctly bound returns: one admissible binding fault plus bounded numerical drift. We prove that certifying the categorical and numerical channels separately does not compose: perturbations that are safe on each channel alone can jointly turn the same action unsafe. CAGE certifies this joint neighborhood directly, enumerating the discrete branches exactly and certifying the continuous perturbation within each branch. Across synthetic, policy-as-code, regulatory, and real-transaction settings, CAGE removes the in-budget false allows that accurate pointwise gates admit, while keeping a useful fraction of decisions autonomous. When the policy is executable, CAGE-Exact certifies the policy itself; otherwise CAGE-Lip and CAGE-RS certify a learned gate under an explicit, measured fidelity assumption.

</details>


### [81] [MirrorCraft: Paired Evaluation under Hidden Rule Changes in Minecraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29218)
*Jianxin Gao,Beini Hu,Runze Li,Wanli Peng,Ruohan Lei,Jinyuan Zhang,Linna Deng,Tianyi Yu,Zining Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MirrorCraft introduces a paired Minecraft benchmark where hidden server-side rule changes are applied to "Mirror" worlds, matched with vanilla counterparts. It evaluates LLM-based agents' adaptability to altered game mechanics using the Rule Intervention Effect (RIE) metric, finding that rule changes produce strongly divergent effects across configurations, with ReAct achieving the best Mirror-world performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Minecraft benchmarks for LLM agents evaluate performance under fixed, familiar game mechanics. High scores in such settings do not demonstrate an agent's ability to adapt when recipes, loot drops, or other rules change—a critical capability for robust, general-purpose agents operating in dynamic or unfamiliar environments.

Method: The authors introduce MirrorCraft, a paired benchmark where each "Mirror" world is an identical copy of a "Vanilla" world except for server-side rule modifications applied via datapacks (e.g., altered crafting recipes, drop tables). Terrain, spawn location, resource placement, objectives, interface, and action budgets are matched within each pair. The benchmark spans five biomes, six rule suites, three progression objectives, two model families, and six agent configurations under a shared Mineflayer interface. Performance is measured via deterministic advancement milestones and success rate, with the Rule Intervention Effect (RIE) quantifying the performance delta between matched Vanilla and Mirror worlds.

Result: Hidden rule changes exert strongly divergent effects across different rule suites. Among all configurations evaluated without access to rule descriptions, ReAct achieves the highest pooled Mirror-world score. Providing agents with the exact rules yields modest gains in average progress and completion rates across all three progression objectives.

Conclusion: MirrorCraft extends Minecraft agent evaluation beyond static game mechanics, offering a controlled framework for studying how agents leverage gameplay outcomes when world rules diverge from familiar priors—a step toward more adaptive and general-purpose embodied agents.

Abstract: With the prosperity of the large language models (LLMs), it has become an interesting topic: how do LLM-based agents work in Minecraft? Unfortunately, most existing benchmarks evaluate them under fixed game mechanics. High performance in these settings does not show whether an agent can continue making progress when familiar recipes, drops, and other rules change. In this paper, we introduce MirrorCraft, a paired benchmark for evaluating agents under hidden rule changes in Minecraft. Each Mirror world is a copy of its paired Vanilla world, with selected server-side rules modified by the corresponding datapack. Terrain, spawn, resource placement, objective, interface, and action budget remain matched within every Vanilla-Mirror pair. MirrorCraft includes five controlled biomes, six rule suites, three progression objectives, two model families, and six agent configurations under a shared Mineflayer interface. We evaluate task progress with deterministic advancement milestones and success rate and use the Rule Intervention Effect (RIE) to measure the performance change between matched Vanilla and Mirror worlds. The experiments show that hidden rule changes have strongly different effects across suites. Among the configurations evaluated without rule descriptions, ReAct achieves the highest pooled Mirror score. Providing the exact rules yields modest gains in average progress and completion across all three objectives. MirrorCraft extends Minecraft evaluation beyond fixed mechanics and provides a controlled setting for studying how agents use gameplay outcomes when the rules of the current world differ from familiar ones.

</details>


### [82] [Don't Mix Rewards, Mix Policies: Policy Decomposition and Optimization for Multi-Reward RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29246)
*Ruiming Liang,Yi Zhong,Yizhen Yuan,Yinan Zheng,Tianyi Tan,Tianyue Wang,Haiyun Guo,Jinqiao Wang,Xianyuan Zhan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRISM proposes a policy-space decomposition approach for multi-reward RL in LLMs, where separate positive policies and a global negative policy are optimized independently and composed at inference, outperforming existing baselines while enabling controllable preference trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-reward RL for LLMs suffers from alignment tax — different optimization objectives can conflict, causing unstable and inefficient post-training. Existing reward-composition methods exacerbate these conflicts.

Method: PRISM decomposes multi-reward optimization into standalone positive policies (one per reward) and one global negative policy, then composes them at inference via a flexible policy composition mechanism, avoiding reward-level conflicts during training.

Result: PRISM consistently outperforms existing multi-reward RL baselines on scientific reasoning, tool-use reasoning, and helpfulness-safety alignment benchmarks, while providing extra controllability for inference-time preference control.

Conclusion: Policy-space decomposition is an effective alternative to reward-space composition for multi-objective RL in LLMs, mitigating the alignment tax and enabling controllable inference.

Abstract: Modern large language models (LLMs) are expected not just to answer correctly, but to adapt their behavior to different human values and use cases. As a result, multi-reward reinforcement learning (RL) has become an increasingly important problem for LLMs, where each reward captures a different aspect of desired behavior. However, optimizing with multiple rewards suffers from a more severe alignment tax issue, where different optimization objectives can trade off or even conflict with each other, leading to unstable and inefficient post-training. In this work, we propose PRISM, a new multi-reward RL framework built upon the idea of policy-space decomposition and composition. Instead of compositing different rewards, PRISM optimizes a set of standalone positive policies and a global negative policy. This alleviates the potential conflict during multi-reward policy optimization, while enabling controllability during inference by flexible policy composition. Experiments on scientific reasoning, tool-use reasoning, and helpfulness-safety alignment show that PRISM consistently outperforms existing multi-reward RL baselines, with extra controllability for inference-time preference control.

</details>


### [83] [Tool Specifications Matter: Uncovering and Mitigating Safety Risks in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29254)
*Minghui Pan,Jiayuxuan Yang,Yuanyuan Yuan,Yu Jiang,Zhenpeng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Safety degradation in LLM agents is largely caused by schema-formatted tool specifications weakening internal refusal signals. SafeKeep, an inference-time safeguard, decouples safety checks (using flattened textual specs) from tool execution (using original schema specs), significantly improving refusal rates and reducing attack success while maintaining task capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs become substantially less safe when deployed as agents with external tools, but the root cause of this safety degradation is poorly understood. The paper aims to identify the source of this degradation and develop an effective mitigation.

Method: First, the authors use white-box representation analysis to identify that schema-formatted tool specifications (e.g., JSON schemas, function-calling formats) weaken the model's internal refusal signals, leading to unsafe tool execution. Building on this finding, they propose SafeKeep, an inference-time safeguard that decouples safety judgment from tool execution: safety assessment uses flattened textual tool specifications, while execution retains the original schema-formatted specifications. This approach is evaluated across two benchmarks and four LLMs (both white-box and black-box).

Result: SafeKeep increases the average refusal rate for harmful requests from 23.8% to 70.6% and reduces the average attack success rate under observation-level prompt injection from 25.6% to 2.5%. It outperforms existing safeguards and preserves task-handling capability.

Conclusion: Schema-formatted tool specifications are a primary source of agent safety degradation. SafeKeep effectively mitigates this by decoupling safety judgment from tool execution, offering a practical inference-time safeguard that works across both white-box and black-box models.

Abstract: AI agents extend large language models (LLMs) with external tools, enabling them to perform complex tasks and translate model outputs into consequential real-world actions. Yet LLMs often become substantially less safe when deployed as agents, and the source of this degradation remains poorly understood. In this paper, we identify schema-formatted tool specifications as a primary source of agent safety degradation and show, through white-box representation analysis, that they weaken the model's internal refusal signals and contribute to unsafe tool execution. Building on this finding, we propose SafeKeep, an inference-time safeguard that decouples safety judgment from tool execution: it assesses requests using flattened textual tool specifications while retaining the original schema-formatted specifications for execution. Across two representative benchmarks and four LLMs, including both white-box and black-box models, SafeKeep increases the average refusal rate for harmful requests from 23.8% to 70.6% and reduces the average attack success rate under observation-level prompt injection from 25.6% to 2.5%. It also outperforms existing safeguards and preserves task-handling capability. We release the code and data at https://github.com/snowcatsmoking/SafeKeep .

</details>


### [84] [MAGA: Multi-Platform Self-Fusion of GUI Agents via Structured Action Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29320)
*Hang Yan,Zhangxuan GU,Beitong Zhou,Jiaxuan Chen,Runze Li,Yusong Hu,Shuheng Shen,Changhua Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAGA introduces a cross-environment GUI agent training method that re-allocates distillation signals based on structured action correctness, suppressing noise and focusing on erroneous actions, outperforming weight merging and standard on-policy distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based GUI agents are domain-specific (mobile, web, desktop). Consolidating them into a single cross-environment policy is desirable, but weight merging causes action corruption under expert disagreement, and on-policy distillation (OPD) treats all tokens equally, ignoring that action tokens are the sole interface between agent and environment.

Method: MAGA (structured action-guided distillation) re-allocates training signal based on whether generated actions are correct: it suppresses unnecessary/invalid distillation signals and focuses learning on erroneous actions. Additionally, a training-only hint mechanism optimizes teacher supervision signals without altering the student's input at inference time.

Result: Across two model scales, MAGA achieves the highest mean success rate among compared methods, outperforming the strongest baseline by 2.0% at the 8B scale, and achieves nearly the same average performance as the domain-specific teacher models.

Conclusion: Structured action-guided distillation with signal re-allocation and training-only hints effectively consolidates specialized GUI agents into a single cross-environment policy, matching teacher-level performance without the conflicts introduced by weight merging or the token-uniformity limitation of standard on-policy distillation.

Abstract: Graphical user interface (GUI) agents based on large language models are increasingly deployed across mobile, web, and desktop environments. However, existing agents are typically domain-specific, limiting the deployment and user experience. This motivates the consolidation of specialized models into a single cross-environment policy. Weight merging directly merges domain-specific experts but can corrupt executable actions under expert disagreement, while on-policy distillation (OPD) avoids conflicting teacher supervision yet still treats all response tokens equally during distillation, ignoring that action tokens are the only interface between the environment and the agent. To address this, We introduce MAGA that re-allocates training signal according to the structured action. Based on the correctness of the generated action, it suppresses unnecessary or invalid distillation signals and focuses learning on erroneous actions. Besides, a training-only hint optimizes the supervision signal provided by domain-specific teachers without changing the student input. Across two model scales, MAGA achieves the highest mean success rate, outperforming the strongest baseline by 2.0% at 8B and achieves almost the same average performance with teachers.

</details>


### [85] [ModelEquivBench: Certifying Multi-Relational Evaluation of LLM-Generated Optimization Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29431)
*Penglin Zhu,Jungang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ModelEquivBench is a multi-relational evaluation system for LLM-generated optimization models that produces fine-grained semantic equivalence profiles (E0–E6) with independently verifiable evidence, replacing coarse binary verdicts. Evaluation of three frontier models on 173 problems reveals distinct failure patterns invisible to traditional accuracy metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation methods for LLM-generated optimization models reduce comparison to simplistic binary labels (equivalent/not-equivalent) or execution-success rates. These coarse metrics are neither independently checkable nor faithful to the multiple distinct senses in which two optimization formulations can agree or diverge (e.g., feasible-set equivalence vs. optimal-value equality vs. optimizer-set equivalence). A richer, certifying evaluation framework is needed.

Method: ModelEquivBench defines a six-level semantic equivalence profile E0–E6: (E0) model construction and exact ingestion, (E1) verified representation alignment, (E2) same-space feasible-set relations, (E3) projected feasible-set relations, (E4) objective-order equivalence, (E5) optimal-value equality, and (E6) optimizer-set equivalence. Each decided entry carries independently re-checkable evidence: replayable traces or explicit maps for E0–E1, exact-rational certificates for positive E2–E6 conclusions, and explicit witnesses for negatives. Incomplete mappings, unsupported structures, and resource limits produce typed UNKNOWN or N/A outcomes; unmet prerequisites yield ABSENT. The system was applied to evaluate GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Qwen3.5-397B-A17B on 173 frozen benchmark problems (346 cells per model) under a no-repair protocol.

Result: The multi-relational profiles exposed distinctions invisible to coarse baselines: 49, 35, and 25 cells (respectively) contained executable candidates certified negative on at least one relation; 25, 8, and 18 structural rejections occurred on pairs where E2 certified mapped feasible-set equality under a verified map. The three models failed at different stages of the profile, demonstrating that they cannot be meaningfully reduced to a single accuracy score.

Conclusion: Coarse binary equivalence metrics obscure critical distinctions in how well LLMs generate optimization models. The layered E0–E6 certification profile with verifiable evidence provides a more faithful and actionable evaluation, revealing that frontier models exhibit qualitatively different failure patterns across semantic equivalence dimensions. A single accuracy score is insufficient for meaningful model comparison in this domain.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly generate optimization models from natural language, but existing evaluation often reduces a generated model and its ground truth to a single equivalent/not-equivalent verdict or an execution-success rate--labels that are neither independently checkable nor faithful to the multiple distinct senses in which two formulations can agree. We present ModelEquivBench, a certifying, multi-relational evaluation system that reports a per-pair semantic profile E0--E6: model construction and exact ingestion (E0), verified representation alignment (E1), same-space and projected feasible-set relations (E2, E3), objective-order equivalence (E4), optimal-value equality (E5), and optimizer-set equivalence (E6). Each decided entry carries relation-appropriate, independently re-checkable evidence: replayable traces or explicit maps for E0--E1, exact-rational certificates for positive E2--E6 conclusions, and explicit witnesses for supported negatives. Incomplete mapping search, unsupported structure, and resource limits produce typed UNKNOWN or N/A outcomes rather than guesses, while unmet prerequisites are reported as ABSENT. Using ModelEquivBench to evaluate three model snapshots--GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Qwen3.5-397B-A17B--on the same frozen cohort of 173 base problems (346 cells per model) under a no-repair protocol, the resulting profiles expose distinctions that coarse baselines do not represent: 49, 35, and 25 cells contain executable candidates that are nevertheless certified negative on at least one supported relation, and 25, 8, and 18 structural rejections occur on pairs for which E2 certifies mapped feasible-set equality under a verified map. The three model snapshots fail at different stages of the profile and therefore cannot be meaningfully reduced to a single accuracy score.

</details>


### [86] [Beyond Retrieval: Analytic Memory for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29440)
*Zhoujin Tian,Yao Tian,Hao Zhang,Cheng Chen,Yakun Li,Lei Zhang,Xiaofang Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdaMM introduces "analytic memory" — organizing multimodal observations into queryable structures for filtering, aggregation, ranking, and temporal comparison — complementing traditional retrieval memory. The framework extracts provenance-linked attribute-value pairs, discovers recurring schemas, and uses a memory-aware planner to route queries to appropriate tools, achieving up to 11.3% improvement on long-term multimodal memory benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-term multimodal memory systems focus on retrieval memory (summaries, indexes for returning relevant information), but neglect the need to compute over accumulated observations — operations like filtering, aggregation, ranking, and temporal comparison. There is a gap in supporting what the authors term "analytic memory."

Method: AdaMM jointly supports retrieval and analytic memory. It extracts provenance-linked attribute-value observations from dialogue, images, and contextual metadata; discovers recurring field structures; and materializes them for analytical access. At inference, a memory-aware planner decomposes queries into retrieval and analytic sub-operations and routes each to the appropriate tools, without relying on application-defined schemas.

Result: On MemEye and MemGallery benchmarks, AdaMM improves performance by up to 11.3% and 7.3%, respectively, compared to existing long-term multimodal memory systems.

Conclusion: Analytic memory is a necessary and complementary abstraction to retrieval memory for long-term multimodal systems. AdaMM demonstrates that automatic schema discovery and a planner-based query decomposition approach can effectively bridge retrieval and analytical operations over accumulated multimodal interactions.

Abstract: Long-term multimodal memory must support not only retrieving relevant information but also computing over observations accumulated across interactions. Existing systems largely emphasize \emph{retrieval memory}, organizing interaction histories through summaries and indexes to return query-relevant information at multiple granularities, from high-level abstractions to underlying records. In this paper, we formulate \emph{analytic memory} as a complementary abstraction that organizes recurring multimodal observations into queryable structures supporting filtering, aggregation, ranking, and temporal comparison. We present AdaMM, a framework that jointly supports retrieval and analytic memory. Rather than relying on application-defined schemas, AdaMM extracts provenance-linked attribute-value observations from dialogue, images, and contextual metadata, discovers recurring field structures, and materializes them for analytical access. At inference time, a memory-aware planner decomposes queries into retrieval and analytic operations and routes each operation to the appropriate tools. Experiments on two long-term multimodal memory benchmarks, MemEye and MemGallery, show that AdaMM improves performance by up to 11.3\% and 7.3\%, respectively.

</details>


### [87] [Self-Play Meets Skill Evolution: Self-Evolving Search Agents that Pose, Solve, and Remember](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29468)
*Zenghuang Fu,Zhaoyang Li,Qiuyuan Ai,Haoyu Wu,Minghui Wu,Chenxu Zhao,Ante Wang,Guannan He,Changwei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SESA introduces a self-play framework where a challenger agent generates problems and a solver agent retrieves skills from an evolving external memory; failures are distilled into reusable skills and written back, creating a co-evolutionary loop between task generation and skill memory that improves both policy learning and inference-time retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-play agents lack persistent state across training — failures only affect gradients but do not explicitly shape future practice. External skill memories exist but are typically learned from fixed task distributions, not from an evolving curriculum. The authors aim to unify these by making procedural memory an evolving state that co-evolves with task generation.

Method: SESA uses a bidirectional self-play loop: (1) a challenger poses problems; (2) a separately parameterized solver retrieves skills from an external memory bank; (3) informative failures are distilled into reusable skills and written back to memory; (4) updated memory changes solver behavior/success, which shifts the challenger's reward and future problem distribution; (5) new failures from this frontier rewrite memory again. Retrieved skills shape on-policy training trajectories, allowing benefits to enter model parameters (enabling memory-free deployment) or remain in the external bank for optional inference-time retrieval.

Result: Across 7 open-domain and multi-hop QA benchmarks, SESA improves average accuracy over SSP by 1.2–3.2 points across multiple backbones. It surpasses the skill-augmented SkillRL baseline by 0.9 points. On Qwen3, SESA-Off (memory-free) retains 1.8–2.2 points of improvement over SSP, and the final skill bank adds a further 0.5–1.0 points at inference time.

Conclusion: Evolving skill memory is not merely an inference-time plug-in; it fundamentally changes policy learning and the future training distribution while retaining value as optional external memory. The co-evolution of task generation and skill memory yields both better trained models and reusable skill banks.

Abstract: Self-play agents can generate training problems without questions from target benchmarks, but their curricula lack persistent state: failures affect gradients yet do not explicitly shape future practice. External skill memories preserve procedural experience but are typically learned from fixed task distributions. We introduce \textbf{SESA} (Self-Evolving Skill-Augmented Agent), which makes procedural memory an evolving state of tool-augmented search self-play. A challenger poses problems, while a separately parameterized solver alone retrieves skills. Informative failures are distilled into reusable skills and written back to memory. The updated memory changes solver behavior and success, which changes the challenger's reward and the distribution of future problems; the resulting frontier produces new failures that rewrite memory. This bidirectional loop makes task generation and skill memory co-evolve. Because retrieved skills shape on-policy training trajectories, their benefits can enter the model parameters as well as remain in the external bank, enabling memory-free deployment and optional inference-time retrieval. Across seven open-domain and multi-hop question-answering benchmarks, SESA improves average accuracy over SSP by 1.2--3.2 points across multiple backbones and surpasses the skill-augmented SkillRL baseline by 0.9 points under a unified evaluation protocol. On Qwen3 models, SESA-Off retains 1.8--2.2 points of improvement over SSP, while the final skill bank adds a further 0.5--1.0 points. These results show that evolving skill memory is not merely an inference-time plug-in: it changes policy learning and the future training distribution while retaining value as optional external memory. Our code is available at https://github.com/Zenghuang-Fu/SESA-Self-Evolving-Search-Agents.

</details>


### [88] [AMTFV: Agentic Mathematical Tool-Flow Verification for LLM Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29549)
*Rui Zou,Yutao Zhu,Mengqi Wei,Ji-Rong Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AMTFV proposes a decoupled verification framework where a verification agent constructs workflows and delegates exact computation to a mathematical toolbox agent via a Mathematical Tool Flow (MTF) interface, improving answer verification accuracy by up to 8.3 percentage points over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing verification methods for LLM-generated mathematical answers either rely on natural-language reflection (imprecise for exact computation) or directly generate verification programs (coupling modeling with low-level implementation too early). There is a need for a decoupled approach that separates verification modeling from concrete execution while supporting exact computation.

Method: AMTFV introduces Mathematical Tool Flow (MTF) as an interrupt–execute–resume interface. A verification agent constructs a verification workflow, encodes mathematical objects and computational intent into MTF requests, and sends them to a toolbox agent. The toolbox agent parses requests, generates executable calls, dispatches them for exact computation, and returns results to support answer adjudication, revision, and workflow revision.

Result: Evaluated on five challenging math reasoning datasets across seven model configurations (DeepSeek, GPT, Gemini), AMTFV outperformed all representative baselines overall, achieving up to 8.3 percentage points improvement in average accuracy over the strongest baseline, with particularly large gains on medium- and high-complexity verification samples.

Conclusion: Decoupling verification modeling from execution via an agentic tool-flow framework enables more reliable and exact mathematical answer verification for LLMs, yielding significant accuracy gains, especially for complex problems.

Abstract: Large language models have demonstrated strong mathematical problem-solving capabilities, yet reliably verifying their candidate answers remains challenging. Existing representative methods mainly revise outputs through natural-language reflection or assist verification by directly generating verification programs; the former may not reliably support exact computation, whereas the latter prematurely couples mathematical modeling with low-level implementation. We propose AMTFV (Agentic Mathematical Tool-Flow Verification). By introducing Mathematical Tool Flow (MTF) as an interrupt--execute--resume interface, AMTFV decouples verification modeling from concrete execution and supports exact computation through a mathematical toolbox. Specifically, the verification agent first constructs a verification workflow, encodes the mathematical objects and computational intent requiring reliable execution in an MTF request, and sends it to the mathematical toolbox agent. The latter parses the request, generates executable calls, and dispatches them to the backend for exact computation. Tool outputs then support candidate-answer adjudication, answer revision, and verification-workflow revision. We evaluate AMTFV on five challenging mathematical reasoning datasets with seven model configurations from DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini. Experimental results show that AMTFV outperforms the representative baselines evaluated in this study overall; under an individual model configuration, it improves average accuracy over the strongest baseline by up to 8.3 percentage points, with larger gains on samples of medium and high verification complexity.

</details>


### [89] [COntExt: Towards Context-Aware Ontology Extension from Operational Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29553)
*Hussain Hussain,Stefan Schöberl,Angelika Schneider,Verena Geist*

Main category: cs.AI
