TL;DR: COntExt is a framework that automatically extends ontologies using structured operational metric definitions, framing the problem as three sub-tasks (parent class prediction, relation type prediction, data property assignment) and showing that metric-derived context improves suggestions over ontology-only baselines, particularly for relations and data properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operational metric definitions encode rich domain knowledge beyond what formal ontologies capture, but linking metric catalogues to ontologies remains manual, ad-hoc, and costly. There is an underexploited opportunity to automate ontology extension using these structured metric definitions.

Method: The COntExt framework decomposes context-aware ontology extension into three sub-tasks: (1) parent class prediction, (2) relation type prediction, and (3) data property assignment. It takes structured metric definitions as input, extracts referenced concepts/properties along with their metric context, and evaluates multiple algorithms per sub-task across four cybersecurity ontologies against ontology-context baselines.

Result: Metric-derived context significantly improves performance over ontology-context baselines for relation type prediction and data property assignment. The framework demonstrates that operational metric catalogues are a practical, previously underexploited source for ontology extension, reducing manual engineering costs.

Conclusion: Operational metric catalogues represent a valuable and underexploited resource for semi-automating ontology maintenance. COntExt shows that leveraging the context in which metrics reference domain concepts yields measurable improvements in extension accuracy, enabling organizations to maintain ontologies at substantially lower cost than purely manual approaches.

Abstract: Organizations increasingly define operational metrics in structured, machine-readable formats to monitor systems, processes, and compliance. These metric definitions implicitly encode domain knowledge, such as referencing concepts, properties, and relationships, that often extends what is captured in formal ontologies. Yet the connection between operational metric catalogues and ontological knowledge remains manual, ad-hoc, and labor-intensive. We present COntExt, a framework for context-aware ontology extension that takes structured metric definitions as input and suggests how referenced concepts and properties should be integrated into an existing ontology, utilizing the context of these metrics. The framework defines the extension problem as three sub-tasks: parent class prediction, relation type prediction, and data property assignment. Across four cybersecurity ontologies, we evaluate different algorithms for each task. Our results show that metric-derived context improves the suggestions over ontology-context baselines for relation type prediction and data property assignment. Our work demonstrates that operational metric catalogues are a practical and underexploited source for ontology extension. This work enables organizations to maintain their ontologies at a significantly lower cost than manual engineering.

</details>


### [90] [LEMUR: Learning to Align with Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning from Preference Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29559)
*Manith Adikari,Bei Peng,Samuele Vinanzi,Angelo Cangelosi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LEMUR is a framework that combines Multi-Objective RL with Preference-based RL, enabling agents to learn optimal multi-objective policies from human preference feedback without requiring pre-defined reward functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world tasks involve multiple competing objectives where ground-truth reward functions are hard to specify. MORL assumes well-specified per-objective rewards, while PbRL avoids predefined rewards via human feedback but has been limited to single-objective settings. Neither approach addresses multi-objective tasks without predefined rewards.

Method: LEMUR jointly learns multi-objective policies and multiple objective-specific reward models interactively from preferences provided by multiple humans. The agent uses this learned reward structure to balance competing objectives during training.

Result: Empirical evaluation on benchmark multi-objective tasks shows LEMUR achieves superior performance compared to existing baseline methods.

Conclusion: LEMUR presents a promising direction for solving multi-objective decision-making tasks without pre-defined reward functions, bridging the gap between MORL and PbRL.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) systems are typically trained using a single, well-specified scalar reward function. However, real-world decision-making tasks often involve multiple, competing objectives, such as performance versus efficiency, where ground-truth reward functions are difficult to specify or inaccessible. While Multi-Objective RL (MORL) addresses such trade-offs by modeling rewards as vectors, existing approaches typically assume access to a well-specified reward function for each objective, inheriting the same challenges faced by single-objective RL. Meanwhile, Preference-based RL (PbRL) has shown great potential in solving complex tasks without access to a pre-defined reward function through reward learning from human feedback, yet has largely been studied in single-objective settings. In this work, we bridge this gap with LEMUR: Learning to Align with Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning with Preference feedback, a novel framework where an agent interactively learns from the preferences of multiple humans to learn optimal multi-objective policies. Our approach jointly learns policies and multiple objective-specific reward models from human feedback, enabling agents to effectively balance competing objectives during learning. We evaluate LEMUR on a variety of benchmark multi-objective tasks, and empirical results demonstrate its superior performance over baseline methods. Our method presents a promising direction for solving multi-objective decision-making tasks without pre-defined reward functions.

</details>


### [91] [DungeonBench: A Benchmark for Rules-Rich Tactical Reasoning in Dungeons & Dragons Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29577)
*Ismayil Ismayilov,Atakan Kara,Kaan Oktay*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DungeonBench is a D&D combat benchmark for tactical reasoning that evaluates decision-making across single encounters and multi-encounter "days" requiring resource management. Frontier language models succeed at individual fights but struggle with long-horizon resource budgeting, rest timing, and rule-aware discipline across linked encounters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current game/simulator benchmarks under-test "rules-rich tactical reasoning" — the capacity to make optimal decisions when geometry, timing, resources, objectives, and rule interactions simultaneously constrain choices. Simplified combat simulators often abstract away mechanics that matter for realistic tactical depth.

Method: DungeonBench implements D&D 5e (2014 SRD) combat with two tracks: (1) Encounter track for single-fight local tactical evaluation, and (2) Day track linking multiple encounters with persistent hit points, spell slots, consumables, preparation, and short-rest timing. At each step, the simulator exposes a full tactical observation, pending decision, and indexed list of executable options (movement, attacks, spells, reactions, objectives, preparation, scarce resources). The shared engine-generated decision stream supports heuristic controllers, language-model policies, learned option rankers, and masked-action RL agents. Frontier LLMs are evaluated as policies on this stream.

Result: Full tactical observations do not saturate the benchmark. Frontier language-model policies often win direct encounters but exhibit failures in the Day track: poor resource budgeting across encounters, suboptimal rest timing, and lapses in rule-aware tactical discipline.

Conclusion: DungeonBench reveals that while frontier models handle local tactical decisions competently, long-horizon strategic planning under persistent resource constraints remains a significant challenge, making it a useful benchmark for advancing rules-rich tactical reasoning in AI.

Abstract: Games and simulators make valuable benchmarks by turning decisions into measurable outcomes, but many current suites under-test rules-rich tactical reasoning: the ability to choose well when geometry, timing, resources, objectives, and rule interactions all matter at once. We introduce DungeonBench, a benchmark for tactical reasoning in Dungeons & Dragons combat, built to cover the vast majority of combat-relevant 2014 System Reference Document content whose effects can be resolved by the simulator while retaining mechanics that simplified combat simulators often abstract away. At each step, DungeonBench exposes a complete tactical observation, a pending decision, and an indexed list of executable options spanning movement, attacks, spells, reactions, objectives, preparation, and scarce resources. The task is to value legal choices whose consequences depend on action economy, creature traits, battlefield geometry, timing windows, and future encounters. DungeonBench has two tracks: Encounter, which evaluates local tactical play in single fights, and Day, which links encounters through persistent hit points, spell slots, consumables, preparation, and short-rest timing, forcing policies to trade off immediate tactical advantage against future survivability. The same engine-generated decision stream supports heuristic controllers, language-model policies, learned option rankers, and masked-action reinforcement-learning agents. We evaluate frontier language-model policies on this shared decision stream. Results show that full tactical observations do not saturate the benchmark: frontier policies often win direct encounters, but linked encounter days expose failures in resource budgeting, rest timing, and rule-aware tactical discipline.

</details>


### [92] [AgentHPOBench: A Benchmark For Evaluating LLM Agents as Sequential Hyperparameter Optimizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29626)
*Tianyu Huai,Tingshuo Fan,Xinchi Chen,Yining Zheng,Yuxin Wang,Shuang Chen,Jie Zhou,Xuanjing Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentHPOBench is a sequential benchmark with 30 executable ML tasks across 7 categories that evaluates LLM agents' ability to interpret experimental evidence and iteratively refine hyperparameters, revealing that current agents show measurable optimization ability but struggle with sustained refinement, log diagnosis, and consistent progress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate static code generation, paper replication, or final answer correctness, but fail to assess whether LLM agents can interpret experimental evidence and use it to guide sequential hyperparameter decisions—a core capability needed for autonomous scientific experimentation.

Method: AgentHPOBench provides 30 executable ML tasks across 7 research categories, each beginning with a validated baseline run. Agents perform sequential interventions, observing accumulated configurations, metrics, and logs at each step before proposing the next valid configuration. The benchmark evaluates 12 widely used agents and conventional HPO baselines under a unified protocol.

Result: Current agents exhibit measurable experimental optimization ability across diverse domains, but face clear limitations in sustained iterative refinement, complex log diagnosis, and consistent progress toward reported reference performance.

Conclusion: While LLM-based agents demonstrate promising experimental reasoning capabilities, they are not yet reliable enough for fully autonomous iterative experimentation, highlighting the need for better evidence interpretation and long-horizon decision-making in scientific agent design.

Abstract: As LLMs evolve from code completion systems into autonomous scientific agents, evaluating their ability to conduct experiments has become increasingly important. Existing benchmarks typically focus on static code generation, paper replication, or final answer correctness, but do not directly assess whether agents can interpret experimental evidence and use it to guide subsequent hyperparameter decisions. To address this gap, we introduce AgentHPOBench, a sequential benchmark comprising 30 executable machine learning tasks across seven research categories. Each task begins with a validated baseline run, after which an agent performs several sequential interventions. At each step, the agent observes the accumulated configurations, metrics, and logs before proposing the next valid configuration. We evaluate 12 widely used agents and conventional HPO baselines under a unified protocol. The results show that current agents exhibit measurable experimental optimization ability across domains, but still face clear limitations in sustained iterative refinement, complex log diagnosis, and consistent progress toward reported reference performance.

</details>


### [93] [Development of FDD-ON: an Ontology for VAV HVAC System Fault Detection and Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29657)
*Yimin Chen,Brian Fricke,Bo Shen,Jamie Lian,Mingkan Zhang,James Lo,Yun Zhang,Shi Ye,Jiajing Huang,Han Hu,Chujie Lu,Rui Tang,George Zhuang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FDD-ON is a modular ontology that formally represents VAV HVAC system components, faults, symptoms, and impacts, enabling semantic interoperability for fault detection and diagnosis across heterogeneous FDD applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current FDD solutions suffer from limited data interpretability and interoperability, creating fragmented information silos that impede deployment of advanced applications such as digital twin-enabled FDD frameworks and AI-driven maintenance decision-making systems.

Method: The authors developed FDD-ON, a modular and extensible OWL ontology that formally models VAV HVAC system components, fault types, symptom statuses, fault impacts, and their attributes. It includes controlled vocabularies, comprehensive fault/symptom/impact libraries, and explicit cause-fault-symptom-impact relational chains. Evaluation used publicly available VAV HVAC datasets and FDD development use cases.

Result: FDD-ON provides a foundational semantic framework that supports scalable, transparent, and interoperable FDD solutions. It enables machine-interpretable diagnostic knowledge querying, heterogeneous FDD output mapping, and interoperable application development.

Conclusion: FDD-ON successfully addresses the semantic interoperability gap in building FDD, offering a structured knowledge representation that bridges heterogeneous data sources and equipment types, thereby advancing the practical deployment of FDD in smart building environments.

Abstract: Fault detection and diagnosis (FDD) technology is essential for improving HVAC system reliability, energy efficiency, and maintenance effectiveness. However, effective deployment of FDD solutions in buildings requires structured domain knowledge that can bridge heterogeneous data sources, diverse equipment types, and varied diagnostic outputs. Limited data interpretability and interoperability within the FDD domain have led to fragmented information silos, hindering the implementation of FDD and related applications, such as the digital twin-enabled FDD frameworks and artificial intelligence (AI)-driven maintenance decision-making systems. This paper presents an FDD Ontology (FDD-ON), a modular and extensible ontology to formally represent variable air volume (VAV) HVAC system components, fault types, symptom statuses, fault impacts and associated attributes. FDD-ON integrates HVAC system FDD semantics to provide comprehensive representations of fault and symptom attributes, supported by the well-defined controlled vocabulary. Additionally, FDD-ON offers comprehensive fault, symptom, and impact libraries to capture a broad spectrum of operational abnormalities and their consequences in VAV HVAC systems. Through explicit contributing cause-fault-symptom-impact relations, FDD-ON serves as a machine-interpretable basis for querying diagnostic knowledge, mapping heterogeneous FDD outputs, and developing interoperable FDD-related applications. FDD-ON is evaluated using publicly available VAV HVAC system datasets and demonstrated through FDD development applications. Results indicate that FDD-ON provides a foundational semantic framework for advancing scalable, transparent, and interoperable FDD solutions across various applications.

</details>


### [94] [ExtractBench: A Benchmark for Schema-Guided Enterprise Document Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29677)
*Boyang Zhang,Adrian Lyjak,Eli Stewart,Zhaoqi Li,Simon Suo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ExtractBench is a new benchmark for evaluating schema-guided document extraction across 4,869 pages, measuring value accuracy, record completeness, grounding, and cost. LlamaExtract Agentic Plus achieves top performance across all metrics, matching coding agents' accuracy at much lower cost, while commercial VLMs struggle with long-document record truncation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise workflows increasingly depend on schema-guided extraction agents, but existing evaluation approaches lack comprehensive assessment combining value accuracy, record completeness at scale, grounding (source traceability), and measured cost. A standardized benchmark is needed to rigorously compare extraction systems across diverse real-world document types and challenge scenarios.

Method: The authors introduce ExtractBench with 4,869 pages across 370 enterprise documents spanning 8 business domains and 67 document types, tagged by challenge scenario. The curation pipeline uses independent-system agreement for real documents, known values for synthetic lists, and human verification for forms. Evaluation uses order-insensitive value F1 for accuracy, plus word- and page-level F1 for grounding, alongside cost measurement.

Result: Commercial VLMs perform well on short documents but frequently truncate record lists on long documents. Coding agents achieve higher accuracy but at substantially higher cost. LlamaExtract Agentic Plus ranks first across all three metrics (value F1, word-level grounding F1, page-level grounding F1), delivering accuracy comparable to coding agents at a fraction of the cost.

Conclusion: ExtractBench provides the first comprehensive evaluation framework for schema-guided extraction, revealing a clear accuracy-cost trade-off between VLMs and coding agents. LlamaExtract Agentic Plus demonstrates that high accuracy with strong grounding is achievable at low cost, and the benchmark is publicly released to facilitate further research.

Abstract: Enterprise workflows increasingly rely on agents for \emph{schema-guided extraction}: given a document and a user-defined schema, the agent faithfully follows the schema to produce the correct output with source evidence as grounding metadata. We present ExtractBench, a benchmark for schema-guided extraction and, to our knowledge, the first to score value accuracy, record completeness at scale, grounding, and measured cost together. The evaluation system contains 4,869 pages across 370 enterprise documents, 8 business domains, and 67 document types, with clear tags differentiating their challenge scenarios. The scalable schema and ground-truth curation pipeline combines independent-system agreement for real documents, known values for synthetic lists, and human verification for forms. We report order-insensitive value F1 for value accuracy, plus two grounding metrics for source traceability: word- and page-level F1. Commercial VLMs perform well on short documents but often truncate record lists on long ones, while coding agents retain higher accuracy at much higher cost. LlamaExtract Agentic Plus ranks first on all three metrics, with accuracy comparable to coding agents at a fraction of the cost. Dataset and evaluation code are available on \href{https://huggingface.co/datasets/llamaindex/ExtractBench}{HuggingFace} and \href{https://github.com/run-llama/ExtractBench}{GitHub}.

</details>


### [95] [Evidence-Ledger Adjudication for Claim-Evidence Traceability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26512)
*Gengyu Chen,Yongjie Yu,Weiling Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI agent workflow, evidence-ledger adjudication, pairs claims with evidence packets and routes unsupported/contradicted claims back to authors. On a 2,335-row benchmark, it achieves 0.676 relation accuracy (vs. 0.383 baseline) and routes 1270 of 1435 problematic claims, enabling auditable traceability for AI-assisted writing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents generate claims faster than authors can verify whether cited evidence actually supports them, creating a verification bottleneck. There is a need for automated, auditable workflows that can adjudicate claim-evidence relationships at scale.

Method: Evidence-ledger adjudication workflow: each claim is paired with an evidence packet and assigned a support relation (supported, contradicted, missing evidence, or mixed). Claims not fully supported are routed back to the author. Evaluation uses a blind 2,335-row benchmark constructed from AVeriTeC, CLIMATE-FEVER, and SciFact, with gold relations and source evidence labels hidden during prediction and joined only for scoring. Agent and non-agent baselines are compared.

Result: The agent evidence-ledger condition achieves 0.676 relation accuracy and 0.601 macro-F1, substantially outperforming the best non-agent baseline (0.383 accuracy, 0.303 macro-F1). It correctly routes 1270 out of 1435 claims with gold labels indicating contradiction, missing evidence, or mixed evidence, while routing 295 out of 900 supported claims.

Conclusion: Evidence-ledger adjudication can transform heterogeneous evidence packets into an auditable traceability layer, offering a practical mechanism for verifying AI-generated claims in writing workflows.

Abstract: AI agents can draft claims faster than authors can check whether the cited or retrieved evidence supports them. We study evidence-ledger adjudication: a claim-evidence traceability workflow that pairs each claim with an evidence packet, assigns a support relation, and routes unsupported, contradicted, or mixed-evidence claims back to the author. The empirical core is a 2,335-row blind benchmark built from independent external labels in AVeriTeC, CLIMATE-FEVER, and SciFact. Gold relations and source evidence labels are hidden during prediction and joined only for scoring. On this benchmark, the agent evidence-ledger condition achieves 0.676 relation accuracy and 0.601 macro-F1, compared with 0.383 accuracy and 0.303 macro-F1 for the best non-agent baseline. It also routes 1270/1435 claims whose gold labels indicate contradiction, missing evidence, or mixed evidence, while routing 295/900 supported claims. These results show that evidence-ledger adjudication can turn heterogeneous evidence packets into an auditable traceability layer for AI-assisted writing.

</details>


### [96] [SVR: Self-Verifying Refinement via Joint Verdict-Confidence Reinforcement Learning for Adaptive Test-Time Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28457)
*Hongyu Chen,Liang Lin,Guangrun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SVR is a multi-turn RL framework that trains models to self-verify and adaptively stop refinement without oracle feedback, achieving strong math reasoning accuracy with fewer inference turns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform test-time compute budgets waste resources on easy problems, while verifier-guided methods rely on external feedback unavailable at inference. The authors aim to internalize the compute-control decision: the model should learn when its answer is good enough to stop.

Method: SVR uses GRPO to train fixed-horizon trajectories where each turn outputs a solution, a discrete correctness verdict, and a confidence score. Retention occurs when verdict is Correct and confidence exceeds a threshold; otherwise refinement continues. Training rewards promote solution correctness, calibrated self-verification, and stop-ready correct states. Only ground-truth correctness constructs rewards; it is never in refinement prompts or required at inference. Adaptive stopping activates only at inference.

Result: On seven math reasoning benchmarks with Qwen3.5-2B, SVR achieves 0.563 macro-average accuracy with only 2.99 average inference turns, outperforming standard GRPO, multi-turn baselines, and a fixed-budget oracle-guided reference, while requiring substantially fewer turns than fixed ten-turn inference.

Conclusion: Learned self-verification can serve as an effective internal control signal for answer retention and adaptive test-time compute allocation, eliminating the need for external verifiers or uniform budgets.

Abstract: Scaling test-time computation can improve language-model reasoning, but uniform budgets waste computation on easy inputs, while verifier-guided refinement relies on external feedback. We introduce Self-Verifying Refinement (SVR), an oracle-free multi-turn reinforcement learning framework that learns to use self-verification as a compute-control policy. At each turn, the model produces a solution together with a discrete correctness verdict and a confidence score; it retains the current answer only when the verdict is Correct and confidence exceeds a threshold, and otherwise continues refinement using its own self-verification. Ground-truth correctness is used only to construct training rewards and is never exposed to the policy through refinement prompts or required at inference. SVR is trained with GRPO on fixed-horizon trajectories using rewards that promote solution correctness, calibration-aware self-verification, and stop-ready correct states; adaptive stopping is activated only at inference. On seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks with Qwen3.5-2B, SVR achieves a macro-average accuracy of 0.563 with only 2.99 inference turns on average. In the evaluated complete-system comparison, it exceeds standard GRPO, strong multi-turn baselines, and a fixed-budget oracle-guided score-feedback reference while requiring substantially fewer turns than fixed ten-turn inference. These results demonstrate that learned self-verification can serve as an effective internal control signal for answer retention and adaptive test-time compute allocation.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [97] [Topology-Aware Data Movement for Disaggregated GPU Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28633)
*Sanjeev Rao Ganjihal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Disaggregated LLM inference suffers from KV cache transfer bottlenecks across heterogeneous interconnects. This paper proposes a topology-aware transfer orchestrator with three mechanisms—pipelined layer-by-layer transfer, NVLink domain-aware MoE placement, and CXL 3.0 memory expanders—projecting 3–18× latency reduction over uniform RDMA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing disaggregated inference systems (DistServe, Splitwise, Mooncake) naively use uniform RDMA for KV cache transfers between prefill and decode pools, ignoring that inter-GPU bandwidth varies by up to 72× depending on physical topology (NVLink: 900 GB/s, InfiniBand: 50 GB/s, TCP: 12.5 GB/s). For a 70B model, each request requires 2.6 GB of KV cache transfer, exceeding 100 GB/s aggregate at production scale, making topology-oblivious transfers a critical bottleneck.

Method: The authors design a topology-aware transfer orchestrator that discovers interconnect hierarchy at startup and selects optimal transport per transfer. Three mechanisms operate jointly: (1) pipelined layer-by-layer transfer that overlaps KV cache transmission with ongoing prefill computation, hiding 60–85% of latency; (2) NVLink domain-aware placement for Mixture-of-Experts models that co-optimizes expert dispatch with KV cache locality; and (3) CXL 3.0 memory expanders serving as a shared overflow tier offering 6× capacity at 86× lower latency than NVMe. Evaluation relies on analytical bandwidth models, component implementations, and projected analysis across three architectures, since requisite multi-node heterogeneous clusters with CXL 3.0 hardware remain unavailable in academic or cloud environments.

Result: Projected analysis across three architectural configurations demonstrates 3–18× transfer latency reduction compared to uniform RDMA baselines. Pipelined transfer hides 60–85% of latency behind computation. CXL 3.0 expanders provide 6× capacity expansion with 86× lower latency than NVMe-based overflow.

Conclusion: Topology-aware KV cache orchestration is essential for practical disaggregated LLM inference. The proposed system demonstrates substantial latency improvements by exploiting interconnect heterogeneity, though full empirical validation awaits availability of CXL 3.0-equipped multi-node GPU clusters.

Abstract: Disaggregated LLM inference creates a datacenter networking problem that no existing system solves correctly. When prefill and decode run on separate GPU pools, the KV cache must be transferred between them. For a 70B model this is 2.6 GB per request, exceeding 100 GB/s aggregate at production scale. Yet DistServe, Splitwise, and Mooncake all use uniform RDMA, ignoring that bandwidth between two GPUs varies by 72x depending on their physical relationship: 900 GB/s via NVLink within a domain, 50 GB/s via InfiniBand across nodes, 12.5 GB/s via TCP across data centers. We design a topology-aware transfer orchestrator that discovers interconnect hierarchy at startup and selects optimal transport per transfer. Three mechanisms work together: (1) pipelined layer-by-layer transfer that overlaps transmission with ongoing prefill, hiding 60 to 85 percent of latency behind computation; (2) NVLink domain-aware placement for Mixture-of-Experts models that co-optimizes expert dispatch with KV cache locality; and (3) CXL 3.0 memory expanders as a shared overflow tier providing 6x capacity at 86x lower latency than NVMe. Full evaluation requires multi-node clusters with heterogeneous interconnects and CXL 3.0 hardware that is beyond academic resources and not yet available in GPU clouds. We present analytical bandwidth models, component implementations, and projected analysis across three architectures showing 3 to 18x transfer latency reduction over uniform RDMA.

</details>


### [98] [Sensitivity Analysis of GRU, LSTM and Transformer Encoder in Classification of Automated Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28665)
*Bidhya Shrestha,Christos Papadopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper classifies which Level 2 automated driving system (Comma Openpilot, Tesla Autopilot, Cadillac Super Cruise, or manual driving) is active using vehicle telematics data. Sequence models (GRU, LSTM, Transformer) achieve macro F1-scores of 0.90–0.93 on clean data. A robustness evaluation framework with five corruption types reveals that temporal jitter is catastrophic (F1 drops to 0.44–0.50), while other corruptions have minimal impact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As SDVs may run multiple ADSs (native and aftermarket), independent monitoring to identify which system is active is critical for safety oversight, regulatory compliance, insurance assessment, and anomaly detection.

Method: Three sequence-based classifiers (GRU, LSTM, Transformer encoder) are trained on vehicle telematics data to distinguish four driving modes. A modular robustness framework applies five corruption families (additive white Gaussian noise with drift, correlated cross-channel noise, temporal jitter, burst loss/delayed transitions for binary signals, and cross-feature inconsistencies) at five severity levels. Clean-data and threat-matched training regimes are compared. Macro F1 is the primary metric.

Result: Clean-data macro F1: Transformer 0.93, GRU 0.92, LSTM 0.90. Threat-matched training yields 0.904–0.916 with modest clean-data penalty. Robustness: event-level corruptions preserve F1 ≥0.87 at max severity; temporal jitter collapses F1 to 0.44–0.50 across all models.

Conclusion: Sequence models can reliably identify ADSs from telematics, but temporal jitter represents a critical vulnerability. The robustness evaluation framework exposes a sharp failure-mode split, highlighting the need for jitter-resistant architectures in safety-critical ADS monitoring deployments.

Abstract: Automated driving systems (ADSs) are becoming ubiquitous. Future Software Defined Vehicles (SDVs) may be able to run multiple ADSs, both native and aftermarket such as Comma.ai's Openpilot. Monitoring systems to independently verify which automated driving system is active are important for safety monitoring, regulatory compliance, insurance assessment, and anomaly detection. In this paper, we first evaluate the effectiveness of three sequence-based classification models: Gated Recurrent Units (GRU), Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, and a Transformer encoder model for identifying Level 2 automated driving systems using vehicle telematics data alone: Comma Openpilot, Tesla Autopilot, and Cadillac Super Cruise, along with manual driving. All three models achieve strong clean-data performance with macro F1-scores of 0.92 (GRU), 0.90 (LSTM), and 0.93 (Transformer encoder model) when trained on clean data; threat-matched training yields 0.904-0.916 macro F1 with only a modest clean-data penalty. Second, we introduce a modular robustness evaluation framework that simulates realistic telematics degradation through five corruption families at five severity levels (L1-L5). Continuous channels are perturbed using additive white Gaussian noise with cumulative drift, correlated cross-channel noise, and temporal jitter. Binary event signals are subjected to burst loss, delayed transitions, spurious toggles and cross-feature inconsistencies inspired by communication errors. Robustness is measured using macro-F1, which gives equal weight to each class and is suitable for imbalanced multiclass evaluation. Our evaluation reveals a sharp failure-mode split: event-level corruptions reduce macro-F1 only slightly (greater than equal to 0.87 at L5), while temporal jitter collapses macro-F1 to 0.44-0.50 across GRU, LSTM, and Transformer encoder model.

</details>


### [99] [Guarantees on Dynamical System Distinguishability for LLM Token Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28667)
*Mohamed Akrout,Dan Wilson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a theoretical foundation for classifying LLM outputs by modeling token embeddings as trajectories of linear dynamical systems. It establishes fundamental accuracy limits, proves that misclassification probability decays exponentially with sequence length, and derives conditions for cross-embedding generalization via an approximate intertwining map.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work empirically showed that LLM response classification succeeds by comparing residuals from two dynamical systems, but lacked theoretical justification for why this works, how performance scales with sequence length, and when the method transfers across different embedding models.

Method: The authors formalize the classification problem as a binary hypothesis test between two stochastic linear dynamical systems. They analyze total variation distance between stationary marginals, derive exponential error decay bounds in terms of a new discriminability metric δ² (spectral distance between the two DSs), and introduce an approximate intertwining condition to characterize cross-embedding transfer, with a lower bound involving the intertwining map's smallest singular value.

Result: (1) Total variation distance between stationary distributions can be arbitrarily small even with substantially different dynamics, setting a fundamental accuracy floor for classifiers ignoring token dynamics. (2) Misclassification probability decays exponentially in sequence length L, governed by δ². (3) Cross-embedding transferable discriminability is lower-bounded by the smallest singular value of the intertwining map between embedding models.

Conclusion: The theoretical results explain the empirical success of DS-based LLM classification and advocate for using dynamical systems theory to analyze AI systems — reversing the dominant paradigm of using AI to model dynamical systems.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that classifying large language models (LLMs)' responses can be distinguished by modeling token embeddings as trajectories of a black-box dynamical system (DS) and comparing prediction residuals of two DSs. Despite the empirical success of this dynamical approach, a theoretical understanding of why it works, how well it scales as a function of the token sequence, and when it transfers across embedding models remains lacking. We address these questions by formalizing the classification task as a binary hypothesis test between two stochastic linear DSs. We show that the total variation distance between the stationary marginal distributions of the two DSs can be arbitrarily small even when the dynamics differ substantially, which provides a fundamental accuracy floor for any classifier that ignores token dynamics. We then show that the misclassification probability of DS-based classification decays exponentially in the sequence length $L$, with the decay governed by a dynamical discriminability quantity $δ^2$ that captures the spectral distance between the two DSs. We also characterize cross-embedding generalization by introducing an approximate intertwining condition between embedding models and establishing a lower bound on the transferable discriminability in terms of the intertwining map's smallest singular value. Together, these results explain the empirical performance of DS-based classification and motivate further investigation into using DS theory to analyze AI systems, in contrast to the more common approach of using AI to model dynamical systems.

</details>


### [100] [LARA: Lightweight Adapters in the Residual Stream for Composable Adaptation and Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28669)
*Pascal Ekin,Hyosun Choi,Wei Jie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LARA adapts frozen models by injecting low-rank corrections into the residual stream at selected layers, rather than modifying weights. It matches LoRA's performance at equal parameter counts, enables smooth interpolation between base and adapted behavior via a scaling factor γ, and supports hosting multiple behaviors simultaneously on a single model with minimal overhead (33 MB for seven behaviors on a 1.5B model).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods like LoRA modify model weights, which (1) does not offer smooth, graded control between base and adapted behavior at inference, and (2) makes it cumbersome to host multiple specialized behaviors on a single frozen model, since each behavior typically requires loading separate weight updates. The authors seek an adaptation paradigm that provides inference-time control and efficient multi-behavior hosting.

Method: LARA operates entirely in the residual stream: at a small set of layers, it reads the hidden state and adds a low-rank correction back into the residual stream, leaving all base weights frozen. This produces a frozen base + residual decomposition, exposing a scalar γ at inference that interpolates between base (γ=0) and fully adapted (γ=1) behavior. Multiple LARA modules (each a small residual) can be trained independently and placed on the same frozen model, with per-token routing to select which behavior is active.

Result: On code fine-tuning and DPO preference optimization tasks, LARA matches LoRA performance at equal parameter counts. Seven distinct behaviors (six fine-tuned, one DPO-optimized) were hosted simultaneously on a single frozen 1.5B parameter model with only ~33 MB of overhead, compared to requiring seven full model instances. Behaviors are trained separately and can be routed per token.

Conclusion: LARA demonstrates that adaptation in the residual stream is a viable and attractive alternative to weight-space adaptation. It matches LoRA's performance while offering unique advantages: smooth interpolative control at inference via γ scaling and efficient multi-behavior hosting through independent, composable residual modules over a shared frozen base.

Abstract: We present LARA (Lightweight Additive Residual Adaptation), a method for efficient adaptation that operates in the residual stream of a frozen model rather than in its weights. Where LoRA adds an update of low rank to weight matrices, LARA reads the hidden state at a small set of layers and adds a correction of low rank back to the residual stream, leaving all base weights untouched. On a code fine-tuning task and on preference optimization (DPO), LARA matches LoRA at equal parameter counts. Because adaptation is a frozen base plus a residual, LARA exposes a scale γ, applied at inference, that interpolates smoothly between base and adapted behavior, a form of graded control that adaptation in weight space does not offer. Finally, because each behavior is a small residual module over a shared frozen base, many behaviors can be held resident at once and routed automatically per token. We place seven behaviors, six fine-tuned and one optimized for preference, on one frozen 1.5B model for roughly 33 MB of overhead, against one full model for each behavior. Because the base is untouched, behaviors are trained separately and selected per token rather than loaded on demand, which suits hosting many behaviors, and adding new ones, on a single model on a device.

</details>


### [101] [Autonomous Repair for Multi-Agent Systems via Monte-Carlo Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29055)
*Hanxiao Lu,Tianyi Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MARS is a search-based framework that uses Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with diagnosis-guided expansion and taxonomy-augmented evaluation to automatically repair multi-agent system failures, outperforming existing methods by 3–12% on a new benchmark called StateMAS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While multi-agent systems (MAS) are widely used for complex tasks, when they produce incorrect outputs, users must manually inspect agent trajectories to attribute failures and provide corrective feedback. Automated repair mechanisms for MAS failures remain largely unexplored, creating a gap this work aims to fill.

Method: The authors propose MARS, which formulates MAS repair as a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) process. Key innovations include: (1) diagnosis-guided expansion to navigate the repair search space efficiently, (2) taxonomy-augmented evaluation for assessing candidate repairs, and (3) partial rollout (rather than full rollout) during MCTS evaluation to reduce token consumption. They also introduce StateMAS, a large-scale benchmark with 1,310 replayable multi-agent failure trajectories across four agent architectures and four LLM backbones.

Result: MARS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods on the StateMAS benchmark, achieving absolute improvements ranging from 3.0% to 12.1% across all settings, while maintaining comparable token consumption. Ablation studies confirm that both taxonomy-augmented evaluation and diagnosis-guided expansion are critical to these performance gains.

Conclusion: MARS provides an effective automated framework for MAS failure repair, demonstrating that search-based approaches with diagnostic guidance and taxonomic evaluation can significantly improve repair outcomes without increasing token costs, validated on the newly introduced StateMAS benchmark.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) are increasingly deployed to solve complex tasks. In case of incorrect or unsatisfactory outputs, users have to manually locate agent mistakes by inspecting agent trajectories (i.e., {\em failure attribution}) and provide feedback to refine the outputs (i.e., {\em repair}). Despite some recent work in MAS failure attribution, automated mechanisms to recover from such mistakes remain largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we propose MARS, a search-based framework that formulates MAS repair as a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) process and navigates the vast space of potential repairs via diagnosis-guided expansion with taxonomy-augmented evaluation. Unlike standard MCTS, which evaluates a complete simulation via full rollout, MARS evaluates the agent trajectory using partial rollout to reduce token consumption. Furthermore, we introduce StateMAS, a large-scale MAS repair benchmark with 1,310 replayable multi-agent failure trajectories spanning four types of agent architectures and four LLM backbones. Experiments on StateMAS demonstrate that MARS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, achieving an absolute improvement from 3.0\% to 12.1\% across all settings, while maintaining a comparable token consumption cost. The ablation study further confirms that taxonomy-augmented evaluation and diagnosis-guided expansion are critical to achieving these performance gains.

</details>


### [102] [Hierarchical Copula-Gumbel-Top-\texorpdfstring{$K$}{K} Routing: Two-Sided Dependence Control for Frozen Mixture-of-Experts at Fixed Per-Token Routing Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28670)
*Richard Yi Da Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Hierarchical Copula-Gumbel-Top-K (CGA), a method that introduces tunable dependence (positive within groups, negative across groups) into the stochastic routing of MoE models while preserving each token's marginal routing distribution. This controls expert-load coherence vs. dispersion without modifying the base model, using a small trainable controller.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In MoE models with stochastic Top-K routing, individual token routing laws are well-understood, but the joint distribution over routing choices across tokens is underexplored. The authors ask what joint distributions are achievable while holding each token's marginal routing law exactly fixed, motivated by the desire to control expert-load variance (coherence vs. load balancing) without altering the base model's routing probabilities.

Method: The proposed Hierarchical Copula-Gumbel-Top-K (CGA) construction has two components: (1) within a group of related tokens, an exchangeable Gaussian copula induces positive correlation among Gumbel perturbations at each expert coordinate, increasing expert-set coherence; (2) across disjoint group pairs, an antithetic construction introduces tunable negative dependence. Both operations are proven to leave each token's ordered Top-K sample, mixture weights, and inclusion probabilities distributionally identical to independent routing conditioned on pre-routing logits. A small controller trained via a score-function estimator drives the dependence dials over frozen features from the base model.

Result: Theoretical results prove the invariance properties hold. A trade-off is characterized: positive within-group coupling inflates variance of realized expert loads relative to independent routing, while nonnegative cross-group opposition reduces it relative to flat coupling at the same within-group strength. A small-scale pilot validates the mechanism and training route.

Conclusion: Coherence and load dispersion in MoE routing can be controlled by two complementary dependence dials on an invariance constraint surface, without modifying the base model. The pilot validates the mechanism, though task-level fine-tuning gains remain to be established.

Abstract: A stochastic Gumbel-Top-$K$ router defines, for every token of a mixture-of-experts (MoE) model, a \emph{routing law}: a distribution over ordered expert lists and mixture weights. We ask which \emph{joint} distributions over the routing choices of different tokens are reachable while every individual token's complete routing law is held exactly fixed. We give a two-sided construction, \emph{Hierarchical Copula-Gumbel-Top-$K$} (\CGA{}). Within a group of related tokens, an exchangeable Gaussian copula positively correlates the Gumbel perturbations at each expert coordinate, which can increase within-group expert-set coherence. Across disjoint pairs of groups, a tunable antithetic construction introduces a selectable amount of negative dependence. We prove that both operations leave each token's ordered Top-$K$ sample, mixture weights, and inclusion probabilities identical in distribution to independent routing \emph{at a routing layer conditioned on its pre-routing logits}; conditional expected expert traffic is preserved as a consequence. We characterize the resulting trade-off: positive within-group coupling can only inflate the variance of realized expert loads relative to independent routing, while nonnegative cross-group opposition can only reduce it relative to flat coupling at the same within-group strength. Coherence and load dispersion are thus controlled by two complementary dependence dials on the invariance constraint surface. Because the base model is untouched, the dials can be driven by a small controller over frozen features, trainable with a score-function estimator: the frozen network is evaluated only in the forward direction, and gradients are confined to the controller. An initial small-scale pilot validates the mechanism and the training route, but does not establish task-level fine-tuning gains.

</details>


### [103] [LAWFUL: Law-Aligned Witness for Faithful Use of Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28672)
*Kevin Chen,Kenneth W. Parker,Anish Arora*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces LAWFUL, a framework to determine whether neural networks that accurately predict physical systems have genuinely learned the governing physical laws as structured, internal representations. It identifies four interpretability gaps, addresses two of them, and demonstrates the approach on a transformer model learning the Doppler frequency law from indirect data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks can achieve high accuracy in predicting physical systems, but it remains unclear whether they internally learn and consistently use the formal governing physical laws. Existing interpretability methods cannot adequately answer this question for physics laws over continuous variables due to four key gaps: lack of coverage-aware causal-consistency measures, no domain-of-validity testing for identified circuits, no verification of physical invariants and forbidden behaviors, and no quantification of how derived physical quantities flow through the model's computation.

Method: The authors develop LAWFUL, a foundational framework that addresses the first two gaps: (1) a coverage-aware causal-consistency measure over continuous counterfactuals to assess whether the network's internal computation aligns with a candidate physical law across its domain, and (2) a domain-of-validity test for identified circuits. The framework also lays groundwork for the remaining two gaps (invariant/forbidden-behavior verification and physical-quantity flow quantification). The approach is illustrated on the Mocap2Radar transformer trained on motion-capture and radar data where neither target frequency nor velocity appears explicitly.

Result: The framework successfully validates whether the Mocap2Radar transformer internally learns and uses the Doppler frequency law f(t) = 2v(t)/λ, despite neither f(t) nor v(t) being explicit inputs or outputs. The paper demonstrates that the identified circuit passes the causal-consistency and domain-of-validity tests, providing evidence that the network has genuinely internalized the physical law as structured knowledge.

Conclusion: LAWFUL provides a principled approach to bridge the gap between a neural network's predictive accuracy and its internalization of formal physical knowledge. By closing two of the four identified interpretability gaps, the framework enables rigorous verification of whether models learn and use governing laws. This has implications for scientific discovery, model trustworthiness, and the interpretability of physics-informed machine learning systems.

Abstract: When a neural network predicts a physical system accurately, has it learned the governing law as formal, structured knowledge, and if so, does the network's internal computation actually use that representation throughout the law's domain of validity? We identify four interpretability gaps that limit answering these questions for {\em physics laws over continuous variables}: the absence of a coverage-aware causal-consistency measure over continuous counterfactuals; of a domain-of-validity test for the identified circuit; of a verification of the law's invariants and forbidden behaviors; and of a quantification of how a derived physical quantity flows through the circuit. We develop a foundational framework, LAWFUL, that closes the first two and lays groundwork for the remaining two, and illustrate it on the Mocap2Radar transformer, validating whether it learns and internally uses the Doppler frequency law $f(t) = \frac{2 v(t)}λ$ from motion-capture and radar data in which neither $f(t)$ nor $v(t)$ appears.

</details>


### [104] [Transcript-Managed Transformers: Monotone Multi-Agent Collapse and Universality with Two Pop-Enabled Transcripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29496)
*Sergey Salishev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Studies finite-precision causal Transformers with transcript management via channels, modeling layers as Transcript-Managed Transducers. Shows pop-free version captures deterministic finite-state transductions; adding pop yields DCFL (k=1) and RE (k≥2), with two pop-enabled channels sufficing for universality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formally characterize the computational power of fixed, finite-precision causal Transformers when transcripts are managed through channel operations (append, pop), establishing how architectural constraints map to classical language classes.

Method: Introduces the Transcript-Managed Transducer (TMT_k) model with finite control, k channels, and push/pop actions under caller-driven status. Analyzes the pop-free variant (RTMT_k) and the full model via reduction to Hopcroft-Ullman stack presentations, connecting to classical automata hierarchies.

Result: RTMT_k (append-only) realizes exactly deterministic finite-state transductions for any fixed k. With pop, k=1 yields DCFL; k≥2 yields RE (Turing completeness). Two pop-enabled transcripts suffice for universality. Simulation costs and invariance under fixed block size/visible radius are established.

Conclusion: The computational hierarchy of transcript-managed Transformers is precisely characterized: append-only is finite-state, single-stack is DCFL, and two stacks achieve universality. Bounds hold under fixed precision, alphabets, and population, with extensions adding further state.

Abstract: We study transcript management for fixed, finite-precision causal Transformers. A transcript is partitioned into channels of bounded blocks. Each transition consults a fixed visible suffix and may append one block, leaving the model, weights, and token protocol unchanged. The operation $P_c:=\PopContext(c)$ deletes the newest block on channel $c$ and exposes its predecessor.
  We model the layer by the Transcript-Managed Transducer $\TMTn{k}$: one finite controller, $k$ channels, and per-round actions from stay, push, and pop under a caller-driven status map. Fixed visible windows encode as finite symbols. The pop-free Restricted Transcript-Managed Transducer $\RTMTn{k}$ is the standard append-only layer and, for every fixed $k$, realizes exactly the deterministic finite-state transductions. The same holds for every fixed finite agent population under a monotone protocol that appends, routes, and copies visible blocks.
  Admitting $\{P_c\}_{c=1}^k$ restores pop. Newest-first, a pop-enabled channel is a stack; compiling to the Hopcroft--Ullman presentation transfers the classical hierarchy: $\DCFL$ for $k=1$ and $\RE$ for every $k\ge2$. Orchestrated one-channel agents match one controller with $k$ channels, so two pop-enabled transcripts---in one agent or two---suffice for universality. Simulation costs and invariance to fixed block size and visible radius are stated. The bounds fix precision, alphabets, blocks, visibility, controller state, and population; growing exact context, hidden-block access, writable stores, and unbounded \textbf{Spawn} add further state.

</details>


### [105] [MPP-GNN: Subject-Adaptive Community Detection for fMRI-Based Alzheimer's Disease Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28681)
*Yang Zhang,Xiao Zhou,Jonathan Warrell,Avram Holmes,Xuan Zhang,Mark Gerstein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MPP-GNN proposes a meta probabilistic pooling graph neural network that adaptively discovers subject-specific brain functional modules via bilevel optimization, using these modules as explicit priors to guide connectivity learning for Alzheimer's disease classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GNN-based fMRI analysis methods assume a fixed number of functional modules across all subjects, ignoring inter-subject variability, and rarely use discovered modules to directly guide connectivity learning.

Method: MPP-GNN formulates the task as a coupled bilevel optimization that hierarchically performs adaptive graph partitioning to discover subject-specific modules, then uses these modules as an explicit prior for edge refinement and representation learning. Classification is evaluated on two public AD datasets.

Result: MPP-GNN achieved the highest AUC on both public AD classification datasets compared to established baselines. The discovered modules align with the Yeo brain atlas's canonical functional-network organization and reveal a network-level dedifferentiation pattern in AD.

Conclusion: Subject-specific module discovery via meta probabilistic pooling improves both classification performance and neuroscientific interpretability, offering insights into AD-related functional reorganization.

Abstract: Functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) is a widely used technique for studying the brain. Recent methods that utilize graph neural networks (GNNs) for analysis of brain functional connectivity have shown great potential for the classification of brain disorders, such as Alzheimer's disease (AD). However, these methods often assume a preset number of functional modules across all subjects, which overlooks inter-subject variability. In addition, the discovered modules are rarely used to directly guide the learned connectivity patterns. Here, to address these issues, we propose a Meta Probabilistic Pooling GNN (MPP-GNN). We frame the model's task as a coupled, bilevel optimization that performs adaptive graph partitioning hierarchically to discover subject-specific modules and then uses the discovered brain modules as an explicit prior to guide edge refinement and representation learning. We validate MPP-GNN on two public datasets for AD classification, achieving the highest AUC in comparison to established baselines for both datasets. Furthermore, our analysis demonstrates that MPP-GNN shows significant alignment with the canonical functional-network organization defined by the Yeo brain atlas and reveals a network-level dedifferentiation pattern for AD.

</details>


### [106] [Technological Advances in Detecting and Managing Cognitive Impairment in Older Adults: Trends, Challenges, and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28687)
*Mohammad Asif,Azizuddin Khan,Mohd Azam,Anurag Rajkumar Bombarde*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a critical synthesis of recent technological advances for detecting and managing cognitive impairment in older adults, integrating EEG, neuroimaging, blood biomarkers, and digital markers through AI/ML/DL. It contributes a cross-disciplinary taxonomy, emphasizes rigorous external validation, and proposes an integrative early-detection framework linking tiered screening to intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Routine clinical assessment often misses the earliest signs of cognitive decline from MCI to dementia, representing a defining health challenge of aging populations. There is a need to synthesize disparate technological advances and critically evaluate their readiness for real-world deployment, particularly regarding methodological rigor and external validity.

Method: The article is a critical review and synthesis. It constructs a cross-disciplinary taxonomy of detection modalities (EEG, MRI, amyloid/tau PET, blood-based biomarkers, digital markers) integrated via AI/ML/DL. It applies a methodological-rigor lens emphasizing subject- and site-independent validation, and proposes an integrative early-detection framework with tiered screening linked to intervention. Comparison tables summarize detection methods, interventions, and risk/protective factors.

Result: EEG markers (alpha/theta changes, P300 latency) and deep models (CNNs, LSTM/BiLSTM, transformers, self-supervised EEG foundation models) show strong accuracy but rely on small, single-site datasets unlikely to survive external validation. Plasma p-tau217 has reached clinical utility with the first blood test cleared for Alzheimer's diagnosis in 2025. Anti-amyloid therapies (lecanemab, donanemab) are approved despite modest, contested benefits. Multidomain lifestyle prevention has matured. Wearable, remote, speech, and VR tools enable ecologically valid monitoring; multimodal fusion improves sensitivity and specificity.

Conclusion: The field's near-term promise lies in trustworthy, multimodal, longitudinally validated systems linking early detection to actionable, personalized care. Significant barriers remain: standardization, explainability, data privacy, and equitable, externally validated deployment.

Abstract: As populations age, cognitive decline from mild cognitive impairment (MCI) to dementia is a defining health challenge of the coming decades, yet routine assessment often misses its earliest signs. This article critically synthesizes recent technological advances for detecting and managing cognitive impairment in older adults, spanning neurophysiological signals (chiefly electroencephalography, EEG), structural and molecular neuroimaging (MRI and amyloid/tau PET), blood-based biomarkers, and digital markers, integrated through artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning (ML), and deep learning (DL). Beyond summarizing, it contributes a cross-disciplinary taxonomy, a methodological-rigor lens foregrounding subject- and site-independent validation, an integrative early-detection framework linking tiered screening to intervention, and comparison tables of detection methods, interventions, and risk and protective factors. EEG markers (alpha/theta changes, P300 latency) and deep models (CNNs, LSTM/BiLSTM, transformers, self-supervised EEG foundation models) report strong accuracy, yet many rest on small, single-site datasets unlikely to survive rigorous external validation. Elsewhere, gains are tangible: plasma p-tau217 has reached clinical utility, with the first blood test cleared to aid Alzheimer's diagnosis in 2025; anti-amyloid therapies (lecanemab, donanemab) are approved despite modest, contested benefits; and multidomain lifestyle prevention has matured. Wearable, remote, speech, and virtual-reality tools enable continuous, ecologically valid monitoring, and multimodal fusion improves sensitivity and specificity. Barriers remain: standardization, explainability, data privacy, and equitable, externally validated deployment. The field's near-term promise lies in trustworthy, multimodal, longitudinally validated systems linking early detection to actionable, personalized care.

</details>


### [107] [SEDR-Seq2P: A Lightweight Dilated Residual Sequence-to-Point Network for Multi-Task Industrial NILM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28693)
*Hatem Haddad,Feres Jerbi,Issam Smaali*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes SEDR-Seq2P, a lightweight Seq2Point extension with dilated residual blocks and squeeze-and-excitation attention, to improve industrial NILM accuracy while maintaining low inference latency for scalable deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial NILM is challenging because measurement noise and concurrent machine operation degrade the generalization of models trained on residential data. Existing models either lack accuracy or incur prohibitively high computational cost, necessitating a method that balances accuracy and inference speed for real-world industrial deployment.

Method: The authors adopt a one-to-many, multi-task disaggregation setting where a single network estimates multiple industrial machine loads from aggregate power. They benchmark Seq2Seq, Seq2SubSeq, Seq2Point, GRU, and WaveNet on the IMDELD dataset using energy-estimation metrics and an accuracy-delay criterion. They then propose SEDR-Seq2P, which augments the Seq2Point architecture with dilated residual blocks and squeeze-and-excitation attention for improved feature extraction with minimal overhead.

Result: Relative to the Seq2Point baseline, SEDR-Seq2P reduces MAE by ~7%, improves R² by ~1%, and increases match rate by ~0.8%. Compared to WaveNet, SEDR-Seq2P reduces inference latency by ~58%, achieving a favorable accuracy-delay trade-off.

Conclusion: SEDR-Seq2P offers a compelling accuracy-delay trade-off for scalable industrial NILM deployment, outperforming Seq2Point on accuracy while being significantly faster than heavier models like WaveNet.

Abstract: Industrial NILM remains challenging because measurement noise and widespread concurrent machine operation reduce the generalization of models tuned on residential data. This work adopts a one-to-many, multi-task disaggregation setting, in which a single network estimates multiple industrial machine loads from aggregate power. Under a unified evaluation protocol on IMDELD, we benchmark Seq2Seq, Seq2SubSeq, Seq2Point, GRU, and WaveNet using energy-estimation metrics and the accuracy-delay criterion. While Seq2Point offers a stronger accuracy-delay balance than Seq2Seq/Seq2SubSeq, GRU and WaveNet achieve higher accuracy at markedly higher computational cost. To close this gap, we propose SEDR-Seq2P, a lightweight Seq2Point extension with dilated residual blocks and squeeze-and-excitation attention. Relative to the Seq2Point baseline, SEDR-Seq2P reduces MAE by approximately 7%, improves the coefficient of determination by approximately 1%, and increases the match rate by approximately 0.8%. In addition, compared to WaveNet, SEDR-Seq2P reduces inference latency by approximately 58%, yielding a favorable accuracy-delay trade-off for scalable industrial deployment.

</details>


### [108] [Predicting Steel Fatigue Life from Micrographs Using Physics-Informed Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28695)
*Aryuemaan Kumar Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A computer vision framework (CV) using ResNet-50 with physics-informed features and Gaussian NLL loss estimates fatigue life from optical micrographs in under 65ms, achieving R²=0.93 on synthetic benchmarks, though real-sample validation remains pending.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional fatigue life evaluation of structural steels requires mechanical testing lasting tens to hundreds of hours, making it impractical for rapid quality control. A fast, non-destructive alternative based on microstructural imaging is needed.

Method: A pipeline consisting of: (1) a seven-stage OpenCV preprocessing routine for artifact removal; (2) a 28-dimensional physics-informed feature extractor capturing crack morphology, grain structure, porosity, and texture; (3) a CNN regression model (three architectures tested: SE-CNN, ResNet-50, VGG-16) trained with Gaussian negative log-likelihood (GNLL) loss to jointly predict log fatigue life (log Nf) and per-sample uncertainty (σ̂). Grad-CAM is used for interpretability.

Result: ResNet-50 achieved R²=0.93, RMSE=0.18 log-cycles, macro-F1=0.91 on a synthetic micrograph benchmark. GNLL loss reduced Expected Calibration Error from 0.089 (MSE baseline) to 0.021 (76% reduction). Grad-CAM confirmed attention to metallurgically relevant features. Inference runs under 65ms per image.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates methodological soundness under simulated conditions. The immediate next step is a domain-transfer study on real field samples, as current validation relies entirely on synthetic micrographs. Both the pipeline and synthetic dataset generator are open-sourced.

Abstract: Here is the plain text version optimized for arXiv's submission form. Custom macros (like \CV and \SI) have been converted to standard text/math so they render correctly on the webpage: Evaluating the fatigue life of structural steels conventionally requires mechanical testing lasting tens to hundreds of hours, making it impractical for rapid quality control. We present CV, a computer vision framework that estimates the fatigue life ($\log N_f$) of lightweight alloy steels directly from optical micrographs without physical testing.The pipeline features a seven-stage OpenCV preprocessing routine to remove artifacts, a 28-dimensional physics-informed feature extractor (quantifying crack morphology, grain structure, porosity, and texture), and a CNN regression model trained with a Gaussian negative log-likelihood (GNLL) loss to jointly predict $\log N_f$ and sample-specific uncertainty $\hatσ$.Evaluating three architectures (SE-CNN, ResNet-50, VGG-16) on a synthetic micrograph benchmark, ResNet-50 achieves $R^2 = 0.93$, RMSE = 0.18 log-cycles, and macro-F1 = 0.91. The GNLL objective reduces Expected Calibration Error by 76% compared to a mean-squared-error baseline (ECE: $0.089 \rightarrow 0.021$). Grad-CAM maps confirm the network attends to metallurgically meaningful microstructural features.Running in under 65 ms per image, the pipeline and synthetic dataset generator are open-sourced. Because validation relies entirely on synthetic micrographs, these results demonstrate methodological soundness under simulated conditions; a domain-transfer study on real field samples is the immediate next step.

</details>


### [109] [Mitigating Class-Tail Undercoverage in Medical Vision-Language Models under Clinical Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28696)
*Mushir Akhtar,M. Tanveer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CALCoDe is a post-hoc conformal prediction method that ensures both marginal and worst-class coverage guarantees for frozen medical VLMs under distribution shift by combining class-tail adaptive thresholds with localized conformal thresholds, empirically achieving 0.95 coverage in all tested settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical VLMs exhibit high marginal coverage after clinical shifts but can severely under-cover individual disease classes. Source prevalence does not reliably indicate which class will fail, and existing methods (localized and tail-aware conformal) do not model held-out class-wise undercoverage. There is a need for a method that guarantees both marginal and class-conditional coverage for frozen VLMs under shift.

Method: CALCoDe operates in three stages: (1) cross-fitted validation identifies classes at risk of undercoverage; (2) a disjoint calibration split estimates class-conditional tail thresholds, which are combined with localized conformal thresholds via a one-sided maximum, yielding a set that contains every label from the localized rule plus extra protection for at-risk classes; (3) an independently calibrated support audit defers cases lacking sufficient inlier support. Finite-sample coverage is proved under exchangeability within each protected class.

Result: Evaluated across two dermatology distribution shifts (HAM10000→ISIC 2019 and HAM10000→PAD-UFES-20) with four frozen VLM backbones, CALCoDe is the only method achieving both marginal and worst-class accepted coverage of 0.95 in all eight settings. On HAM10000→ISIC 2019, average worst-class accepted coverage is 0.970 vs. 0.926 for sTACP and 0.864 for LCP-VLM.

Conclusion: CALCoDe effectively addresses class-wise undercoverage in medical VLMs under distribution shift, providing the first approach to simultaneously guarantee both marginal and worst-class coverage at a prespecified level for frozen models, with strong empirical performance across diverse backbones and shift scenarios.

Abstract: Medical vision-language models (VLMs) can retain high observed marginal coverage after clinical shift while substantially under-covering an individual disease class. The affected class varies with acquisition protocol and backbone geometry, so source prevalence does not reliably reveal the failure. Existing localized and tail-aware conformal methods respectively adapt to test neighborhoods and source-frequency tails, leaving held-out class-wise coverage failure unmodeled. We introduce Class-Tail Adaptive Localized Conformal Deferral (CALCoDe), a post-hoc reliability layer for frozen medical VLMs. Cross-fitted validation predictions identify classes at risk of undercoverage, and a disjoint calibration split estimates their class-conditional tail thresholds. CALCoDe combines each protected threshold with a localized conformal threshold using a one-sided maximum. The resulting set contains every label admitted by the localized rule, with additional protection confined to validation-identified classes. An independently calibrated support audit defers cases with insufficient inlier support. Under exchangeability among accepted examples within each protected class, CALCoDe provides finite-sample coverage at the prespecified guard level and contains the corresponding localized conformal sets; coverage on shifted external cohorts is evaluated empirically. Among standard conformal baselines and recent VLM-specific conformal methods evaluated across two dermatology shifts (HAM10000 to ISIC 2019 and HAM10000 to PAD-UFES-20) and four frozen VLM backbones (BiomedCLIP, OpenAI CLIP ViT-B/32, PubMedCLIP ViT-B/32, and MedSigLIP-448), CALCoDe is the only approach whose observed marginal and worst-class accepted coverage both reach 0.95 in all eight settings. On HAM10000 to ISIC 2019, its average worst-class accepted coverage is 0.970, compared with 0.926 for sTACP and 0.864 for LCP-VLM.

</details>


### [110] [Flow Matching with Missing Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28698)
*Fairoz Nower Khan,Nabuat Zaman Nahim,Peizhong Ju*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Missing-Data Flow Matching, which treats missing coordinates as latent variables and averages the flow matching loss over possible completions. It proves this correction is exact under MCAR, shows missingness transfers variance rather than adding it, demonstrates one completion per example suffices for matching complete-data variance, and bounds the irreducible bias from learned completion models by their expected conditional Wasserstein distance to the true law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow matching assumes fully observed training data, but many real-world applications involve missing data. Standard approaches like imputation-then-flow-matching introduce biases and distortions (e.g., deterministic imputation collapses the generated distribution). The authors aim to develop a principled framework that properly handles missingness within the flow matching paradigm.

Method: The method treats missing coordinates as latent variables and averages the conditional flow matching loss over a completion model. The authors prove exactness under MCAR, derive finite-sample variance and bias properties, analyze the effect of the number of completions per example, and bound the irreducible bias from an imperfect completion model using expected conditional Wasserstein distance.

Result: Key theoretical results: (1) under MCAR with true completions, the incomplete-data objective equals the complete-data objective exactly; (2) missingness transfers estimator variance rather than adding it; (3) one completion per example matches complete-data variance; (4) under a fixed budget, one completion is optimal; (5) learned completion introduces a single irreducible bias bounded by Wasserstein distance. Experiments confirm these predictions and show deterministic/frozen imputation collapses the generated distribution.

Conclusion: Missing-Data Flow Matching provides a theoretically grounded framework for flow matching with incomplete data. The difficulty shifts entirely to learning a good completion model, while the flow matching objective itself requires no modification beyond expectation over completions. The method competes favorably with classical and deep imputation baselines on real tabular data.

Abstract: Flow matching assumes fully observed training data, which many real-world applications rarely provide. We propose Missing-Data Flow Matching, which treats the missing coordinates of training samples as latent variables and averages the flow matching loss over the values they could take. We first prove the correction is exact rather than approximate. Under missing completely at random with true completions, the incomplete-data objective equals the complete-data objective, so missingness changes nothing about what flow matching learns and the entire difficulty relocates to the completion model. Our finite-sample analysis then answers design questions that the algorithm leaves open, and the answers are not the ones intuition suggests. Missingness transfers estimator variance rather than adding it, one completion per example already matches complete-data variance exactly, and under a fixed evaluation budget one completion is optimal. A learned completion model contributes a single irreducible bias, which we bound by its expected conditional Wasserstein distance to the true completion law. Experiments numerically validate the theoretical predictions, show that deterministic rather than frozen imputation is what collapses the generated distribution, and place our method alongside strong classical and deep imputation baselines on real tabular data.

</details>


### [111] [MMFGU: Multimodal Federated Graph Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28708)
*Haodong Lu,Zekai Chen,Weiwei Ji,Shihao Li,Xunkai Li,Xun Wu,Yinlin Zhu,Rong-Hua Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MMFGU is a multimodal federated graph unlearning framework that handles fine-grained unlearning requests (removing specific modalities, attributes, or correspondences) through target-specific representation decoupling, achieving 41.5× speedup over full retraining while preserving retained utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing federated graph unlearning methods only handle coarse-grained removal (entire entities, relations, or clients), but multimodal settings introduce fine-grained unlearning needs (removing a specific image/text while retaining the entity, or revoking learned cross-modal correspondences).
