These fine-grained requests pose three challenges: (1) precisely removing only requested information without damaging retained content, (2) preventing target recovery through remaining modalities or graph neighborhoods, and (3) stopping related traces on other clients from re-entering the global model after aggregation.

Method: MMFGU uses four key mechanisms: (1) mapping heterogeneous unlearning requests into unified target carriers; (2) decoupling requested representations while anchoring retained semantics; (3) exposing and repairing propagated residuals using lightweight probes; and (4) selectively purging affected clients through compact prototype and response signals.

Result: MMFGU effectively removes requested information, preserves retained graph utility, and achieves a 41.5× speedup over full retraining.

Conclusion: MMFGU addresses the previously unsolved problem of fine-grained multimodal unlearning in federated graph settings, demonstrating both effectiveness and significant efficiency gains over full retraining baselines.

Abstract: Multimodal federated graph learning enables clients to collaboratively train graph models over structural, textual, and visual signals without sharing private local data. However, the presence of heterogeneous multimodal content also makes unlearning requests more frequent and fine-grained: users may delete accounts or interactions, remove a particular image or text while retaining the associated entity, or revoke the learned correspondence between retained modalities or graph attributes. Existing federated graph unlearning mainly handles entity/relation or client removal and cannot directly satisfy these multimodal requests. They introduce three challenges: removing only the requested information without damaging retained content, preventing the target from being recovered through remaining modalities or graph neighborhoods, and stopping related traces on other clients from re-entering the global model after aggregation. To address them, we propose \textsc{\textbf{MMFGU}}, a multimodal federated graph unlearning framework built around target-specific representation decoupling. \textsc{MMFGU} maps heterogeneous requests into unified target carriers, decouples requested representations while anchoring retained semantics, exposes and repairs propagated residuals with lightweight probes, and selectively purges affected clients through compact prototype and response signals. Experiments show that \textsc{MMFGU} effectively removes requested information, preserves retained graph utility, and achieves a $\boldsymbol{41.5\times}$ speedup over full retraining.

</details>


### [112] [Mirror Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28737)
*Yunpeng Liu,Matthew Niedoba,Oluwanifemi A. Adekanye,Jason Yoo,Yingchen He,Berend Zwartsenberg,Frank Wood*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes "mirror learning," a framework that transforms third-person observational data into actionable first-person training data via a video diffusion model for perspective transformation and an inverse dynamics model for action inference, enabling policy learning without teleoperation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Behavior cloning is limited to first-person, well-aligned data and cannot exploit abundant third-person observational signals that humans and animals naturally use for learning. The authors aim to bridge this gap by enabling imitation learning from passive, third-person observation.

Method: The framework has two core components: (1) a learned perspective transformation using a fine-tuned video diffusion model that translates third-person views into first-person views (putting the learner in the demonstrator's shoes), and (2) an inverse dynamics model that infers action trajectories from the transformed first-person video in the learner's control space. Together these synthesize "mirror data" — pseudo first-person expert demonstrations derived from third-person observation.

Result: Mirror data alone is sufficient to train effective policies. Augmenting standard first-person behavior cloning with mirror data further improves downstream policy performance, demonstrating both standalone utility and complementary value.

Conclusion: Modern generative world models implicitly encode sufficient structural understanding to support perspective transformation, offering a scalable and safer alternative to data collection methods that rely heavily on teleoperation.

Abstract: We investigate imitation learning through the lens of third-person observation and propose a framework for mirror learning: acquiring actionable policies from passive observation. While behavior cloning (BC) excels under dense, well-aligned first-person data, it fundamentally fails to leverage the rich observational signals arising from third-person demonstrations that humans and animals routinely exploit. We introduce a method that composes (i) a learned perspective transformation that places learners in demonstrators' shoes using a fine-tuned video diffusion model and (ii) an inverse dynamics model that infers action trajectories in the learners' control space. This enables the synthesis of mirror data, pseudo first-person expert data generated from third-person observations of demonstrator behavior. Empirically, we show that mirror data alone can train effective policies, and that augmenting first-person BC training with mirror data further improves downstream policy performance. Our results suggest that modern generative world models implicitly encode sufficient structure to enable a scalable and safe alternative to teleoperation-heavy data collection.

</details>


### [113] [TAGTorch: A PyTorch Library for Geometry, Topology, and Symmetry-Aware Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28755)
*Brendan Kennedy,Tegan Emerson,Gregory Roek,Emilie Purvine,Henry Kvinge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TAGTorch is an open-source PyTorch library that unifies topology-, algebra-, and geometry-inspired tools for neural networks, addressing the fragmented software ecosystem by providing data preprocessing, architectures, training techniques, and model analysis in a single framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite growing use of topology/algebra/geometry-inspired neural network methods, the software ecosystem is fragmented—many methods exist only as unmaintained research prototypes, hindering reproducibility and adoption.

Method: The authors develop TAGTorch, a PyTorch-based library that consolidates tools spanning data preprocessing, neural architectures, training techniques, and model analysis, all under a unified design philosophy and architecture.

Result: TAGTorch fills critical gaps in the current software ecosystem by providing a maintained, open-source, integrated library for topology/algebra/geometry-inspired deep learning methods.

Conclusion: The paper presents TAGTorch's design and current capabilities, outlines where it addresses ecosystem gaps, and discusses future development priorities for the library.

Abstract: Over the last decade, neural networks have been applied to an increasingly diverse range of applications, including data with rich geometric, topological, or symmetry-related structure. As a result, researchers have increasingly drawn inspiration from topology, algebra, and geometry. Despite this rich algorithmic development, the supporting software ecosystem remains fragmented. Many important methods exist only as research prototypes in unmaintained repositories. We address this by introducing Topology, Algebra, and Geometry Torch (TAGTorch), an open-source, PyTorch-based library that unifies tools inspired by topology, algebra, and geometry, including data-preprocessing methods, architectures, training techniques, and model analysis tools. We describe the design philosophy of TAGTorch and then discuss its current architecture and capabilities, highlighting areas where it can fill gaps in the current software ecosystem. We conclude with a discussion of our future development priorities for the library.

</details>


### [114] [Feature Interaction Modeling for Physics-Informed Neural Networks and Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28762)
*Quan Gu,Hongxia Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper integrates factorization machine (FM)-based feature interaction modules into PINNs and neural operators (DeepONet) to capture cross-variable couplings in parameterized PDEs. FM-PINN improves accuracy for smooth high-order PDEs, while FM-Operator and FM-DeepONet excel on nonlinear conservation laws with shocks/discontinuities but offer no consistent gains on smooth operator benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PINNs and neural operators often struggle to capture complex dependencies among spatial, temporal, and physical parameter variables, particularly for PDEs with strong cross-field interactions. The authors leverage the insight that second-order Taylor expansions can explicitly model pairwise variable interactions, motivating the use of factorization machines to enhance model expressiveness for solution manifolds of parameterized PDEs.

Method: The authors propose three architectures: (1) FM-PINN, which embeds FM-style feature interaction modules into PINNs to capture spatio-temporal variable couplings; (2) FM-Operator and (3) FM-DeepONet, which group features (spatial coordinates, time, physical parameters, initial/boundary conditions) into independent sets and model cross-group interactions using FM mechanisms within neural operator learning frameworks.

Result: FM-PINN demonstrates improved approximation accuracy for smooth high-order PDEs. FM-Operator and FM-DeepONet deliver substantial accuracy gains on shock-dominated equations and nonlinear conservation laws with sharp gradients/discontinuities. However, they provide no consistent advantage on smooth operator learning benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed FM-based feature interaction mechanism is a promising direction for physics-consistent modeling of parameterized PDEs with strong cross-field dependencies, particularly for challenging shock-dominated problems, though its benefits are not universal across all problem types.

Abstract: This work embeds feature interaction modules derived from factorization machines (FMs) into physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) and neural operator learning, to enhance model expressiveness for solution manifolds of parameterized partial differential equations (PDEs). Motivated by the second-order Taylor expansion of multivariate functions to characterize variable couplings, we first propose FM-PINN. It explicitly captures spatio-temporal variable interactions and improves the approximation accuracy for smooth high-order PDEs. We further group spatial coordinates, time, physical parameters, and initial and boundary conditions into independent feature sets and model their cross-group interactions. Based on this strategy, we develop FM-Operator and FM-DeepONet, which are particularly effective for nonlinear conservation laws and problems with sharp gradients or discontinuities, while offering no consistent advantage on smooth operator learning benchmarks. Numerical tests demonstrate that the proposed mechanism delivers substantial accuracy gains on challenging shock-dominated equations, indicating a promising direction for physics-consistent modeling of parameterized PDEs with strong cross-field dependencies.

</details>


### [115] [Representations from Pretrained Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials as Coarse Coordinates for Material Generation and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28776)
*Paul Hagemann,Katharina Ueltzen,Simon Müller,Janine George,Philipp Benner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a distribution-based evaluation framework for crystal structure generation using atom-averaged features from pretrained MLIPs (specifically MACE). It proposes the Coarse-Fine Transport Distance (CFTD) to jointly assess quality and novelty, and demonstrates that coarse MACE features can also guide generative models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative models and evaluation metrics for inorganic crystal structures rely on overly simplistic representations. There is a need for richer featurization that captures both the quality and novelty of generated structures in a unified framework, while also detecting issues like memorization.

Method: The authors leverage atom-averaged features from pretrained MACE (a state-of-the-art MLIP). They propose the Coarse-Fine Transport Distance (CFTD), a distribution-based metric that uses two featurizers: coarse MACE features for assessing quality and a complementary fine featurizer for novelty. CFTD is framed as an optimal transport problem comparing generated and reference distributions. The method is benchmarked against the recently proposed continuous SUN metrics. Additionally, coarse MACE features are used directly as conditioning guidance for a crystal structure generative model.

Result: CFTD effectively captures crystal-structure quality while also detecting memorization (mode collapse / overfitting to training data). It proves versatile across different evaluation scenarios. Coarse MACE features also show promise as guidance signals for generative models, improving the generation process.

Conclusion: Pretrained MLIP features, particularly coarse MACE features, provide a powerful and versatile basis for both evaluating and guiding inorganic crystal structure generation. CFTD offers a unified, distribution-based alternative to existing metrics like continuous SUN.

Abstract: Generative machine learning is increasingly used for inorganic crystal structure generation. Most models and the corresponding evaluation approaches rely on simple forms of crystal structure representation. In this paper, we showcase the power of atom-averaged features from pretrained Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials (MLIPs), such as MACE, for such tasks. We first introduce a distance measure that assesses the output of material generative models by capturing both quality and novelty in a single distribution-based evaluation framework. In particular, we introduce the Coarse-Fine Transport Distance (CFTD) using two different featurizers, where the quality component is based on coarse MACE features. We showcase CFTD's versatility in capturing crystal-structure quality while also detecting memorization, and compare it with the recently introduced continuous SUN metrics. We further show that coarse MACE features can be used as guidance for a material generative model.

</details>


### [116] [Distilling Knowledge from Large Language Models into Lightweight Reinforcement Learning Agents for Autonomous Cyber Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28826)
*Konur Tholl,François Rivest,Mariam El Mezouar,Adrian Taylor,Ranwa Al Mallah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An 8B-parameter cybersecurity-pretrained LLM, used via prompt engineering, outperforms a baseline RL agent in autonomous cyber defense. Policy distillation compresses this LLM policy into a 64,910-parameter RL agent, enabling lightweight deployment. Teacher-guided RL stabilization strategies fail to surpass the distilled teacher policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL agents for autonomous cyber operations suffer from unstable behavior and poor initial decision-making due to extensive exploration requirements during training. The authors seek to leverage LLMs' cybersecurity knowledge to bootstrap better defensive policies and then distill them into deployable lightweight agents.

Method: The authors use an 8B-parameter LLM pretrained on cybersecurity data with prompt engineering (no fine-tuning) in a modified CybORG CAGE Challenge 2 environment. They propose an online policy distillation framework that transfers the LLM's defensive policy into a small RL agent (64,910 parameters). Evaluation spans CybORG scenarios from 4 to 12 hosts with varying network configurations, and they test teacher-guided RL stabilization strategies against the distilled policy.

Result: The LLM outperforms the baseline RL agent in the ACO environment. Policy distillation successfully compresses the LLM policy by several orders of magnitude while retaining effective defensive capabilities. No teacher-guided RL stabilization strategy consistently surpasses the optimized teacher policy, indicating policy-alignment limitations between reward-driven RL and teacher-guided strategies.

Conclusion: Cybersecurity-focused LLMs are viable sources of expertise for autonomous cyber defense. Policy distillation offers a practical pathway to operationalize frontier models within efficient, scalable, lightweight agents suitable for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Autonomous Cyber Operations (ACO) are increasingly important for defending enterprise networks as cyber threats continue to evolve in sophistication. ACO applications commonly employ Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents to learn defensive behaviors through interaction with environments. However, RL agents typically require extensive exploration during training, often resulting in unstable behavior and poor initial decision-making before converging toward effective defense strategies.
  In this work, we investigate the use of a Large Language Model (LLM) to improve autonomous defensive decision-making within an ACO environment. Through prompt engineering rather than fine-tuning, we demonstrate that an 8-billion parameter LLM pretrained on cybersecurity data can outperform a baseline RL agent in a modified CybORG CAGE Challenge 2 environment. We then propose an online policy distillation framework that transfers the LLM's defensive policy into a lightweight RL agent containing only 64,910 parameters, reducing model size by several orders of magnitude while maintaining effective defensive capabilities. This provides a pathway toward operationalizing frontier cybersecurity models within lightweight, deployable agents.
  To evaluate transferability, we construct CybORG scenarios ranging from 4 to 12 hosts and assess the approach across varying network configurations. We also evaluate teacher-guided RL stabilization strategies and observe that none consistently surpass the optimized teacher policy, suggesting policy-alignment limitations between reward-driven RL optimization and teacher-guided defense strategies.
  Our results demonstrate the potential of cybersecurity-focused LLMs as sources of expertise for autonomous cyber defense, while policy distillation provides a practical path toward operationalizing frontier cybersecurity models within efficient, scalable agents.

</details>


### [117] [Hypergradient-based Bilevel Reinforcement Learning with Improved Sample Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28849)
*Naman Saxena,Mudit Gaur,Vaneet Aggarwal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Hessian-free hypergradient-based bilevel RL algorithm using Boltzmann policy optimality, achieving state-of-the-art sample complexity of Õ(ε⁻²) and iteration complexity of O(ε⁻¹) without requiring the Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition on the outer-level objective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bilevel RL algorithms face a fundamental trade-off: hypergradient methods using Hessians are not scalable, while penalty-based approximations suffer from high sample complexity. The authors aim to bridge this gap by developing an algorithm that is both scalable (Hessian-free) and sample-efficient.

Method: The authors leverage the optimality of the Boltzmann policy for the entropy-regularized discounted RL objective. They design a Hessian-free hypergradient-based algorithm that avoids second-order computations. The convergence analysis removes the Polyak-Łojasiewicz (PL) condition on the outer-level objective, which was required in prior state-of-the-art sample complexity results.

Result: The proposed algorithm achieves an iteration complexity of O(ε⁻¹) and a sample complexity of Õ(ε⁻²) under mild regularity conditions, matching the best-known rates while relaxing key assumptions (no PL condition on the outer loop).

Conclusion: The work successfully develops a scalable, sample-efficient bilevel RL algorithm that eliminates the need for both Hessian computations and the restrictive PL condition, advancing the state of the art in bilevel reinforcement learning for applications like meta-learning, hierarchical RL, and RLHF.

Abstract: Bilevel reinforcement learning (RL) is an important framework within the literature of RL that can be used to formalize various categories of problems, such as meta-learning, hierarchical task decomposition, and reinforcement learning from human feedback (RL-HF). Most of the bilevel RL algorithms are either not scalable because of using hypergradient with Hessian, or they suffer from high sample complexity because of using penalty-based approximation methods. In this work, we propose a hypergradient-based bilevel RL algorithm using the optimality of the Boltzmann policy for the entropy regularized discounted RL objective function. Our proposed algorithm is Hessian-free and obtains an iteration complexity of $O(ε^{-1})$ and state-of-the-art sample complexity of $\tilde{O}(ε^{-2})$ under mild regularity conditions. Further, in our convergence analysis, we are able to remove the assumption of the Polyak-Lojasiewicz (PL) condition on the outer-level objective function present in the prior state-of-the-art sample complexity work.

</details>


### [118] [An analysis of machine learning approaches for enhancing decision-making in complex discrete choice tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28854)
*Sheng Lun Christine Cao,Destenie Nock,Alex Davis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study benchmarks four machine learning models (MNL, GAM, TNN, GP) against five behavioral choice rules under varying experimental conditions, finding that semi-parametric and non-parametric models consistently outperform parametric ones, with performance gains of 6–96% from more training data and 0–55% from greater choice determinism. A real-world energy policy case study confirms TNN's superior fit (BIC 13.351).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete choice modeling in policy-making is dominated by parametric models, yet little is known about how well machine learning methods can recover individual-level choice rules under heterogeneity — especially under challenges common to preference elicitation (limited training data, many attributes, varying determinism). The study aims to fill this gap by systematically evaluating semi-parametric and non-parametric models across behaviorally meaningful choice rules.

Method: Monte Carlo simulation experiments systematically varied (a) number of attributes, (b) number of training choice sets, and (c) choice rule determinism. Four models — multinomial logistic regression (parametric), generalized additive model (semi-parametric), twinned neural network and Gaussian process (non-parametric) — were trained to recover five choice rules: linear strong utility, monotonic strong utility, ideal point, lexicographic semiorder, and multiattribute linear ballistic accumulator. A case study on real energy policy preference data validated findings using BIC as the selection criterion.

Result: Semi-parametric (GAM) and non-parametric (TNN, GP) models consistently outperformed parametric MNL across all choice rules and experimental conditions. Increasing training choice sets improved performance by 6–96%; increasing choice rule determinism improved performance by 0–55%. In the energy policy case study, TNN achieved the best BIC of 13.351.

Conclusion: Semi-parametric and non-parametric machine learning models are viable and often superior alternatives to parametric models for policy-centric discrete choice modeling. Model selection should be driven by the specific choice task context, including the expected choice rule, number of attributes, available training data, and level of decision determinism.

Abstract: Discrete choice modeling is a common tool used for preference elicitation during policy-making, but this is typically done through parametric models. Machine learning can push the boundaries of discrete choice modeling for policy-based preference elicitation by adopting a data-driven approach or learning individual preferences. However, there is limited knowledge of how well machine learning methods can estimate individual discrete choice rules under individual heterogeneity, especially in the context of challenges often experienced during preference elicitation. This study evaluates four machine learning models (multinomial logistic regression, generalized additive model, twinned neural network, and Gaussian process) with respect to their capacity to learn and predict five choice rules that are important in the behavioral and social sciences (linear strong utility, monotonic strong utility, ideal point, lexicographic semiorder, and multiattribute linear ballistic accumulator). Monte Carlo experiments were performed to assess model performance when increasing a) the number of attributes in the choice alternatives, b) the number of training choice sets, and c) the choice rule's determinism. The simulation results demonstrated that semi-parametric and non-parametric models generally outperform parametric models across all choice rules and experimental contexts. Model performance also generally improves by 6% to 96% and 0% to 55%, respectively, with an increase in training choice sets and choice rule determinism. A case study using real energy policy preference data was also conducted, where TNN performed best with a BIC of 13.351. This work demonstrated the viability and limitations of semi-parametric and non-parametric models in the context of policy-centric discrete choice modeling and showed how the choice task context should drive model selection.

</details>


### [119] [StraightDP: Geometry-Aware Differential Privacy for Rectified-Flow Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29100)
*Xujun Che,Depeng Xu,Xintao Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: StraightDP leverages the heterogeneous geometry of rectified flows — where the noise end depends on class-conditional moments and the data end on sample-specific structure — to allocate a small DP budget for releasing whitened moments and the rest for DP-SGD near the data end, achieving strong utility under strict privacy constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Differentially private training of text-conditioned generative models experiences a severe utility drop under strong privacy guarantees. The authors seek to overcome this "utility cliff" by exploiting the structural properties of rectified flows.

Method: The method, StraightDP, splits the DP budget: a small portion releases whitened class-conditional moments (distilled into weights or injected at sampling time), while the remainder is allocated to DP-SGD focused toward the data end of the flow, beyond what moments can capture. Per-token stream norm constraints are applied to the multimodal backbone, and the released moments are shown to port to frozen models like SD3-medium for sampling-time injection.

Result: At ε=1 on MNIST, released moments alone achieve 0.76 accuracy and FID 237 (vs. 0.21 for uniform DP-SGD). The full pipeline reaches 0.81 accuracy at FID 56 in a public latent space. Per-token stream norm constraints improve downstream accuracy in extreme-noise regimes, with monotonically increasing benefit as privacy strengthens. On frozen SD3-medium, sampling-time moment injection outperforms DP-LoRA training at a fraction of the budget.

Conclusion: The heterogeneous geometry of rectified flows enables effective budget splitting for DP generative modeling, where cheaply released class-conditional moments handle the noise end and DP-SGD handles the data end, yielding state-of-the-art privacy-utility trade-offs across both pixel-space and latent-space generative models.

Abstract: Differentially private (DP) training of text-conditioned generative models suffers a utility cliff at strong privacy. We revisit this problem through the geometry of rectified flows: along the straight interpolation between noise and data, the Bayes-optimal velocity is governed to leading order at the noise end by a few class-conditional moments, and increasingly sample-specific structure matters toward the data end. StraightDP exploits this heterogeneity end to end. A small budget share releases whitened class-conditional moments once, to be distilled into the weights or injected at sampling time. The rest is spent by pre-declared DP-SGD toward the data end, beyond the moments' reach. At $\varepsilon=1$ on MNIST, the released moments alone already attain $0.76$ downstream accuracy with prototype-like samples and an FID of $237$, and uniform DP-SGD attains $0.21$. The pipeline built on the release reaches $0.81$ accuracy at FID $56$ in a public latent space. Constraining per-token stream norms of the multimodal backbone leaves the pretraining loss unchanged yet improves downstream accuracy in the extreme-noise pixel-space regime, and its accuracy effect becomes monotonically more favorable as privacy strengthens. The released moments also port to frozen SD3-medium, where sampling-time injection beats DP-LoRA training at a fraction of the budget.

</details>


### [120] [Fast Rates for Swap-Agnostic Learning of Proper Losses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28856)
*Princewill Okoroafor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Swap-agnostic learning allows a comparator to choose a different hypothesis on each prediction level set. This paper shows that for proper losses, these comparisons can be controlled jointly rather than per-prediction, yielding a reduction to second-order multicalibration via Blackwell approachability with Bernstein-style variance correction. The resulting offline and online rates substantially improve prior work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical agnostic learning compares the learner to a single best hypothesis. Swap-agnostic learning strengthens this by allowing the comparator to swap predictions—selecting a different hypothesis on each level set of the learner's predictions—which captures prediction-dependent postprocessing (e.g., fairness adjustments, calibration). A naive approach requires solving a separate agnostic learning problem for every possible prediction value, which is computationally prohibitive. The authors aim to show that for proper losses, these per-prediction comparisons can be controlled jointly, yielding efficient algorithms.

Method: The core technical contribution is a reduction from swap-agnostic learning to a second-order form of multicalibration. This is achieved via Blackwell approachability augmented with a Bernstein-style variance correction. This reduction allows the learner to jointly control prediction-level comparisons rather than enumerating them. For smooth proper losses, they obtain rates of O~((log|H|/m)^{2/3}) offline and O~(T^{1/3}(log|H|)^{2/3}) online. For all bounded proper losses in [-1,1], they achieve O~(sqrt(log|H|/m)) offline and O~(sqrt(T log|H|)) online via simultaneous swap-agnostic algorithms. For convex 1-Lipschitz proper losses, rates improve to match the smooth case.

Result: For any finite hypothesis class H and smooth proper loss: offline excess risk is O~((log|H|/m)^{2/3}), online swap-regret is O~(T^{1/3}(log|H|)^{2/3}). For all proper losses bounded in [-1,1]: O~(sqrt(log|H|/m)) offline, O~(sqrt(T log|H|)) online. For convex 1-Lipschitz proper losses: O~((log|H|/m)^{2/3}) offline, O~(T^{1/3}(log|H|)^{2/3}) online. All bounds are tight up to logarithmic factors and improve upon the O~(T^{2/3}(log|H|)^{1/3}) rate implied by the swap-omniprediction guarantee of Luo et al. (2025).

Conclusion: Swap-agnostic learning for proper losses is efficiently achievable without per-prediction-level enumeration. The reduction to second-order multicalibration via Blackwell approachability with Bernstein-style variance correction provides tight rates that improve on prior swap-omniprediction bounds. The results unify and advance the theory of postprocessing-resilient learning, with implications for fairness, calibration, and omniprediction.

Abstract: Swap-agnostic learning strengthens classical agnostic learning by allowing the comparator to select a different hypothesis on each level set of the learner's predictions. This benchmark captures prediction-dependent postprocessing, but appears to require solving a separate agnostic-learning problem for every possible prediction value. We show that, for proper losses, these prediction-level comparisons can instead be controlled jointly. Our main result is an offline swap-agnostic learner for any fixed proper loss. For a finite hypothesis class $H$ and any fixed smooth proper loss, the excess risk from $m$ i.i.d. samples is $\widetilde{O}((\log |H|/m)^{2/3})$, with a corresponding online swap-regret bound of $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/3}(\log |H|)^{2/3})$. We also give algorithms whose predictions are simultaneously swap-agnostic for entire families of losses. For all proper losses bounded in $[-1,1]$, we obtain online and offline rates of $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{T\log |H|})$ and $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\log |H|/m})$, respectively. For convex, $1$-Lipschitz proper losses, these rates improve to $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/3}(\log |H|)^{2/3})$ online and $\widetilde{O}((\log |H|/m)^{2/3})$ offline. These bounds are tight up to logarithmic factors and improve upon the $\widetilde{O}(T^{2/3}(\log |H|)^{1/3})$ rate implied by the swap-omniprediction guarantee of Luo et al. (2025). Our main technical contribution is a reduction from swap-agnostic learning to a second-order form of multicalibration, obtained via Blackwell approachability with a Bernstein-style variance correction.

</details>


### [121] [Adaptivity via a Parallel Architecture for Stochastic Gradient Methods Adaptivity via a Parallel Architecture for Stochastic Gradient Methods Adaptivity via a Parallel Architecture for Stochastic Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28902)
*Bin Fu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A parallel framework that runs multiple static gradient descent instances with geometrically spaced iteration budgets across processors, adaptively searching for the optimal number of iterations to satisfy convergence conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitation of static gradient methods that require a predetermined total number of iterations T, by enabling adaptive selection of T through parallel exploration, thereby achieving better adaptivity without prior knowledge of the optimal iteration budget.

Method: The framework employs p processors executing static gradient descent GD(x₀, T) in stages. Each processor j at stage i runs GD with T_{j,i} = h(j,i), where h is a prescribed function. Processors search along a geometric sequence of T values. The step size is determined by s = S(T), a predetermined function, with iterations x_{i+1} = x_i - (η/s)·g_i using stochastic gradients.

Result: The paper presents the framework architecture and convergence analysis, demonstrating that the parallel search over geometrically spaced T values yields gradient descent that satisfies desired convergence conditions adaptively.

Conclusion: Parallel assembly of static gradient methods with geometrically scheduled iteration budgets enables adaptive stochastic gradient descent, leveraging multi-processor architectures to automatically determine appropriate iteration counts for convergence.

Abstract: We develop a parallel framework that assembles static gradient methods to achieve better adaptivity. A static gradient method, denoted by $\mathrm{GD}(x_0,T)$, takes as input an initial point $x_0\in\mathbb{R}^n$ and $T\in \mathbb{R}^+$ specifying the number $\floor{T}$ of iterations. The step size is chosen as $s=S(T)$, where $S(\cdot)$ is a predetermined function of $T$. The method then performs the iterations $ x_{i+1}=x_i-\fracη{s}\cdot g_i,$ where $g_i$ is a stochastic gradient evaluated at $x_i$, and $η$ is a scaling factor. For an integer $p\ge1$, the $p$ processors in the proposed parallel framework search for an appropriate value of $T$ according to a geometric sequence so that the resulting gradient descent satisfies the desired convergence conditions. Each processor executes an infinite sequence of stages indexed by $i=1,2,\ldots$. At stage $i$, processor $j$ is assigned $ T_{j,i}=h(j,i),$ where $h:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N} \rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}$ is a prescribed function. Processor $j$ $(j=0,1,\ldots,p-1)$ executes $\mathrm{GD}(x_0, T_{j,i})$ at stage $i$.

</details>


### [122] [Reflection or Re-Generation? Why LLM Revision Fails Where Human Revision Succeeds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28908)
*Yefan Tao,Gerald Friedland,Madhusudhanan Chandrasekaran,Luyang Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM "reflection" fundamentally differs from human revision: on objective tasks it behaves as neutral re-generation with near-zero information gain, while on subjective tasks it actively degrades predictions. Cross-agent experiments localize the failure to the revision step itself, not input quality, suggesting LLM reflection is better understood as conditioned re-generation rather than genuine error-driven revision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly prompted to "reflect" on their outputs, yet it remains unclear whether this process genuinely resembles human revision. The paper seeks to systematically characterize and diagnose the failure modes of LLM reflection compared to human revision using a rigorous, controlled framework.

Method: The authors introduce the Human-LLM Reflection Framework (HRF), a controlled two-pass protocol comparing human and LLM revision under identical conditions across self-, peer-, and cross-agent settings. They employ an information-theoretic analysis based on per-iteration cross-entropy reduction (ΔI) to quantify information gain. Diagnostic analyses include conditioning revision on first-pass correctness, oracle-guided revision against a random-reshuffle baseline, and cross-agent experiments where LLMs revise human outputs and vice versa.

Result: Two distinct failure modes emerged: (1) On objective tasks with finite answer spaces, LLM reflection yields near-zero information gain (ΔI ≈ 0), indistinguishable from re-sampling. (2) On subjective tasks, reflection yields significant negative gain (ΔI < 0), moving predictions away from the target. Human revision consistently yields positive gain. Cross-agent experiments localize failure to the revision step itself — LLMs degrade even high-quality human responses. Self-error detection exists on objective MC tasks but is weak on subjective ones, and oracle-guided recovery varies by model.

Conclusion: The structural account is that without external information, self-conditioned revision cannot reduce uncertainty about the target. LLM reflection is better understood as conditioned re-generation rather than genuine error-driven revision, challenging the anthropomorphic framing of LLM "reflection" and suggesting current prompting-based reflection strategies are fundamentally limited.

Abstract: Reflection, the ability to revisit and revise prior reasoning, is central to how humans improve their answers. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly prompted to "reflect," yet whether this resembles human revision remains unclear. We introduce the Human-LLM Reflection Framework (HRF), a controlled two-pass protocol comparing human and LLM revision under identical conditions across self-, peer-, and cross-agent settings. Using an information-theoretic analysis based on per-iteration cross-entropy reduction, we find two failure modes of LLM reflection. On objective tasks with finite answer spaces, reflection yields near-zero information gain (Delta I approx 0), behaving as neutral re-generation indistinguishable from re-sampling. On subjective tasks, it yields significant negative gain (Delta I < 0), moving predictions away from the target. Human revision, by contrast, yields positive gain in both settings. Cross-agent experiments localize the failure to the revision step, not input quality: LLMs degrade even high-quality human responses. Diagnostic analyses (revision conditioned on first-pass correctness, and oracle-guided revision against a random-reshuffle baseline) show that which sub-step dominates varies by task and by model rather than reducing to a single mechanism: self-error detection is present on objective multiple-choice tasks but weak on subjective ones, and recovery under an oracle error signal exceeds the baseline for some models and falls below it for others. The unifying account is structural: without external information, self-conditioned revision cannot reduce uncertainty about the target, so LLM reflection is better understood as conditioned re-generation than as genuine error-driven revision.

</details>


### [123] [Gated Q-learning: Add Off-Policy Bias to Taste](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28916)
*Brett Daley*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gated Q-learning introduces a continuous, state-action-dependent gating mechanism that interpolates between Watkins' Q(λ) (zero bias, short traces) and Peng's Q(λ) (biased, long traces), resolving the 30-year off-policy bias dilemma in multistep Q-learning without importance sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: For three decades, reinforcement learning practitioners have faced a binary choice in multistep Q-learning: Watkins' Q(λ) eliminates off-policy bias by truncating eligibility traces but severely limits credit assignment horizons, while Peng's Q(λ) uses longer traces for faster learning at the cost of injecting detrimental bias. Modern off-policy estimators using importance sampling fail under Q-learning's greedy target policy. A method is needed that can smoothly trade off between these extremes.

Method: The authors propose Gated Q-learning, which employs a continuous, state-action-dependent gating mechanism to selectively attenuate eligibility traces in an exploration-aware manner — unlike importance sampling. The gating factor interpolates smoothly between Watkins' and Peng's Q(λ) extremes. Theoretical analysis proves the expected operator is a contraction mapping and derives its exact fixed point.

Result: Theoretical results confirm the expected Gated Q-learning operator is a contraction mapping with a well-defined fixed point. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that intermediate gating values safely enable longer credit-assignment horizons, yielding faster initial learning compared to either Watkins' or Peng's Q(λ) alone.

Conclusion: Gated Q-learning ends the longstanding binary choice in multistep off-policy Q-learning by providing a simple, importance-sampling-free alternative that allows practitioners to customize the effective multistep horizon and the amount of permissible off-policy bias.

Abstract: Multistep credit assignment is critical for sample-efficient reinforcement learning, yet managing off-policy bias in Q-learning remains a fundamental challenge. For 30 years, practitioners have been limited to a binary choice: eliminate the bias at the cost of severely truncated eligibility traces (Watkins' Q($λ$)), or ignore the bias to learn faster while injecting detrimental errors into the value estimates (Peng's Q($λ$)). Modern off-policy estimators fail to resolve this tension, as importance-sampling ratios collapse under Q-learning's greedy target policy. We introduce Gated Q-learning, a novel algorithmic framework that ends this dilemma by smoothly interpolating between the two historical extremes. Rather than relying on importance sampling, our approach employs a continuous, state-action-dependent gating mechanism to selectively attenuate eligibility traces in an exploration-aware manner. We provide a rigorous theoretical foundation for this mechanism, proving that the expected operator remains a contraction mapping and deriving its exact fixed point. Empirical evaluations verify that intermediate gating safely enables longer credit-assignment horizons, yielding faster initial learning than either extreme. Gated Q-learning offers a simple alternative to importance sampling while enabling customization of the effective multistep horizon and the amount of off-policy bias in Q-learning agents.

</details>


### [124] [Learning Optimal Dynamic Matching via Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28925)
*Genta Okada,Shunya Noda,Junpei Komiyama,Akira Matsushita*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops a reinforcement learning framework for dynamic matching on evolving graphs, proving that optimal decisions can be reduced to a continuation-value function on post-decision residual graphs. Using graph neural networks and temporal-difference learning, the learned policy adaptively balances immediate versus delayed matching, outperforming heuristic baselines in both synthetic and kidney-exchange benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic matching markets (e.g., kidney exchange, ridesharing) face a fundamental timing trade-off: matching now yields immediate value but consumes participants who might enable more valuable future matches. Existing approaches lack principled methods for state-dependent dynamic matching policies that adapt to evolving graph structure and stochastic arrivals/departures.

Method: The authors model the problem as an infinite-horizon continuous-time MDP with stochastic arrivals, node-type transitions, edge realizations, and exogenous exits. They prove an "event-time reduction" showing optimality of acting only at exogenous event times. The key theoretical insight is that the edge-wise Q-function reduces to a single continuation-value function on post-decision residual graphs. This value function is approximated via a graph neural network trained with temporal-difference learning and deployed within a forward-greedy matching heuristic. The framework is evaluated on a binary-type benchmark and a kidney paired donation simulator.

Result: In the binary-type benchmark, the learned policy significantly outperforms immediate-greedy and threshold-greedy rules by strategically preserving common-type nodes for rare valuable arrivals. In the kidney paired donation benchmark, it matches immediate-greedy performance under unpredictable exits, recovers patient-matching logic under reliable exit warnings, and outperforms both baselines across intermediate warning-probability regimes.

Conclusion: Residual-graph value learning enables state-dependent dynamic matching policies that adapt to realized connectivity and exit information. The reduction of the Q-function to a graph-value function makes the approach tractable, and GNN-based function approximation paired with TD learning yields policies that outperform heuristic baselines across diverse information settings.

Abstract: Dynamic matching markets require decisions about whom to match and when: matching now yields value but removes participants who may create better future opportunities. We develop a value-based reinforcement-learning framework for this problem on finite, evolving weighted graphs. We study an infinite-horizon continuous-time model with stochastic arrivals, node-type transitions, edge realizations, and exogenous exits. We prove an event-time reduction: without loss of optimality, the planner acts immediately after each exogenous event and then waits for the next one. We further show that the optimal edge-wise $Q$-function is characterized by a single continuation-value function on post-decision residual graphs, reducing the learned object from state-action values to graph values. Exact action selection still requires combinatorial matching optimization; we approximate the value with a graph neural network, train it by temporal-difference learning, and use it in a forward-greedy matching heuristic. In a binary-type benchmark, the learned policy substantially outperforms immediate and threshold-greedy rules by preserving common nodes for rare arrivals of valuable matches while forming lower-value matches only in thick pools. In a kidney paired donation benchmark, it performs similarly to immediate greedy when exits are unpredictable, recovers the logic of patient matching when warnings are reliable, and outperforms the better of Immediate Greedy and Patient Greedy across intermediate warning probabilities. These results show that residual-graph value learning yields state-dependent dynamic matching policies that adapt to realized connectivity and exit information.

</details>


### [125] [Latent Lie-Poisson Neural Networks (LLPNNs): Discovering the motion of Lie-Poisson systems through observable data and latent dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28939)
*Vakhtang Putkaradze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent Lie–Poisson Neural Networks (LLPNNs) are introduced as a structure-preserving framework for learning Hamiltonian dynamics that admit symmetry reduction to Lie–Poisson systems, directly from observable data (configurations/velocities) even when momentum and co-state variables are unobservable. The method uses a Hamiltonian decoder or pseudo-Lagrangian encoder, a universal Noether invariant, and Magnus-based Lie-group integrators, achieving excellent long-term prediction on rigid body, underwater vehicle, and optimal control examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning Hamiltonian systems from data is hindered when dynamics evolve in unobservable momentum variables while only configurations and velocities are measurable. In optimal control, latent co-states are also unobservable, and degenerate Hamiltonians prevent Lagrangian formulations, making standard encoder-decoder approaches fail. There is a need for a structure-preserving learning framework that can operate directly on observable data for both regular and degenerate Lie–Poisson systems.

Method: The LLPNN framework leverages three geometric ingredients: (1) a Hamiltonian decoder (for regular systems) or a pseudo-Lagrangian encoder (for degenerate systems) that maps between observable and latent spaces on the active variables; (2) a universal Noether invariant from Lie–Poisson symmetry reduction to construct consistent latent trajectories; and (3) Lie–Poisson flows with Magnus-based Lie-group updates for reconstructing both observable and latent dynamics in a structure-preserving manner. This handles both regular and degenerate Hamiltonians without requiring a corresponding Lagrangian.

Result: Numerical experiments on three systems—a generalized rigid body on SO(3), Kirchhoff's underwater vehicle on SE(3), and an optimal-control problem for interacting vehicles on SE(2)^N—demonstrate excellent long-term predictive accuracy, strong robustness to noise, and competitive performance using only modest datasets and lightweight neural network architectures.

Conclusion: LLPNNs provide a principled, geometrically grounded approach for learning Lie–Poisson dynamics from observable data, overcoming the limitations of existing methods when latent variables are unobservable or the Hamiltonian is degenerate. The framework preserves the underlying geometric structure, ensuring reliable long-term predictions across a range of mechanical and control systems.

Abstract: Structure-preserving neural networks are essential for the long-term prediction of Hamiltonian systems from data. Many important Hamiltonian systems in mechanics and control admit symmetry reduction to Lie--Poisson systems, including rigid bodies, underwater vehicles, fluids, plasmas, and optimal control problems. A fundamental challenge in learning such systems is that their dynamics evolve in momentum variables that are typically unobservable, while available data consist only of observable quantities such as configurations and velocities. In optimal control applications, the situation is further complicated because the latent variables contain unobservable co-states and the Hamiltonian may be degenerate, preventing the existence of a corresponding Lagrangian and rendering the encoder-decoder approaches inapplicable.
  We introduce Latent Lie--Poisson Neural Networks (LLPNNs), a structure-preserving framework for learning Lie--Poisson dynamics directly from observable data. The proposed approach exploits three geometric ingredients: (i) learning either a Hamiltonian decoder or a pseudo-Lagrangian encoder on the active variables, (ii) constructing latent trajectories through a universal Noether invariant arising from Lie--Poisson symmetry reduction, and (iii) reconstructing observable and latent dynamics through Lie--Poisson flows combined with Magnus-based Lie-group updates. The resulting method preserves the geometric structure and is applicable to both regular and degenerate Hamiltonian systems.
  We demonstrate the method on three examples: a generalized rigid body on SO(3), Kirchhoff's underwater vehicle on SE(3), and an optimal-control problem for interacting vehicles on $SE(2)^N$. Numerical experiments show excellent long-term predictive accuracy, strong robustness to noise, and competitive performance using only modest datasets and lightweight neural-network architectures.

</details>


### [126] [FairDiffuseVQVAE: Sampling-Time Fairness in Tabular Diffusion via Conditional Refinement of Vector-Quantized Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28945)
*Nitish Nagesh,Mahdi Bagheri,Amir M. Rahmani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FairDiffuseVQVAE is a two-stage architecture for fair synthetic tabular data generation that decouples fidelity from fairness. Stage 1 uses a VQ-VAE with a row-level discriminator (no fairness terms); Stage 2 applies a diffusion refiner conditioned on reconstruction and protected attributes via classifier-free guidance. Fairness is achieved by uniform sampling of protected attributes at inference, enforcing demographic parity by construction rather than via loss penalties, yielding state-of-the-art fairness metrics on Adult, Bank, and COMPAS datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tabular diffusion models (e.g., TabDDPM, TabSyn) achieve high distributional fidelity but lack any fairness mechanism. Conversely, fairness-aware generators (DECAF, FairTGAN, FairTabDDPM) impose explicit fairness penalties during training, which yields only modest fairness improvements at a substantial cost to sample quality or downstream utility. This trade-off motivates the need for an architecture where fairness is not a competing training objective.

Method: FairDiffuseVQVAE is a two-stage architecture. Stage 1: a vector-quantized autoencoder (VQ-VAE) with a row-level discriminator, trained without any fairness terms, learns a discrete latent representation of the tabular data. Stage 2: a DiffuseVAE-style continuous diffusion refiner conditions on both the Stage 1 reconstruction and the protected attribute, using classifier-free guidance. At inference time, demographic parity is enforced by construction through uniform sampling of the protected attribute distribution, rather than through adversarial or penalty-based training losses.

Result: On Adult, Bank, and COMPAS datasets, FairDiffuseVQVAE achieves the highest mean Demographic Parity Ratio (0.702, +47% over FairTabDDPM) and Equalized Odds Ratio (0.686, +100% over FairTabDDPM). It also achieves the lowest mean pair-wise correlation error (0.034) among all published methods. These fairness gains come at the cost of approximately 15 AUC points in downstream utility.

Conclusion: Decoupling fidelity from fairness via a two-stage design—where fairness is a property of the sampling distribution rather than a training objective—enables state-of-the-art fairness metrics without the destructive trade-offs inherent in penalty-based approaches. The architecture demonstrates that uniform protected-attribute sampling at inference is an effective and parsimonious mechanism for achieving demographic parity in synthetic tabular data.

Abstract: Synthetic tabular data is increasingly used in privacy-preserving data sharing, data augmentation, and to mitigate downstream classifier bias. State-of-the-art tabular diffusion models such as TabDDPM and TabSyn achieve excellent distributional fidelity but offer no mechanism for fairness; conversely, fairness-aware tabular generators (DECAF, FairTGAN, FairTabDDPM) impose explicit fairness penalties at training time, yielding modest fairness gains at substantial cost to either sample quality or downstream utility. We introduce FairDiffuseVQVAE, a two-stage architecture that decouples fidelity from fairness: a vector-quantized autoencoder with a row-level discriminator (Stage~1, no fairness terms) is followed by a DiffuseVAE-style continuous diffusion refiner that conditions on both the Stage-1 reconstruction and the protected attribute via classifier-free guidance (Stage~2). Fairness emerges as a property of the sampling distribution -- uniform sampling of the protected attribute at inference time enforces demographic parity by construction, rather than from competing loss terms. On the Adult, Bank and COMPAS datasets, FairDiffuseVQVAE achieves the highest mean Demographic Parity Ratio ($0.702$, $+47\%$ over FairTabDDPM) and Equalized Odds Ratio ($0.686$, $+100\%$). It also attains the lowest mean pair-wise correlation error ($0.034$) of any published method, while explicitly trading $\sim$$15$ AUC points for these fairness gains.

</details>


### [127] [Shapley-Value-Based Feature Attribution for Data Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28946)
*Xinxue,Qu,Francis Bilson Darku,Hong Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Shapley-value-based feature attribution framework is proposed to balance disclosure risk and data utility in data privacy, operating at the feature level and remaining agnostic to masking methods, models, and evaluation metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Widespread access to personal data creates severe privacy concerns. Existing literature primarily addresses the risk-utility tradeoff at the dataset level, leaving a gap for feature-level analysis that can provide more granular control over data masking decisions.

Method: A novel framework adapts Shapley-value-based feature attribution to data privacy, capturing two dimensions — disclosure risk and data utility — and addressing the risk-utility tradeoff at the feature level. The framework is agnostic to data masking methods, statistical/ML methods, and evaluation metrics.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method effectively reduces disclosure risk while preserving data utility.

Conclusion: The Shapley-value-based fair feature attribution approach offers an effective, holistic solution for feature-level privacy-preserving data masking, successfully balancing disclosure risk and data utility across diverse methods and metrics.

Abstract: Despite its many benefits, widespread access to individuals' personal data also causes severe privacy concerns for consumers, companies, and policymakers. This study proposes a novel framework that adapts the Shapley-value-based feature attribution approach to the problem domain of data privacy by capturing the two crucial dimensions of data privacy---disclosure risk and data utility. Our proposed framework takes a holistic view of data masking through a fair feature attribution approach based on Shapley values. Different from the existing literature that mostly focuses on the risk-utility tradeoff at the dataset level, the proposed framework addresses the tradeoff at the feature level. Furthermore, the proposed framework is agnostic to data masking methods, statistical and machine learning methods, and data utility and disclosure risk evaluation metrics. Experimental results show that our proposed method can effectively reduce disclosure risk while preserving data utility.

</details>


### [128] [Overcoming the Weakest-Link Effect in LLM-Driven Program Optimization via Heterogeneous Edit Recombination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28947)
*Jingwen Fu,Zhen Liu,Yuhan Liu,He Zhang,Nanning Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HERO is a zeroth-order LLM-based program optimizer that generates diverse atomic edits and selectively composes them using evaluator scores, overcoming the weakest-link effect in bundle-based edit acceptance. It outperforms prior LLM optimizers across multiple domains while using fewer tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based program optimizers rely on textual "gradients" (first-order update directions) inferred from prior evaluations or LLM feedback. These gradient estimates become unreliable as the program-score mapping grows more complex. The authors argue explicit gradients are unnecessary—LLMs can propose plausible edits directly. However, naive zeroth-order search suffers from the weakest-link effect: accepting/rejecting a bundle of edits as a whole allows one harmful edit to negate all beneficial ones.

Method: HERO prompts an LLM to generate diverse, non-overlapping atomic edits from the current program. It then uses a systematic selection-and-composition mechanism driven by evaluator scores to combine beneficial edits into coherent program improvements, rather than accepting or rejecting the entire edit bundle. This sidesteps the weakest-link effect while retaining the benefits of zeroth-order optimization.

Result: Across algorithmic problems, strategy games, LLM-based agentic system design, and robotic path planning, HERO consistently discovers higher-scoring programs, converges substantially faster than prior LLM-based optimizers, and consumes fewer tokens.

Conclusion: Explicit textual gradients are not essential for effective LLM-driven program optimization. A principled zeroth-order approach with atomic edit decomposition and selective composition (HERO) offers superior performance, faster convergence, and better token efficiency than gradient-based alternatives.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to solve complex problems by searching over program space, offering a general paradigm for scientific problems that can be naturally represented and solved as programs. Despite recent progress, identifying effective optimization directions for a candidate program remains challenging. By analogy with automatic differentiation, existing methods typically guide the search using a textual ``gradient'': a first-order update direction expressed as textual edits. Such gradients are inferred either from previously evaluated programs or from LLM-generated feedback on the implicit program-score mapping. However, these estimates become increasingly unreliable as the program--score mapping grows more complex, limiting their practical utility. We argue that explicit gradients are not essential for effective program optimization. Leveraging their prior knowledge, LLMs can propose plausible atomic edits directly from the current program, thereby enabling a zeroth-order optimization strategy. However, zeroth-order search suffers from a \textit{weakest-link effect}: when a bundle of edits is accepted or rejected as a whole, a single harmful edit can negate the benefits of all remaining edits. To address this issue, we introduce HERO, a program optimizer that prompts an LLM to generate diverse, non-overlapping atomic edits and then systematically selects and composes them into coherent program improvements using evaluator scores. We evaluate HERO across algorithmic problems, strategy games, the design of LLM-based agentic systems, and robotic path planning. Across these domains, HERO consistently discovers higher-scoring programs and converges substantially faster than prior LLM-based optimizers, while consuming fewer tokens.

</details>


### [129] [Mining Verdict Boundaries for Neural Network Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28954)
*Jiawei Ren,Guanqin Zhang,Zhenya Zhang,Yulei Sui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes efficient search strategies for Branch and Bound (BaB) neural network verification that leverage path monotonicity to locate verdict boundaries by splitting multiple ReLU activations simultaneously, using exponential search and quantitative estimation to skip irrelevant subproblems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing BaB approaches naively traverse the search tree depth-by-depth, solving each subproblem sequentially and performing costly bounds propagation at every node, which is inefficient for locating the boundary between verified and unverified subproblems along tree paths.

Method: The authors exploit monotonicity along BaB tree paths to design: (1) an exponential search strategy that simultaneously splits multiple activation functions to rapidly narrow down the verdict boundary, skipping boundary-unrelated subproblems; and (2) an enhanced version that estimates the boundary's position using quantitative feedback from subproblem solving to further accelerate the search.

Result: Experimental evaluation on commonly-used benchmarks demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed search techniques compared to recent BaB-based verification approaches.

Conclusion: Exploiting path monotonicity and splitting multiple activations simultaneously enables significantly more efficient identification of verdict boundaries in BaB-based complete verification, reducing wasted computation on irrelevant subproblems.

Abstract: Branch and Bound (BaB) aims to achieve complete verification of neural networks by adaptively partitioning the problem and applying off-the-shelf verifiers to subproblems. Its problem-splitting history can be represented as a tree, where each subproblem corresponds to a child node. A key problem of BaB lies in searching for the verdict boundaries across all the paths that divide the verified and unverified subproblems. We observe that the existing BaB approach tackles this problem by solving each expensive subproblem sequentially along the tree path as its depth increases, requiring costly bounds propagation at every visited BaB tree node (i.e., subproblem), which is inefficient. To address this issue, we propose effective search approaches that leverage the monotonicity of each path to efficiently and precisely locate the verdict boundary by simultaneously splitting multiple activation functions (e.g., ReLU), rather than processing them one at a time as in the classical approach. Our approach performs an effective exponential search along each path, allowing us to skip many boundary-unrelated subproblems when identifying the verdict boundary. The enhanced version further improves this process by estimating the boundary's position using quantitative information obtained from subproblem solving. We perform experimental evaluation on commonly-used benchmarks to assess our proposed techniques, and compare them with recent BaB-based approaches.

</details>


### [130] [Exploring Block Anomaly Detection In HDFS Log Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29383)
*WenYang Zhong,Tutut Herawan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a streaming anomaly detection workflow for HDFS logs using a hybrid LLM-BiLSTM deep learning model combined with a Kafka-based streaming pipeline to detect block anomalies in real time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HDFS generates massive volumes of unstructured, unstable logs. Manual, log-by-log inspection for anomaly detection is tedious and inefficient for system operators, creating a need for automated, real-time anomaly detection using machine learning and NLP techniques.

Method: The authors propose a streaming HDFS log block anomaly detection workflow that (1) uses a parallel computing network to process historical logs, (2) constructs an LLM-BiLSTM hybrid deep learning model for detecting block anomalies, and (3) builds a real-time streaming log pipeline based on Apache Kafka for online anomaly detection.

Result: Not specified in the abstract.

Conclusion: Not explicitly stated in the abstract; the paper presents a solution that enables maintenance practitioners to rapidly and accurately locate and fix HDFS block anomalies through a combination of parallel log processing, hybrid deep learning, and streaming infrastructure.

Abstract: In recent years, with the development of big data technology, increasingly more companies use HDFS for data processing and storage. As a result, the maintenance of distributed file systems has become an extremely important part of data management. As the function of server systems is becoming increasingly diversified and their services are becoming complex, the logs, recording real-time events make it easier for system operators to locate the failures and errors that happened in the server systems to make server always available. HDFS, a distributed file system, which contains large data sets, will record a large number of logs. Moreover, the logs are not always structured data, they are not stable as well. However, to detect the problems that occur in the system by checking one log by one log, it's complicated and boring work for the system operators. Using machine learning techniques and natural language processing techniques to detect the HDFS block anomaly will help the system operators to locate and fix the anomaly rapidly and accurately. This paper proposes a streaming HDFS log block anomaly workflow. It helps maintenance practitioners to use parallel computing network in processing historical log, and construct LLM-BiLSTM hybrid deep learning model to detect anomaly block in HDFS, then build streaming log pipeline based on Kafka to give one real-time HDFS log block anomaly detection solution.

</details>


### [131] [Efficient LLM Adversarial Training via Low-Rank Defense and Circuit-Guided Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28959)
*Weiyi He,Yuping Lin,Jiliang Tang,Yue Xing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates computation-efficient strategies to accelerate Latent Adversarial Training (LAT) for LLMs from two angles: (1) combining ReFT with LAT while addressing token-mismatch issues, and (2) using circuit-extracted lightweight surrogate models for attack generation. The proposed method reduces per-step FLOPs by 48.1% with only 0.0118% trainable parameters compared to standard LAT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarial training is highly effective for robustness but computationally prohibitive at LLM scale. Even existing acceleration methods like LAT still impose substantial computational overhead, motivating the need for further efficiency improvements.

Method: A dual-perspective optimization: (1) Defense-side — integrates Representation Fine-Tuning (ReFT) into LAT and addresses the token-mismatch problem between where ReFT is applied and where attacks occur. (2) Attack-side — constructs a lightweight surrogate model by extracting only the relevant circuits from the full LLM, avoiding full forward-backward passes during adversarial perturbation generation. Both strategies are supported by theoretical justification and numerical evidence.

Result: Compared to standard LAT with full fine-tuning, the proposed method achieves an average 48.1% reduction in per-step adversarial-training FLOPs while using only 0.0118% of trainable parameters.

Conclusion: The proposed computation-efficient strategies — combining ReFT-based defense optimization with circuit-extracted surrogate attack models — significantly reduce the computational burden of adversarial training for LLMs without compromising effectiveness, making robust training more practical at modern scales.

Abstract: Adversarial training is one of the most effective defenses against adversarial attacks, yet the computational cost remains prohibitive at modern scales, especially for large language models (LLMs). While existing mitigation strategies, e.g., latent adversarial training (LAT), have been developed, they still incur a high computational cost. In this work, we comprehensively investigate computation-efficient strategies to speed up LAT from two complementary perspectives: (1) Defense-side optimization: We explore the representation fine-tuning (ReFT) within LAT, and reveal a potential issue if there is a mismatch on which tokens to apply ReFT and the attack. (2) Attack-side optimization: When computing adversarial attacks in each LAT iteration, we extract only the relevant circuits from the LLM to construct a lightweight surrogate model, avoiding the computation in the forward-backward passes through the full model during the attack generation. For both perspectives, we provide theoretical justifications and numerical evidence to illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed strategies. Ultimately, compared to standard LAT with full fine-tuning, our method on average reduces per-step adversarial-training FLOPs by 48.1% while requiring only 0.0118% trainable parameters.

</details>


### [132] [Simulation Code Generation for Fluid Systems using Large Language Models: Benchmarking Models and Prompting Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29389)
*Jan Marius Stürmer,Jascha Knack,Tobias Koch,Andreas Weinmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study investigates using LLMs to translate graph-based fluid system model representations into executable simulation code (Python/WNTR and Modelica), systematically comparing 10 LLMs and 6 prompting strategies. While syntactic code quality is acceptable in best configurations, significant gaps remain in simulation fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how LLMs can be leveraged for automated translation of neutral graph representations of fluid system models into executable simulation code, addressing the gap between natural-language-to-code capabilities and domain-specific simulation environments.

Method: Systematic comparison of 10 state-of-the-art LLMs combined with 6 prompting strategies varying in contextual information (code snippets, documentation). Evaluation uses software-quality metrics for syntactic correctness and benchmark fluid system scenario reproduction for functional/simulation fidelity validation.

Result: Best-performing LLM-prompt configurations achieve acceptable syntactic code quality; however, substantial gaps persist in simulation fidelity when reproducing benchmark fluid system scenarios.

Conclusion: LLM-driven code synthesis shows promise for model-based design pipelines but is not yet reliable for simulation-critical applications. The study provides concrete guidance for researchers and engineers on current capabilities and limitations.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated a strong ability to generate syntactically correct code from natural-language specifications. In this study, we explore how LLMs can be harnessed to automatically translate a neutral graph representation of fluid system models into executable code for two widely adopted simulation environments: the Python library WNTR and the Modelica Standard Library. We conduct a systematic comparison of ten state-of-the-art LLMs and six prompting strategies that differ in the contextual information supplied (e.g., code or documentation). For each configuration we assess the generated code using a suite of software-quality metrics and we validate the functional fidelity of the resulting simulation models by reproducing benchmark fluid system scenarios. Our findings offer concrete guidance for researchers and engineers seeking to integrate LLM-driven code synthesis into model-based design pipelines. While the best-performing configurations achieve acceptable syntactic quality, we observe substantial gaps remain in simulation fidelity.

</details>


### [133] [Beyond Feature and Structure Alignment: Learning Transferable Propagation Knowledge for Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28980)
*Yi Wang,Jitao Zhao,Di Jin,Dongxiao He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProGFM proposes a graph foundation model that treats propagation relationships between edges and feature dimensions as transferable knowledge units, using a prototype bank to enable adaptive cross-domain generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GFMs focus on feature/structure alignment but overlook (1) identifying fundamental transferable knowledge units in graphs (unlike tokens in language or patches in vision), and (2) heterogeneity in propagation patterns where different edges exhibit distinct propagation behaviors across feature dimensions.

Method: ProGFM introduces propagation relationships between edges and feature dimensions as transferable knowledge units, and builds a propagation relationship prototype bank to learn cross-domain transferable propagation knowledge, enabling adaptive information aggregation on unseen graph domains.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse cross-domain transfer scenarios show ProGFM achieves strong cross-domain knowledge transfer capability and superior generalization performance compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: ProGFM effectively addresses the key limitations of prior GFMs by identifying propagation relationships as the core transferable units and employing a prototype bank for adaptive propagation, yielding state-of-the-art cross-domain generalization.

Abstract: Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for enabling knowledge transfer across diverse domains. Unlike traditional graph learning methods that are typically designed for in-domain settings, GFMs aim to learn transferable knowledge that can generalize to unseen graph domains. However, unlike language or visual data, graphs lack intrinsic and unified representation units, such as tokens in language and patches in vision, making it challenging to identify transferable knowledge units for building graph foundation models. Existing graph foundation models mainly focus on mitigating domain discrepancies through feature alignment and structure alignment, while overlooking the exploration of transferable knowledge units underlying graph data. Moreover, these methods generally rely on fixed propagation mechanisms during message passing, overlooking the heterogeneity in propagation patterns, as different edges may exhibit distinct propagation patterns for different feature dimensions. To address these limitations, we propose a Propagation-aware Graph Foundation Model (ProGFM), which regards the propagation relationships between edges and feature dimensions as transferable knowledge units. Through a propagation relationship prototype bank, ProGFM learns cross-domain transferable propagation knowledge, enabling adaptive information aggregation in unseen graph domains. Extensive experiments across various cross-domain transfer scenarios demonstrate that ProGFM possesses strong cross-domain knowledge transfer capability and exhibits superior generalization performance compared with existing methods.

</details>


### [134] [SILVA Networks as Structured Implicit Layers and Vector Attractors via Dynamic Interaction Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28989)
*Jose Luis Lima de Jesus Silva*

Main category: cs.LG
