TL;DR: SILVA Networks introduces a structured implicit layer architecture that cleanly separates stimulus, local interaction, global interaction, damping, and readout components within a fixed-point framework, enabling trainable, ablatable, and diagnosable internal dynamics across diverse domains including images, molecules, citation networks, and long-range graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard implicit neural layers conflate multiple information sources (direct input, local structure, global context, and solver dynamics) into a single fixed-point update, making it impossible to disentangle and analyze what drives the learned representation. There is a need for an architecture that makes these distinct influences explicit and independently manipulable.

Method: The authors propose SILVA (Structured Implicit Layers and Vector Attractors via Dynamic Interaction Fields), which factorizes the fixed-point computation into five explicit terms: stimulus (direct input), local interaction (neighborhood structure), global interaction (broader context), damping, and readout. The template is domain-agnostic and instantiated across images, molecules, citation networks, and long-range graph benchmarks by defining domain-specific notions of nodes, neighborhoods, and global summaries.

Result: Ablation experiments reveal task-dependent roles for each term: local interactions are critical for graph tasks; MNIST benefits little from recurrence at the tested model capacity; and global interaction yields the clearest gains on a long-range node-classification benchmark.

Conclusion: SILVA provides a principled implicit representation whose internal interaction dynamics can be trained, ablated, visualized, and diagnosed. Explicitly separating interaction types enables both improved interpretability and task-specific optimization of the fixed-point computation.

Abstract: Many learning problems require representations that reconcile direct input, nearby structure, and broader context. In implicit neural layers, these influences are usually absorbed into a single fixed-point update, making it hard to identify what enters from the stimulus, what propagates locally, what comes from global context, and what is produced by solver dynamics. Here we introduce SILVA Networks, Structured Implicit Layers and Vector Attractors via Dynamic Interaction Fields. SILVA separates stimulus, local interaction, global interaction, damping, and readout inside one fixed-point architecture. The same template is instantiated for images, molecules, citation networks, and long-range graph benchmarks through domain-specific definitions of nodes, neighborhoods, and global summaries. Experiments and ablations show task-dependent roles for these terms: local interactions are load-bearing in the graph tasks, MNIST gains little from recurrence at the tested capacity, and the clearest global benefit appears in a long-range node-classification benchmark. SILVA therefore provides an implicit representation whose internal interaction dynamics can be trained, ablated, visualized, and diagnosed.

</details>


### [135] [Dynamics-aware identification of governing equations from sparse and noisy data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29036)
*Pongpisit Thanasutives,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Koopman-based upsampling (DMD, EDMD, optimized DMD) is evaluated as a preprocessing step for SINDy/PDE-FIND to improve derivative estimation from sparse, noisy temporal data. Polynomial EDMD excels on ODEs; DMD helps specific PDEs; overall, dynamics-aware upsampling reduces derivative error and stabilizes sparse regression when observables and low-rank structure align with the data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SINDy and PDE-FIND recover parsimonious governing equations from data, but sparse and noisy temporal measurements degrade derivative estimates, undermining equation discovery. Existing interpolation methods lack awareness of the underlying dynamics, motivating a dynamics-aware preprocessing approach.

Method: The authors evaluate Koopman-based upsampling using DMD, extended DMD (EDMD), and optimized DMD to learn finite-dimensional Koopman operator approximations on selected observables. These learned operators interpolate and denoise snapshots within the observed time window prior to derivative estimation and sparse regression. The approach is compared against linear and smoothing-spline interpolation baselines. Pareto-based non-oracle support-size selection is also tested with DMD-assisted upsampling.

Result: Polynomial EDMD yields the strongest ODE results, particularly in coefficient accuracy. For PDEs, low-rank DMD improves Burgers and advection-diffusion discovery, while the raw baseline remains competitive on Fisher-KPP. Koopman-based preprocessors outperform non-dynamical linear and spline interpolation overall. DMD-assisted upsampling also stabilizes Pareto-based support-size selection.

Conclusion: Koopman-based upsampling is a dynamics-aware preprocessing step that reduces derivative-estimation error when the chosen observable representation and low-rank structure suit the data. Its effectiveness varies by system, and it should be viewed as a beneficial but not universally superior complement to sparse equation discovery pipelines.

Abstract: Sparse identification of nonlinear dynamics (SINDy) and PDE functional identification (PDE-FIND) recover parsimonious ordinary and partial differential equations (ODEs and PDEs) from data. However, sparse and noisy temporal measurements can make derivative estimates unreliable. To address this problem, we evaluate Koopman-based upsampling techniques implemented with dynamic mode decomposition (DMD), extended DMD (EDMD), and optimized DMD. These methods learn finite-dimensional approximations of Koopman evolution on selected observables and are used to interpolate and denoise snapshots inside the observed time window before derivative estimation and sparse regression. The empirical benchmark comprises two ODE systems, Lorenz-63 and Van der Pol, and three periodic PDE systems, Burgers, Fisher-Kolmogorov-Petrovskii-Piskunov (Fisher-KPP), and linear advection-diffusion, over sparse and noisy sampling regimes. Polynomial EDMD gives the strongest ODE results, especially in coefficient accuracy. The PDE results are system-dependent: low-rank DMD-assisted reconstructions improve Burgers and advection-diffusion discovery, while the raw baseline (without upsampling) remains competitive for the Fisher-KPP data. A comparison against linear and smoothing-spline interpolation techniques shows that the selected Koopman-based preprocessors provide overall performance gains over these non-dynamical alternatives. We also demonstrate that DMD-assisted upsampling can stabilize Pareto-based non-oracle support-size selection. Overall, Koopman-based upsampling is best viewed as a dynamics-aware preprocessing step that can reduce derivative-estimation error when its observable representation and low-rank structure are appropriate for the data.

</details>


### [136] [DFSC: Error-Controlled Differentiable Mittag-Leffler Propagation for Fractional Scientific Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29038)
*Ning Hu,Haitao Duan,Shuqun Li,Chuyang Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DFSC is a PyTorch framework centered on the Mittag-Leffler Spectral Layer (MLSL) that separates known fractional propagators from data-driven neural corrections, enabling efficient differentiable fractional scientific machine learning with adaptive truncation, certified error bounds, and GPU-accelerated batched computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fractional scientific ML approaches wastefully relearn known Mittag-Leffler propagation from data or repeatedly reconstruct it with history solvers. There is a need for a differentiable framework that separates known linear fractional dynamics from unknown residuals, optimizing both jointly while providing error awareness.

Method: The MLSL separates known Mittag-Leffler fractional propagation (via Lanczos-based matrix function evaluation) from data-driven neural corrections. An adaptive algorithm increases special-function truncation depth or Lanczos dimension until successive evaluations meet a tolerance. In the negative-real alternating-series regime, a certified first-omitted-term bound is returned; otherwise, estimates are labeled empirical. The framework supports dense, sparse, matrix-free, self-adjoint, generalized, and complex operator paths with trainable fractional orders.

Result: The certified series bound covers all 59 eligible reference cases with median bound/error effectivity of 1.246. Reusing a prepared batched Lanczos basis yields identical fixed-path values and reduces repeated-query time by 4.61–7.11× on CPU and 13.07–16.22× on an RTX 5070. A 27-case inverse problem finds full-rank local curvature throughout. External solver and mixed real-data results validate the approach as an error-aware primitive for matched fractional structure.

Conclusion: DFSC with MLSL provides an efficient, error-aware primitive for problems with known fractional structure, enabling joint optimization of fractional orders and neural residuals. It is not a general replacement for fractional solvers or neural models but excels when the dominant linear fractional evolution operator is known.

Abstract: Fractional scientific machine learning requires numerical operators that can be differentiated, batched, accelerated, and composed with neural networks. When the dominant linear fractional evolution is known through a Mittag-Leffler propagator, repeatedly reconstructing that response with a history solver or relearning it from data is unnecessary. We present DFSC, a PyTorch environment organized around the Mittag-Leffler Spectral Layer (MLSL). The layer separates known fractional propagation from data-driven corrections, so neural modules learn only unresolved dynamics while fractional orders and residual-network parameters are optimized jointly. Its adaptive algorithm increases special-function truncation depth or Lanczos dimension until successive differentiable evaluations satisfy a requested tolerance. In the negative-real alternating-series regime, DFSC additionally returns a certified first-omitted-term bound; outside that regime it explicitly labels estimates as empirical.
  DFSC supports dense, sparse, matrix-free, self-adjoint, generalized, and controlled complex operator paths; trainable fractional orders; direct inverse problems; residual neural composition; and CPU/GPU execution. The certified series bound covers all 59 eligible reference cases, with median bound/error effectivity 1.246 for resolved errors. Reusing a prepared batched Lanczos basis gives identical fixed-path values and reduces repeated-query time by 4.61--7.11 times on CPU and 13.07--16.22 times on an RTX 5070, excluding one-time preparation. A 27-case inverse matrix finds full-rank local curvature throughout, while remaining explicitly model-conditional. External solver and mixed real-data results support DFSC as an error-aware optional primitive for matched fractional structure, rather than a general replacement for fractional solvers or neural models.

</details>


### [137] [Learning Lookahead Lemmas for Neural Network Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29051)
*Liam Davis,Haoze Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a lookahead-based inprocessing framework for neural network verification that derives lemmas from unstable ReLU phases, builds an implication graph, and uses it for search space pruning and cut vivification, improving both Marabou and α-β-CROWN verifiers with up to 34% more unsatisfiable instances proved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing branch-and-bound based neural network verifiers lack effective inprocessing mechanisms to dynamically derive and exploit lemmas during the solving process. The authors aim to fill this gap by adapting the lookahead technique from SAT solving to the neural network verification domain.

Method: The authors propose an inprocessing framework centered on lookahead: for unstable ReLU neurons, lookahead tentatively assigns phase values (active/inactive) to assess their implications. Derived lemmas are encoded into an implication graph, which enables two key operations — pruning the search space by detecting conflicting assignments early, and vivifying boolean cuts to strengthen the branching decisions.

Result: The framework was instantiated in two state-of-the-art verifiers, Marabou and α-β-CROWN. Experimental results show performance improvements in both tools, with up to 34% more unsatisfiable instances successfully proved compared to baseline configurations without the lookahead inprocessing.

Conclusion: Lookahead-driven inprocessing is an effective and general technique for neural network verification, portable across different solver architectures (Marabou's SMT-based approach and α-β-CROWN's bound-propagation approach), and yields significant gains on hard unsatisfiable instances.

Abstract: State-of-the-art neural network verifiers use the branch-and-bound procedure as their core solving mechanism. We introduce an inprocessing framework for neural network verification driven by the lookahead procedure. Under this framework, lookahead derives new lemmas over the phases of unstable ReLUs, which are collected into an implication graph that is used to prune the search space and vivify boolean cuts. We instantiate the framework in two state-of-the-art verifiers, Marabou and $α$-$β$-CROWN, and demonstrate that it improves performance in both, proving up to 34% more instances unsatisfiable.

</details>


### [138] [Who Wins Where? Conformal Model Comparison for Local Superiority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29053)
*Yi Zhou,Baishi Li,Xuan Yao,Ke-Wei Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a conformal inference framework for localized model comparison that produces calibrated maps showing which model is best at each covariate point, controlling erroneous declarations with finite-sample guarantees and abstaining under uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global model comparison (aggregating losses across the entire covariate space) can mask heterogeneous performance where different models excel in different regions. Existing approaches typically declare a single winner, ignoring spatial variation in model superiority.

Method: A split-sample framework using three disjoint data splits: (1) fit competing models, (2) estimate local centers and scales from out-of-sample score differences (e.g., squared-loss differences), and (3) conformally calibrate residual uncertainty. At a target point, a one-sided conformal bound is constructed; a local winner is declared only when the bound excludes a tie (zero difference), with sign determining the favored model. Otherwise, the method abstains.

Result: Proves finite-sample marginal control for one-sided erroneous declarations on the realized future comparison score. Establishes pointwise consistency of the localized mean-score estimator away from tie boundaries. Shows aggregate comparison can sharply disagree with local superiority prevalence. Derives a squared-loss bias-variance decomposition clarifying how model structure affects local wins. Synthetic and real-data experiments demonstrate recovery of heterogeneous winner regions, appropriate abstention under uncertainty, and higher conditional gain versus global selection.

Conclusion: The conformalized local model comparison framework provides a principled, distribution-free method for identifying which model performs best at each covariate point, with rigorous finite-sample error control and practical utility demonstrated through both theory and experiments.

Abstract: Standard model comparison is global, aggregating losses across the covariate space to declare a single winner. This can obscure heterogeneous performance, where different models are preferable in different regions. We introduce conformalized local model comparison, a split-sample framework for constructing calibrated local best-model maps. Given a model comparison score, such as the difference between two squared losses, the method uses three disjoint splits to fit competing models, estimate local centers and scales from out-of-sample scores, and conformally calibrate residual uncertainty. At a target point, the procedure declares a local winner only when a one-sided conformal bound excludes a tie, with the score's sign determining the favored model. We prove finite-sample marginal control for one-sided erroneous declarations on the realized future comparison score, establish pointwise consistency of the localized mean-score estimator away from tie boundaries, show that aggregate comparison can disagree sharply with the prevalence of local superiority, and derive a squared-loss bias--variance decomposition that clarifies how model structure affects local wins. Synthetic and real-data experiments show that the method recovers heterogeneous winner regions, abstains under uncertainty, and yields higher conditional gain than global selection.

</details>


### [139] [Benchmarking Frontier Large Language Models Against Official Crash Database Coding Using Police Crash Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29064)
*Sudhir Bharati,Rajendra K C Khatri,Sudip Bharati*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study benchmarked six frontier LLMs on coding six crash attributes from Arkansas fatal-crash narratives, comparing their performance against official structured data. GPT-5.5 High performed best among LLMs, but simple baselines (always-majority, keyword-rule) matched or exceeded LLM performance on several metrics. Crash attribute differences outweighed model differences, and the authors recommend attribute-specific evaluation with transparent baselines before deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Police crash narratives contain rich supplemental information, but manual coding is resource-intensive. It remains unclear whether LLMs can reliably reproduce official crash coding, and existing evaluations lack systematic benchmarking against transparent baselines across multiple attributes and models.

Method: 5,587 fatal-crash narratives were linked with 5,889 structured records from Arkansas (2015–2025), yielding 4,194 matched crashes. Six frontier LLMs were evaluated via identical zero-shot prompts to code six attributes: crash manner, non-motorist relation, intersection type, work-zone relation, roadway surface condition, and light condition. Performance was measured using raw agreement, macro-averaged F1, Cohen's kappa, coverage, and selective agreement, benchmarked against always-majority, always-Unknown, and keyword-rule baselines. Repeated-measures analyses and generalized estimating equations (GEE) assessed differences across models and attributes.

Result: GPT-5.5 High achieved the highest agreement among LLMs, but the always-majority baseline produced higher raw agreement, and the keyword-rule baseline yielded comparable macro-F1 and kappa to the best LLM. Agreement was highest for non-motorist relation and crash manner; lowest for light condition, roadway surface condition, and work-zone relation. Differences across attributes exceeded differences across models.

Conclusion: LLM-based crash coding shows promise but is not uniformly reliable. Simple baselines can rival or surpass LLM performance. Deployment decisions should be made on an attribute-specific basis using transparent baselines and should incorporate human review rather than assuming LLM superiority.

Abstract: Police crash narratives contain information that may supplement structured crash databases, but manual review is labor-intensive and it remains unclear how well large language models (LLMs) reproduce official crash coding. This study benchmarked six frontier LLMs by comparing narrative-derived crash attribute codes with corresponding fields in the Arkansas fatal-crash database. The analysis linked 5,587 fatal-crash narratives with 5,889 structured crash records from Arkansas (2015-2025), yielding 4,194 matched crashes. Six LLMs were evaluated using an identical zero-shot prompt to code crash manner, non-motorist relation, intersection type, work-zone relation, roadway surface condition, and light condition. Performance was evaluated using agreement, macro-averaged F1 score, Cohen's kappa, coverage, selective agreement, and comparisons with always-majority, always-Unknown, and keyword-rule baselines. Repeated-measures analyses and a generalized estimating equations model assessed differences among models and attributes. GPT-5.5 High achieved the highest agreement among the evaluated LLMs, but the always-majority baseline produced higher raw agreement and the keyword-rule baseline achieved macro-averaged F1 score and Cohen's kappa comparable to the best-performing LLM. Agreement was highest for non-motorist relation and crash manner and lowest for light condition, roadway surface condition, and work-zone relation. Differences across crash attributes exceeded differences across models. These results provide a benchmark for evaluating LLM-based crash coding and show that deployment should be evaluated on an attribute-specific basis using transparent baselines and human review.

</details>


### [140] [Federated Foundation Models Fine-Tuning with Heterogeneous Compressed Clients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29071)
*Shengkun Zhu,Jinshan Zeng,Zhihua Allen-Zhao,Mayi Xu,Quanqing Xu,Wei Ren,Qiang Yang,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedSLM addresses the resource asymmetry in federated learning of large foundation models by using SVD-based low-rank decomposition to create compressed, self-contained client models. It employs a two-stage aggregation protocol (within compression groups and across groups via subspace alignment) followed by a weak-to-strong knowledge elicitation step, achieving strong performance while roughly halving client GPU memory requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In federated learning of foundation models, resource-rich institutions can host large models, while those with the most valuable domain-specific data often cannot. Existing solutions (parameter-efficient tuning, pruning, knowledge distillation) each sacrifice one of three critical properties: full-model memory reduction, architectural self-containedness, or representational fidelity. No prior approach simultaneously satisfies all three.

Method: FedSLM introduces a parameter-centric framework with three key components: (1) SVD-based decomposition producing compressed, self-contained client models whose low-rank subspaces form nested manifolds structurally compatible for aggregation; (2) a two-stage protocol synchronizing lightweight adapters within compression groups and fusing full-rank reconstructions across groups via structural alignment; and (3) a weak-to-strong elicitation step using auxiliary confidence loss to transfer aggregated knowledge to the full-scale server, with an explicit bias-variance trade-off to mitigate compression artifacts.

Result: FedSLM outperforms existing federated baselines on both natural language and vision-language benchmarks under IID and non-IID data partitions, while reducing client GPU memory usage to approximately 50% of the full model requirement.

Conclusion: FedSLM resolves the foundational resource-asymmetry tension in federated fine-tuning by simultaneously achieving full-model memory reduction, architectural self-containedness, and representational fidelity. Theoretical guarantees are provided for adapter-level aggregation, subspace-alignment bounds for cross-group fusion, and confidence loss mitigation of weak-supervision noise.

Abstract: Federated learning of foundation models faces a fundamental resource-asymmetry challenge: the institutions holding the most valuable domain-specific data cannot host billion-parameter models. Existing heterogeneous federated approaches attempt to bridge this gap through parameter-efficient tuning, model pruning, or knowledge distillation, yet each trades away a critical property, whether full-model memory reduction, architectural self-containedness, or representational fidelity, leaving the core tension unresolved. We propose FedSLM, a parameter-centric framework for federated fine-tuning with heterogeneous compressed clients. FedSLM uses SVD-based decomposition to produce self-contained client models, whose low-rank subspaces form nested manifolds that are structurally compatible for aggregation. It then applies a two-stage protocol that synchronizes lightweight adapters within compression groups and fuses full-rank reconstructions across groups via structural alignment. Finally, a weak-to-strong elicitation step with auxiliary confidence loss transfers the aggregated knowledge to the full-scale server, while an explicit bias--variance trade-off mitigates compression artifacts. We provide theoretical guarantees for adapter-level aggregation, subspace-alignment bounds for cross-group fusion, and a characterization of how the confidence loss mitigates weak-supervision noise. Experiments on natural language and vision--language benchmarks show that FedSLM outperforms existing federated baselines under both IID and non-IID partitions, while client models operate at roughly 50% of the GPU memory required by the full model.

</details>


### [141] [DASH-OPD: Discrepancy-Aware Switching with Hysteresis for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29078)
*Yuchen Xia,Qianguo Sun,Chao Song,Junlong Wu,Yiyan Qi,Yunjian Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DASH-OPD proposes a discrepancy-aware, hysteresis-based switching mechanism for on-policy distillation in multi-turn agent tasks, adaptively alternating between student and teacher executors based on accumulated drift and recovery signals to mitigate exposure bias while maintaining training stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-turn agent scenarios, standard on-policy distillation suffers from exposure bias: early student errors can derail trajectories into regions the teacher has not covered. Existing curriculum learning methods adjust teacher support based only on training progress, lacking the ability to detect when support is genuinely needed on a per-turn basis.

Method: DASH-OPD computes a mean log-probability ratio between student and teacher over action tokens as a discrepancy measure on each turn. Student-to-teacher ratios yield drift signals; teacher-to-student ratios yield recovery signals. These signals are normalized and accumulated over multiple turns into drift and recovery evidence. Switching between executors occurs when accumulated evidence exceeds a threshold, introducing hysteresis to prevent high-frequency switching from transient noise.

Result: On the ALFWorld benchmark, DASH-OPD outperforms all baselines and demonstrates superior training and deployment efficiency.

Conclusion: DASH-OPD offers an adaptive, bidirectional switching strategy that effectively addresses exposure bias in multi-turn agent OPD by detecting when teacher intervention is needed, with hysteresis ensuring stable switching behavior. The work is in progress, with code and models to be released.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains student models on their own rollouts to reduce exposure bias. However, in multi-turn agent scenarios, early student errors can lead a trajectory away from the teacher's familiar domain. Existing curriculum learning methods regulate how much teacher support is used according to training progress, but cannot determine when it is needed. In light of this, we propose DASH-OPD, Discrepancy-Aware Switching with Hysteresis for OPD, a new agentic OPD method that can switch executors adaptively and bidirectionally. On each turn, DASH-OPD calculates a mean log-probability ratio between the two executors over action tokens as their discrepancy. Student-to-teacher ratios on student turns form drift signals, while teacher-to-student ratios on teacher turns form recovery signals. These signals are normalized and accumulated over multiple turns into drift and recovery evidence. DASH-OPD switches executors when the evidence exceeds its corresponding switching threshold. This multi turn accumulation makes the switching hysteretic, preventing high-frequency switches caused by transient fluctuations. On ALFWorld, DASH-OPD outperforms all the baselines and demonstrates superior training and deployment efficiency. This paper is a work in progress. Code, training logs, and model checkpoints will be released later.

</details>


### [142] [What Is Missing in Surgical Risk Stratification and Outcome Prediction: A Scoping Review of End-to-End Machine Learning Approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29090)
*Yizhi Dong,Yuhe Ke,Hairil Rizal Abdullah,Yucheng Xing,Kevan Kai Bing Teo,Ling Huang,Mengling Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scoping review of 190 studies examining machine learning pipelines for surgical risk stratification using EHR data, revealing widespread methodological gaps including limited open-access data, incomplete preprocessing reporting, scarce deep learning and multimodal approaches, absent benchmarking standards, and low adoption of explainability methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Postoperative adverse events remain a major global burden, and many are preventable through early risk identification. While ML offers promise for leveraging large-scale EHR data to model complex clinical patterns, existing studies vary widely in design with fragmented methodological practices, necessitating a systematic characterization of current ML pipelines.

Method: Scoping review of 190 studies covering the complete ML workflow: data preprocessing, algorithm selection, model evaluation, and explainability in the context of surgical risk stratification using EHR data.

Result: Most studies used single-center private datasets with limited data modalities; open-access surgical datasets were scarce. Reporting of missing data handling, feature selection, and class imbalance was often incomplete. Conventional ML and simple neural networks dominated; deep learning and multimodal approaches were rare. Benchmark datasets and standardized evaluation protocols were largely absent. Only ~1/3 of studies included explainability methods.

Conclusion: Significant methodological gaps limit the development of clinically robust postoperative ML tools. The review provides a structured reference to guide more rigorous, reproducible, and clinically meaningful ML development for perioperative care.

Abstract: Postoperative adverse events, including mortality and morbidity, remain a major global burden, many of which are preventable through early identification of high-risk patients and targeted perioperative care. Accurate risk stratification is therefore essential. With the growing availability of large-scale electronic health records (EHRs), machine learning (ML) provides a data-driven approach to model complex clinical patterns. However, existing studies vary widely in design, and methodological practices remain fragmented. This scoping review characterizes ML pipelines for surgical risk stratification and outcome prediction using EHR data. We reviewed 190 studies covering the ML workflow, including data preprocessing, algorithm selection, model evaluation, and explainability. Most studies relied on single-center private datasets with limited data modalities, while the scarcity of open-access surgical datasets constrained reproducibility and generalizability. Reporting of key preprocessing steps, including missing data handling, feature selection, and class imbalance, was often incomplete. Conventional ML models and simple neural networks predominated, whereas deep learning and multimodal approaches remained uncommon. Benchmark datasets and standardized evaluation protocols were largely absent, hindering cross-study comparisons. Only about one-third of studies incorporated explainability methods. This review identifies methodological gaps limiting clinically robust postoperative ML tools and provides a structured reference to support more rigorous, reproducible, and clinically meaningful ML development for perioperative care.

</details>


### [143] [PiDDM: Physics-Informed Differentiable Degradation Modeling for Lithium-Ion Battery State-of-Health Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29095)
*Zeping Chen,Ruda Jian,Sachin Sigdel,Guoping Xiong,Jian-Xun Wang,Tengfei Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PiDDM integrates Arrhenius-based degradation kinetics (SEI growth, lithium inventory loss) into neural network training, achieving accurate and physically consistent battery SOH prediction, including improved end-of-life extrapolation versus purely data-driven baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Purely data-driven battery SOH models often generalize poorly across diverse cycling protocols and produce physically implausible behavior (e.g., nonphysical capacity regeneration) during long-term extrapolation.

Method: PiDDM framework that incorporates empirical Arrhenius degradation kinetics (SEI growth, loss of lithium inventory) as physics-informed terms in the training objective, evaluated on a public dataset of 55 batteries across six operating protocols. Models trained on first 90% of cycle life, tested on final 10% for extrapolation assessment.

Result: PiDDM achieved the lowest average prediction error among all evaluated models, substantially reduced MSE versus MLP and baseline PINN, and successfully captured accelerated end-of-life degradation without producing nonphysical capacity regeneration artifacts.

Conclusion: Incorporating degradation physics into neural network training improves both predictive accuracy and physical consistency, offering a promising approach for practical battery health monitoring.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of lithium-ion battery state of health (SOH) is essential for reliable energy storage operation. However, purely data-driven models may generalize poorly across cycling protocols and produce physically implausible behavior during long-term extrapolation. We developed a physics-informed differentiable degradation modeling framework (PiDDM) for battery SOH prediction. PiDDM incorporates empirical Arrhenius degradation kinetics associated with solid electrolyte interphase growth and loss of lithium inventory into the training objective, encouraging physically consistent capacity fade under diverse operating conditions. The framework was evaluated using a public dataset of 55 batteries cycled under six operating protocols. PiDDM achieved the lowest average prediction error among the evaluated models and substantially reduced mean squared error relative to a multilayer perceptron and a baseline physics-informed neural network. For extrapolation, the models were trained on the first 90% of each battery's cycle life and evaluated on the unseen final 10%. PiDDM captured accelerated end-of-life degradation while avoiding the nonphysical capacity regeneration produced by the baseline models. These results show that incorporating degradation physics into neural network training improves predictive accuracy and physical consistency, providing a promising approach for practical battery health monitoring.

</details>


### [144] [Curriculum Matters: Data-Efficient Relational PFN Pretraining with Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29120)
*Mohammad Sadeq Abolhasani,Viswanath Ganapathy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates relational Prior-Data Fitted Networks (PFNs), showing that curriculum learning and synthetic data diversity — not just generator choice or scale — drive performance. A progressive single-table curriculum achieves strong tabular and relational benchmark results with far less data than prior work, and a single-table model nearly matches a dedicated relational pipeline on relational tasks without any relational fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle three factors in relational PFN pretraining: (1) whether an alternative synthetic generator can substitute for the original prior, (2) the impact of data presentation order (curriculum), and (3) how much relational reasoning emerges from single-table pretraining alone.

Method: The authors use PluRel as the sole synthetic data generator throughout. They design a progressive single-table curriculum (widening from 7 to 17 columns, ~13,300 tables) and a relational curriculum (~5,500 databases). They compare curriculum vs. all-at-once training on both the 23-task tabular benchmark and the 19-task RelBench/4DBInfer relational benchmark, and evaluate the single-table model directly on relational tasks without adaptation.

Result: (1) The single-table curriculum reaches 0.703 ROC-AUC on the tabular benchmark (vs. 0.541 for all-at-once training) with ~45x fewer tables than RDB-PFN's warm-up. (2) The relational curriculum reaches 0.638 ROC-AUC on the relational benchmark, recovering 88% of RDB-PFN's performance with ~220x less data. (3) The single-table curriculum model achieves 0.631 on the relational benchmark without any relational adaptation, nearly matching the dedicated relational pipeline.

Conclusion: Curriculum design and synthetic data diversity matter more for relational PFN pretraining than the choice of relational generator or raw synthetic dataset size alone. A well-structured single-table curriculum can implicitly impart substantial relational reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Relational Prior-Data Fitted Networks (PFNs) such as RDB-PFN approximate Bayesian inference over multi-table relational databases by pretraining on millions of synthetic tasks. We investigate three intertwined questions about this paradigm. First, can a structurally different synthetic generator PluRel substitute for RDB-PFN's prior? Second, how much does the order in which synthetic data is presented to the PFN affect downstream performance? Third, how much relational reasoning can a PFN acquire from single-table synthetic pretraining alone, before any relational data is introduced? Using PluRel as the sole synthetic data source across all experiments, we find: (i) a progressive single-table curriculum that gradually widens schema complexity from 7 to 17 columns reaches 0.703 average ROC-AUC on the 23-task tabular benchmark using only approximately 13,300 synthetic tables (approximately 45x fewer single-table datasets than RDB-PFN's reported warm-up recipe), while the same data trained all-at-once collapses to 0.541 ROC-AUC; (ii) a relational curriculum trained from scratch on only approximately 5,500 PluRel databases reaches 0.638 average ROC-AUC on the 19-task RelBench/4DBInfer benchmark, recovering 88% of RDB-PFN's reported performance with approximately 220x less relational synthetic data; and (iii) the single-table curriculum model, evaluated directly on the relational benchmark without any relational adaptation, achieves 0.631, nearly matching the dedicated relational pipeline. Together, these findings suggest that curriculum design and synthetic data diversity may matter more for relational PFN pretraining than the specific relational generator or raw synthetic scale alone.

</details>


### [145] [PluRel-to-RDB-PFN: Schema-Guided Synthetic Relational Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29129)
*Mohammad Sadeq Abolhasani,Viswanath Ganapathy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Using an external synthetic database generator (PluRel) with only ~33K tasks — 55x fewer than the original — and a schema-guided-first curriculum, the authors recover 87.6% of RDB-PFN's performance on relational benchmarks, demonstrating that decoupled data generation is viable for pretraining Relational Foundation Models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for pretraining Relational Foundation Models tightly couple synthetic data generation with the model training pipeline, limiting flexibility and reproducibility. The authors investigate whether a general-purpose, decoupled synthetic relational database generator (PluRel) can serve as an effective external data source, and what curriculum strategies best leverage such data.

Method: The authors build a conversion pipeline that maps PluRel-generated relational databases and externally constructed binary prediction tasks into RDB-PFN's training format. They evaluate three curriculum strategies: (1) SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST — real-world schema data first, then fully synthetic; (2) FULLY SYNTHETIC — diverse synthetic schemas throughout; (3) SCHEMA-GUIDED LAST — fully synthetic first, then real-world schema. Training uses only ~5,500 databases (~33K tasks) with no single-table warm-up, compared to the original ~1.8M-task protocol. Evaluation is on 19 real benchmark tasks at 64-shot and 1024-shot context.

Result: The SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST curriculum achieves 0.6346 average ROC-AUC at 1024-shot, recovering 87.6% of the original RDB-PFN performance (0.7245). At 64-shot, the gap narrows to 93.8% (0.6116 vs. 0.6517). This is achieved with ~55x fewer tasks and no single-table warm-up. SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST substantially outperforms the other two curriculum strategies.

Conclusion: External synthetic generators like PluRel can provide useful pretraining signals for RFMs when paired with appropriate curriculum design. Early exposure to real-world schemas is significantly more effective than late-stage schema adaptation, suggesting that schema realism is a critical factor in curriculum design for relational in-context learners.

Abstract: Relational Foundation Models (RFMs) require large-scale synthetic relational databases for pretraining, but existing approaches tightly couple data generation with the model training pipeline. We study whether PluRel, a general-purpose synthetic relational database generator, can serve as an external data source for RDB-PFN, a relational in-context learner originally pretrained with a 600K-task single-table warm-up followed by an approximately 1.8M-task adaptation stage.
  We build a conversion pipeline that maps PluRel-generated databases, including externally constructed binary prediction tasks, into the RDB-PFN training format and evaluate three curriculum strategies: SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST (real-world schema then fully synthetic), FULLY SYNTHETIC (diverse synthetic schemas throughout), and SCHEMA-GUIDED LAST (fully synthetic then real-world schema). Using only approximately 5,500 relational databases (approximately 33K tasks), roughly 55x fewer tasks than the original protocol, and no single-table warm-up, our best curriculum (SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST) achieves 0.6346 average ROC-AUC across 19 real benchmark tasks at 1024-shot context, recovering 87.6% of the published RDB-PFN performance (0.7245). At 64-shot context, the gap narrows to 93.8% (0.6116 vs. 0.6517). Our results demonstrate that external synthetic generators can provide useful pretraining signals for RFMs when combined with appropriate curriculum design and that exposure to a real-world schema early in training is substantially more effective than late-stage schema adaptation.

</details>


### [146] [HERO: History-Enriched Rollout Training for Long-Horizon Autoregressive Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29135)
*Jiaquan Zhang,Shuxu Chen,Haifan Meng,Yi Lu,Zhihan Lyu,Fan Mo,Wei Dong,Yang Yang,Chaoning Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HERO augments standard absolute trajectory supervision with relative supervision from the model's optimization history, using a margin-based objective that reweights the ground-truth rollout gradient based on the strongest failure trajectory from a set of candidate rollouts, improving long-horizon autoregressive PDE prediction across nine benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive neural operators for time-dependent PDEs suffer from error accumulation during rollout. Existing training strategies reduce input mismatch but rely on absolute discrepancy supervision, which is uninformative about whether the operator has overcome specific long-horizon failure behaviors observed earlier during optimization.

Method: HERO (History-Enriched Rollout Training) generates three candidate rollouts at each training step: from a lagged (periodically refreshed) operator, the current model, and a perturbed input. It ranks these by multiple criteria (rollout error, spectral discrepancy, energy drift, error growth) and selects the worst as a failure reference. This reference enters a margin-based objective as a fixed comparison baseline, producing a bounded, sample-dependent reweighting of the ground-truth rollout gradient rather than an independent gradient direction.

Result: Experiments on nine PDE benchmarks with spectral and attention-based backbones show HERO consistently improves long-horizon accuracy, stable rollout length, and out-of-distribution robustness, with no added inference cost.

Conclusion: History-enriched relative supervision effectively stabilizes long-horizon autoregressive prediction by leveraging the model's own optimization history to identify and suppress persistent failure modes.

Abstract: Neural operators provide fast surrogates for time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs) by applying a learned evolution operator recursively to its own predictions, but this autoregressive rollout feeds every prediction error back as input, so local errors accumulate. Existing rollout-training strategies reduce the mismatch between training inputs and self-generated states, yet their supervision still measures only the absolute discrepancy from the ground-truth trajectory. Such supervision is therefore uninformative about whether the operator has overcome the long-horizon failure behaviors it exhibited earlier during optimization. We propose history-enriched rollout training (HERO), which augments conventional absolute trajectory supervision with relative supervision derived from the model's optimization history. HERO ranks detached candidate rollouts from a periodically refreshed lagged operator, the current model, and a perturbed input by rollout error, spectral discrepancy, energy drift, and error growth, and selects the strongest failure trajectory as reference. This reference enters a margin-based objective as a fixed comparison baseline, inducing a bounded, sample-dependent reweighting of the ground-truth rollout gradient rather than an independent gradient direction, which we further analyze theoretically. Experiments on nine PDE benchmarks with spectral and attention-based backbones show that HERO consistently improves long-horizon accuracy, stable rollout length, and out-of-distribution robustness at no inference-time cost. These results indicate that history-enriched relative supervision is effective for stabilizing long-horizon autoregressive prediction.

</details>


### [147] [Implicit Machine Learning Force Fields Accelerate Molecular Dynamics Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29158)
*Johannes Maeß,Leon Werner,J. Thorben Frank,Winfried Ripken,Martin Michajlow,Joshua Futterer,Klaus-Robert Müller,Stefan Chmiela*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Implicit machine learning force fields (I-MLFFs) replace explicit neural network layers with self-consistent fixed-point equations, enabling warm-starting of force evaluations across MD timesteps. This yields 2–5× compute/memory reductions across invariant, Cartesian-tensor, and SO(3)-equivariant GNN architectures while retaining full atomistic resolution, advancing the scaling frontier of quantum-mechanically faithful molecular simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional MLFFs treat force prediction and trajectory integration as separate problems, discarding intermediate representations between timesteps and incurring the full computational cost of deep neural networks at every step. The authors aim to bridge this gap by reusing representations across successive timesteps, reducing computational footprint without sacrificing accuracy, resolution, or integration timestep.

Method: The authors introduce implicit machine learning force fields (I-MLFFs), which replace explicit stacks of neural network layers with self-consistent fixed-point equations. In molecular dynamics simulations, this formulation allows intermediate representations from one timestep to warm-start the force evaluation at the next timestep. The approach effectively combines the computational cost of a shallow single-layer model with the representational capacity of a deep network. The method is demonstrated across three major GNN classes: invariant, equivariant Cartesian tensor, and SO(3)-equivariant spherical-tensor architectures.

Result: The I-MLFF approach achieves a two- to five-fold reduction in both compute and memory footprint across all three tested GNN architecture classes. These efficiency gains are realized while maintaining full atomistic resolution and the original integration timestep, without resorting to spatial or temporal coarse graining.

Conclusion: I-MLFFs enable longer MD trajectories and simulations of larger atomistic systems within fixed GPU memory and compute budgets. This advances the scaling frontier of quantum-mechanically faithful molecular simulation and opens access to new insights in biomolecular and material systems.

Abstract: We introduce implicit machine learning force fields (I-MLFFs), which replace explicit stacks of neural network layers with self-consistent fixed-point equations. In molecular simulations, this formulation enables intermediate representations to be reused across successive timesteps, thereby warm-starting force evaluation. The resulting models effectively combine the computational footprint of a shallow, single-layer MLFF with the representational capacity and accuracy of a deep neural network. Our approach unlocks architecture-agnostic efficiency gains that are inaccessible when force prediction and trajectory integration are considered separately. We demonstrate this across three major classes of graph neural networks: invariant, equivariant Cartesian tensor, and SO(3)-equivariant spherical-tensor architectures. Each yields a two- to five-fold reduction in compute and memory footprint. Crucially, these gains are achieved while retaining full atomistic resolution and the original integration timestep, avoiding spatial or temporal coarse graining. Our contribution therefore advances the scaling frontier of quantum-mechanically faithful molecular simulation, enabling longer trajectories and larger atomistic systems within fixed GPU memory and compute budgets, and thereby opening access to new insights across biomolecular and material systems.

</details>


### [148] [MBDiff: Multi-view Behavior-aware Diffusion Model for Probabilistic Utility Data Imputation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29177)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Dahai Yu,Ximiao Li,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MBDiff is a multi-view behavior-aware diffusion model for probabilistic imputation of missing utility data (electricity, water, gas). It extracts comprehensive user behavior from global, local, and instance-level views, then uses a conditional diffusion model with reference selection and attentional denoising to impute missing values, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines on real-world municipal utility datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Utility data (electricity, water, gas) from sensors often contains substantial missing values due to device failures and transmission issues, severely impacting billing, demand forecasting, and supply management. Existing imputation methods rely on aggregated datasets and overlook rich user behavior information. However, learning comprehensive user behavior from long-term, diverse, and incomplete data is challenging, and leveraging such behavior to guide imputation is nontrivial due to indirect correlations.

Method: MBDiff (Multi-view Behavior-aware Diffusion Model) consists of two key components: (1) a multi-view User Behavior Extraction module that learns user behavior from global, local, and instance-level perspectives; and (2) a behavior-aware conditional diffusion model comprising a reference selection module and a conditional attentional denoising network for computationally efficient probabilistic imputation.

Result: Evaluated in collaboration with a large municipal utility provider in Florida, MBDiff outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving 7.04% improvement on electricity and 29.1% improvement on water usage datasets for block missingness imputation.

Conclusion: MBDiff effectively addresses the utility data imputation problem by leveraging multi-view user behavior information through a diffusion-based framework, demonstrating significant performance gains over existing methods, particularly for challenging block missingness scenarios.

Abstract: Utility data (e.g., electricity, water, and gas consumption), collected by ubiquitous sensors and embedded devices, often contains substantial missing values due to various factors such as device failures and data transmission issues. The data missingness can severely impact utility billing accuracy, hinder demand forecasting, and disrupt efficient utility supply management. As a result, utility data imputation has attracted much interest from both industry and academia. While many studies have attempted to address this issue, most of them rely on aggregated datasets for training, overlooking rich user behavior information, which could provide valuable insights for more accurate imputation. However, learning comprehensive user behavior from long-term, diverse, and incomplete utility data remains a significant challenge. Moreover, leveraging user behavior information to guide imputation is nontrivial due to the indirect nature of the correlations. To address these challenges, we propose MBDiff, a Multi-view Behavior-aware Diffusion Model for Probabilistic Utility Data Imputation. MBDiff incorporates two key technical components: (i) a multi-view User Behavior Extraction module that learns comprehensive user behavior from multiple perspectives, including global, local, and instance-level views; and (ii) a behavior-aware conditional diffusion model consisting of a reference selection module and a conditional attentional denoising network to impute utility data in a computationally efficient manner. We implement and evaluate MBDiff by collaborating with one of the largest municipal utility providers in Florida. Experimental results demonstrate our proposed MBDiff effectively outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, e.g., it improves 7.04% and 29.1% on the electricity and water usage datasets for block missingness imputation, respectively.

</details>


### [149] [SERUM: State Extraction and Refinement for User Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29181)
*Andy J. Phu,James Mooney,Karin de Langis,Khanh Chi Le,Dongyeop Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SERUM is a multi-pass framework that extracts finite-state behavioral models from unstructured egocentric screen videos using hierarchical VLM annotation. It alternates between activity-recognition and intent-inference passes, merges synonymous states via sentence embeddings, and fits Markov models over the resulting label sequences. Evaluated across 61 videos in four domains, SERUM converges to stable state vocabularies, achieves lower perplexity than frequency baselines, and produces labels validated by human annotators—enabling interpretable user process modeling without manual annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic assistants require structured models of user intent and workflow for proactive, personalized interactions. Current approaches lack scalable methods to build such models from raw, unstructured screen activity. Single-pass VLM annotation suffers from hallucination and temporal conflation, motivating a refined, multi-pass approach that leverages accumulated context for more coherent behavioral modeling.

Method: SERUM operates via a sliding window over screen recordings, alternating between activity-recognition and intent-inference passes. Each pass refines labels using prior context to reduce errors. Synonymous states are merged via sentence embeddings with human-calibrated thresholds into a compact taxonomy. First-order Markov models are then fit over action and intent label sequences, with predictive accuracy measured against frequency baselines.

Result: (1) Iterative label refinement converges to a stable state vocabulary ("schematic equilibrium") after several passes. (2) Normalized Markov models achieve substantially lower perplexity and higher action prediction accuracy than baselines, especially on structured tasks like coding. (3) Human annotators rated final-pass labels as accurate and significantly improved over first-pass labels.

Conclusion: SERUM is the first system to produce interpretable process models from unstructured egocentric screen video without manual annotation, offering a scalable pathway for user modeling and behavioral understanding in the wild. Code, demo, and results are publicly available.

Abstract: Agentic assistants capable of proactive, personalized interactions require structured models of user intent and workflow. However, building these models from raw, unstructured screen activity remains an open challenge. We present SERUM, a multi-pass framework that extracts finite-state behavioral models directly from unstructured egocentric video using hierarchical VLM annotation. Processing screen recordings through a sliding window, SERUM alternates between activity-recognition and intent-inference passes, with each pass refining labels using accumulated prior context to reduce hallucination and temporal conflation seen in single-pass annotation. Synonymous states are then merged via sentence embeddings and human-calibrated thresholds into a compact, coherent taxonomy. We evaluate behavioral structure by fitting first-order Markov models over the resulting label sequences (both actions and intents) and measuring predictive accuracy against frequency baselines. Across 61 egocentric videos in four domains (coding, cooking, physical activities, and daily life), we find: (1) iterative label refinement converges to a stable state vocabulary, which we term schematic equilibrium, after several passes; (2) normalized Markov models achieve substantially lower perplexity and higher action predictions than frequency baselines, with the largest gains on structured tasks like coding; and (3) human annotators rate final-pass labels as accurate and meaningfully improved over first-pass labels. To our knowledge, SERUM is the first system to produce interpretable process models from unstructured egocentric screen video without manual annotation, opening a scalable pathway for user modeling and behavioral understanding in the wild. Our demo, code, and results are publicly available

</details>


### [150] [SAF-OPD: Stable Advantage Fusion for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29209)
*Yifan Ding,Xincheng Wei,Yoshua Y. Li,Ziheng Li,Yuquan Lu,Siyu Zhang,Dongsheng Ma,Rongxiang Weng,Xunliang Cai,Yun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes SAF, a Stable Advantage Fusion framework that combines RLVR and on-policy distillation (OPD) by addressing two miscalibrations — magnitude mismatch and temporal mismatch — through sparsify-then-compress and warm-up-then-anneal mechanisms, achieving improved stability and performance over fixed-coefficient fusion across math and code benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR provides sparse, response-level rewards that encourage exploration, while OPD provides dense, token-level advantages that cap performance at teacher quality. Their combination is promising but naive fixed-coefficient fusion causes entropy collapse due to: (1) magnitude mismatch where token-level OPD advantages dominate the bounded RLVR signal, and (2) temporal mismatch where sustained OPD guidance limits the exploration needed to surpass the teacher.

Method: SAF applies a lightweight, four-stage pipeline exclusively to the OPD advantage: a sparsify-then-compress mechanism for magnitude control and a warm-up-then-anneal mechanism for temporal control. RLVR is instantiated with GRPO. Each stage is independently switchable with negligible overhead. Evaluated on Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B across seven math reasoning and code generation benchmarks.

Result: SAF avoids entropy collapse and consistently outperforms fixed-coefficient GRPO+OPD fusion, improving aggregate scores by 0.51–2.70% across all six model-domain settings while achieving more stable training.

Conclusion: The SAF framework successfully resolves the magnitude and temporal miscalibrations that plague naive RLVR+OPD fusion, enabling stable training and consistent performance gains across diverse model sizes and benchmark domains.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) broadcasts a single response-level reward to every token, while on-policy distillation (OPD) scores each token against a stronger teacher for a dense advantage but caps performance at teacher quality and discourages exploration beyond it. Their complementarity makes combining RLVR and OPD promising, but we find that fusing the two advantages with a fixed coefficient triggers entropy collapse from two miscalibrations: a magnitude mismatch, where token-level OPD advantages can spike far beyond the bounded RLVR advantage and erase its signal, and a temporal mismatch, where sustained full-strength OPD keeps pulling the student toward the teacher and limits exploration needed to surpass it. We propose SAF, a Stable Advantage Fusion framework that resolves both issues via a lightweight, four-stage pipeline applied only to the OPD advantage: a sparsify-then-compress mechanism for magnitude control paired with a warm-up-then-anneal mechanism for temporal control, with each stage independently switchable and adding negligible overhead. Instantiating RLVR with GRPO, we evaluate SAF across seven mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks with Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B: SAF avoids entropy collapse and consistently outperforms fixed-coefficient GRPO+OPD fusion, improving the aggregate score by 0.51-2.70% across all six model-domain settings while achieving more stable training.

</details>


### [151] [Frugal Bayesian Optimization: Scalable Surrogates for Data- and Resource-Limited Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29225)
*Panagiotis Krokidas,Christoforos Rekatsinas,Vassilis Sioros,Grigorios M. Chatziathanasiou,Efi-Maria Papia,George Giannakopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a compute-aware benchmarking study of Bayesian Optimization surrogates, showing Gaussian Processes incur high computational cost without performance gains. The authors introduce FruBO, a reproducible baseline, and a surrogate-recommendation framework that predicts the best surrogate from cheap dataset features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bayesian Optimization is widely used for data-efficient optimization, yet the computational cost of different surrogate models is rarely evaluated alongside optimization performance. Practitioners default to Gaussian Processes without evidence that their computational overhead is justified, creating a gap in understanding the cost-performance trade-offs among surrogate choices.

Method: The authors benchmark four surrogate models — Gaussian Processes, Random Forests, NGBoost, and Bayesian Adaptive Spline Surfaces — across eight synthetic benchmark functions and nine real-world datasets from diverse domains (materials science, mechanics, robotics, chemistry, ML). They evaluate along two axes: optimization quality (final optimum found, sample efficiency) and computational frugality (time, memory). They further develop a surrogate-recommendation framework that predicts the most suitable BO surrogate using inexpensive dataset characteristics, and establish FruBO as a reproducible, compute-aware BO baseline.

Result: Gaussian Process-based BO consistently incurs the highest time and memory overhead without delivering superior optimization or sample efficiency. Scalable alternatives (Random Forests, NGBoost, BASS) achieve equal or better optimization performance at a fraction of the computational cost. The surrogate-recommendation framework successfully predicts suitable surrogates from inexpensive dataset characteristics.

Conclusion: The default use of Gaussian Processes in BO is computationally wasteful; cheaper surrogates often match or exceed GP performance. FruBO provides a reproducible, compute-aware baseline, and the recommendation framework offers practical guidance for surrogate selection under limited computational and experimental budgets.

Abstract: Bayesian Optimization (BO) is widely adopted for data-efficient optimization in scientific and engineering applications, yet its computational cost is rarely evaluated alongside optimization performance. Here we present a systematic, compute-aware study of BO that evaluates surrogate models along two axes: optimization quality and computational frugality. Across eight benchmark functions and nine real-world datasets spanning materials science, mechanics, robotics, chemistry, and machine learning, we benchmark four surrogate models: Gaussian Processes, Random Forests, NGBoost, and Bayesian Adaptive Spline Surfaces. We show that Gaussian Process-based BO consistently incurs the highest time and memory overhead without delivering superior optimization or sample efficiency. In contrast, scalable alternatives achieve equal or better performance at a fraction of the computational cost. Motivated by these findings, we introduce a surrogate-recommendation framework that predicts the most suitable BO surrogate from inexpensive dataset characteristics. Together, these results establish FruBO as a reproducible, compute-aware baseline for Bayesian Optimization and provide practical guidance for surrogate selection under limited computational and experimental budgets.

</details>


### [152] [UniPolymer: A Unified Framework for Property Prediction, Structure Recommendation, and Evaluation in Polyimide Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29256)
*Junquan Hu,Zhihui Wang,Peng Xu,Xinru Guo,Xintong Li,Kun Lu,Ben Fei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UniPolymer is a unified framework for polyimide design that integrates property prediction, target-conditioned generation, candidate evaluation, and structure recommendation. It improves consistency between generated structures and target glass transition temperatures (Tg) using self-supervised learning, multi-scale fusion, and a continuous-discrete Tg representation, achieving R²=0.93 prediction accuracy and a 73.79% candidate pass rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for polyimide Tg design focus on target-conditioned generation without verifying consistency between generated structures and target properties. This leads to low-quality candidates entering downstream validation, increasing invalid experiments and prolonging development cycles. There is a need for a framework that unifies generation with rigorous evaluation to filter out off-target candidates.

Method: The authors propose UniPolymer, a unified framework with four components: (1) a property predictor built via self-supervised chemical semantic learning, structural consistency enhancement, and multi-scale information fusion to establish reliable structure-property mapping; (2) a continuous-discrete joint Tg representation guiding autoregressive generation of SELFIES; (3) a frozen property predictor with polyimide-specific structural constraints for candidate evaluation; and (4) ranking candidates by deviation from target Tg for recommendation. A curated dataset (PITg-Curated) of 10,066 deduplicated polyimide repeating units with Tg labels was also constructed.

Result: UniPolymer achieved an R² of 0.93 for property prediction accuracy and a candidate structure evaluation pass rate of 73.79%, outperforming the best baseline by 2% and 1.21%, respectively. The predicted Tg values of recommended candidates showed high agreement with molecular dynamics simulation results, effectively reducing the number of candidates entering costly experimental validation.

Conclusion: UniPolymer addresses the consistency gap between generation and property targets in polyimide Tg design by integrating prediction, generation, evaluation, and recommendation into a single framework. The high prediction accuracy and evaluation pass rate, combined with agreement with MD simulations, demonstrate its potential to reduce invalid experiments and accelerate polyimide development cycles.

Abstract: Designing polyimide structures with specific glass transition temperatures (Tg) is highly challenging. Existing methods primarily focus on target-conditioned generation, lacking an assessment of the consistency between the generated structure and the target properties. This leads to low-quality candidates deviating from the design objective entering subsequent processes, increasing invalid experiments and prolonging the development cycle. To address this issue, we propose UniPolymer, a unified framework for property prediction, target-conditioned generation, candidate evaluation, and structure recommendation in polyimide design and a dataset containing 10066 deduplicated polyimide repeating units with Tg tags (PITg-Curated) was constructed. To improve the consistency between generated candidate structures and the target Tg, UniPolymer first establishes a reliable structure-property relationship mapping through self-supervised chemical semantic learning, structural consistency enhancement, and multi-scale information fusion. Subsequently, the model employs a continuous-discrete joint Tg representation to guide the autoregressive generation of SELFIES. The generated candidate structures are further evaluated using a frozen property predictor and polyimide-specific structural constraints, and ranked according to their deviation from the target Tg, thereby preventing structures deviating from the target from entering the subsequent validation stage. Experimental results show that UniPolymer achieved a property prediction accuracy of R^2=0.93 and a candidate structure evaluation pass rate of 73.79%, which are 2% and 1.21% higher than the best baseline, respectively. Meanwhile, the predicted Tg values of the recommended candidates are in high agreement with the results of molecular dynamics simulations, thereby reducing the number of candidates that enter the high-cost experimental stage.

</details>


### [153] [Assessing the Generalization of Graph Neural Networks for Fault Location Across Increasing Distributed Energy Resource Penetration Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29293)
*Burak Karabulut,Olayiwola Arowolo,Carlo Manna,Chris Develder,Jochen L. Cremer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STGATv2, a spatio-temporal graph neural network, achieves 92-94% F1 for distribution network fault location and demonstrates asymmetric generalization across DER penetration levels. Training at high penetration (50%) retains strong performance at lower levels, but training at low penetration (10%) degrades significantly at 50% — though STGATv2 (81-84% F1) still substantially outperforms GATv2 (69-74%) and GRU (73-75%). Under measurement noise, STGATv2 maintains >85% F1 while GRU collapses to 33.5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Increasing DER penetration complicates distribution network fault location due to intermittent generation and bidirectional power flows that alter fault signatures. While STGNNs are promising for joint spatial-temporal modeling, their behavior under varying DER penetration levels has not been rigorously studied.

Method: The authors benchmark STGATv2 against purely temporal (GRU), purely spatial (GATv2), and traditional machine learning baselines on a reconfigured IEEE 123-bus feeder with multiple DER injection points and moderate-to-high impedance faults. Models are evaluated at 10%, 25%, and 50% DER penetration levels, including cross-penetration generalization tests and robustness analysis under realistic measurement noise.

Result: STGATv2 achieves 92-94% macro F1 in-distribution. Generalization across penetration levels is asymmetric: training at 50% retains near in-distribution F1 at lower levels, but training at 10% degrades at 50% — STGATv2 retains 81-84% F1 vs. GATv2 (69-74%) and GRU (73-75%). Under measurement noise, STGATv2 exceeds 85% F1, while GRU drops to 33.5%.

Conclusion: Topological awareness provided by graph-based spatial modeling is critical for robust fault location in active distribution networks. STGATv2 demonstrates superior in-distribution performance, asymmetric generalization capability across DER penetration shifts, and resilience to measurement noise, making it well-suited for real-world deployment scenarios with evolving DER penetration.

Abstract: Accurate fault location is critical for distribution network reliability. However, increasing distributed energy resource (DER) penetration complicates fault location due to intermittent generation and bidirectional power flows that reshape fault signatures. Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks (STGNNs) have shown promise by jointly modeling spatial and temporal dependencies, but their behavior under increasing DER penetration has not been studied rigorously. In this paper, we (i) systematically benchmark spatio-temporal graph attention network (STGATv2) against purely temporal (gated recurrent unit, GRU), purely spatial (GATv2) and traditional machine learning baselines, and (ii) evaluate how well models generalize across increasing DER penetration levels (10%, 25%, 50%) on a reconfigured IEEE 123-bus feeder with multiple DER injection points and moderate-to-high impedance faults. Results show that STGATv2 consistently outperforms neural baselines, achieving 92-94% macro F1 in-distribution. Notably, generalization across penetration levels is asymmetric: training at 50% penetration retains near in-distribution F1 score at lower levels, whereas training at 10% degrades considerably at 50% - with STGATv2 retaining 81-84% F1 under these drastic shifts, substantially higher than GATv2 and GRU which drop to 69-74% F1 and 73-75% F1 respectively. Under realistic measurement noise, STGATv2 maintains > 85% F1, while GRU drops as low as 33.5% F1, highlighting the critical role of topological awareness for robust fault location in active distribution networks.

</details>


### [154] [Sample Efficient Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning via Best Policy Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29294)
*Anders Jonsson,Emilie Kaufmann,Gianmarco Tedeschi,Lorenzo Steccanella*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HBPI-UCRL is a model-based hierarchical RL algorithm that learns high- and low-level policies in parallel, with provably lower sample complexity than non-hierarchical methods in sparse-reward, goal-directed settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL) has shown empirical success, but lacks theoretical justification. The authors aim to provide formal sample complexity guarantees for HRL, demonstrating when and why it outperforms flat (non-hierarchical) approaches.

Method: The paper introduces HBPI-UCRL (Hierarchical Backward Planning with Improved UCRL), a model-based algorithm that learns high-level and low-level policies simultaneously. It leverages the correspondence between high-level transitions and multi-step low-level transitions. Two sufficient conditions on low-level dynamics are introduced to ensure learnability of parallel HRL.

Result: The authors prove that HBPI-UCRL achieves polynomial sample complexity when the two conditions hold. In sparse-reward, goal-directed environments, the sample complexity upper bound is strictly lower than that of the non-hierarchical counterpart, providing the first theoretical justification for HRL's empirical success.

Conclusion: Parallel HRL can be provably more sample-efficient than flat RL under identifiable structural conditions. The work bridges the gap between the empirical success of HRL and its theoretical understanding.

Abstract: We present HBPI-UCRL, a model-based algorithm for hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL) that learns high-level and low-level policies in parallel. HBPI-UCRL exploits the fact that a high-level transition corresponds to a multi-step transition at the low level. We introduce two conditions on the low-level dynamics that are sufficient to make parallel HRL learnable. When these conditions hold, we prove that HBPI-UCRL has a polynomial sample complexity in the problem parameters. In the sparse-reward, goal-directed setting, our sample complexity upper bound for HBPI-UCRL is strictly lower than that of its non-hierarchical counterpart, providing theoretical justification for the empirical success of HRL.

</details>


### [155] [Analysing User Reviews to Identify User Concerns Around Permissions in AI Apps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29343)
*Babar Shah,Faheem Ullah,Myles Watkinson,Muhammad Moiz Khalid,Tehmina Karamat Khan,Muhammad Junaid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning model classifies AI mobile app reviews by permission-related concerns using AI-generated training data, achieving 82% accuracy. Users organize concerns by app sentiment rather than specific permission types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI mobile app developers often lack security/privacy expertise, forcing users to self-monitor. App reviews are a rich source of real user experience but are unstructured and hard to label at scale. The paper aims to automatically classify permission-related concerns in these reviews to inform users, developers, and platform administrators.

Method: The approach uses AI-generated security and permission reviews as seed data to identify relevant training examples from a large corpus of real human-written reviews, bypassing costly manual annotation. A machine learning classifier is then trained on these automatically curated examples to categorize reviews into permission-related categories.

Result: The proposed model achieved 82% classification accuracy on permission-related reviews. Analysis further revealed that users structure their privacy/security concerns around sentiment toward the requesting app rather than around specific Android permission types.

Conclusion: AI-generated training data can effectively bootstrap review classification without manual labeling. The finding that users frame concerns by app sentiment rather than permission type carries practical implications for how developers communicate data practices and how platform administrators design permission interfaces.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is increasingly embedded in everyday software, making its integration into mobile apps inevitable. However, AI mobile app developers are not always versed in security and privacy best practices, leaving users to monitor their own security and understand how apps use their data. App reviews capture real user experiences, helping others make informed decisions before downloading. This paper presents a machine learning model for classifying AI app reviews into permission-related categories.
