Because user reviews are unstructured, assembling a conventional labeled training set is difficult. To address this, AI-generated security and permission reviews are used to identify relevant training examples from a large corpus of human-written reviews, eliminating the need for manual annotation. The proposed approach classified permission reviews with an accuracy of 82%. Analysis shows that users organise their concerns by sentiment toward the requesting app rather than specific permission types, with implications for users, developers, and platform administrators.

</details>


### [156] [Versatile On-device Adaptation at the Edge by Unifying Few-shot, Zero-shot, Continual, and In-context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29353)
*Douwe den Blanken,Martin Lefebvre,Charlotte Frenkel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ECL is a framework unifying four online learning scenarios (FSL, CL, ZSL, ICL) for resource-constrained edge devices, demonstrated in silicon with state-of-the-art results across multiple benchmarks at micro-to-milliwatt power.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge devices lack on-device learning capabilities for personalization; when they do support learning, they are limited to a single scenario (e.g., FSL). Supporting multiple learning paradigms currently requires cloud offloading or specialized hardware, causing energy/latency overheads, privacy issues, and loss of real-time capability.

Method: Introduces embedder-centric learning (ECL), a unified framework that supports four online learning paradigms — few-shot learning, continual learning, zero-shot learning, and in-context learning — demonstrated via a silicon implementation on resource-constrained hardware across four real-world use cases.

Result: Established new SOTA for FSL character recognition (Omniglot: 96.8% 5-way 1-shot, 83.3% 32-way 1-shot); first hardware baseline for CL keyword spotting (NeuroBench FSCIL: 71.8% 200-way 5-shot); first hardware demonstrations of ZSL (60.6% 5-way spoken sentence classification) and ICL (46.2% at 500th token, RegBench), all at micro-to-milliwatt power.

Conclusion: ECL enables smart, versatile edge devices capable of adapting across multiple learning scenarios without cloud reliance, paving the way for truly personalized on-device intelligence.

Abstract: With the ever-increasing pervasiveness of smart edge devices, the demand is growing for applications that can be tailored to users (e.g., custom keyword spotting) or patients (e.g., adaptive health monitoring). Yet, most edge devices rely on fixed inference algorithms and thus cannot learn on-device to personalize predictions. When they can, devices typically support only a specific learning scenario, such as few-shot learning (FSL): going beyond this requires resorting either to another specialized device or to cloud-based retraining, which implies significant energy and latency overheads, a lack of real-time capabilities, and privacy concerns. In this work, we introduce embedder-centric learning (ECL), a framework that unifies four different online learning scenarios: FSL for on-the-fly customization, continual learning (CL) for knowledge accumulation, zero-shot learning (ZSL) for leveraging semantic data, and in-context learning (ICL) for adapting beyond classification. We demonstrate in silicon that ECL can be deployed on resource-constrained devices across four real-world use cases representative of the aforementioned learning scenarios. Our approach establishes a new state-of-the-art performance for FSL character recognition (Omniglot: 96.8% for 5-way 1-shot, 83.3% for 32-way 1-shot), and the first hardware baseline for CL in keyword spotting (NeuroBench keyword FSCIL: 71.8% for 200-way 5-shot). Moreover, we present the first hardware demonstrations of ZSL with semantic data (60.6% for 5-way spoken sentence classification) and ICL (46.2% at the 500th token of RegBench) operating at micro-to-milliwatt power budgets. Therefore, by unifying multiple learning scenarios, we pave the way for smart and versatile devices that can adapt right at the edge, without reliance on the cloud.

</details>


### [157] [Cross-Resolution Semantic Learning for Graph Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29365)
*Yingxu Wang,Haoze Huang,Zhongkai Zheng,Shangsong Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CReSL is a graph domain adaptation method that explicitly models cross-domain correspondence between source and target propagation resolutions (neighborhood ranges) via prototype-based routing, outperforming existing GDA methods under diverse distribution shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GDA methods align representations or regularize structures without modeling how class-discriminative knowledge at different neighborhood ranges (propagation resolutions) should map across domains. Semantic resolution shift—where the optimal resolution for class discrimination differs between source and target—can cause negative transfer when fixed same-resolution pairing is used.

Method: CReSL has three components: (1) a multi-resolution representation bank using a shared GNN with learnable resolution embeddings and resolution-indexed experts; (2) Cross-Resolution Prototype Transport, which builds class-resolution prototypes from source labels and soft target posteriors, converting prototype discrepancies into expert-specific routing weights over target resolutions; (3) Cross-Resolution Target Grafting, which computes posterior-weighted target-to-source prototype displacements and enforces correspondence-weighted prediction consistency for instance-level adaptation under class uncertainty.

Result: Extensive experiments on graph benchmarks across diverse domain shifts demonstrate that CReSL outperforms strong representative baselines in most settings.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling cross-resolution correspondence between domains is effective for graph domain adaptation. CReSL's prototype-based soft routing mechanism successfully addresses semantic resolution shift and mitigates negative transfer.

Abstract: Graph Domain Adaptation (GDA) transfers predictive knowledge from labeled source graphs to unlabeled target graphs under distribution shift. Existing methods align representations or regularize graph structures, but do not explicitly model how class-discriminative knowledge learned at different source neighborhood ranges should be routed across target ranges. We call the neighborhood range encoded by a graph representation its propagation resolution and define semantic resolution shift as a cross-domain change in the propagation resolutions at which class-discriminative evidence is strongest. Such shifts can make fixed same-resolution pairing suboptimal and increase the risk of negative transfer. To address this issue, we propose Cross-Resolution Semantic Learning (CReSL), a GDA method that learns soft sourceto-target resolution correspondence from cross-domain class structure. First, CReSL constructs a multi-resolution representation bank using a shared Graph Neural Network and learnable resolution embeddings, with a resolution-indexed expert for each source resolution. Second, CReSL introduces Cross-Resolution Prototype Transport, which constructs class-resolution prototypes from source labels and soft target posteriors and converts cross-domain prototype discrepancies into expert-specific routing over target resolutions. Third, CReSL introduces Cross-Resolution Target Grafting, which constructs posterior-weighted target-to-source prototype displacements and enforces correspondence-weighted prediction consistency for instance-level adaptation under class uncertainty. Extensive experiments on graph benchmarks under diverse domain shifts show that CReSL outperforms strong representative baselines across most settings.

</details>


### [158] [OnlineCache: Learning Dynamic Caching Policies with Error Correction for Efficient Diffusion Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29398)
*Zhikang Xie,Xichen Ye,Yifan Wu,Haoshen Yu,Li chenan,Peizhu Gong,Weizhong Zhang,Cheng Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OnlineCache is a dynamic caching framework for diffusion models that jointly learns when to cache and how to correct errors via policy gradient and a learnable corrector under bilevel optimization, achieving ~3× speedup on FLUX.1-dev without quality loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cache-based acceleration methods for diffusion models use static, sample-agnostic schedules, ignoring two empirical facts: (1) generation difficulty varies across prompts, so simpler inputs waste computation while complex ones are under-allocated; (2) error sensitivity fluctuates across timesteps, meaning static policies may cache at high-error steps or waste computation on low-error ones.

Method: OnlineCache uses policy gradient to train a lightweight network that makes adaptive caching decisions per sample and per timestep, jointly optimized with a learnable corrector module that mitigates approximation errors from caching. The two modules are trained under a bilevel optimization framework: the policy optimizes for global generation quality, while the corrector minimizes local caching-induced errors.

Result: On FLUX.1-dev, OnlineCache achieves nearly 3× speedup while preserving generation fidelity. On DiT and CogVideoX, it delivers competitive acceleration without quality degradation. Across all tested scenarios, it consistently outperforms existing cache-based acceleration baselines.

Conclusion: Dynamic, sample- and timestep-adaptive caching with joint error correction significantly outperforms static caching strategies, enabling faster diffusion model inference while maintaining or improving generation quality.

Abstract: Diffusion models have revolutionized generative tasks but incur high latency due to iterative denoising. While cache-based strategies accelerate inference by reusing intermediate features, they largely rely on static, sample-agnostic schedules. We argue that this rigidity overlooks two facts empirically validated in this paper: (i) generation difficulty varies across prompts, requiring adaptive resource allocation--complex inputs demand more computation while simpler ones require less; (ii) error sensitivity fluctuates across timesteps, where static policies may cache high-error steps or waste computation on low-error ones. We therefore propose OnlineCache, a dynamic caching framework that jointly learns when to cache and how to correct approximation errors. We leverage policy gradient to train a lightweight network for adaptive speed-quality trade-offs, and incorporate a learnable corrector to mitigate caching-induced errors. Both modules are jointly optimized under a bilevel optimization framework, with the policy targeting global generation quality and the corrector minimizing local errors. Our method automatically allocates computational resources across both samples and timesteps, improving overall generation quality. Extensive experiments demonstrate clear superiority. On FLUX.1-dev model, OnlineCache achieves nearly 3 speedup while preserving generation fidelity. On DiT and CogVideoX, it similarly delivers competitive acceleration without compromising quality; across all scenarios, it consistently outperforms existing cache-based acceleration baselines.

</details>


### [159] [ALIVE: Warnings Before Exclusion in Budgeted Multi-Source Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29400)
*Xiyang Zhang,Hongzhi Wang,Yuanhe Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ALIVE is an auditable control layer that ties persistent source-exclusion actions to rigorous statistical evidence. It uses a randomized-without-replacement audit prefix, heuristic routing, and capacity-feasible latching based on two simultaneous certificate separations. The system provides anytime familywise error bounds and is demonstrated on PPR and CIFAR tasks, revealing a restraint–power–cost–utility trade-off boundary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Routing decisions are transient (revisable at the next transaction), but source exclusions persist across future decisions. The core question is: what evidence standard should authorize such unequal-persistence actions when auditing and learning share a finite budget? The paper aims to formalize this evidence-action asymmetry and provide a principled, auditable mechanism.

Method: ALIVE (Action-Layered Intervention via Evidence) is proposed as an auditable control layer with: (1) a randomized without-replacement prefix supplying cached evidence, (2) heuristic warnings driving non-latching floor-bounded routing, and (3) two simultaneous certificate separations required to latch an exclusion, subject to capacity constraints. Under an ideal uniform audit permutation with fixed support/labels, any predictable controller preserving the interface inherits an anytime familywise δ-bound against sources failing a pre-fixed absolute or relative strict-majority-disagreement predicate. Evaluated with a published all-strict-majority PPR engine and a matched CIFAR controller.

Result: PPR engine: median evidence count fell from 304→96 (e40) and 171→62 (e60), with both at 48 (e80). CIFAR controller: persistent-action layer added +0.1935 accuracy-AUBC percentage points over routing-only across ten paired seed clusters. Full-system contrast vs CBR was +0.1954 points but did not meet the predeclared multiplicity-adjusted criterion (conditional Holm-adjusted sign-flip reference p=0.097656). Exploratory PPR on a fixed natural panel used median closure prefix 95 vs 105 for Serfling/FPC, exposed 88.0% of the panel, and had no downstream task.

Conclusion: The results delineate a restraint–power–cost–utility boundary: the action contract controls a defined persistent decision, but net value depends on evidence margin, audit cost, and the prevailing budget regime. The framework provides a principled bridge between statistical auditing guarantees and operational persistent-action decisions.

Abstract: A routing decision can be revised at the next transaction, but a latched source exclusion persists across later decisions. We ask what evidence should authorize these unequal-persistence actions when finite-population auditing and learning share a budget. ALIVE (Action-Layered Intervention via Evidence) is an auditable control layer: one randomized without-replacement prefix supplies cached evidence, heuristic warnings drive non-latching floor-bounded routing, and only two fresh simultaneous certificate separations may latch an exclusion request subject to capacity-feasible activation. Conditional on fixed support and labels under an ideal uniform audit permutation, any predictable controller preserving this interface inherits an anytime familywise bound of δon acting against a source that fails the pre-fixed absolute or relative strict-majority-disagreement predicate. With a published known-size, all-strict-majority PPR engine, median evidence count fell from 304 to 96 identities in e40 and from 171 to 62 in e60, while both engines used 48 in e80. In the matched CIFAR controller, the persistent-action layer added +0.1935 accuracy-AUBC percentage points over routing-only in all ten paired seed clusters. The +0.1954-point full-system contrast against CBR was also positive but did not meet the predeclared multiplicity-adjusted criterion (conditional Holm-adjusted sign-flip reference value =.097656). On a fixed natural panel, exploratory PPR used a median closure prefix of 95 rather than 105 for exploratory Serfling/FPC, but still exposed 88.0% of the panel and had no downstream task. Together these results map a restraint--power--cost--utility boundary: the action contract controls a defined persistent decision, while net value depends on evidence margin, audit cost, and budget regime.

</details>


### [160] [Explore Beyond the Boundary Using Entropic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29419)
*Bumgeun Park,Donghwan Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ENTINEX proposes a novel exploration method for reinforcement learning that uses entropic information to identify state distribution boundaries and assign intrinsic rewards, improving performance in sparse and delayed reward environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning struggles with sparse and delayed rewards because limited feedback hampers learning. Existing methods require extensive state-space exploration to discover valuable signals, motivating a more principled approach to guide exploration.

Method: ENTINEX (Entropic Information for Exploration) incentivizes agents to explore beyond the boundaries of the current state distribution by computing entropic information to identify these boundaries and assigning intrinsic rewards to them, thereby encouraging the agent to visit under-explored regions of the state space.

Result: Extensive experimentation demonstrates that ENTINEX consistently improves exploration performance over existing methods in environments with sparse and delayed rewards, outperforming competing approaches in both sparse and delayed reward scenarios.

Conclusion: ENTINEX is an effective exploration strategy that leverages entropic information to push agents beyond familiar state distributions, yielding superior performance in challenging sparse and delayed reward settings.

Abstract: In reinforcement learning, exploration with sparse and delayed rewards presents a significant challenge due to the limited feedback available for guiding the learning process. Addressing this issue requires extensive exploration in the state space to discover valuable reward signals. In this paper, we propose Entropic Information for Exploration (ENTINEX), a novel method that enhances exploration by incentivizing agents to explore beyond the boundaries of the state distribution. ENTINEX achieves this by assigning intrinsic rewards to these boundaries, leveraging entropic information to identify them effectively. Through extensive experimentation, we demonstrate that ENTINEX consistently improves exploration performance in environments characterized by sparse and delayed rewards. Our experimental results show that ENTINEX outperforms existing exploration methods, highlighting its effectiveness in both sparse and delayed reward scenarios.

</details>


### [161] [End-to-End Fairness Optimization with Fair Decision-Focused Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29441)
*Yu Wang,Violet,Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes end-to-end fairness optimization (E2EFO) for prediction-to-decision pipelines, introducing fair decision-focused learning (FDFL) that jointly optimizes prediction accuracy, prediction fairness, and decision regret using gradient descent with derived decision Jacobians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fairness concerns in real-world systems span both prediction and decision stages, but existing approaches treat them separately. There is a need for a unified framework that integrates fairness across the entire prediction-to-decision pipeline, particularly for resource allocation problems where prediction disparities propagate into inequitable decisions.

Method: The authors propose E2EFO, a framework integrating group-based fairness across prediction and decision stages. FDFL is the training paradigm that combines three objectives: prediction accuracy loss, prediction fairness disparity, and decision regret (loss in decision fairness from imperfect predictions). The predictor is trained via gradient descent using multi-task learning techniques. The key technical contribution is deriving exact closed-form Jacobians of the fair allocation decision with respect to predictor parameters for tractable cases, and using differentiable optimization layers for general cases. A finite-sample generalization bound is also established.

Result: Numerical experiments on healthcare-based single resource allocation and synthetic multiple resource allocation demonstrate that FDFL outperforms approaches that ignore either prediction fairness or decision fairness. The closed-form Jacobian derivations enable efficient gradient-based training.

Conclusion: Jointly accounting for prediction fairness and decision fairness yields better outcomes than addressing either in isolation. The E2EFO framework and FDFL paradigm provide a principled approach for fair prediction-informed decision-making in resource allocation settings.

Abstract: Many real-world systems rely on predictive models to inform decisions, and fairness concerns arise in both the prediction and decision stages. We introduce end-to-end fairness optimization (E2EFO) as a unifying framework that integrates fairness across the prediction-to-decision pipeline. We focus on resource allocation with group-based fairness: the prediction task estimates allocation impacts while limiting accuracy disparity across groups, and the decision task distributes those impacts equitably by optimizing a group-based alpha-fairness measure. Within this framework, we propose fair decision-focused learning (FDFL), a training paradigm that jointly accounts for prediction accuracy, prediction fairness, and decision regret -- the loss in decision fairness due to imperfect predictions. FDFL trains the predictor by gradient descent, combining the objective gradients through multi-task learning techniques. The core computational challenge is the decision Jacobian with respect to the predictor parameters: we derive exact closed-form formulas for a tractable class of fair allocation and apply a differentiable optimization layer in the general case. We further establish a finite-sample generalization bound for the scalarized FDFL objective. Numerical experiments on a healthcare-based single resource allocation and a synthetic multiple resource allocation illustrate the value of jointly accounting for prediction fairness and decision fairness in prediction-informed decision-making.

</details>


### [162] [TFGformer: Multivariate Time Series Forecasting via Time-Frequency Graph Learning and Covariate Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29459)
*Yu Sun,Yuan Chang,Xiaohou Shi,Yan Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CrossRAG is a retrieval-augmented time series forecasting framework that addresses magnitude heterogeneity and history–future mismatch via shape-aware retrieval, future-consistent contrastive learning, and cross-attention temporal fusion, achieving SOTA on seven benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time series foundation models rely on static parametric knowledge and cannot dynamically access external historical patterns. While RAG could address this, its application to time series forecasting is hindered by two key challenges: (1) magnitude variations across heterogeneous IoT sensor sources that degrade similarity-based retrieval, and (2) a fundamental mismatch where historically similar series may diverge in the future, making naive retrieval potentially harmful.

Method: CrossRAG introduces three components: (1) Shape-Aware Memory (SAM) combined with RevIN normalization to enable magnitude-robust, shape-level retrieval from a historical reference pool; (2) Future-Consistent Contrastive (FCC) learning that constructs hard negatives — series with similar history but divergent futures — and uses contrastive loss to ensure retrieved references are both historically similar and future-consistent; (3) Cross-Attention Temporal Fusion (CATF), which fuses retrieved historical–future reference pairs into the backbone model's representations at the representation level via cross-attention mechanisms.

Result: Experiments on seven public benchmark datasets show that CrossRAG consistently outperforms both parametric-only baselines (including recent time series foundation models) and existing retrieval-augmented forecasting methods.

Conclusion: Retrieval-augmented generation can be effectively adapted to time series forecasting by explicitly addressing magnitude robustness and future consistency. CrossRAG provides a principled framework for dynamically leveraging external historical patterns, yielding consistent and significant improvements over static parametric models.

Abstract: Large-scale multivariate time series from heterogeneous IoT sensors demand accurate long-term forecasting for resource scheduling and predictive maintenance. While recent time series foundation models exhibit strong generalization, they rely on static parametric knowledge and lack dynamic access to external historical patterns during inference. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) offers a potential remedy, yet its application to time series forecasting is challenged by magnitude variations across heterogeneous sources and the mismatch between historical similarity and future consistency. We propose CrossRAG, a retrieval-augmented forecasting framework that integrates Shape-Aware Memory (SAM) with RevIN normalization for magnitude-robust shape-level retrieval, Future-Consistent Contrastive (FCC) learning to distinguish informative references from hard negatives with similar history but divergent futures, and Cross-Attention Temporal Fusion (CATF) to fuse retrieved historical--future reference pairs into the backbone's representations at the representation level. Experiments on seven public benchmarks show that CrossRAG consistently outperforms both parametric-only baselines and existing retrieval-augmented forecasting methods.

</details>


### [163] [Parameter-Free Heavy-Tailed Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29460)
*Gianmarco Genalti,Alberto Maria Metelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper resolves the open problem of adapting to unknown tail parameters in heavy-tailed bandits, establishing sharp regret trade-offs when the moment bound $u$ and tail exponent $ε$ are unknown, and providing a scheduled-exploration algorithm that achieves optimal adaptation without distributional assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heavy-tailed bandits model sequential decision-making where extreme outcomes dominate, but existing algorithms require knowledge of tail parameters ($ε$ and $u$) that are precisely the hardest to infer from limited data. The work is directly motivated by an open problem posed by Genalti and Metelli at COLT 2025.

Method: The authors first study adaptation to the moment bound $u$ with fixed $ε$, proving a fundamental trade-off between distribution-dependent and distribution-free regret. They then introduce a scheduled-exploration algorithm that requires no knowledge of $u$ and matches this adaptation frontier. For unknown $ε$, they calibrate the exploration schedule to the endpoint $ε=1$, achieving sublinear regret for any fixed $ε>0$ while proving that uniform sublinear regret over all $ε$ is impossible.

Result: (1) A sharp impossibility result: any algorithm unaware of $u$ must suffer a trade-off between distribution-dependent and distribution-free regret. (2) A scheduled-exploration algorithm matching the adaptation frontier up to logarithmic factors without knowing $u$. (3) The same algorithm, calibrated to $ε=1$, achieves sublinear regret for every fixed $ε>0$, with a matching impossibility result showing no algorithm can guarantee sublinear regret uniformly over all $ε$. This fully resolves the COLT 2025 open problem.

Conclusion: The paper provides a complete characterization of the statistical cost of adapting to unknown heavy tails in bandit problems, resolving the COLT open problem without additional distributional assumptions. The scheduled-exploration approach is both practical and theoretically tight.

Abstract: Heavy-tailed distributions arise naturally in sequential decision-making problems such as financial investment, online advertising, and network management, where rare but extreme outcomes can dominate performance. Heavy-tailed bandits model online decision-making in these settings by assuming only that rewards $X$ satisfy $\mathbb{E}[|X|^{1+ε}]\leq u$, for some tail exponent $ε\in(0,1]$ and moment bound $u<+\infty$. However, most existing regret minimization algorithms require these parameters to be known. This assumption is particularly restrictive in practice: $ε$ and $u$ govern the frequency and magnitude of rare events and are therefore precisely the quantities that are hardest to infer reliably from limited observations.
  Motivated by an open problem posed by Genalti and Metelli at COLT 2025, we resolve the assumption-free adaptation problem for heavy-tailed bandits and characterize the price in the regret of not knowing the tail parameters. We first study adaptation to the moment bound $u$ for a fixed tail exponent $ε$. We prove that every algorithm unaware of $u$, or of any upper bound on it, must obey a sharp trade-off between its distribution-dependent and distribution-free regret guarantees. We then introduce a scheduled-exploration algorithm that requires no knowledge of $u$ and matches the resulting adaptation frontier up to logarithmic factors. Finally, we show that the same algorithm can be instanced without knowing $ε$ by calibrating its exploration schedule to the endpoint $ε=1$. It achieves sublinear regret for every fixed $ε>0$, while no algorithm can guarantee sublinear regret uniformly over all $ε\in(0,1]$. Altogether, our results resolve the COLT open problem without additional distributional assumptions and provide a sharp characterization of the statistical cost of adapting to unknown heavy tails.

</details>


### [164] [MolGVR: A Chemistry-Grounded Framework for Text-to-Molecule Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29479)
*Qian Tan,Xuanyu Zhu,Lei Jiang,Zhonghang Yuan,Chen Zhang,Yuqiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MolGVR introduces a three-stage Generator-Verifier-Refiner framework that grounds text-to-molecule generation in executable chemical constraints, improving exact-match accuracy by verifying structural correctness and iteratively refining rejected candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-molecule generation treats the task as one-shot sequence generation, ignoring that molecular descriptions impose hard structural constraints. Violating these constraints changes molecular identity, yet chemical verification and error correction remain underexplored.

Method: MolGVR consists of three components: (1) a Generator that infers structural evidence from descriptions and produces candidate molecules; (2) a Verifier that converts textual descriptions into executable chemical constraints and checks candidate compliance; (3) a Refiner that revises candidates rejected by the Verifier using feedback-guided revision.

Result: Experiments on the ChEBI-20 and PCDes benchmarks demonstrate that MolGVR achieves improved exact-match performance over baseline approaches.

Conclusion: Coupling generation with executable chemical verification and feedback-guided refinement is an effective strategy for improving text-to-molecule generation quality and reliability.

Abstract: Text-to-molecule generation is typically formulated as a one-shot sequence generation problem, where a model directly maps target descriptions to molecular representations. However, molecular descriptions often contain informative structural constraints, and violating such constraints can change the molecular identity. This makes chemical verification and error correction important but underexplored. To fill this gap, we propose MolGVR, a chemistry-grounded Generator--Verifier--Refiner framework. The Generator infers structural evidence and generates candidate molecules. The Verifier addresses the lack of chemical validation by converting descriptions into chemical constraints and checking candidates against them. The Refiner addresses generation failures by revising candidates rejected by the Verifier. Experiments on ChEBI-20 and PCDes show that MolGVR improves exact-match performance. These results suggest that coupling generation with executable verification and feedback-guided refinement is an effective way to improve text-to-molecule generation.

</details>


### [165] [DreamQAS: Learning a Decision-Useful World Model for VQE-Efficient Quantum Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29491)
*Jiayang Niu,Yan Wang,Jie Li,Ke Deng,Azadeh Alavi,Muhammad Usman,Yongli Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DreamQAS introduces a model-based RL framework for quantum architecture search that learns only the expensive post-VQE feedback using a recurrent randomized-prior ensemble, achieving superior sample efficiency (1.6x–10.6x fewer VQE calls) and lower energy error across molecular tasks compared to standard RL-QAS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL-QAS wastefully re-optimizes VQE after every circuit extension step despite circuit construction dynamics being fully deterministic and known. The key insight is that the expensive part is not the circuit dynamics but the post-VQE energy feedback, motivating a world model that preserves exact dynamics and learns only what is costly and uncertain.

Method: DreamQAS uses a model-based RL framework with: (1) a recurrent randomized-prior ensemble that predicts an oracle-free score relative to an empirical energy frontier; (2) multi-step imagined policy learning over explicit legal circuits using ranking-based activation; (3) uncertainty-aware pessimism and truncation for reliability; and (4) selective real-VQE verification forming a reliability-controlled learning loop.

Result: Under a 15,000-episode budget, DreamQAS achieves the lowest mean frozen-policy energy error on 4/5 molecular tasks and second-lowest on one. At fine-error targets, it uses 1.6x–2.0x fewer VQE calls on four tasks and 10.6x fewer on BeH2-8q. Counterfactual action-ranking utility increases across all tasks (mean +0.346, 95% CI [0.185, 0.507]). Direct greedy/beam use of the same model does not recover the gains. Ensemble disagreement improves risk-coverage over random rejection on all probed tasks.

Conclusion: The results establish a world-model design paradigm for QAS where value lies in decision-useful feedback rather than exact energy prediction, demonstrating that model-based RL with learned feedback can significantly outperform model-free approaches in sample-constrained quantum architecture search.

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning-based quantum architecture search (RL-QAS) repeatedly optimizes a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) after extending a circuit, although circuit construction and action legality are deterministic and known. We introduce DreamQAS, a model-based RL framework that preserves these exact circuit dynamics and learns only the expensive post-VQE feedback. A recurrent randomized-prior ensemble predicts an oracle-free score relative to an empirical energy frontier and supports multi-step imagined policy learning over explicit legal circuits. Ranking-based activation, uncertainty-aware pessimism and truncation, and selective real-VQE verification form a reliability-controlled learning loop. Under a common 15,000-episode budget and frozen evaluation for the RL methods, DreamQAS has the lowest mean frozen-policy energy error on four of five molecular tasks and the second-lowest on one. At fine-error targets reached by all seeds of both methods, it uses 1.6x to 2.0x fewer real VQE calls on four tasks and 10.6x fewer on BeH2-8q. Counterfactual action-ranking utility increases across all five tasks, with a mean increase of 0.346 and a 95 percent confidence interval of [0.185, 0.507], while direct greedy and beam use of the same model does not recover the gains of imagined policy learning. Ensemble disagreement also improves risk-coverage over random rejection on all three probed tasks. These results establish a world-model design for QAS whose value lies in decision-useful feedback rather than exact energy prediction.

</details>


### [166] [Adaptive FastOPD: Progress-Aware Rollout Horizon Expansion for Efficient On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29494)
*Qian Tan,Huaifei Liang,Xuanyu Zhu,Lei Jiang,Yuqiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive FastOPD introduces a progress-aware rollout horizon expansion strategy for on-policy distillation that uses relative teacher-student signals to trigger horizon increases only when learning plateaus, achieving 49–71% training time reduction versus OPD 15K while maintaining high performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation (OPD) produces strong results via dense teacher supervision along student trajectories, but online rollouts incur high computational cost due to long responses delaying batch completion. Existing acceleration methods use fixed rollout budgets or absolute agreement thresholds, which fail to account for varying learning dynamics across different models and training stages.

Method: Adaptive FastOPD expands the rollout horizon only when two conditions are met: (1) learning near the current boundary region has plateaued, as measured by four teacher-student signals evaluated relative to their values upon entering each horizon (making expansion responsive to stage-specific progress rather than fixed intervals or absolute thresholds), and (2) the current horizon is sufficiently utilized, preventing a small number of long responses from triggering premature cost increases.

Result: Across two teacher-student pairs, Adaptive FastOPD achieves the highest average performance while reducing training time by 49.1–71.2% relative to OPD 15K, and demonstrates robustness across a range of hyperparameter settings.

Conclusion: A progress-aware, relative-signal-based adaptive horizon expansion strategy effectively accelerates on-policy distillation without sacrificing performance, offering a robust alternative to fixed-budget or absolute-threshold-based approaches.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense teacher supervision along student-generated trajectories, but its online rollout process incurs substantial computational cost, particularly when a few long responses delay batch completion. Existing acceleration methods typically control rollout length using fixed budgets or absolute teacher--student agreement thresholds, which may not reflect learning progress across different models and training stages. We propose Adaptive FastOPD, a progress-aware strategy that expands the rollout horizon only when learning near the current boundary region has plateaued and the current horizon is sufficiently utilized. The former is determined from four teacher--student signals measured relative to their values upon entering each horizon, making expansion responsive to stage-specific progress rather than a predefined step interval or an absolute threshold on the raw agreement signals, while the latter prevents a small number of long responses from triggering increases in rollout cost. Across two teacher--student pairs, Adaptive FastOPD achieves the highest average performance while reducing training time by 49.1--71.2\% relative to OPD 15K, and remains robust across a range of hyperparameter settings.

</details>


### [167] [The Grokked Illusion: True Equilibrium Mitigates Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29503)
*Xiaotian Zhang,Lai Shun Chan,Yue Shang,Entao Yang,Ge Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: High-entropy neural networks, sampled via Wang-Landau Molecular Dynamics, exhibit significantly greater robustness against catastrophic forgetting than conventionally AdamW-trained models with identical test performance, revealing that perfect generalization masks hidden fragility — termed the "grokked illusion."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior work established that high-entropy solutions generalize better, it remains unknown whether this entropy advantage extends to robustness — specifically, a model's ability to retain learned knowledge when subsequently trained on new information (resistance to catastrophic forgetting).

Method: Using grokking in modular arithmetic as a controlled setting, the authors compare AdamW-trained transformers against high-entropy models sampled via Wang-Landau Molecular Dynamics, both at identical saturated performance. A noise injection experiment forces both models to memorize new data with random labels, after which original task accuracy is measured. Singular value decomposition (SVD) of network weights is used to analyze effective rank in attention and MLP layers.

Result: AdamW-trained models suffer severe catastrophic forgetting (test accuracy drops from 100% to below 75%), while high-entropy models retain approximately 95% accuracy. SVD reveals high-entropy networks possess significantly higher effective rank in both attention and MLP layers before and after noise injection, suggesting richer feature representations act as a buffer against forgetting.

Conclusion: Perfect generalization does not imply equal robustness. The higher effective rank in high-entropy models provides a mechanistic explanation for their resilience, offering a new perspective on what makes trained models robust to interference.

Abstract: While neural networks are typically evaluated by their training and test performance, these metrics do not reveal how robust a learned representation is. Recent studies have shown that solutions occupying larger volumes in parameter space, as quantified by Boltzmann entropy, often exhibit superior generalizability compared to those reached by conventional optimization, a phenomenon known as the high entropy advantage. Here we ask whether this advantage persists beyond generalization. Specifically, we investigate models' robustness, the ability to retain the learned knowledge when the model is subsequently trained to acquire new information. Using grokking in modular arithmetic as a controlled setting, we design a noise injection experiment to evaluate the robustness difference between AdamW-trained transformers and high-entropy model sampled from Wang-Landau Molecular Dynamics with identical saturated performance. By forcing both models to fully remember new data with random labels, we find that AdamW-trained models suffer from catastrophic forgetting, with original task test accuracy dropping from 100% to below 75%, whereas the high-entropy models maintain approximately 95% test accuracy. We term this hidden fragility behind apparent generalization the "grokked illusion." Through singular value decomposition of the neural network weights, we discover that high-entropy neural networks possess significantly higher effective rank in attention and MLP layers both before and after noise injection, indicating richer feature representations can serve as a buffer against catastrophic forgetting. Our findings demonstrate that perfect generalization does not imply equal robustness, offering a new perspective on what makes a trained model robust to interference.

</details>


### [168] [TerraNova: A Foundation Model for the Anthropocene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29527)
*Carlos Rodriguez-Pardo,Massimo Tavoni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TerraNova is a foundation model that jointly represents gridded Earth-system fields and national-level societal indicators in their native geometries, using cross-modal transformers, hypernetworks, and contrastive objectives to bridge the geometric gap between continuous physical data and administrative-boundary social data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modeling the coupled Earth-human system is hindered by a fundamental geometric mismatch: physical Earth data are continuous fields ignoring political borders, while societal data are reported for discrete administrative units. Existing Earth-system foundation models operate only in the gridded geometry, and coupling them to societal data requires lossy averaging across borders.

Method: TerraNova is trained on 512 gridded Earth-system fields and 512 national indicators. It uses dedicated encoders for location, country, time, and task; cross-modal transformers to fuse them into a shared spatiotemporal state; a hypernetwork to generate per-query decoders; and an evidential head for predictive distributions. Two contrastive objectives couple the representation: population-weighted alignment between countries and their territorial coordinates, and alignment with pretrained geospatial embeddings that capture image-derived semantics.

Result: The representation is competitive with purpose-built geospatial encoders while spanning axes they lack (time, oceans, uncertainty) and supporting country-level tasks. The frozen backbone can reconstruct dense fields from sparse observations and adapt to unseen variables in minutes on consumer hardware.

Conclusion: TerraNova successfully bridges the geometric divide between physical and societal data modalities, producing a unified spatiotemporal representation that is both versatile and efficient, enabling downstream tasks across both geometries with minimal fine-tuning.

Abstract: A defining problem of the Anthropocene is to model the physical Earth and human societies as one coupled system, yet no learned representation spans their observational breadth. We argue the obstacle is geometric: the physical Earth is measured as continuous fields that ignore political borders, whereas societies are reported for administrative units. Earth-system foundation models serve the first geometry; coupling it to the second has required lossy averaging over borders. We introduce TerraNova, a foundation model trained on 1,024 physical and societal records in their native geometries: 512 gridded Earth-system fields and 512 national indicators. Dedicated encoders represent location, country, time and task, cross-modal transformers fuse them into a shared spatiotemporal state, and a hypernetwork generates a per-query decoder whose evidential head returns a predictive distribution. Two contrastive objectives couple the representation: a population-weighted alignment between each country and coordinates in its territory, and one to pretrained geospatial embeddings carrying image-derived semantics. Read out through that decoder, the representation is competitive with purpose-built geospatial encoders while spanning axes they do not represent (time, oceans and uncertainty) and supporting country-level capabilities. The frozen backbone reconstructs dense fields from sparse observations and adapts to unseen variables in minutes on consumer hardware.

</details>


### [169] [A Neurosymbolic Approach for Explainable Early Diagnosis of Alzheimer's Disease](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29530)
*Ranveer Singh,Pranuthi Tenali,Saurabh Mathur,Ameet Soni,Vaishali Phatak,Karla Lynch,Daniel Murman,Matthew Rizzo,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An automated pipeline uses pretrained foundation models to extract clinical variables from verbal fluency test audio, constructing a Bayesian Network that both recovers known Alzheimer's disease markers and discovers novel linguistic marker relationships.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual transcription and expert analysis for identifying Alzheimer's disease markers is labor-intensive and limits scalability, creating a need for automated methods that can extract qualitative knowledge about AD progression directly from raw audio.

Method: An automated pipeline leveraging pretrained foundation models processes raw audio recordings of verbal fluency tests to extract clinically relevant variables, which are then used to construct a Bayesian Network (BN) for reasoning about AD progression markers and their qualitative relationships.

Result: The system successfully recovers known clinical knowledge about AD progression and identifies novel relationships between linguistic markers that were previously unrecognized.

Conclusion: Foundation model-based audio processing combined with Bayesian Network reasoning offers a scalable, automated alternative to manual AD marker identification, capable of both validating established clinical knowledge and uncovering new linguistic indicators of disease progression.

Abstract: Identifying reliable Alzheimer's disease (AD) markers typically requires manual, labor-intensive transcription and expert analysis, limiting its scale. We introduce an automated pipeline that extracts qualitative knowledge about potential AD progression indicators directly from audio recordings of verbal fluency tests. Our method uses pretrained foundation models to process raw audio and extract clinically relevant variables to construct a Bayesian Network (BN); this BN is used to reason about the AD progression markers and infer their qualitative relationships. Our system successfully recovers known clinical knowledge and identifies novel relationships between linguistic markers.

</details>


### [170] [Pyramidal Width Can Increase Under Vertex Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29555)
*Jinze Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper refutes a 2015 conjecture by Lacoste-Julien and Jaggi by providing an explicit counterexample in ℝ³ where adding a vertex to a polytope strictly increases its pyramidal width, contrary to the claim that pyramidal width is monotone non-increasing under vertex addition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The pyramidal width is a key geometric parameter in the analysis of Frank-Wolfe algorithms and conditional gradient methods. Lacoste-Julien and Jaggi conjectured that pyramidal width cannot increase when a vertex is added to a polytope (with all original vertices preserved). Resolving this conjecture has implications for convergence rate guarantees in optimization.

Method: The authors construct an explicit counterexample with six integer-coordinate points in ℝ³. They use the equivalence between pyramidal width and facial distance, certify both face lattices using integer supporting hyperplanes, and compute all facial distances exactly via finite rational arithmetic. A dependency-free exact verifier is provided to confirm the computation.

Result: The polytope P (5 vertices) has squared pyramidal width 48/353, while Q (6 vertices, containing all vertices of P) has squared pyramidal width 36/133. This yields an increase factor of approximately 1.4109, definitively disproving the conjecture.

Conclusion: The Lacoste-Julien–Jaggi conjecture is false. Vertex addition can increase pyramidal width, which means theoretical analyses relying on the monotonicity assumption may require revision.

Abstract: Lacoste-Julien and Jaggi conjectured in 2015 that the pyramidal width of a polytope cannot increase when a vertex is added, provided that every old point remains a vertex. We give an exact counterexample with six integer points in $\R^3$. For \[ P=\conv\{v_0,\ldots,v_4\},\qquad Q=\conv\{v_0,\ldots,v_5\}, \] where \[ \begin{aligned} v_0&=(-1,-3,-1), & v_1&=(3,2,-2), & v_2&=(0,2,1),\\ v_3&=(-1,-3,3), & v_4&=(-2,0,1), & v_5&=(-1,0,-2), \end{aligned} \] all five vertices of $P$ remain vertices of $Q$, but \[ \PWidth(P)^2=\frac{48}{353} \quad\text{and}\quad \PWidth(Q)^2=\frac{36}{133}. \] Thus vertex insertion increases pyramidal width by the factor $\sqrt{1059/532}\approx 1.410886779$. The proof uses the equivalence between pyramidal width and facial distance, certifies both face lattices by integer supporting hyperplanes, and evaluates every facial distance by a finite rational calculation. A dependency-free exact verifier accompanies the paper.

</details>


### [171] [MOT-SR: Multi-Objective Tool-Augmented Scientific Equation Discovery with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29561)
*Boxiao Wang,Runxiang Wang,Kai Li,Chongming Li,Zhiwei Chen,Yifan Zhang,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MOT-SR integrates LLMs with external analytical tools and multi-objective optimization (accuracy, complexity, generalization) to discover interpretable equations from data, outperforming existing methods on standard benchmarks and an EMRI gravitational-wave modeling task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based symbolic regression methods lack mechanisms for uncovering variable dependencies and rely solely on fitting error for evaluation, leading to premature convergence to local optima and failure to explore the broader equation space.

Method: MOT-SR is a closed-loop framework with two collaborative LLM modules: a Meta Strategy Generator that selects analytical tools and synthesizes structural optimization strategies based on a dynamic Pareto front, and an Equation Generator that produces candidate equations. The framework jointly optimizes for accuracy, complexity, and generalization using multi-objective evaluation.

Result: Across 40 standard SR tasks, MOT-SR outperforms existing methods in accuracy, generalization, and efficiency. On EMRI orbital modeling, it discovers an interpretable correction achieving the lowest trajectory-level integration error on held-out configurations.

Conclusion: MOT-SR demonstrates that combining LLMs with external tools and multi-objective optimization enables reliable, interpretable modeling of long-horizon scientific dynamics, with strong potential for gravitational-wave astronomy and beyond.

Abstract: Symbolic Regression (SR) aims to discover analytical equations from observational data and plays a central role in scientific modeling. While recent Large Language Model (LLM) based approaches show promise, they face two limitations. First, they lack data analysis mechanisms for uncovering variable dependencies, which reduces the efficiency of equation discovery. Second, most methods rely on single-objective evaluation focused solely on fitting error. This neglect of structural complexity and generalization often causes models to converge prematurely to local optima, limiting their ability to explore the broader equation space. We propose Multi-Objective Tool-augmented Symbolic Regression (MOT-SR), a unified framework that integrates external analytical tools to extract structural priors and guide equation generation, while jointly optimizing for accuracy, complexity, and generalization via a multi-objective evaluation module that maintains a dynamic Pareto front. MOT-SR employs two collaborative LLM modules: a Meta Strategy Generator, which selects tools and synthesizes structural optimization strategies based on Pareto-optimal equations, and an Equation Generator, which produces new candidate equations accordingly. The system operates in a closed-loop manner, continuously refining both strategies and equation structures. Across 40 standard tasks, MOT-SR outperforms existing SR methods in accuracy, generalization, and efficiency. We further validate MOT-SR on extreme mass-ratio inspiral (EMRI) orbital modeling, an important problem in space-based gravitational-wave astronomy where small local errors can accumulate substantially over long-term evolution. The discovered interpretable correction achieves the lowest trajectory-level integration error on held-out configurations. These results demonstrate the potential of MOT-SR to enable reliable modeling of long-horizon scientific dynamics.

</details>


### [172] [Convergence and Regret of the Policy Gradient for Multi-Armed Bandits in Diffusion Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29593)
*Yanwei Jia,Du Ouyang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes policy gradient for a multi-arm bandit in a diffusion environment modeled by an SDE. Using logit parameterization, it proves almost sure convergence to the optimal arm under constant learning rates and derives an O(log T) regret bound. A novel Lyapunov function improves upon prior analysis and bridges continuous- and discrete-time settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the theoretical understanding of policy gradient methods in continuous-time reinforcement learning, specifically addressing convergence and regret in a diffusion-modeled multi-arm bandit, and to refine the prior analysis by Lattimore (2026a) through a more transparent SDE-based approach.

Method: The authors employ the continuous-time RL framework (Wang et al., 2020; Jia & Zhou, 2022b), logit parameterization of stochastic policies, and construct a novel Lyapunov function to analyze the SDE governing the policy gradient update. This Lyapunov approach is applied to both continuous-time (diffusion) and discrete-time settings.

Result: (1) Almost sure convergence to the optimal arm under arbitrary constant learning rates. (2) Non-asymptotic regret upper bound of order O(log T) when the learning rate is below a time-invariant threshold. The novel Lyapunov function yields a cleaner, more transparent analysis than prior work.

Conclusion: Policy gradient with logit parameterization is provably efficient for diffusion bandits, achieving logarithmic regret and almost sure optimal-arm convergence. The Lyapunov-based SDE analysis offers a unifying and transparent framework applicable to both continuous- and discrete-time policy gradient algorithms.

Abstract: This paper studies the policy gradient update for a multi-arm bandit problem in diffusion environment that is described by a stochastic differential equation (SDE) under the continuous-time reinforcement learning framework by Wang et al. (2020), Jia and Zhou (2022b). With the logit parameterization for the stochastic policy, we show that it converges almost surely to the optimal arm under an arbitrary constant learning rate. Furthermore, we derive the non-asymptotic regret upper bound when the constant learning rate is below a time-invariant threshold; and the regret bound has order $O(\log T)$. We improve the analysis in Lattimore (2026a) for the same SDE by constructing a novel Lyapunov function and demonstrate the transparency of analyzing policy gradient using the tools in SDEs. In addition, the same Lyapunov function is also helpful in analyzing the discrete-time policy gradient algorithm.

</details>


### [173] [The Parts Are Greater Than the Sum: Automated Task Sequencing for Efficient Training of Multi-Policy LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29601)
*Jiajia Tang,Sizhe Yuen,Francisco Gomez Medina,Yali Du,Adam Sobey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an automatic multi-policy PEFT framework that organizes optimization paths via task grouping and sequencing, using independent QLoRA adapters to decouple heterogeneous tasks while preserving positive transfer, outperforming single-adapter approaches under fixed parameter budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single shared LoRA adapters suffer from optimization interference when fine-tuning on heterogeneous task sequences, causing poor knowledge transfer and catastrophic forgetting. Existing solutions increase parameter capacity or compose multiple adapters but still rely on a shared optimization path, which fails to address the fundamental interference problem.

Method: An optimization-path organization framework that automatically groups compatible tasks and sequences them under a fixed parameter budget. Each group gets an independent Quantized Low-Rank Adapter (QLoRA), creating decoupled adaptation spaces. This multi-policy architecture enables heterogeneous tasks to optimize separately while maintaining positive transfer within compatible task groups.

Result: On the TRACE benchmark, the proposed automatic multi-policy PEFT framework achieved 44.78, outperforming conventional single-policy PEFT approaches under identical trainable capacity constraints. Results demonstrate that organizing optimization paths is more effective than simply increasing adapter capacity.

Conclusion: Optimization-path organization via automatic task grouping and sequencing provides a more effective strategy for heterogeneous PEFT than merely scaling adapter parameters. Decoupling incompatible tasks into independent QLoRA adapters mitigates interference while preserving positive transfer among compatible tasks.

Abstract: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) commonly adapts large language models using a single shared Low-Rank Adapter (LoRA). This shared optimization space often suffers from interference when adapting heterogeneous task sequences, leading to poor transfer and catastrophic forgetting. Existing approaches mainly improve adapter expressiveness by increasing parameter capacity or composing multiple adapters, yet they still rely on a shared optimization path. In this paper, we propose an optimization-path organization framework for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models, implemented as an automatic multi-policy PEFT architecture. Specifically, optimization-compatible adaptation paths are automatically organized through task grouping and task sequencing under a fixed parameter budget. The organized optimization paths are implemented as independent Quantized Low-Rank Adapters (QLoRA), enabling heterogeneous tasks to be optimized in decoupled adaptation spaces while preserving positive transfer among compatible tasks. Experiments on the TRACE benchmark demonstrate that performance consistently improves from conventional single-policy PEFT to multi-policy PEFT, with the proposed automatic multi-policy framework achieving the best performance of 44.78 under the same trainable capacity. This suggests that optimization-path organization is more effective than simply increasing adapter capacity for heterogeneous parameter-efficient fine-tuning.

</details>


### [174] [A Human-Centered Validation of the Explainability-Performance Coefficient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29614)
*Christian Oliva,Luis F. Lago-Fernández*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces the EPC score, a model-agnostic metric extending the Explainability-Performance Coefficient to quantify explanation quality by balancing sparsity and preserved model performance. Validated across tabular, text, and image modalities, the metric reveals operational dependencies among activations, dimensionality, and explainer performance, and correlates strongly with human judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing XAI evaluation lacks objective metrics that reliably measure explanation fidelity and align with human-centered understanding. There is an unmet need for a principled, model-agnostic method to quantify the trade-off between explanation sparsity and fidelity to the original model's performance.

Method: The authors propose the EPC score, an extension of the Explainability-Performance Coefficient, which explicitly balances feature selection sparsity against preserved model performance. The metric is empirically validated across three modalities (tabular, text, image) and benchmarked against independent human-derived explanations, including lexical sentiment judgments and spatial visual annotations.

Result: The EPC score effectively uncovers operational dependencies among network activations, data dimensionality, and explainer performance. Higher EPC scores show strong alignment with human lexical sentiment judgments and spatial visual annotations, demonstrating the metric's reliability as a proxy for human-centered explanation quality.

Conclusion: The EPC score provides a robust, model-agnostic framework for objectively evaluating explanation quality, bridging the gap between quantitative fidelity metrics and human interpretability. It offers a practical tool for selecting and benchmarking XAI methods across diverse data modalities.

Abstract: The rapid adoption of deep learning models in high-risk domains has intensified the need for trustworthy Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI). However, objectively evaluating explanation fidelity and aligning XAI metrics with human-centered understanding remain critical open challenges. In this work, we propose a model-agnostic metric, the EPC score, which is an extension of the Explainability-Performance Coefficient (EPC), that quantifies explanation quality by explicitly balancing the trade-off between feature selection sparsity and preserved model performance. Through an empirical validation across tabular, text, and image modalities, we show that the EPC score effectively uncovers operational dependencies among network activations, data dimensionality, and explainer performance. Furthermore, we validate the EPC score against independent human-based explanations, proving that higher EPC scores strongly align with human lexical sentiment judgments and spatial visual annotations.

</details>


### [175] [When Does On-Policy Interaction Help? Representational Tradeoffs in Value-Based Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29617)
*Luca Viano,Antoine Moulin,Audrey Huang,Volkan Cevher,Philip Amortila,Dylan J. Foster*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that interactive (on-policy) imitation learning fundamentally relaxes representational requirements: a learner only needs to realize the expert's value function rather than the expert's policy. The authors propose OVI, an algorithm that is statistically efficient under value-function realizability with a linear maximization oracle, prove that interaction is necessary for this relaxation, and demonstrate empirically that OVI outperforms both offline and interactive policy-based baselines, especially when the learner is less expressive than the expert.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Behavior Cloning suffers from compounding errors and plateaus when the learner cannot perfectly represent the expert's policy (a common scenario in distillation). While interactive querying and value-function methods are known to help empirically, the theoretical nature of these improvements and their interplay remains poorly understood. The paper seeks to formally characterize why interaction helps and what structural conditions enable efficient IL.

Method: The authors introduce OVI (Online Value-based Imitation), an interactive on-policy IL algorithm. Theoretically, they prove that under expert value-function realizability (a weaker condition than policy realizability), OVI achieves statistical efficiency with access to a linear maximization oracle. They also prove a lower bound: without interaction, any offline IL algorithm must scale with the complexity of the expert policy class, showing interaction is necessary to relax representational requirements. Empirically, they compare OVI against BC (offline policy-based), DAgger (interactive policy-based), and offline value-based methods across varying learner expressiveness.

Result: OVI provably achieves statistical efficiency under value-function realizability alone. The lower bound establishes that offline methods cannot match this without interaction (or stronger assumptions). Empirically, OVI consistently outperforms all baselines—BC, DAgger, and offline value-based IL—with the largest performance gains observed when the learner network is substantially less expressive than the expert's.

Conclusion: Interactive imitation learning fundamentally reduces the representational burden from policy realizability to value-function realizability. OVI leverages this insight to achieve strong theoretical guarantees and empirical performance, particularly in the practically relevant regime where the learner is less expressive than the expert (e.g., distillation).

Abstract: Imitation learning (IL)---training an agent to replicate expert behavior from demonstrations---underpins applications from robotics to language model training. Standard approaches such as Behavior Cloning (BC) are known to suffer from compounding errors and performance plateaus, particularly when the learner cannot perfectly represent the expert's policy (as is typical, e.g., in distillation). Two interventions are widely understood empirically to improve performance: querying the expert interactively along the learner's own trajectories, and using value function estimation en route to generating a policy rather than directly fitting the expert's full action distribution.
  We investigate the nature of these improvements and their potentially surprising interplay. Our main finding is that expert interaction relaxes the representational demands on the learner: one only needs a model capable of realizing the expert's value function, bypassing the (often stricter) requirement of realizing the expert's policy itself. Concretely, we introduce OVI, an interactive on-policy IL algorithm that is statistically efficient whenever the learner can represent the expert's value function and computationally efficient given access to a linear maximization oracle. We complement this with a negative result showing that interaction is necessary. Namely, without stronger assumptions beyond expert-value realizability alone, any offline IL algorithm must scale with the complexity of the expert policy class. Our findings bear out empirically. OVI outperforms offline policy-based (BC), interactive policy-based (DAgger), and offline value-based IL methods, with the largest gains when the learner network is substantially less expressive than the expert's.

</details>


### [176] [CENDRe: Concept Extraction with Natural Domain Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29621)
*Antonia Holzapfel,Andres Felipe Posada Moreno,Sebastian Trimpe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CENDRe is a concept extraction method for CNN-based time-series classifiers that automatically discovers, localizes, and scores concepts in both time and frequency domains, overcoming limitations of prior methods that are time-only, require pre-specifying concept counts, and produce misaligned localizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying CNNs for time-series classification in critical domains demands interpretability—understanding the temporal and spectral patterns driving predictions. Existing concept extraction methods suffer from three shortcomings: (1) they operate exclusively in the time domain, ignoring frequency-domain features; (2) they require users to predefine the number of concepts; and (3) their concept localizations are misaligned with the regions the model actually uses.

Method: CENDRe operates in three stages. First, it discovers concepts via two-stage clustering of per-timestep latent representations, using silhouette-guided aggregation to automatically determine the number of concepts. Second, it localizes each concept by computing gradients of a presence score that contrasts latent representations with prototypes, yielding time-domain masks; these gradients are propagated through a differentiable invertible mapping (e.g., Fourier transform) to produce frequency-domain localizations for the same concepts. Third, it assigns each concept a relevance score quantifying its contribution to each class.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks, CENDRe achieves representation correctness comparable to state-of-the-art CE methods and significantly higher importance correctness. On real bearing-fault data, it successfully extracts frequency bands driving model predictions—bands aligned with regions commonly inspected in fault diagnosis—providing evidence for model assessment that time-domain-only CE methods cannot produce.

Conclusion: CENDRe advances time-series model interpretability by unifying time- and frequency-domain concept extraction with automatic concept-count selection and faithful localization, enabling more trustworthy CNN deployment in safety-critical applications like fault diagnosis.

Abstract: Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are widely used for time-series classification, but their deployment in critical domains requires understanding the temporal and spectral patterns that drive their predictions. Concept extraction (CE) methods identify such patterns by analyzing representations within the models' latent space. However, existing time-series CE methods have three limitations: they operate only in the time domain and overlook frequency features, predefine the number of concepts, and produce localizations misaligned with the regions the model uses. We address these limitations by proposing CENDRe, a concept extraction method for CNNs. It first discovers concepts by clustering per-timestep latent representations in two stages, where silhouette-guided aggregation selects the number of concepts automatically. Then, it localizes each concept through gradients of a presence score that contrasts the latent representations with their prototypes, producing masks that concentrate on the regions driving the concept. These gradients, propagated through a differentiable invertible mapping of the input such as a Fourier transform, yield localizations for the same concepts in the frequency domain. Finally, each concept receives a relevance score that quantifies its contribution to each class. On synthetic benchmarks, CENDRe achieves representation correctness comparable to state-of-the-art CE methods and significantly higher importance correctness. On real bearing-fault data, CENDRe extracts the frequency bands driving the model's predictions, located in regions commonly inspected for fault diagnosis, producing evidence to assess the model that time-domain CE methods cannot.

</details>


### [177] [GQ-FSL: Green Quantized Federated Split Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29659)
*Idan Roth,Lutz Lampe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes GQ-FSL, a green quantized federated split learning framework that uses stochastic quantization with asymmetric precision across client/server submodels to minimize energy consumption while meeting accuracy constraints, outperforming quantized FL and full-precision FSL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying deep neural networks on resource-constrained wireless edge devices faces severe energy bottlenecks. While federated split learning (FSL) offloads computation to edge servers, the continuous exchange of cut-layer activations/gradients and submodel parameters still incurs significant energy consumption, creating a systemic overhead that needs to be addressed.

Method: The GQ-FSL framework integrates stochastic quantization into both local collaborative training and wireless transmission, supporting asymmetric quantization precision levels for client-side and server-side submodels. The authors develop parameterized energy models for the split architecture, derive a theoretical convergence bound under statistically heterogeneous (non-IID) data, and formulate a joint optimization problem to select the optimal DNN split point and precision levels that minimize total system energy consumption subject to a target accuracy constraint.

Result: GQ-FSL enables large-scale DNN deployment on resource-constrained devices and achieves superior energy efficiency compared to both quantized federated learning and full-precision federated split learning baselines.

Conclusion: Asymmetric precision quantization in federated split learning effectively decouples device-side energy constraints from global model convergence, making GQ-FSL a practical solution for energy-efficient DNN inference/training at the wireless edge under heterogeneous data and resource conditions.

Abstract: Deploying state-of-the-art deep neural networks (DNNs) at the wireless edge is severely bottlenecked by the strict energy and resource constraints of mobile devices. While federated split learning (FSL) mitigates on-device computation by offloading workloads to an edge server, this may introduce systemic overheads, while the continuous exchange of cut-layer data, and submodels still incurs significant energy consumption (EC). To address this, we propose a green quantized FSL (GQ-FSL) framework that incorporates stochastic quantization for both local collaborative training and wireless transmissions. Notably, GQ-FSL supports asymmetric precision levels for the client- and server-side submodels, effectively decoupling device energy constraints from global convergence degradation. To quantify these tradeoffs, we develop parameterized energy models for the split architecture and derive a theoretical convergence bound under statistically heterogeneous data. Building on that, we formulate a joint optimization problem to configure the DNN split point and precision levels, minimizing the total system EC while satisfying a strict target accuracy constraint. Ultimately, we demonstrate that GQ-FSL enables large-scale DNN deployment on resource-constrained devices, achieving superior energy efficiency compared to quantized federated learning and full-precision FSL.

</details>


### [178] [Freeze, Then Select: Structured Field Adapters and Stability-Validated Weak Selection for PDE Discovery from Sparse Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29665)
*Juncheng Zhong,Chenghuang Shen,Jianfeng Liu,Zhengdong Xiao,Longjiu Luo,Qianrong Wang,Wenjun Xu,Wenlian Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A freeze-then-select method for PDE discovery from sparse observations that decouples field reconstruction from equation selection, achieving state-of-the-art exact support recovery across MDBench regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural PDE discovery from sparse data suffers from coupling between continuous field reconstruction and differential term selection, where optimization paths exhibit unstable support patterns (persistent, transient, or absent). This coupling undermines reliable equation identification.

Method: A two-stage approach: (1) a structured field adapter factorizes the field into learned spatial features and cubic-spline temporal coefficients, trained purely from observations without PDE residuals; (2) after freezing the field, Stability-Validated Weak Selection (SVWS) identifies recurrent terms across independent weak-form systems, refits candidate supports, and selects the final equation on held-out weak-form data. The method extends beyond fixed libraries to genetic programming for discovering unknown nonlinear functions.

Result: The method achieves the highest exact support recovery rate across all six sparse MDBench regimes, with the largest gains over classical and neural baselines on Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics. It also successfully recovers the power-law form of an unknown nonlinear diffusion function from sparse, noisy observations.

Conclusion: Decoupling field reconstruction from equation selection via freeze-then-select resolves optimization-path instability in neural PDE discovery, enabling robust and accurate PDE identification even under sparse, noisy conditions.

Abstract: PDE discovery from sparse observations requires reconstructing a continuous field and selecting the correct differential terms. Our analysis of optimization paths in coupled neural PDE discovery reveals three behaviors: the exact support can persist to the end of training, appear only transiently, or fail to emerge. To decouple equation selection from neural optimization, we develop a freeze-then-select method combining a structured field adapter with Stability-Validated Weak Selection (SVWS). Trained from observations without a PDE residual, the adapter factorizes the field into learned spatial features and temporal coefficients represented by cubic splines. After freezing the field, SVWS identifies recurrent terms across independent weak-form systems, refits candidate supports, and selects the final equation on held-out weak-form systems. Beyond fixed libraries, we apply the same principle to expressions generated by genetic programming and recover the power-law form of an unknown nonlinear diffusion function from sparse, noisy observations.
