Across all six sparse MDBench regimes, our method attains the highest exact support recovery rate, with its clearest gains over classical and neural baselines on challenging Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [179] [Bridging the Gap Between PHE and FHE: A Performance and Trade-off Analysis of The Somewhat Homomorphic BGN Cryptosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28700)
*Sefik Serengil,Alper Ozpinar*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper presents the first accessible open-source implementation of the BGN Somewhat Homomorphic Encryption scheme within the lightphe Python framework, benchmarking it against PHE and FHE schemes, revealing BGN's unique advantage of microscopic public key sizes (3-6 KB) despite being computationally slower, making it ideal for bandwidth-constrained decentralized settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite BGN's elegant algebraic design bridging PHE and FHE by supporting unlimited additions and one multiplication, practical adoption has been hindered by a lack of accessible software implementations. The authors aim to fill this gap and provide an empirical comparative analysis to clarify BGN's practical trade-offs relative to other HE paradigms.

Method: The authors integrate BGN into the lightphe Python library, enabling deployment in a few lines of code. They benchmark encrypted 128-dimensional vector operations at 80-bit, 112-bit, and 128-bit security levels against Paillier, Damgård-Jurik, Okamoto-Uchiyama (PHE), and CKKS via TenSEAL (FHE), measuring computation time, public key size, and precision requirements for tasks including linear regression inference, cosine similarity, and squared Euclidean distance.

Result: BGN exhibits a computation-communication trade-off: it is computationally slower than PHE and SIMD-optimized FHE due to bilinear pairings, but its public key size is only 3–6 KB — up to five orders of magnitude smaller than FHE. An optimized precision of 2 decimal digits suffices to match plaintext ranking baselines, overcoming the discrete logarithm decryption bottleneck. BGN enables boundless homomorphic aggregation after a single multiplication.

Conclusion: BGN is established as a practical engine for bandwidth-constrained, decentralized architectures, where its microscopic key sizes outweigh computational overhead. The open-sourced lightphe pipeline lowers the barrier to BGN adoption for privacy-preserving analytics tasks requiring both additions and a single multiplication followed by unlimited aggregation.

Abstract: Homomorphic encryption (HE) enables privacy-preserving data analytics, but practitioners often face a trade-off between lightweight Partially Homomorphic Encryption (PHE) and computationally dominant Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE). The Boneh-Goh-Nissim (BGN) cryptosystem bridges this gap as a Somewhat Homomorphic Encryption (SWHE) scheme supporting unlimited additions and one ciphertext multiplication. Despite its algebraic elegance, practical BGN adoption has been hindered by a lack of accessible software implementations. This paper presents a comparative analysis of BGN against PHE and FHE paradigms through its integration into the lightphe Python framework, allowing deployment in just a few lines of code. We benchmark encrypted 128-dimensional vector operations under 80-bit, 112-bit and 128-bit security levels against Paillier, Damgard-Jurik, Okamoto-Uchiyama, and the FHE CKKS scheme via TenSEAL. Results reveal a computation-communication trade-off: BGN is computationally slower due to bilinear pairings compared to PHE and SIMD-optimized FHE, but retains a microscopic public key size of 3-6 KB, up to five orders of magnitude smaller than FHE. Crucially, BGN enables boundless homomorphic aggregation after a single multiplication, supporting complex tasks such as linear regression inference, Cosine Similarity, and Squared Euclidean Distance. Furthermore, an optimized precision of 2 digits suffices to match plaintext ranking baselines, overcoming the target-group discrete logarithm decryption bottleneck. By open-sourcing this pipeline in lightphe, this work establishes BGN as a practical engine for bandwidth-constrained, decentralized architectures.

</details>


### [180] [Costs of Arbitrary Real Matrix Factorizations for Pure-DP Continual Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28703)
*Awnon Bhowmik,Mahmudul Hasan*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proves that the optimal factorization costs for the lower-triangular prefix-sum matrix under pure ε-differential privacy are Θ((log(n+1))^{3/2}), establishing matching upper and lower bounds for arbitrary real factors. This implies optimized mean and maximum per-coordinate squared errors of Θ(ε^{-2} log^3(n+1)) for the Laplace matrix mechanism. The lower bound uses a p-nuclear obstruction technique, while Fenwick interval factorization supplies the upper bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the open problem of determining the exact asymptotic order of factorization costs (κ_F and κ_2) for the prefix-sum matrix T_n under pure ε-differential privacy with arbitrary real factors, extending prior work restricted to {0,1}-valued factors. This characterizes the optimal error achievable by the Laplace matrix mechanism for cumulative sum queries.

Method: The lower bound proceeds via a p-nuclear obstruction: deriving an aggregate column-width estimate D_k(T_n) ≍ n^{3/2} k^{-1/2} for k ≤ n/16, converting this through Pietsch/Hinrichs-Pietsch approximation-space theory at the critical exponent p=2/3, and applying Hölder's inequality to transfer the obstruction to both factorization costs. The upper bound uses an explicit Fenwick interval factorization construction. The paper also determines the p-nuclear norm ∥T_n∥_p for all fixed 0<p<1.

Result: κ_F(T_n), κ_2(T_n) = Θ((log(n+1))^{3/2}); optimized max and mean squared errors = Θ(ε^{-2} log^3(n+1)). The p-nuclear norm: order n for p<2/3, n log n at p=2/3, and n^{3p/2} for p>2/3.

Conclusion: Within the pure-ε-DP matrix mechanism class, the prefix-sum problem incurs squared error growing as log^3(n), which is the optimal achievable order. The results close the open problem for arbitrary real factors and are confined to pure-ε-DP Laplace mechanisms with the two stated squared-error criteria.

Abstract: Let \(T_n\) be the lower-triangular prefix-sum matrix and let \(\cfrob(T_n)\) and \(\ctwo(T_n)\) be the factorization costs that govern mean and maximum per-coordinate squared error of the Laplace matrix mechanism under pure \(\eps\)-differential privacy, for \(\eps>0\). We prove \(\cfrob(T_n),\ctwo(T_n)=Θ\bigl((\log(n+1))^{3/2}\bigr)\) with no sign, sparsity, or squareness restriction and with arbitrary finite inner dimension. Consequently, within the pure-\(\eps\)-DP matrix-mechanism class the optimized maximum and mean squared errors are both \(Θ(\eps^{-2}\log^{3}(n+1))\). Under the factorization contract of Arkhipov and Kalinin (arXiv:2607.08963v1), who prove the matching lower order for factors with entries in \(\{0,1\}\) and state the arbitrary-factor extension as open, the theorem below establishes the order for arbitrary real factors. The lower bound runs through a \(p\)-nuclear obstruction: an aggregate column-width estimate \(D_k(T_n)\asymp n^{3/2}k^{-1/2}\), valid in the low-rank range \(1\leq k\leq n/16\), for the prefix chain, fed into the classical approximation-space conversion of Pietsch and Hinrichs--Pietsch, becomes harmonic at the critical exponent \(p=2/3\), and Hölder's inequality transfers it to both factorization costs. The same computation determines \(\nucpow_p(T_n)\) for each fixed \(0<p<1\): order \(n\) below \(2/3\), \(n\log n\) at \(2/3\), and \(n^{3p/2}\) above. A Fenwick interval factorization supplies matching upper bounds. The claims are confined to pure-\(\eps\)-DP Laplace matrix mechanisms and the two stated squared-error criteria; they do not cover non-matrix continual mechanisms, approximate-DP sensitivity, or expected maxima across coordinates.

</details>


### [181] [Blockchain Transaction Simulation Phishing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28747)
*Xiaocan Wang,Shixuan Guan,Tong Yang,Xiapu Luo,Yue Duan,Kai Li*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Transaction simulation, a defense in crypto wallets against phishing, is itself vulnerable to "transaction simulation phishing," where smart contracts exploit dynamic state differences between simulation and execution. The paper presents a taxonomy, detection system (SIMGUARD), and a large-scale study across four blockchains, uncovering over 4,000 phishing contracts, 5,700+ victims, and $3.48M in losses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crypto wallets like MetaMask have adopted transaction simulation to preview balance changes and prevent fund-draining attacks. However, the authors identify that this defense mechanism can be subverted: attackers can craft smart contracts that behave benignly during simulation but maliciously during actual on-chain execution, exploiting the gap between simulated and real blockchain state. No prior systematic study of this threat existed.

Method: The authors first develop a taxonomy of phishing contracts that enable transaction simulation phishing. Then, they propose SIMGUARD, a bytecode-level detection system combining static and dynamic program analysis to identify such phishing contracts at scale. SIMGUARD is applied to four major blockchains: Ethereum, Binance Smart Chain, Avalanche, and Polygon.

Result: SIMGUARD detected over 4,000 phishing contracts deployed between August 2024 and June 2025. The analysis identified more than 5,700 victims and approximately $3.48 million USD in total losses. Ethereum accounts for 91.5% of these losses. Clustering analysis revealed that a single large phishing contract cluster is responsible for roughly 83% of total losses, indicating highly concentrated attack activity.

Conclusion: Transaction simulation phishing exposes a critical, previously unstudied weakness in current wallet defenses. The scale of losses and concentration of attacks highlight an urgent need for more robust transaction simulation mechanisms that can resist state-dependent manipulation by malicious contracts.

Abstract: Cryptocurrency users have increasingly become targets of phishing and scam attacks. To mitigate these threats, leading crypto wallets (e.g., MetaMask) have introduced transaction simulation, which previews a transaction's balance changes before on-chain execution. While effective against traditional fund-draining attacks, we show that this defense can itself be exploited by a new phishing technique, which we term transaction simulation phishing. This attack uses carefully crafted smart contracts whose execution depends on dynamic blockchain state, causing simulations to display benign or profitable outcomes while the actual on-chain execution redirects users' funds to attacker-controlled addresses.
  We present the first comprehensive study of transaction simulation phishing. We first develop a taxonomy of phishing contracts that can be utilized to facilitate this attack. Then, we propose SIMGUARD, a bytecode-level detection system that combines static and dynamic program analysis to identify phishing contracts. Applying SIMGUARD to Ethereum, Binance Smart Chain, Avalanche, and Polygon, we detect over 4,000 phishing contracts deployed between August 2024 and June 2025. Our analysis identifies more than 5,700 victims and approximately $3.48 million USD in losses, 91.5% of which occurred on Ethereum. Moreover, our clustering result reveals that the largest phishing contract cluster alone accounts for about 83% of the total losses. These results expose a critical weakness in current wallet defenses and highlight the urgent need for more robust transaction simulation mechanisms.

</details>


### [182] [Partial Derandomization for Leakage-Resilient Shamir's Secret Sharing over Composite Order Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28757)
*S. Venkitesh*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper presents a partial derandomization of evaluation places for leakage-resilient Shamir's secret sharing over composite-order fields, reducing randomness from nd log p bits to d log p bits by using iterates of a fixed rational function, valid for n = O(d/log_p d) and k ≥ 2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work (Maji et al. EUROCRYPT 2024) required fully random, independent evaluation places for statistical security against physical-bit leakage in Shamir's scheme over composite-order fields. Nguyen (EUROCRYPT 2025) established a dichotomy (statistically secure or completely insecure) for linear code-based schemes. The authors aim to reduce the entropy required for selecting evaluation places while preserving leakage resilience.

Method: The authors replace n independent random evaluation places with iterates x_j = Φ^j(x_0) of a simple fixed rational function Φ, where only the initial point x_0 ∈ F_{p^d}^* is randomly chosen. Security is proved via a partial fraction nondegeneracy argument that exploits the distinct poles of the rational iterates. The construction achieves perfect security (zero statistical distance) against single-block leakage.

Result: Randomness in evaluation places is reduced from nd log p bits to d log p bits. The construction is valid for n = O(d / log_p d) and any reconstruction threshold k ≥ 2, and attains perfect security against single-block leakage.

Conclusion: The work provides a partial derandomization of evaluation places for leakage-resilient Shamir secret sharing over composite-order fields, significantly reducing the required randomness while maintaining strong security guarantees, albeit in a restricted parameter regime.

Abstract: We make progress on the question of constructing explicit evaluation places for leakage-resilient Shamir's secret sharing, over composite order fields. Previously, Maji et al. (EUROCRYPT 2024) showed that random evaluation places yield Shamir's secret sharing over the composite order field $\mathbb{F}_{p^d}$ that is statistically secure against physical-bit leakage. Later, Nguyen (EUROCRYPT 2025) established a dichotomy that linear code-based secret-sharing scheme over the field $\mathbb{F}_{p^d}$ is either statistically secure or completely insecure against such leakage.
  Building upon Nguyen's dichotomy, we present a partial derandomization of evaluation places, improving upon the Maji et al. result for a restricted regime of parameters. We replace the random choice of $n$ independent evaluation places by the iterates $x_j = Φ^j(x_0)$ of a simple fixed rational function $Φ$, where the initial point $x_0 \in \mathbb{F}_{p^d}^*$ is randomly chosen. The randomness in the evaluation places thus drops from $nd \log p$ bits to $d\log p$ bits. Our construction is valid for the regime $n = O(d/\log_p d)$, and any reconstruction threshold $k \ge 2$; in fact, the scheme attains perfect security (statistical distance exactly zero) against single-block leakage. Our technique is a partial fraction nondegeneracy argument that exploits the distinct poles of the rational iterates.

</details>


### [183] [YazSes: An Offline, Privacy-First, Cross-Platform Hold-to-Talk Voice-Dictation System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28878)
*Mohsen Seyedkazemi Ardebili*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: YazSes is an open-source, cross-platform, on-device voice dictation daemon that transcribes speech locally, injects text into focused applications, and supports voice commands via regex grammar with an optional LLM router — all while preserving privacy through push-to-talk, no telemetry, and encrypted personalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cloud dictation services compromise privacy by streaming speech to remote servers, which is unacceptable in sensitive professions and offline/air-gapped settings. Existing on-device alternatives are either platform-locked or designed for expert scripting rather than general dictation. There is a need for a privacy-preserving, cross-platform, plug-and-play dictation solution.

Method: YazSes uses a staged pipeline behind a protocol-based platform abstraction with a JSON-RPC control plane. Speech is transcribed locally using faster-whisper (CPU, int8 quantization). A fast regex command grammar, optionally backed by a small-language-model router, maps utterances to editor/terminal actions. The system employs push-to-talk recording, no telemetry, and an opt-in personalization loop with encrypted on-device corpus storage. A single codebase targets Linux, macOS, and Windows.

Result: Evaluated on 200 LibriSpeech test-clean utterances (40 speakers): word error rate ranges from 4.82% (tiny.en) to 2.59% (small.en) with real-time factor of 0.520 for small.en (faster than real-time on CPU). Command grammar achieves 100% action accuracy with 0.0% false-positive rate on plain dictation at 0.021 ms per call. Non-decode pipeline overhead is 0.289 ms.

Conclusion: YazSes demonstrates that a fully on-device, privacy-respecting, cross-platform dictation system can achieve strong transcription accuracy and command recognition with minimal latency on commodity CPU hardware. The system and its reproducible benchmark harness are publicly available.

Abstract: Cloud voice-dictation services deliver strong accuracy but require streaming a user's speech to a remote provider, an unacceptable trade-off in privacy-sensitive professions and offline or air-gapped settings; the leading on-device alternatives are either platform-locked or aimed at expert scripting rather than plug-and-play dictation. We present YazSes, an open-source (Apache-2.0) hold-to-talk voice dictation daemon that runs entirely on-device, with a single codebase targeting Linux, macOS, and Windows through a protocol-based platform abstraction. YazSes transcribes speech locally with faster-whisper (CPU, int8) and injects the result into the focused application; a fast regex command grammar, backed by an optional small-language-model router, maps utterances to editor and terminal actions. Nothing leaves the machine: recording is push-to-talk rather than always-listening, there is no telemetry, and an opt-in personalization loop keeps its corpus encrypted on-device and proposes configuration changes instead of shipping data out. We describe the system architecture -- a staged pipeline behind a protocol-based platform abstraction with a JSON-RPC control plane -- and its privacy and threat model. We evaluate the shipping Python implementation on a single commodity Linux laptop; the macOS and Windows backends are implemented and unit-tested but not end-to-end evaluated here. On 200 LibriSpeech test-clean utterances spanning 40 speakers, word error rate ranges from 4.82% (tiny.en) to 2.59% (small.en) at a real-time factor of 0.520 for small.en, decoding faster than real time on CPU with no GPU. The command grammar reaches 100% action accuracy with a 0.0% false-positive rate on plain dictation at 0.021 ms per call, and the non-decode pipeline adds 0.289 ms of overhead. The system and the reproducible benchmark harness behind every number in this paper are public.

</details>


### [184] [Hollow-LLM Attack: Computationally Trivial Weights in Zero-Knowledge Verification of LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28884)
*Chen Gong,Beijie Liu,Mengyuan Li*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Current zero-knowledge proofs for LLM inference verify architectural correctness and weight commitment but fail to ensure the prover actually expended the computational effort of a large model. The authors present the Hollow-LLM Attack, where ghost weights satisfy the verification circuit while collapsing effective computation to that of a much smaller model, undermining the economic fairness of verifiable inference services.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly served by third-party providers, zero-knowledge proofs of inference promise public verifiability that outputs were produced by a specific architecture with committed weights. However, the authors identify a critical blind spot: existing ZK schemes prove equational correctness but do not bind the computational effort to the declared model size, creating a gap that dishonest providers can exploit to overcharge for small-model computation.

Method: The authors formalize the "effort gap" in ZK LLM inference and propose the Hollow-LLM Attack. In this attack, a dishonest provider retains the declared architecture and parameter count but embeds "ghost weights" — algebraically structured parameters that cause most of the network's computation to collapse or become trivial. These weights are committed and produce valid ZK proofs, yet the actual computation performed is equivalent to a much smaller model. The paper characterizes concrete families of ghost weights that compose with standard transformer blocks.

Result: The Hollow-LLM Attack successfully produces valid zero-knowledge proofs while dramatically reducing serving cost. The dishonest provider achieves a profitable equilibrium: delivering provably correct outputs at small-model computational cost while overclaiming model size. Critically, this incurs zero quality degradation relative to the hollow model and passes the same verification circuit undetected.

Conclusion: Proof of correct inference, as currently implemented in ZK schemes, is insufficient to guarantee large-model execution. The findings demonstrate that verifiable inference protocols must incorporate additional protections to bind equational correctness to verifiable computational work, closing the effort gap to prevent hollow deployments.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) grow in scale and are predominantly served from remote platforms, verifying faithful inference execution becomes critical (i.e., ensuring that a provider actually executes the advertised model and computational workload rather than a tampered or downsized variant). Zero-knowledge (ZK) LLM inference offers an appealing approach. It promises public verifiability and delivers per-instance guarantees of equational correctness by proving that an output is consistent with executing a public architecture under committed, private weights. Though, we show that it does not bind the effort expended to produce the output. In this paper, we formalize this overlooked effort gap and introduce the Hollow-LLM Attack, in which a dishonest provider retains the declared architecture and parameter count but embeds ghost weights whose algebraic structure collapses effective computation. These witnesses satisfy the verification circuit and yield valid proofs, even though the dishonest model owner, who serves as the prover, performs computation commensurate with a much smaller model than the declared public architecture. This creates a profitable equilibrium in which providers deliver provably correct outputs at small-model cost while overclaiming model size. Accordingly, we characterize concrete families of ghost weights that compose with standard transformer blocks and show that such hollow deployments substantially reduce serving cost with zero quality loss under the same verification circuit. These findings underscore that proof of correct inference is not proof of large-model execution and necessitate additional protections to bind correctness to verifiable computational work.

</details>


### [185] [A Biometric Sensor Network to Enable Real-Time Measurement of Individual Student Engagement in STEM Lecture Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28944)
*Ahmed Elsayed*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Proposes a Biometric Sensor Network (BSN) with distributed Student Processing Units (SPUs) for real-time, non-intrusive measurement of student engagement in STEM classrooms via on-device camera-based sensing of behavioral, emotional, and cognitive indicators, with strong privacy safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing student engagement measurement methods in STEM education are intrusive, manually intensive, and impractical for real-time classroom use. There is a need for a system that can continuously track engagement unobtrusively while respecting ethical and privacy constraints.

Method: A Biometric Sensor Network architecture is introduced, comprising distributed SPUs operating in two modes: (1) dataset-collection mode for temporary raw video recording to build private SE datasets, and (2) analysis mode performing real-time on-device inference over 10-second video segments (face detection, gaze estimation, affective analysis) without storing or transmitting raw frames. The system integrates hardware design, computer-vision pipelines, wireless networking, security protocols, and a secure backend for device authentication, session orchestration, and encrypted data ingestion.

Result: The abstract describes the system design and its five design objectives (non-intrusive, non-invasive, non-stigmatizing, real-time, automatic) but does not report empirical results or evaluation metrics from deployment or experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed BSN with SPUs offers a principled architecture for real-time, privacy-preserving student engagement sensing in STEM classrooms, though the abstract does not present validation outcomes.

Abstract: Student engagement (SE) is a critical predictor of academic performance and retention in STEM education, yet existing measurement approaches are often intrusive, manually intensive, or unsuitable for real-time classroom use. This thesis proposes a novel $\textit{Biometric Sensor Network}$ (BSN) designed to enable real-time measurement and continuous tracking of individual student engagement in STEM classroom environments. The system enables capturing of behavioral, emotional, and cognitive indicators through camera-based sensing while preserving ethical and privacy constraints.
  To measure these indicators unobtrusively and ethically, we propose a BSN composed of $\textit{Student Processing Units}$ (SPUs) that function as distributed sensing nodes. The network is explicitly designed to satisfy five objectives: it must be $\textbf{non-intrusive}, \textbf{non-invasive}, \textbf{non-stigmatizing}, \textbf{real-time}$, and $\textbf{automatic}$, while ensuring rigorous protection of student data security and privacy.
  Each SPU supports two operational modes: (i) a $\textit{dataset-collection mode}$, in which raw student video is temporarily recorded to construct a private SE dataset for model training and validation, and (ii) an $\textit{analysis mode}$, in which the SPU performs real-time inference on 10-second video segments without storing or transmitting raw frames. In this analysis role, each SPU enables fully on-device processing---including face detection, gaze estimation, and affective analysis---ensuring that no identifiable video data leaves the device. A secure backend infrastructure manages device authentication, session orchestration, and encrypted data ingestion. The full system integrates hardware design, computer-vision pipelines, wireless networking, security protocols, and session-level data management.

</details>


### [186] [MESS: Fast and Private Semantic Search on Multi-Graph HNSW](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28999)
*Haoyu Cui,Zengpeng Li,Tien Tuan Anh Dinh,Mei Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MESS is a privacy-preserving semantic search system that combines binary coding, locality-sensitive hashing, randomized response, and a multi-graph HNSW index to achieve privacy, accuracy, and efficiency simultaneously, outperforming HE/ORAM/DP-based baselines by up to 15.08× in latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing privacy-preserving semantic search systems rely on homomorphic encryption, ORAM, or differential privacy, but none simultaneously satisfies all three desiderata: privacy (data, query, access pattern, search pattern), accuracy, and efficiency. The authors aim to close this gap.

Method: MESS maps original vectors to binary codes, applies locality-sensitive hashing (LSH) and randomized response for perturbation, and builds a multi-graph Hierarchical Navigable Small World (HNSW) index over the perturbed codes. A two-phase query perturbation mechanism ensures search pattern privacy. Search executes directly on perturbed codes without HE or ORAM overhead.

Result: MESS achieves up to 15.08× lower latency compared to state-of-the-art baselines while maintaining privacy guarantees. Formal privacy analysis and extensive performance evaluations are provided.

Conclusion: MESS successfully realizes a privacy-preserving semantic search system that balances privacy, accuracy, and efficiency, overcoming the limitations of prior HE-, ORAM-, and DP-based approaches.

Abstract: Semantic search systems map data to a high-dimensional vector space and support retrieval of similar data via approximate nearest neighbor search. When the system is hosted by a trusted cloud provider, there is no privacy for the data or the query. Our goal is to design a system with three properties: privacy, accuracy, and efficiency. Existing works adopt either homomorphic encryption (HE), oblivious RAM (ORAM), or a differential privacy (DP) approach. They fall short of achieving all three properties.
  In this paper, we present MESS, a system that realizes our goal. It maps the original vectors into binary codes, applies locality-sensitive hashing (LSH) and randomized response, and constructs a multi-graph Hierarchical Navigable Small World (HNSW) index over the perturbed codes. MESS ensures data, query, and access pattern privacy. It also ensures search pattern privacy via a two-phase query perturbation mechanism. The multi-graph index mitigates the impact of perturbation on result quality, thereby achieving accuracy. MESS is efficient because search is performed directly over perturbed codes, without the overhead of homomorphic encryption or ORAM. We give formal analysis of the system's privacy and extensive evaluation of its performance. The results show that MESS achieves up to 15.08\times lower latency than state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [187] [GoldenRetriever: Non-Interactive Homomorphic Encrypted Retrieval for Privacy-Preserving RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29019)
*Yang Gao,Gang Quan,Scott Piersall,Qian Lou,Dongdong Wang,Liqiang Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A non-interactive encrypted retrieval framework for RAG that uses threshold-based document selection (instead of top-k ranking) with CKKS homomorphic encryption, reducing computational complexity from quadratic to linear while preserving privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing privacy-preserving retrieval for RAG relies on interactive protocols or ranking-based selection with high latency and potential information leakage. There is a need for a practical, non-interactive, encrypted retrieval method that reduces computational overhead.

Method: The authors propose a threshold-based selection mechanism under CKKS fully homomorphic encryption, where documents with similarity scores exceeding a predefined threshold are selected (linear complexity vs. quadratic for top-k ranking). They introduce a precision-stable mask polarization method to bridge approximate encrypted computation and discrete token reconstruction for accurate document recovery.

Result: Competitive retrieval effectiveness on standard benchmarks with significantly reduced latency compared to ranking-based encrypted methods.

Conclusion: Threshold-based selection is a practical foundation for building scalable and secure RAG systems, offering a viable alternative to ranking-based encrypted retrieval.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances large language models by incorporating external knowledge, but existing pipelines typically operate on plaintext data, raising significant privacy concerns. Prior work on privacy-preserving retrieval leverages cryptographic techniques such as homomorphic encryption (HE) and private information retrieval (PIR), but often relies on interactive protocols or ranking-based selection mechanisms that incur high latency and potential information leakage. In this paper, we propose a practical non-interactive encrypted retrieval framework for RAG based on threshold selection. Instead of performing expensive top-$k$ ranking under encryption, our approach selects documents whose similarity scores exceed a predefined threshold, reducing computational complexity from quadratic to linear in the corpus size. We implement this design using CKKS-based homomorphic computation, enabling fully encrypted similarity evaluation and document selection without revealing query content, intermediate scores, or selected indices. To bridge the gap between approximate encrypted computation and discrete token reconstruction, we introduce a precision-stable mask polarization method that ensures accurate recovery of selected documents. Experiments on standard retrieval benchmarks demonstrate that our approach achieves competitive retrieval effectiveness while significantly reducing latency compared to ranking-based encrypted methods. These results highlight threshold-based selection as a practical foundation for scalable and secure RAG systems.

</details>


### [188] [Memory Provenance Laundering in LLM Agents: A Non-Amplification Firewall for Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29167)
*Jinghan Xu,Yiyong Xiao,Wanru Shao,Hankai Liu,Xinjin Li*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Memory consolidation in LLM agents can erase low-trust provenance, enabling attacks. The proposed PPMF middleware preserves provenance metadata to authorize tool calls based on risk-authority matching, reducing ASR from 1.0 to zero for unauthorized high-risk actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents use long-term memory to reuse preferences and workflows, but untrusted observations can be consolidated into memory, losing their low-trust provenance. This "memory provenance laundering" allows malicious observations to masquerade as trusted user history, creating persistent action triggers that bypass existing safeguards (prompt filters, content sanitizers, tool guards), which fail to enforce source-authority non-amplification after lossy consolidation.

Method: The authors formalize the memory provenance boundary problem and propose Provenance-Preserving Memory Firewall (PPMF), a lightweight memory middleware. PPMF preserves platform-maintained provenance metadata (source trust level, confirmation status, risk labels) during memory consolidation. At inference time, it authorizes tool calls by matching the risk level of a proposed action against the authority level of all action-relevant memories, blocking actions whose risk exceeds the provenance authority of their supporting memories.

Result: In schema-grounded evaluation with fixed risk policies, vulnerable consolidated memories without provenance protection achieve up to 1.000 Attack Success Rate (ASR). With intact platform-maintained provenance, confirmation, and risk labels, no evaluated unauthorized high-risk action passes the PPMF gate, while confirmed benign actions and targeted low-risk memory use remain executable.

Conclusion: PPMF effectively mitigates memory provenance laundering attacks by enforcing source-authority non-amplification through provenance-preserving middleware. The approach demonstrates that maintaining and verifying provenance metadata at the memory layer is both necessary and sufficient to prevent unauthorized high-risk tool calls without blocking legitimate low-risk memory use.

Abstract: Long-term memory lets large language model(LLM) agents reuse prior preferences and work flows, but it also turns untrusted observations into persistent action context. We identify memory provenance laundering: during LLM-based memory consolidation, an external observation may be rewritten as apparent user history or workflow support, preserving an action trigger while erasing the low-trust source that should limit its authority. Existing prompt filters, content sanitizers, and tool guards do not enforce source-authority non-amplification after lossy memory consolidation. We formalize this boundary and instantiate it as Provenance-Preserving Memory Fire wall (PPMF), a lightweight memory middleware that preserves platform-maintained provenance and authorizes tool calls by matching action risk to the authority of action-relevant memories. In our schema-grounded evaluation with fixed risk policies, vulnerable consolidated memories reach up to 1.000 attack success rate(ASR); with intact platform-maintained provenance, confirmation, and risk labels, no evaluated unauthorized high-risk action passes the PPMF gate while confirmed benign actions and targeted low-risk memory use remain executable.

</details>


### [189] [Alignment Is Local: A Paired Diagnostic for GUI Agents under User-Side Persuasion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29199)
*Haoxin An,Yunpeng Song,Zihao Bai,Zhongmin Cai,Guojun Xiong,Chenhao Lin,Wentao Chen,Feng Wei,Chao Shen*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Prompt-level alignment in GUI agents is a brittle, local phenomenon: effective on single-turn explicit harmful requests but degrades significantly under multi-turn escalation chains. A one-line guardrail reduces single-shot Attack Success Rate (ASR) by ~40 points at minimal over-refusal cost, but four-turn escalation erodes this by ~20 points across all models. Concealed intent bypasses the guardrail, revealing that static single-turn ASR systematically overstates real-world robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current mobile GUI agents rely on prompt-level alignment as a lightweight defense, but this is evaluated almost exclusively under narrow conditions: single-turn interactions with explicitly verbalized harmful intent. The authors argue that real-world deployment exposes agents to two orthogonal axes of risk — multi-turn escalation and concealed/implied requests — that any user can traverse, and that robustness claims based on static single-turn evaluations are systematically misleading.

Method: A paired diagnostic framework is applied to three frontier GUI agents. The method introduces screen-grounded, user-side persuasion without environment injection. Two axes are tested: (1) turn depth — comparing independent single-shot probes against four-turn escalation chains; (2) intent salience — contrasting explicitly verbalized harmful requests with concealed requests. A one-line guardrail is deployed and evaluated for ASR reduction, over-refusal rates, and erosion under multi-turn dynamics. The "salience gap" (explicit vs. concealed ASR difference) is measured with and without the guardrail.

Result: Key findings: (1) The one-line guardrail achieves up to ~40-point ASR reduction at near-zero over-refusal on single-turn probes. (2) Four-turn escalation chains raise guarded ASR by ~20 points universally. (3) For Qwen, this reflects substantial guardrail erosion; for Claude and GPT, it reflects a defense-orthogonal dynamic risk. (4) The salience gap flips: without a guardrail, concealed requests are not systematically more successful than explicit ones; with a guardrail, concealed requests become significantly more successful, indicating the defense engages primarily when harmful intent is explicitly named.

Conclusion: Static single-turn ASR overstates deployed robustness by a systematic and predictable margin. Prompt-level alignment is inherently local — it functions in the narrow evaluation slice where it is measured but degrades along dimensions that real users naturally traverse. Reliable deployment requires evaluating and hardening defenses against multi-turn escalation and concealed-intent attacks, not merely optimizing for single-turn explicit-refusal benchmarks.

Abstract: Trustworthy deployment of GUI agents in ubiquitous computing settings requires alignment that survives dynamic interaction and precise threat conditions, not just single-turn refusal of explicit harmful requests. We argue that prompt-level alignment, the dominant lightweight defense in current mobile agents, is a local phenomenon: it works reliably only in the narrow evaluation slice where it is typically measured, namely single-turn, explicitly-verbalized intent, and degrades systematically along two axes that any real user can traverse. Using a paired diagnostic on three frontier GUI agents, screen-grounded, user-side persuasion, with no environment injection, we show that a one-line guardrail achieves large single-shot ASR reductions, up to roughly 40 points, at near-zero over-refusal cost. Nevertheless, moving from independent probes to four-turn escalation chains raises guarded ASR by approximately 20 points on every model. Relative to the neutral baselines, this increase reflects substantial guardrail erosion for Qwen but a largely defense-orthogonal dynamic risk for Claude and GPT. The sign of the salience gap flips under the guardrail: concealed requests are not systematically more successful than explicit ones without a guardrail, but are more successful with one, indicating that the defense engages primarily when intent is named. Static single-turn ASR therefore overstates deployed robustness by a systematic and predictable margin.

</details>


### [190] [MOSAIC: Masked Outsourcing of Secure AI Computations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29221)
*James Hsin-yu Chiang,Sheila Zingg,Kari Kostiainen,Srdjan Capkun*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MOSAIC is a secure outsourcing protocol for AI inference that uses a novel matrix-multiplication masking protocol with small noise injection, achieving optimal asymptotic client overhead and orders-of-magnitude speedups over prior work. It handles large transformer models (70B parameters) via error-scaling with random Hadamard rotations, matching full-precision quality on benchmarks like HumanEval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable a computationally weak but trusted client to securely outsource AI inference (where the client owns both the model and input) to powerful untrusted servers, without the server learning either the model weights or the input data. Prior approaches are too slow for modern large-scale transformer models.

Method: Core innovation is a matrix-multiplication masking protocol that (1) injects small amounts of noise into multiplication results, relaxing correctness to achieve efficiency; (2) uses random Hadamard rotations as an error-scaling mechanism to bound accumulated noise across many transformer layers; (3) reduces security to decisional LWE and LPN assumptions; (4) demonstrates an end-to-end system architecture that keeps the TCB small while outsourcing computation to untrusted accelerators using RDMA-like networking across prefill/decode phases, layers, and time.

Result: On 70B transformer models, MOSAIC achieves perplexity comparable to popular quantization methods and matches full-precision BF16 inference on HumanEval. Concrete runtimes are orders of magnitude faster than prior secure outsourcing work, with optimal asymptotic client overhead.

Conclusion: MOSAIC demonstrates a viable path toward large-scale confidential AI inference in modern data centers by combining a noise-relaxed masking protocol with structured error control, enabling small TCB and outsourcing of bulk computation to untrusted hardware.

Abstract: We address the challenge of securely and efficiently outsourcing AI computations from a trusted but computationally weak client to an untrusted but powerful server, in the setting where the client holds both the input and the model, and the server must learn neither. We present MOSAIC, whose core is a novel matrix-multiplication masking protocol that scales to far larger matrices than prior work, enabling the safe outsourcing of modern workloads such as large transformer inference. By introducing small amounts of noise to the multiplication result and thereby relaxing correctness, MOSAIC achieves optimal asymptotic client overhead and concrete runtimes orders of magnitude faster than prior work. Its security reduces to the decisional LWE and LPN assumptions.
  Because this noise accumulates across the many layers of a transformer, a key technical challenge is bounding error growth; MOSAIC addresses this with an error-scaling mechanism based on random Hadamard rotations. On large 70B transformer models, MOSAIC's perplexity is comparable to popular quantization approaches and even matches full-precision BF16 inference on HumanEval.
  Finally, we present an end-to-end implementation showing how ideas like MOSAIC can promise a path towards large-scale confidential AI in modern data centers. Non-confidential inference is already distributed across phase (prefill/decode), layer, and time to maximize utilization of heterogeneous hardware, using RDMA-like networking to move activations, cached KV values, and weights across nodes. MOSAIC enables scaling of confidential compute by keeping the trusted computing base (TCB) small and outsourcing the bulk of the AI computation to untrusted accelerators.

</details>


### [191] [JUNO: Aggregated Vector Consensus for Optimal Asynchronous Common Subset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29244)
*Liangrong Zhao,Qin Wang,Joseph K. Liu,Jiangshan Yu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper introduces aggregated vector consensus, a new primitive for asynchronous networks that outputs a vector of aggregated values from independent process inputs. It then presents Juno, an asynchronous common subset (ACS) protocol that achieves optimal O(n²) message complexity, outperforming HoneyBadgerBFT by 93% and Dory by 47% in throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap in applying vector consensus protocols in fully asynchronous networks. Existing ACS protocols like HoneyBadgerBFT and Dory do not achieve optimal message complexity, creating a need for a more efficient primitive and protocol that can aggregate independent process inputs with better performance.

Method: The authors propose aggregated vector consensus, a novel primitive that outputs an aggregated vector of values from independent process inputs. They then design and implement Juno, an ACS protocol that fully leverages this primitive to achieve optimal O(n²) message complexity. The protocol is evaluated experimentally against HoneyBadgerBFT and Dory.

Result: Juno demonstrates a 93% average throughput improvement over HoneyBadgerBFT and a 47% improvement over Dory. The protocol achieves optimal O(n²) message complexity while maintaining correctness in fully asynchronous networks.

Conclusion: The proposed aggregated vector consensus primitive and Juno protocol significantly advance the state of the art in asynchronous consensus, closing the gap in vector consensus application for asynchronous networks while delivering substantial performance gains.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose \textit{aggregated vector consensus}, a new vector consensus primitive designed for asynchronous networks. The primitive achieves agreement by outputting a vector of values aggregated from independent process inputs. We then introduce \textsc{Juno}, an asynchronous common subset (ACS) protocol that fully implements our aggregated vector consensus to attain optimal $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$ message complexity.
  We further implement and evaluate \textsc{Juno} in comparison with the legacy HoneyBadgerBFT and the state-of-the-art Dory. Experiment results demonstrate its efficacy and efficiency. Our protocol demonstrates an average throughput performance improvement of 93\% compared with HoneyBadgerBFT and a 47\% improvement compared with Dory. Notably, our study makes significant progress in addressing the gap in applying vector consensus protocol in fully asynchronous networks.

</details>


### [192] [On the Resilience of 5G NR Against Jamming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29384)
*Sotiris Michaelides,Fidelius Lula,Daniel Eguiguren Chavez,Michael Rademacher,Martin Henze*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents an open-source 5G jammer implemented in ns-3 to systematically evaluate jamming resilience across different 5G configurations. Key findings reveal that channel bandwidth and frequency range significantly affect jamming resilience, while cellular technology generation and subcarrier spacing have minimal impact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on 5G jamming resilience relies on isolated evaluations under fixed physical-layer configurations, making cross-study comparisons unreliable. There is a need for a systematic, reproducible framework to compare jamming impact across diverse 5G parameters such as frequency bands, channel bandwidths, subcarrier spacing, and cellular technologies.

Method: The authors implement and release an open-source 5G jammer module within the ns-3 network simulator. Using simulation's controllable determinism, they conduct a systematic comparative analysis of cellular jamming impact across multiple dimensions: cellular technology generations, frequency ranges, subcarrier spacing configurations, and channel bandwidths.

Result: Cellular technology generation (e.g., 4G vs. 5G) and subcarrier spacing have negligible influence on jamming resilience. In contrast, channel bandwidth and frequency range are critical factors: wider bandwidths and certain frequency ranges substantially improve resilience to jamming attacks.

Conclusion: To harden 5G networks against jamming in availability-critical deployments (industrial networks, critical infrastructure), operators should prioritize wider channel bandwidths and judicious frequency range selection, rather than relying on choice of cellular generation or subcarrier spacing.

Abstract: With the increasing use of 5G networks in availability-critical systems, including industrial networks and critical infrastructure, a comprehensive understanding of their resilience to cellular jamming has become imperative. However, research so far has focused on isolated evaluations under fixed 5G physical-layer configurations, making it difficult to perform sound comparisons, for example, to identify differences between frequency bands or channel bandwidths w.r.t. jamming resilience. To fill this gap, we implement and release an open-source 5G jammer within the ns-3 simulator. Leveraging the controllable determinism of simulation, we compare the impact of cellular jamming across different dimensions such as cellular technologies, frequencies, subcarrier spacing, and channel bandwidth. Our results show that while cellular technology and subcarrier spacing have little impact, channel bandwidth and frequency range play a critical role in improving jamming resilience.

</details>


### [193] [On fair and realistic performance evaluations for graph-based lateral movement detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29390)
*Corentin Larroche*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper surveys preprocessing and labeling practices for lateral movement detection benchmark datasets, demonstrates their significant impact on evaluation fairness/realism, proposes well-grounded policies, and re-evaluates three detection methods under these policies with substantially different results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite progress in lateral movement detection, the literature lacks standardized preprocessing and labeling methodologies for benchmark datasets. This inconsistency undermines the fairness and realism of detector evaluations, making it difficult to compare methods meaningfully and potentially producing misleading performance claims.

Method: The authors (1) survey preprocessing and labeling methodologies across the literature for two popular benchmark datasets, (2) analyze how these variations affect evaluation outcomes, (3) propose principled preprocessing and labeling policies grounded in domain knowledge, and (4) re-evaluate three widely cited lateral movement detection methods under the newly proposed policies to quantify the impact.

Result: The survey reveals substantial heterogeneity in both preprocessing and labeling practices across papers. The re-evaluation of three detectors under the proposed standardized policies yields results that differ significantly from those originally reported, demonstrating that preprocessing and labeling choices can dramatically alter perceived detector performance.

Conclusion: Dataset preprocessing and labeling practices are critically important for fair and realistic evaluation of lateral movement detectors. The community needs well-grounded, standardized policies to ensure meaningful benchmarking and comparison. The proposed policies offer a concrete step toward more rigorous evaluation methodology.

Abstract: Research on lateral movement detection has made significant progress in recent years, spurred by the widespread availability of benchmark datasets that make evaluating detectors practical. However, the exact way in which these benchmark datasets are used varies across the literature: both the preprocessing applied before feeding the data to the detector and the labeling of lateral movement-related events change substantially from one paper to another. We survey preprocessing and labeling methodologies for two popular datasets and demonstrate their impact on the fairness and realism of downstream evaluations. We also propose well-grounded preprocessing and labeling policies for these datasets. Finally, we re-evaluate three widely cited lateral movement detection methods under these new policies; our results differ significantly from those reported in the original papers, further highlighting the critical importance of dataset preprocessing and labeling practices in evaluating lateral movement detectors.

</details>


### [194] [Enforcing Cryptographic Distributed-VCS Access Control with No Trust on Servers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29417)
*Xin Xu,Zhen Yang,Quanwei Cai,Jingqiang Lin,Liangqin Ren,Bo Chen,Yongfeng Huang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: DVAC is a cryptographic access control scheme for distributed version control systems (DVCS) that uses attribute-based encryption (ABE) and attribute-based signatures (ABS) to achieve fine-grained, file-level read/write separation without a central server, while leveraging Ethereum smart contracts for policy management and integrating with Git with modest performance overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VCS access control schemes require a central server and cannot operate in fully distributed scenarios. They also lack fine-grained write permission enforcement, which is essential for collaborative work in distributed environments. DVCS like Git lack built-in cryptographic access control for decentralized settings.

Method: DVAC employs attribute-based encryption (ABE) for read access control and attribute-based signature (ABS) for write access control, enabling file-granularity separation of read/write permissions without a central server. It includes a version control adaptation protocol to preserve core VCS integrity while securing data. Ethereum smart contracts are used for distributed, trusted storage and management of access control policies. The architecture is designed for minimal-modification integration with existing DVCS like Git.

Result: A DVAC prototype was implemented and integrated with Git. Comprehensive performance evaluation demonstrated that the cryptographic and protocol overhead introduced by DVAC is modest.

Conclusion: DVAC successfully enables fully decentralized, fine-grained access control for DVCS using ABE/ABS and blockchain-based policy management, with practical performance characteristics suitable for real-world deployment alongside mature systems like Git.

Abstract: Version control systems (VCS), including central VCS (CVCS) and distributed VCS (DVCS), are widely adopted to manage changes to software code and various types of documents. Unlike CVCS, where entities obtain data from a central server, each entity in DVCS stores the entire repository and shares it independently. In VCS, existing access control schemes require the participation of a central server and cannot be deployed in a completely distributed scenario. Additionally, these schemes often fail to enforce fine-grained access control for write permissions, which is crucial for collaborative work in a distributed environment. In this paper, we propose a distributed version control system access control scheme (named DVAC), which enforces cryptographic access control on distributed user nodes based on attribute-based encryption (ABE) and attribute-based signature (ABS). DVAC is designed to enforce a cryptographic access control protocol for DVCS, which enables file granularity read and write separation access control without the support of a central server. To ensure the integrity of the core version control functions in DVCS while protecting data security, DVAC incorporates a version control adaptation protocol. Additionally, DVAC leverages Ethereum smart contracts to maintain access control policies, ensuring distributed storage and trusted management of access policies. The architecture of DVAC is designed to seamlessly integrate with existing mature DVCS, such as Git, with minimal modifications. We have implemented a prototype of DVAC and integrated it with Git. A comprehensive performance evaluation was conducted to assess the overhead introduced by DVAC, and it was demonstrated that the overhead is modest.

</details>


### [195] [Bending the Curve: Operational Cyber Epidemiology for Ransomware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29444)
*Stephen V Flowerday,Nikolay Lipskiy,Steven Furnell,Callum E Flowerday,John Hale*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper adapts the SEIR epidemiological model to ransomware incident response, creating an operational cyber epidemiology framework that distinguishes propagation state from observation status, uses R0 and Re as near-real-time decision aids, and provides a shared language linking technical telemetry to containment decisions under resource constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ransomware incidents often behave like outbreaks rather than isolated detections — a single foothold can spread rapidly through identities, administrative tools, and shared services. Responders must make time-critical decisions with incomplete visibility, yet existing approaches lack a structured, interoperable framework for understanding and managing propagation dynamics. The authors aim to bridge this gap by borrowing proven epidemiological concepts to support earlier isolation, credential containment, and restoration sequencing.

Method: The framework adapts the Susceptible-Exposed-Infectious-Removed (SEIR) compartmental model to ransomware contexts, where "Exposed" captures latent compromise/staging (including dwell time before confirmed secondary compromise) and "Infectious" captures active lateral propagation. It draws on ISO 5477:2023 and the 2025 UNDRR-ISC Hazard Information Profiles to define interoperable ransomware case definitions and Essential Elements of Information. Basic and effective reproduction numbers (R0 and Re) serve as directional, near-real-time metrics for SOC decision-making. Propagation state is deliberately separated from observation status to avoid conflating spread dynamics with detection capability. The approach is illustrated using publicly reported incidents (WannaCry, NotPetya, SolarWinds, MGM/Caesars) and operationalized via protection-threshold heuristics and a tool-agnostic playbook card.

Result: The framework yields practical heuristics for reducing Re below 1 (the outbreak containment threshold) and produces a playbook card that maps operational information to explicit action triggers. The case studies demonstrate how outbreak-style measures can conceptually support earlier isolation, credential containment, and restoration sequencing in real-world ransomware incidents.

Conclusion: The primary contribution is a shared language bridging technical telemetry and containment decisions under resource constraints, enabling cross-incident comparison and structured, epidemiology-informed ransomware response.

Abstract: Ransomware is often treated as a detection problem, but the most disruptive incidents behave more like outbreaks. A single foothold can spread through identities, administrative tools, and shared services while responders make time-critical decisions with incomplete visibility. This paper presents an operational cyber epidemiology framework that adapts the Susceptible-Exposed-Infectious-Removed (SEIR) model to ransomware incident management. In this ontology, Exposed denotes latent compromise and staging, including the dwell period before confirmed secondary compromise, while Infectious denotes active lateral propagation. Drawing on ISO 5477:2023 guidance for public health emergency preparedness and response information management and the 2025 UNDRR-ISC Hazard Information Profiles, the framework defines interoperable ransomware case definitions and Essential Elements of Information for cross-incident comparison. Basic and effective reproduction numbers, R0 and Re, are used as directional, near-real-time decision aids for security operations centers. Propagation state is separated from observation status to avoid confusing spread dynamics with detection capability. Publicly reported incidents, including WannaCry, NotPetya, SolarWinds, and MGM and Caesars, illustrate how outbreak-style measures can support earlier isolation, credential containment, and restoration sequencing. The paper also derives practical protection-threshold heuristics aimed at reducing Re below 1 and provides a tool-agnostic playbook card linking operational information to explicit action triggers. The primary contribution is a shared language that connects technical telemetry to containment decisions under resource constraints.

</details>


### [196] [Beyond Resilience: Antifragility in Critical Infrastructure Cybersecurity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29550)
*Stephen Flowerday,Mauricio Papa,Ethan Flowerday*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper develops a Theory of Antifragility (AFT) for critical infrastructure cybersecurity, grounded in a five-state Resilient System Model, and empirically tests it using two datasets, demonstrating differentiated fragility burden and perturbation observability as prerequisites for antifragility testing in OT environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critical infrastructure cybersecurity lacks empirically grounded theoretical frameworks that move beyond recovery toward bounded improvement under disruption. Existing approaches remain limited for operational technology (OT), necessitating a formal theory of antifragility tailored to CI contexts.

Method: The authors construct a Theory of Antifragility (AFT) anchored in a five-state Resilient System Model with a bounded mathematical definition using Jensen gain and post-disruption gain. Empirical validation employs a two-layer design: (1) analysis of OT-adjacent vs. comparison sectors using the CISSM Cyber Events Database, and (2) analysis of the HAI hardware-in-the-loop industrial control dataset. Three confirmatory hypotheses and one exploratory proposition are tested using statistical methods including proportion comparison, percentile-based thresholding, and Spearman rank correlation.

Result: OT-adjacent sectors exhibited a significantly higher share of disruptive/mixed events (65.3%) versus comparison sectors (46.8%, p < 0.001), with greater concentration of physical-attack and data-attack subtypes. In the HAI dataset, attack-labeled observations were 7.43 times more likely to exceed the 95th percentile of baseline deviation (p < 0.001). Mean process-state deviation across successive attack windows declined significantly (Spearman ρ = -0.688, p = 0.007), indicating measurable response variation rather than definitive adaptive gain.

Conclusion: The findings establish two necessary prerequisites for future antifragility testing in CI cybersecurity: (1) differentiated fragility burden across sectors, and (2) process-level perturbation observability. The work provides an empirically grounded theoretical scaffold for moving beyond resilience toward antifragility in OT security.

Abstract: Critical infrastructure cybersecurity increasingly requires frameworks that move beyond recovery toward bounded improvement under disruption, yet empirically grounded theories for operational technology remain limited. This paper develops a Theory of Antifragility (AFT) for critical infrastructure (CI) cybersecurity, anchored in a five-state Resilient System Model and a bounded mathematical definition based on Jensen gain and post-disruption gain. A two-layer empirical design pairs a CI-relevant subset of the CISSM Cyber Events Database with the HAI hardware-in-the-loop industrial control dataset and tests three confirmatory hypotheses and one exploratory proposition. OT-adjacent sectors show significantly higher shares of disruptive or mixed events than comparison sectors (65.3 percent versus 46.8 percent, p less than 0.001), together with a greater concentration of physical-attack and data-attack subtypes. In HAI, attack-labeled observations were 7.43 times more likely than normal observations to exceed the 95th percentile of baseline deviation (p less than 0.001). Across successive attack windows, mean process-state deviation declined significantly (Spearman rho = -0.688, p = 0.007), indicating measurable response variation rather than proof of adaptive gain. Together, the findings establish two prerequisites for future antifragility testing: differentiated fragility burden and process-level perturbation observability.

</details>


### [197] [CWEEP: A Lexical Static Analysis Framework for CWE Early Prevention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29604)
*Bryan Kwan,Benjamin Tan*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CWEEP is a static analysis framework for detecting security weaknesses in RTL code without requiring a detailed security specification. It pinpoints vulnerability locations and suggests automatic code repairs. Evaluated on two SoC designs and a dataset of 3,874 buggy modules, CWEEP achieves up to 60.8% correct warning rate, significantly outperforming a prior tool's 17.5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hardware security verification currently demands substantial manual effort from security experts, and no standardized method exists to localize faults within RTL code. There is a pressing need for automated, early-stage security analysis that does not depend on complete security specifications.

Method: CWEEP is a static analysis framework that operates at the register transfer level. It detects security weaknesses without requiring a detailed security specification, enabling use during early RTL development. The framework identifies the exact RTL location of potential vulnerabilities and provides automatic code repair suggestions where applicable.

Result: CWEEP issues correct warnings up to 60.8% of the time across evaluations on two SoC designs with manually inserted bugs and an LLM-generated dataset of 3,874 buggy modules. In comparison, a prior tool achieved only a 17.5% correct warning rate on the same dataset.

Conclusion: CWEEP significantly advances automated hardware security verification by reducing reliance on manual expertise and complete security specifications, while offering precise vulnerability localization and repair suggestions. Its 3.5× improvement in correct warning rate over prior work demonstrates its effectiveness for early-stage RTL security analysis.

Abstract: As the hardware layer becomes a focus point for attackers, the need for improved hardware security verification techniques is more important than ever. State-of-the-art security verification techniques require significant manual effort from individuals with security expertise. Furthermore, there is no standard method to locate where the fault lies within the register transfer level (RTL) code. This paper presents CWEEP, a static analysis framework for detecting security weaknesses in RTL. CWEEP does not require a detailed security specification, so it can be used in the early stages of RTL development while properties are still under construction. Furthermore, CWEEP can identify the exact location in the RTL where the potential vulnerability resides and supports automatic code repair suggestions when applicable. Using datasets from the literature, we evaluate the performance of CWEEP on a set of two SoC designs with manually inserted bugs and on a large language model generated dataset, consisting of 3874 buggy modules. We find that CWEEP issues a correct warning up to 60.8% of the time. In contrast, the tool from a previous work issued a correct warning 17.5% of the time for the same dataset.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [198] [Metaphor-Induced Algorithmic Steering: Cross-Domain Procedural Transfer in LLM Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28683)
*Zhibo Hu,Chen Wang,Yanfeng Shu,Hye-young Paik,Liming Dong,Liming Zhu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Metaphorical instructions can subtly steer code-generation LLMs toward inefficient algorithms by transferring procedural patterns from benign source domains, a phenomenon the authors term "metaphorical algorithmic steering." They introduce MASC, a framework to elicit and study this effect, showing it operates through hidden-state shifts toward low-efficiency procedural prototypes rather than surface-level language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While metaphors and analogies enhance LLM generalizability, they may also implicitly transfer inappropriate procedural schemas into new tasks. The authors investigate whether metaphorical instructions can cause code-generation models to favor inefficient algorithms (e.g., exhaustive search) without explicitly mentioning the target algorithm, revealing a subtle failure mode in LLM reasoning.

Method: The authors develop MASC (Metaphorical Algorithmic Steering for Code Generation), a framework that iteratively metaphorizes and refines benign skills to elicit low-efficiency code. They evaluate behaviorally by measuring algorithmic efficiency degradation and mechanistically by analyzing hidden-state representations, detecting metaphorical skills, and identifying shifts toward low-efficiency procedural prototypes.

Result: MASC achieves high detection rates for metaphorical skills and less-efficient implementations. Metaphorical skills induce hidden-state shifts toward lower-efficiency procedural behavior prototypes, indicating that the steering operates through transfer of procedural patterns from metaphorical source scenarios rather than surface-level language alone.

Conclusion: Metaphorical algorithmic steering represents a meaningful failure mode where code-generation models carry domain-appropriate procedures into programming tasks where they become inefficient. The mechanistic findings suggest deeper representational transfer of procedural schemas, not merely superficial linguistic effects, highlighting a novel safety and robustness concern for LLM deployment.

Abstract: Large language models benefit from elements in natural language, such as metaphors and analogies in training data and inference input to achieve generalisability across different domains. However, these language elements may also lead to unwanted behaviors when metaphorical expressions implicitly transfer inappropriate procedural patterns into new tasks. In this paper, we show that metaphorical instructions can induce analogical transfer of procedural mechanisms, thus steering code-generation models towards less efficient algorithms. We refer to this metaphor-induced effect as metaphorical algorithmic steering: a skill that is benign and plausible within its source domain transfers an abstract procedural schema into a programming task, causing the model to favor exhaustive search, full scans, or repeated reconstruction without explicitly mentioning the target algorithm. More broadly, this suggests that code-generation models can carry procedures that are appropriate in a task's background domain into the task's programming problem, where they can lead to unwanted outcomes. To study this phenomenon, we develop MASC (Metaphorical Algorithmic Steering for Code Generation), a framework that iteratively metaphorizes and refines benign skills to elicit low-efficiency code while remaining benign and task-relevant. Beyond behavioral evaluation, we study whether this phenomenon is detectable and mechanistically reflected in model representations. Our method achieves high detection rates for metaphorical skills and less-efficient implementations. We also find that metaphorical skills induce a hidden-state shift towards lower-efficiency procedural behavior prototypes. These results suggest that metaphorical algorithmic steering operates through the transfer of procedural patterns associated with metaphorical source scenarios rather than surface level metaphorical language alone.

</details>


### [199] [Code Is the Body: Agent-Owned Software Bodies for Recursive Evolution and Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28691)
*Roy Zhao,Zhenyu Zhao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: OurArk proposes an architecture for persistent personal AI agents where the agent's "software body" — containing code, prompts, tools, skills, policies, tests, and evolution mechanisms — is an inspectable, versioned, identity-bearing artifact under human custody. It supports governed self-evolution (human-reviewed, validated changes) and recursive descent (creating independently versioned descendant agents), implemented in the open-source Genesis engine and Enoch reference agent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current personalized AI agents allow configuration but deny users meaningful control over the underlying artifacts that determine future behavior. Users cannot inspect, version, govern, or evolve the core software body of their agents. The authors aim to give people genuine ownership and governance over persistent personal agents through a transparent, versioned, and human-controlled evolution mechanism.

Method: The OurArk architecture centers on an agent-owned "software body" comprising code, prompts, tools, skills, policies, tests, and evolution mechanisms, all identity-bearing, inspectable, and versioned. Private state (memories, credentials) stays isolated; model inference is an external replaceable service. Two key mechanisms are defined: (1) Governed self-evolution — producing isolated candidate changes that are validated, reviewed, and merged under human control; (2) Recursive descent — creating independently versioned descendants with distinct identity, mission, history, and private-state boundaries, with selective adaptation from parent changes and peer skills. The architecture is implemented in the open-source Genesis creation engine and Enoch reference agent, validated via a four-agent, three-descent linear lineage with executable regression tests.

Result: A four-agent, three-descent linear lineage with executable regression tests successfully demonstrated: recursive creation of descendant agents, inherited validation contracts, isolated body changes, human-controlled review, and failed-update recovery. The open-source Genesis engine and Enoch reference agent provide a working implementation.

Conclusion: OurArk provides a concrete, implementable substrate for personal agents that people can genuinely possess, govern, specialize, and evolve over time. By making the agent's software body an inspectable, versioned artifact under human custody with governed evolution and descent mechanisms, the architecture enables long-term human-agent co-development.

Abstract: Personalized AI agents are often configurable without giving users control over the artifacts that determine their future behavior. We present OurArk, an architecture for persistent personal agents centered on an agent-owned software body: an identity-bearing, inspectable, and versioned artifact under human custody. The body contains behavior-defining code, prompts, tools, skills, policies, tests, and evolution mechanisms. Memories and credentials remain private instance state, while model inference is treated as a replaceable external service. OurArk defines governed self-evolution and recursive descent over the same body. Self-evolution produces isolated candidate changes that are validated, reviewed, and merged under human control, enabling human-agent co-development of the agent's software body. Descent creates an independently versioned descendant with a distinct identity, mission, history, and fresh private-state boundary; compatible descendants can themselves source further descent. After divergence, direct-parent changes and peer skills can be inspected for selective local adaptation. We implement the architecture in the open-source Genesis creation engine and Enoch reference agent. A four-agent, three-descent linear lineage and executable regression tests demonstrate recursive creation, inherited validation contracts, isolated body changes, human-controlled review, and failed-update recovery. OurArk provides a concrete substrate for personal agents that people can possess, govern, specialize, and evolve over time.

</details>


### [200] [DragonCrawl: A Generative, Intent-Based Framework for Scalable Mobile End-to-End Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28750)
*Sowjanya Puligadda,Mengdie Zhang,Ali Zamani,Dhruva Dixith Kurra,Eric Chen,Juan Marcano*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DragonCrawl is an AI-driven mobile regression testing system that evolved from embedding-based matching to GPT-4o multimodal intent-based reasoning, achieving ~92% pass rates across 1,013 CI/CD tests, reducing onboarding from 100+ hours to under 4 hours, and saving ~27 developer-years in test maintenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional E2E mobile testing frameworks are brittle due to UI volatility, incur heavy maintenance overhead, and lack cross-platform scalability. Existing LLM-based testing research focuses narrowly on exploratory testing and crash detection rather than validating specific user flows on every code change to block breaking commits.

Method: DragonCrawl employs a two-phase architectural evolution: V1 uses semantic embedding similarity matching for UI element location; V2 adopts generative intent-based reasoning via GPT-4o multimodal capabilities, combining multimodal vision for end-state detection and tool calling for backend state transitions to bridge UI interactions with system state in CI/CD pipelines.

Result: The system achieved 91.6% pass rate on iOS and 92.2% on Android across 1,013 automated tests; reduced test onboarding time from 96–120 hours to under 4 hours; and saved an estimated 27 developer-years in test maintenance effort.

Conclusion: AI-driven testing with multimodal LLMs can maintain stability while eliminating the brittleness of traditional automated tests, enabling continuous quality assurance at scale. The architectural shift from embedding matching to generative reasoning proved critical, though challenges like token explosion and memory constraints remain.

Abstract: As mobile applications grow in complexity, traditional End-to-End (E2E) testing frameworks struggle with UI volatility, maintenance overhead, and cross-platform scalability.
