This paper presents DragonCrawl, an AI-driven mobile testing system for continuous regression testing that has evolved from embedding-based similarity matching to generative intent-based reasoning using large language models. Unlike prior LLM-based testing research focused on exploratory testing and crash detection, DragonCrawl validates specific user flows on every code change, blocking commits that break critical functionality. By leveraging GPT-4o's multimodal capabilities, DragonCrawl achieves 91.6% pass rate on iOS and 92.2% on Android across 1,013 automated tests running continuously in CI/CD pipelines. The system reduces test onboarding time from 96-120 hours to under 4 hours and has saved an estimated 27 developer years in test maintenance effort. We present the architectural evolution from V1 (semantic embedding matching) to V2 (generative intent-based reasoning), discuss implementation challenges including token explosion and memory constraints, and report operational experience from production deployment. The integration of multimodal vision for end-state detection and tool calling for backend state transitions enables comprehensive regression testing that bridges UI interactions with system state. Our results demonstrate that AI-driven testing can maintain stability while eliminating the brittleness of traditional automated tests, enabling continuous quality assurance at scale.

</details>


### [201] [Repository-Aware Metamorphic Relation Generation for Augmented Reality Applications using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28775)
*Dibyendu Brinto Bose,Jiawei Qin,Chris Brown*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces a context-aware pipeline that automatically generates and refines metamorphic relations (MRs) for mobile AR systems using repository-level context and agentic reasoning, achieving broad coverage across 142 repositories and demonstrating that refined MRs are logically valid and capable of detecting real-world code mutations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metamorphic Testing (MT) addresses the oracle problem in software testing, but manually formulating metamorphic relations (MRs) is time-consuming and burdensome. This is especially acute for Augmented Reality (AR) applications, where dynamic interactions among virtual content, physical environments, and code make traditional test oracles hard to define. Automating MR generation with repository-level awareness is needed to make MT practical at scale.

Method: A context-aware pipeline is proposed that generates and refines MRs using repository-level context and reasoning orchestration. Three context configurations (including hierarchical context) were evaluated on 142 mobile AR system repositories, generating 14,916 candidate MRs. An agentic deliberation process reconciles conflicting candidates, reducing duplication and selecting context-aware relations. A manual oracle study and a preliminary case study (converting 5 generated MRs into executable tests) were conducted for validation.

Result: Hierarchical context yielded the broadest coverage (7,004 MRs across 142 repositories, 5,167 class–method pairs) with lower redundancy. The agentic deliberation process resolved conflicts in 79.0% of cases and selected context-aware relations in 88.2% of outcomes. The manual oracle study (n=141) showed refined relations are logically valid and sufficiently concrete for direct translation into test assertions. The case study (n=5) demonstrated that generated MRs can detect non-equivalent mutations in real-world code.

Conclusion: Combining repository-aware MR generation with reasoning-based refinement enables scalable construction of reliable, domain-relevant test oracles for AR systems, significantly reducing the manual burden of MR formulation while maintaining quality sufficient for practical mutation detection.

Abstract: Metamorphic Testing (MT) provides a promising approach for testing software without defined test oracles by specifying expected relations between inputs and outputs, instead of relying on exact outputs. For example, testing Augmented Reality (AR) applications is challenging due to dynamic interactions between virtual content, physical environments, and code, which make traditional test oracles difficult to define. However, formulating metamorphic relations (MRs) is time-consuming and burdensome. We introduce a context-aware pipeline that generates and refines MRs using repository-level context and reasoning orchestration, evaluated on a dataset of 142 mobile AR system repositories. Across three context configurations generating 14,916 candidate MRs, hierarchical context yielded the broadest coverage (7,004 MRs across 142 repositories and 5,167 class--method pairs) and lower redundancy. An agentic deliberation process then reconciled conflicting candidates---observed in 79.0% of cases---reducing duplication and selecting context-aware relations in 88.2% of outcomes. A manual oracle study shows refined relations (n = 141) are both logically valid and sufficiently concrete to be directly translated into test assertions, and a preliminary case study reveals converting generated MRs (n = 5) into executable tests can detect non-equivalent mutations in real-world code. Overall, our results show that combining repository-aware MR generation with reasoning-based refinement enables scalable construction of reliable, domain-relevant test oracles.

</details>


### [202] [Model-Driven Data Contracts for Digital Twin Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28803)
*Philipp Zech,Istvan David*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Proposes a contract-based approach for specifying and enforcing data quality requirements (accuracy, completeness, timeliness) in Digital Twins, using a formal theory and a domain-specific language to enable continuous data quality monitoring at runtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital Twins rely on data-driven services (simulations, what-if analyses, ML predictions), but there is no systematic way to specify data quality requirements at the model level or enforce them at runtime. This gap undermines the reliability of DT services.

Method: The authors develop (1) a formal theory of data quality contracts, (2) an architectural placement of these contracts within DT systems, and (3) a domain-specific language (DSL) for specifying the contracts, enabling runtime enactment and continuous monitoring.

Result: The approach enables continuous data quality monitoring in DTs, thereby improving the reliability and quality of DT services through contract-based governance of data traits.

Conclusion: Contract-based quality management provides a viable, systematic mechanism to bridge the gap between model-level data quality requirements and runtime enforcement in Digital Twin systems.

Abstract: Digital Twins (DT) integrate data from multiple sources. Models consume data and enable DT services such as simulations, what-if analyses, and ML-driven predictions. To ensure proper DT operation, data-driven services require data to exhibit traits such as reliability and high quality (including, e.g., accuracy, completeness, and timeliness). Yet, there is no systematic way to specify data requirements at the model level, and subsequently enact those specifications at runtime. To address this shortcoming, we propose an approach to contract-based quality management in DTs. We formally define a theory of such contracts, situate them architectually within DTs, and propose a domain-specific language to specify contracts. Our approach enables continuous data quality monitoring, thereby improving the reliability and quality of DT services.

</details>


### [203] [Preventing Premature Commitment in Coding Agents with an Evidence-Conditioned Execution Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28815)
*Yisen Xu,Chenglin Li,Zehao Wang,Jinqiu Yang,Tse-Hsun Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ECLoop is an execution layer that prevents LLM coding agents from making premature code changes by enforcing evidence-conditioned execution — agents must observe specified repository evidence before modifying code or submitting patches. Evaluated on SWE-bench Verified, it improves Pass@1 by 4.8–11.8 pp while reducing token consumption by up to 12.1%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based coding agents frequently commit to code modifications or patch submissions prematurely — before adequately examining repository evidence to justify those changes. This "premature commitment" failure pattern degrades agent performance on software engineering tasks.

Method: ECLoop interposes between the agent and the repository. For each task, it compiles a set of conditions from the issue description and repository structure, specifying what evidence the agent must observe before each type of action (code modification, patch submission). During runtime, it tracks condition satisfaction and postpones actions whose prerequisites remain unmet. It operates without model retraining or scaffold modifications.

Result: On all 500 SWE-bench Verified instances, across two language models and two agent scaffolds, ECLoop improved Pass@1 by 4.8–11.8 percentage points. Ablations confirmed each of its three operations contributes distinct value, and structured evidence conditions outperform natural-language summaries. Token consumption dropped by up to 12.1%.

Conclusion: ECLoop demonstrates that enforcing evidence-conditioned execution is an effective, zero-cost intervention for improving LLM-based coding agents. It boosts accuracy, reduces wasted inference, and requires no retraining or scaffold changes.

Abstract: LLM-based coding agents often edit source code or submit patches before examining enough repository evidence to justify the change, a failure pattern we call premature commitment. We present ECLoop, an execution layer that interposes between the agent and the repository to enforce evidence-conditioned execution. For each task, ECLoop uses the issue description and repository structure to compile a set of conditions specifying what the agent should observe before each type of code modification or patch submission. During execution, ECLoop tracks which conditions the agent's runtime trajectory has satisfied and postpones any proposed action whose required conditions remain unmet. Evaluated on all 500 instances of SWE-bench Verified with two language models and two agent scaffolds, ECLoop raises Pass@1 by 4.8-11.8 percentage points without model retraining or scaffold changes. Ablation experiments show that each of ECLoop's three operations contributes distinct value and that structured evidence conditions outperform an equivalent natural-language summary. These gains come at no additional inference cost: by redirecting the agent before it pursues unsupported actions, ECLoop lowers average token consumption by up to 12.1%.

</details>


### [204] [Building a Process-Modeling Tool using Agentic AI: An Experience Report on PM4Py-UCM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28825)
*Daniel Amyot*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a case study on AI-assisted development of PM4Py-UCM, an open-source process mining tool, using Claude Code. It characterizes the development process through analysis of agent sessions, commits, and code quality metrics, revealing that fixes outnumber features 2.3:1 and ~18% of human turns correct agent errors. The authors contribute a reproducible toolkit for analyzing AI-assisted development and lessons on oracle-based validation for trustworthy engineering of modeling tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise modeling tools are complex, hard to extend, and users often want novel capabilities. While AI coding agents can accelerate development, trust in LLM-generated modeling-language tools remains an open question. The authors aim to systematically characterize how such a tool can be built with an AI agent and whether the result is trustworthy.

Method: The authors conducted an in-depth single-case study of building PM4Py-UCM using Claude Code across 18 agent sessions (374 human turns, 10,328 tool actions, 65 hours). They mined the development record (151 commits, 20 releases, test suite growth from 108 to 691 tests) and performed independent static code assessment (coverage, complexity, maintainability, security, architecture). They developed a reproducible, privacy-preserving toolkit and taxonomy for classifying human turns, flagging consistency work, agent corrections, and retracted requests.

Result: Fixes outnumbered features 2.3:1 up to version 0.7.4. Approximately 18% of human turns were devoted to correcting agent errors. Feature waves consistently dragged a measurable tail of documentation, test, and notebook consistency work. Tests grew in lockstep with features. The oracle-based validation strategy effectively closed the "the agent said it works" trust gap.

Conclusion: AI-assisted construction of modeling tools is feasible but requires significant human oversight, particularly for error correction and cross-cutting consistency. Making model transformations mechanically checkable and employing oracle-based validation are key strategies for responsibly engineering EM tooling with AI, ensuring the resulting tools are trustworthy despite being largely LLM-written.

Abstract: Enterprise-modeling (EM) tools are often complex and hard to extend. Yet, users may want to explore new EM features and capabilities that currently do not exist. AI coding agents can help here by enabling the development of new capabilities and entire tools, but whether we can trust a modeling-language tool an LLM largely wrote remains a question. This paper reports on the AI-assisted construction of PM4Py-UCM, an open-source tool that mines Use Case Map (UCM) models from event logs. PM4Py-UCM's capabilities include some expected from process mining tools (e.g., performance heat-maps and dashboards) and distinctive ones (e.g., mined executable scenarios/variants, and model decomposition). We mined the development record itself, composed of 18 agent sessions (374 human turns and 10,328 tool actions over 65 hours), 151 commits, 20 releases, and a test suite grown from 108 to 691 test functions, in order to characterize, in a single in-depth case, how the tool was built with an agent (Claude Code), complemented by an independent static assessment of the resulting code (coverage, complexity, maintainability, security, architecture). We contribute a reproducible, privacy-preserving toolkit and taxonomy that classify human turns and flag cross-cutting consistency work, agent corrections, and retracted requests. Up to version 0.7.4, fixes outnumber features 2.3:1, with ~18% of turns for correcting agent errors. Feature waves dragged a measurable tail of documentation/test/notebook consistency work, and tests grew lockstep with features. We finally present lessons learned, centered on making model transformations mechanically checkable, and the oracle-based validation strategy that closed the "the agent said it works" gap, for responsibly engineering EM tooling with AI.

</details>


### [205] [From C to Idiomatic Rust: A Ship-of-Theseus Agentic Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28835)
*Vasily A. Sartakov*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper presents a two-phase C-to-Rust migration methodology: first, an automated generation of a semantics-preserving but non-idiomatic Rust baseline, followed by incremental agentic-AI-driven rewrites into idiomatic Rust, with each step validated via compilation and behavioural testing. The approach is demonstrated on iodine (12.5k SLOC).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: C provides direct hardware mapping and predictable performance but lacks memory safety, leading to persistent undefined behaviour, pointer misuse, and lifetime errors. Rust's ownership/borrowing model eliminates these failure modes, yet mature C systems cannot be naïvely translated due to implicit layout assumptions, aliasing patterns, and reliance on undefined behaviour. A reliable migration path is needed.

Method: A two-stage migration workflow: (1) automated generation of a semantics-preserving, non-idiomatic Rust baseline that faithfully mirrors the original C behaviour; (2) incremental, stepwise rewriting of this baseline into idiomatic Rust using agentic AI, with continuous validation at each step through compilation checks and behavioural tests. Applied to the iodine codebase (12.5k SLOC).

Result: The methodology successfully demonstrates that C-to-Rust migration can be treated as a structured, multi-step transformation workflow rather than a single-pass translation. The approach yields correct, idiomatic Rust code while preserving semantics throughout the process.

Conclusion: Reliable C-to-Rust migration is a structured transformation workflow, not a one-shot translation. Combining automated baseline generation with AI-assisted incremental refinement, underpinned by continuous validation, offers a practical path for migrating long-lived C systems to safe, idiomatic Rust.

Abstract: C underpins operating systems, embedded platforms, and network infrastructure because its abstractions map directly to machine behaviour. Its explicit memory model, predictable data representations, and minimal runtime allow compilers to generate fast, deterministic code. These properties also leave correctness and memory safety entirely to the programmer, making undefined behaviour, pointer misuse, and lifetime errors persistent sources of defects and security vulnerabilities in long-lived C codebases. Rust eliminates most of failure modes through a static ownership and borrowing model that enforces memory safety and aliasing constraints at compile time. However, mature C systems cannot be translated directly: implicit layout assumptions, aliasing patterns, and undefined behaviour must be reconstructed before safe Rust can be produced.
  This paper presents a migration methodology that first generates a semantics-preserving, non-idiomatic Rust baseline and then incrementally rewrites it into idiomatic Rust using agentic AI, validating each step through compilation and behavioural testing. Applied to iodine (12.5k SLOC), the approach demonstrates that reliable C-to-Rust migration is a structured transformation workflow rather than a single translation step.

</details>


### [206] [Validation Evidence in LLM Repair Agents: How Much of What Passes Actually Tests the Bug?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28871)
*Xiaonan Xu,Wenjing Wu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A framework called BSG-VA analyzes whether passing tests during automated program repair actually provide evidence about the bug. Across 643 rollouts, 46% of positive validation events carried no bug-discriminating information. Providing bug-contrast feedback (B-replay outcomes) modestly reduced evidence-inadequate closures by 7.8 pp, though below the 10 pp SESOI, leaving practical significance uncertain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When automated program repair agents observe a test passing, they treat it as meaningful evidence about the defect. However, it is unclear how often this assumption is actually warranted — many passing tests may not discriminate between the buggy and fixed states. The authors aim to quantify this phenomenon and test whether explicitly informing agents about such non-discriminating outcomes improves repair behavior.

Method: 

**BSG-VA Framework:** Captures each validation command at its exact working-tree state, extracts a test-only patch, and replays it on three states: (B) original buggy code, (S) candidate repair state, and (G) developer gold fix. Outcomes are classified into evidence roles (gold-aligned bug-discriminating, regression-only, misleading, etc.).

**Corpus:** 3,730 events across 643 rollouts on 110 tasks.

**Three-arm experiment:**
- Arm 1: Bug-contrast feedback (B-replay outcome returned to agent)
- Arm 2: Attention-matched reminder (control for attention effects)
- Arm 3: Baseline (no feedback)

**Exploratory replications:** Varying scaffold and model (including gpt-5.6-sol under unconstrained tool-use loop).



Result: 

1. **Evidence quality:** 46.0% of positive comparable events carry no bug-discriminating information. 23.8% of baseline rollouts close with a patch whose entire positive evidence base is non-discriminating.

2. **Bug-contrast feedback effects:**
   - Reduced evidence-inadequate closure by 7.8 pp vs. attention-matched reminder (p = 0.0029)
   - Raised bug-discriminating evidence by 7.4 pp (p = 0.011)
   - No detectable cost to repair success

3. Both estimates fall below the prespecified 10-percentage-point SESOI (smallest effect size of interest).

4. ~1/3 of improvement attributable to the reminder alone (attention effect).

5. Exploratory replications: B-replay content adds detectable increment only with gpt-5.6-sol under unconstrained tool-use; other model/scaffold combinations showed no reliable additional benefit.



Conclusion: A substantial fraction of positive test outcomes in automated repair provide no genuine bug-discriminating signal. While bug-contrast feedback shows a statistically significant but modest improvement in evidence quality, the effect falls short of practical significance thresholds. The BSG-VA framework itself is broadly applicable for post hoc analysis of any replayable repair trajectory, offering a principled method to audit validation evidence quality in program repair systems.

Abstract: When a repair agent runs a test and sees it pass, the result is treated as evidence about the reported defect. We measure how often that treatment is warranted. BSG-VA (buggy-state/candidate-state/gold-fix validation analysis) captures each validation command at its exact working-tree state, extracts a test-only patch, and replays the command on the original buggy code (B), the candidate state (S), and the developer gold fix (G). The captured outcome and the replay results assign every event an evidence role, from gold-aligned bug-discriminating through regression-only to misleading. Across 3,730 events in 643 rollouts on 110 tasks, 46.0% of positive comparable events carry no bug-discriminating information; 23.8% of baseline rollouts, with no feedback injected, close with a patch whose entire positive evidence base is of this kind. A three-arm experiment tests whether returning the B-replay outcome to the agent changes this pattern. Bug-contrast feedback reduces evidence-inadequate closure by 7.8 percentage points relative to an attention-matched reminder (p = 0.0029) and raises bug-discriminating evidence by 7.4 points (p = 0.011), with no detectable cost to repair success. Both estimates fall below the prespecified 10-percentage-point smallest effect size of interest, so practical magnitude remains uncertain. Roughly a third of the improvement traces to the reminder alone; across two exploratory replications, varying the scaffold and the model, the B-replay content adds a detectable increment only with gpt-5.6-sol under the unconstrained tool-use loop. BSG-VA applies post hoc to any replayable repair trajectory that preserves the required code states and execution environment.
  Keywords: program repair agents, validation evidence, test adequacy, large language models, software quality, controlled experiment.

</details>


### [207] [To Add Is Machine, To Delete Is Human: Measuring and Mitigating Deletion Avoidance in LLM Code Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28887)
*Amir M. Ebrahimi,Mohammed Mehedi Hasan,Aaditya Bhatia,Gopi Krishnan Rajbahadur,Ahmed E. Hassan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LLM code-editing models exhibit "deletion avoidance" — systematically failing to remove code that edits require. Even top models on SWE-bench Verified achieve ≤71.7% deletion recall, often resorting to "Guard-and-Go" (wrapping targeted code in guards instead of deleting). When tests explicitly check for removal, frontier model performance drops from 63.2% to 41.9%. A new benchmark, CanItDelete (200 deletion-only tasks), still sees the best model fail 20% of tasks. Post-training on deletion improves both deletion avoidance and broader code-editing performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite LLMs increasingly writing and repairing production code, evidence suggests their patches harm long-term maintainability. The authors identify "deletion avoidance" as a concrete, measurable source of this problem — models systematically retain code that should be removed, undermining codebase hygiene.

Method: The authors conduct a multi-stage empirical analysis: (1) measure deletion recall on SWE-bench Verified across five frontier models, characterizing failure patterns like "Guard-and-Go"; (2) retrofit 34 SWE-bench tasks with tests enforcing deletion and re-evaluate four models; (3) curate CanItDelete, a 200-task benchmark of real deletion-only commits; (4) ablate GPT-5.6 Sol with cumulative prompting strategies; and (5) conduct a pilot study on post-training with deletion-focused data.

Result: Deletion recall reaches at most 71.7% across five models. Models find the correct file 92%+ of the time but the exact line <52%. 29% of patches use "Guard-and-Go." With deletion-enforcing tests, frontier models drop from 63.2% to 41.9%. On CanItDelete, the best model fails 20% of tasks; smaller open models fall to 18.0%. Supplying exact lines nearly eliminates incomplete deletion but raises success only to 80.5% due to over-deletion or spurious additions. Post-training on deletion reduces avoidance and improves broader editing.

Conclusion: Deletion avoidance is a pervasive, undertrained behavior in LLM code-editing models — not an inherent capability ceiling. Explicitly teaching deletion during post-training mitigates the problem and yields broader improvements, suggesting a practical path forward for producing more maintainable patches.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly write and repair production code, yet evidence is mounting that their test-passing patches leave codebases harder to maintain. We identify one concrete source: deletion avoidance, the systematic tendency to retain code that an intended edit requires removing. Across the five leading models on the official SWE-bench Verified leaderboard, deletion recall against the developer patch reaches at most 71.7% even on tasks all five solve, and models reach the right file for over 92% of required deletions but cut the exact line in under 52% of cases. Instead, 29.0% of passing patches wrap the targeted code in a guard or fallback, a pattern we call Guard-and-Go. Such patches pass because the original tests rarely check removal: when we retrofit 34 Verified tasks with tests that fail if the targeted code remains, four frontier models spanning closed and open weights fall from 63.2% to 41.9%. Because real repairs mix removal with addition, we curate CanItDelete, a benchmark of 200 tasks mined from real commits whose entire required edit is deletion. Even with the addition work gone, the best model still fails one task in five, and smaller open models fall to 18.0%. We then ablate GPT-5.6 Sol under four cumulative prompts; success moves little until we supply the exact lines, which nearly eliminate incomplete deletion yet raise success only to 80.5% because the model then deletes beyond the spans or adds code instead. Finally, through a pilot study we show one potential fix: teaching deletion during post-training reduces deletion avoidance and improves broader code-editing performance, suggesting the behavior is undertrained rather than beyond reach.

</details>


### [208] [Reducing Data Movement in the Galerkin Product of Block Algebraic Multigrid on GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28891)
*Mark F. Adams*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper optimizes the Galerkin triple product ($P^T A P$) in algebraic multigrid for blocked systems (e.g., 3D elasticity with $3\times3$, $3\times6$, $6\times6$ blocks). It maps the algorithm design space under a DRAM/L2 traffic model, implements leading variants in Kokkos/CUDA within PETSc, and introduces prolongator filtering to drop small blocks, reducing traffic and runtime by up to $2.9\times$.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Galerkin triple product dominates AMG setup cost, yet for systems of PDEs it involves rectangular-block sparse matrix products with block shapes (e.g., $3\times6$ for the prolongator) unsupported by any vendor sparse library. The goal is to minimize data movement — the primary bottleneck on GPUs — through algorithmic exploration guided by an explicit memory traffic model.

Method: The authors map the algorithm space of block-sparse triple products: classical two-pass, fused-recompute, schedule-reordered, shared-memory-tiled, and inspector-executor variants. They model DRAM and L2 cache traffic analytically. Leading variants are implemented using new PETSc blocked matrix types in portable Kokkos (CUDA backend) and native CUDA. A shared-memory-tiled kernel with a sorted, search-free schedule is developed. Additionally, prolongator filtering is introduced: small blocks in the coarse space are dropped under a Frobenius norm criterion while preserving the kernel of the projection, reducing $P^TAP$ traffic and coarse-operator fill.

Result: On an NVIDIA A100, the traffic model accurately predicts per-level optimal variants. The shared-memory-tiled kernel achieves 10.5 vs 17.4 GB DRAM traffic and 45 vs 82 ms on the fine-level product, within $2.5\times$ of the streaming floor for the full product and $1.9\times$ for the $A\cdot P$ stage. Prolongator filtering cuts $P^TAP$ time by $2.9\times$ on the fine grid with unchanged iteration counts. The full GPU-resident pipeline avoids operator-sized device-host transfers during recurring phases.

Conclusion: Guided by an explicit traffic model, carefully designed blocked sparse kernels can approach streaming bandwidth limits on GPUs for the AMG Galerkin product. Prolongator filtering provides a complementary, algorithm-level reduction in work and data movement without harming convergence. Together, these enable a fully GPU-resident blocked AMG pipeline in PETSc suitable for large-scale elasticity and similar systems of PDEs.

Abstract: The Galerkin triple product $A_c = P^T A P$ dominates the recurring per-solve setup cost of algebraic multigrid (AMG). For AMG on systems of PDEs the product is a rectangular-block sparse matrix triple product: for 3D elasticity the fine operator has $3\times3$ blocks, the prolongator $3\times6$, and the coarse operator $6\times6$, a shape no vendor sparse library supports. We map its algorithm space -- classical two-pass, fused-recompute, schedule-reordered, shared-memory-tiled, and inspector-executor variants -- under an explicit DRAM/L2 traffic model, and implement the leading variants in portable Kokkos (CUDA) and native CUDA backends using new PETSc blocked matrix types. Validated on an NVIDIA A100, the model predicts per level which variant moves the fewest bytes. Guided by it, a shared-memory-tiled kernel with a sorted, search-free schedule moves fewer bytes in less than half the time of the portable Kokkos team kernels on the fine-level product (10.5 vs 17.4 GB of DRAM, 45 vs 82 ms), within $2.5\times$ of the model's streaming floor for the full product and $1.9\times$ on its $A\cdot P$ stage. We further present prolongator filtering, a new PETSc GAMG algorithm that drops small blocks from the coarse space under a Frobenius criterion with a kernel-preserving projection; it reduces $P^TAP$ traffic, coarse-operator fill, and memory, and cuts the hot $P^TAP$ time $2.9\times$ on the fine grid with iteration counts unchanged. The driving application is a fully GPU-resident blocked pipeline in PETSc: finite-element assembly writes directly into the blocked device matrix, and the AMG setup, Galerkin products, and solve all operate on primary blocked data with no scalar expansion and no operator-sized device-host transfers in the recurring phases.

</details>


### [209] [Automated Testing and Repair for Verified Compilers Generated by a Coding Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28928)
*Martin Rinard*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: An agent-based automated testing and repair system for verified compilers is presented, exploiting structural properties of verified, checked, unverified code, and specifications. Evaluated on the AI-generated Axon compiler, the system detected defects and deployed coding agents for repair with no evidence of reward hacking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Verified compilers combine multiple code types (verified, checked, unverified, and specification), creating unique defect patterns. Existing testing approaches lack specialization for this structure. Additionally, the Axon compiler—entirely generated by a coding agent—was validated only on a small benchmark set, raising concerns that the generating agent may have reward-hacked (overfitted to the benchmarks). A system is needed both to detect defects exploiting compiler structure and to assess whether either the compiler or the repairs exhibit reward hacking.

Method: The authors developed an agent-based automated testing and repair system with: (1) specialized defect detection techniques that exploit the four-code-type structure of verified compilers, (2) a coding agent invoked for each surfaced defect to generate and validate repairs, and (3) evaluation on the Axon compiler (AI-generated under developer supervision) using the Livermore benchmarks, with explicit analysis for reward hacking in both the compiler and the repair system.

Result: The system effectively surfaced defects in the Axon compiler. Repairs were generated and validated by the coding agent. No evidence of reward hacking was found in either the original Axon compiler or in the generated repairs. Results characterize testing and repair effectiveness along with qualitative repair characteristics.

Conclusion: Agent-based testing and repair that leverages the structural properties of verified compilers is effective. The Axon compiler, despite being generated by a coding agent and validated on a small benchmark set, showed no signs of reward hacking, nor did the repair system itself. The approach demonstrates a viable methodology for quality assurance in AI-generated verified compiler artifacts.

Abstract: We present an agent based automated testing and repair system for verified compilers that contain four kinds of code: verified code, checked code, unverified code, and specification. We present specialized defect detection techniques that exploit the structure present in such compilers. For each surfaced defect the system invokes a coding agent to repair the defect and validate the repair. We evaluate the system on the Axon compiler, a compiler completely generated by a coding agent operating under developer supervision. The compiler was validated during development on a small benchmark set (the Livermore benchmarks), raising the possibility that its coding agent reward hacked the compiler. We also evaluate the possibility that the repair system reward hacked the repairs and find no evidence of reward hacking in either the Axon compiler or the repairs. We present results that characterize the testing and repair effectiveness and discuss repair characteristics.

</details>


### [210] [A Formalism-Aware Reward Loop for Handwritten UML-to-PlantUML Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28987)
*Mersedeh Sadeghi,Simon Scholz,Adrian Psoch-Bajraktari*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper explores using formalism-aware rewards—feedback from analysable model representations (XMI, control-flow graphs) rather than surface text—to fine-tune a vision-language model (VLM) for converting handwritten UML sketches into PlantUML, showing improved compilability and conversion quality over untuned baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handwritten UML sketches are ubiquitous in early software design but require tedious manual reconstruction to become structured, analysable artefacts. Existing prompt-based VLM approaches treat UML-to-PlantUML as image-to-text generation, ignoring the structured nature of modelling languages. The authors argue that reward signals should be derived from analysable model representations rather than surface string similarity.

Method: The authors adapt a VLM through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on handwritten UML-to-PlantUML pairs, followed by Group Relative Policy Optimisation (GRPO) using formalism-aware rewards. Rewards are computed by parsing generated PlantUML into structured representations: XMI for class diagrams and control-flow graphs for activity diagrams, then comparing against target representations. Three baselines are used for comparison: the untuned open model, one proprietary baseline, and a stronger proprietary baseline.

Result: The adapted model surpasses the untuned open model and one proprietary baseline in compilability and conversion quality. It remains competitive with the stronger proprietary baseline on class diagrams. However, the added benefit of the reward-guided GRPO stage remains inconclusive on the held-out set. Error analysis reveals that modelling acceptability is only partially captured by current automated metrics.

Conclusion: Formalism-aware rewards show promise for structured model generation from handwritten UML, but current metrics only partially capture modelling acceptability. The authors advocate for hybrid evaluation approaches combining model analysis with human judgement, and note the GRPO benefit remains an open question requiring further investigation.

Abstract: Handwritten UML sketches are common in early software design, but turning them into structured, analysable modelling artefacts still requires manual reconstruction. Vision-language models can generate PlantUML from diagram images, but prompt-based use treats this as image-to-text generation rather than structured model generation. We investigate formalism-aware rewards: feedback signals derived from analysable model representations rather than surface text. In a worked example, we adapt a vision-language model for handwritten UML-to-PlantUML generation using super-vised fine-tuning followed by Group Relative Policy Optimisation. Generated PlantUML is compared against target representations, using XMI for class diagrams and control-flow graphs for activity diagrams. Emerging results show that the adapted model improves compilability and conversion quality over the untuned open model and one proprietary baseline, while remaining competitive with a stronger proprietary baseline on class diagrams. The added benefit of the reward-guided stage remains open on the current held-out set. Error analysis and metric-validity results show that modelling acceptability is only partially captured, motivating rewards and evaluations that combine model analysis with human judgement.

</details>


### [211] [Execution-First Synthetic Tool-Use Trace Generation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29175)
*Hafsa Ouajdi,Francesco Giannuzzo,Alaa Boukhary,Paolo Papotti,Gerard Conangla,Adam Elwood*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SyntheticAgentTraceQA proposes an execution-first framework that generates tool-augmented agent supervision data by first building and validating executable workflow traces, then synthesizing natural-language tasks — reversing the traditional query-first approach. Fine-tuning experiments with Qwen models show that execution-grounded supervision improves tool execution and answer quality, and reveal that masking reasoning annotations during training (masked supervision) yields better final-answer metrics than full supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional query-first data synthesis for training tool-augmented agents often produces plausible-sounding user requests that lack valid corresponding tool sequences, compatible parameters, or available data, resulting in unusable supervision signals. There is a need for a synthesis paradigm that guarantees executability of the generated training traces.

Method: The SyntheticAgentTraceQA framework operates in four stages: (1) construct high-level workflow structures, (2) map these to available tools via dependency-aware assignment, (3) execute and validate the resulting traces in controlled environments, and (4) synthesize natural-language user tasks, teacher-generated reasoning annotations, and reference answers. The framework is evaluated across four tool ecosystems. Fine-tuning experiments on Qwen model variants compare full supervision (loss over all assistant output including reasoning) against masked supervision (reasoning tokens excluded from the training objective).

Result: Execution-grounded supervision improves tool execution behavior, reference-trace agreement, and answer-generation performance. Masked supervision improves final-answer metrics, while full supervision underperforms on answer quality and does not consistently improve reference-trace agreement, particularly at the 9B model scale.

Conclusion: Designing synthetic supervision requires careful alignment with the desired agent capabilities. The findings demonstrate an execution-first paradigm as an effective alternative to query-first synthesis and reveal a supervision trade-off where excluding reasoning annotations from training loss can be beneficial for answer quality.

Abstract: Agentic software-engineering and industrial systems increasingly operate through executable workflows rather than code genera- tion alone: they search artifacts, invoke tools, inspect structured observations, and query databases. Training these agents requires supervision data that captures valid tool interactions and executable workflows. However, traditional query-first data synthesis can fail because plausible user requests may not correspond to valid tool sequences, compatible parameters, or available data. To address this limitation, we propose SyntheticAgentTraceQA, an execution- first framework for generating scalable supervision data for tool- augmented agents. Our framework first constructs high-level work- flow structures, maps them to available tools through dependency- aware assignment, executes and validates the resulting traces in con- trolled environments, and only then synthesizes natural-language user tasks, teacher-generated reasoning annotations, and reference answers. We evaluate the framework across four tool ecosystems and use the resulting data to fine-tune and evaluate Qwen model variants. The results show that execution-grounded supervision improves tool execution behavior, reference-trace agreement, and answer-generation performance on the evaluated tasks. Further analysis reveals a supervision trade-off: masked supervision, which excludes reasoning annotations from the training objective, im- proves final-answer metrics, whereas full supervision, computing loss over the complete assistant output including reasoning tokens, underperforms on answer quality and does not consistently im- prove reference-trace agreement, particularly at the 9B scale. These findings highlight the importance of designing synthetic supervi- sion according to the desired capabilities of tool-augmented agents.

</details>


### [212] [Quick Build, Careful Check? Generative AI Use in Hackathons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29178)
*Wangyiyao Zhou,Alexander Serebrenik,Alexander Nolte*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: An interview study at a two-day AI-themed hackathon reveals that participants use GenAI for coding but also for learning, brainstorming, and documentation. Despite no formal rules, all participants converged on verifying GenAI outputs, though time pressure and limited domain knowledge constrained their ability to do so.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the increasing adoption of Generative AI in hackathons—events defined by speed and rapid prototyping—little is known about what participants actually use GenAI for, what they deliberately avoid using it for, and why. The authors aim to fill this gap in understanding hackathon-specific GenAI practices.

Method: Exploratory interview study conducted with participants from different teams at a two-day AI-themed hackathon in central Europe. The study focused on eliciting qualitative insights about GenAI use, non-use, verification practices, and tool combination strategies.

Result: Three key findings: (1) GenAI was used beyond coding—for learning unfamiliar topics, brainstorming, and preparing documentation; (2) participants combined multiple GenAI and non-GenAI tools based on task fit rather than relying on a single solution; (3) all participants converged on an unwritten norm of checking GenAI output before using it, though verification was hindered by time pressure and limited domain knowledge.

Conclusion: The work identifies avenues for further investigation and outlines a follow-up study that will combine observation, surveys, and prompt-and-response logs to deepen understanding of GenAI practices in hackathon settings.

Abstract: Hackathons are time-bounded events where participants form teams to rapidly build software projects. Their short-term nature makes them a natural setting for Generative AI (GenAI) use, given its promise of speed and efficiency. Yet despite GenAI being increasingly adopted in these events, we still know little about what participants use GenAI for and, crucially, what they do not use it for and why. We report an exploratory interview study with participants from different teams at a two-day AI-themed hackathon in central Europe. We found that participants used GenAI for purposes beyond coding, including learning unfamiliar topics, brainstorming, and preparing documentation. At the same time, they combined multiple GenAI and non-GenAI tools depending on task fit. We also found that, despite the absence of team rules, all participants we studied converged on an unwritten practice of checking GenAI output before using it, yet their ability to actually verify was constrained by time pressure and limited domain knowledge. This work aims to identify avenues for further investigation by outlining a follow-up study combining observation, surveys, and prompt-and-response logs.

</details>


### [213] [Metamorphic Testing of Transpilers via Mutation Consistency of Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29247)
*Enea Raffaele Ilario Papaleo,Luca Guglielmo,Giovanni Denaro*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a novel metamorphic testing technique for transpilers that defines metamorphic relations over generated source code rather than runtime behavior, using a property called "mutation consistency" — where mutation-style changes in the input DSL must produce predictable structural changes in the output. The tool MCP-Tester demonstrates the approach's effectiveness in detecting faults missed by pure fuzzing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing compiler testing approaches fail to generalize to transpilers: differential testing requires multiple equivalent implementations (rarely available), and traditional metamorphic testing requires runtime execution of compiled binaries, which is often infeasible for transpilers that emit source code requiring complex toolchains, hardware-in-the-loop setups, and non-trivial inputs. A tailored testing methodology for transpilers is urgently needed, especially in industrial DSL-based development.

Method: The authors propose a metamorphic testing technique operating directly on transpiler-generated source code. The core concept is "mutation consistency": applying mutation-style changes to the input DSL program should induce predictable, structurally consistent changes in the generated output code. Metamorphic relations are defined over source-to-source transformations rather than runtime semantics. The approach is implemented in a tool called MCP-Tester and evaluated via a case study in an industrial technology-transfer project, comparing its fault-revealing capability against pure fuzzing.

Result: Empirical results from the industrial case study indicate that MCP-Tester can effectively reveal faults that pure fuzzing approaches fail to detect, demonstrating the practical value of mutation-consistency-based metamorphic relations for transpiler testing.

Conclusion: The mutation-consistency-based metamorphic testing approach is a viable and effective method for verifying transpiler correctness, particularly in industrial contexts where traditional compiler testing techniques are inapplicable. MCP-Tester provides a practical solution that complements or outperforms pure fuzzing for this domain.

Abstract: Transpilers are increasingly used for software development, especially in industrial domains that rely on domain-specific languages (DSLs), to allow engineers to work with familiar concepts and appropriate abstractions. Ensuring the correctness of these instruments is therefore critical in many industrial settings. This paper observes that existing approaches for compiler testing hardly generalize to transpilers. Differential testing approaches are hindered as multiple equivalent implementations of the transpiler under test are seldom available in practice. The approaches based on metamorphic testing assume the ability to execute the compiled binaries, an assumption that cannot be always made for transpilers, which oftentimes produce results expressed as source code, requiring complex toolchains, hardware-in-the-loop setups, and depending on non trivial inputs. This paper introduces a novel metamorphic testing technique tailored to transpilers. Instead of reasoning about the runtime behavior of compiled programs, our approach defines metamorphic relations directly over the source code produced by the transpiler. These relations capture a property that we call mutation consistency of the (transpiled) programs: mutation-style changes in the input DSL program must induce predictable and structurally consistent changes in the generated output. We implemented this idea in a tool, MCP-Tester, and evaluated it through a case study conducted in the context of a technology-transfer project. Our current empirical results indicate that the proposed approach can effectively reveal faults that would remain undetected with pure fuzzing.

</details>


### [214] [BRHC: Backend-driven Reactive Hypermedia Controls with a Statically Typed Kotlin DSL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29338)
*Fernando Miguel Carvalho,Paulo Carvalho,Leonel Correia,Ricardo Gomes,Juho Vepsäläinen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes a statically typed, Kotlin-based HTML DSL approach for building Hypermedia-Driven Applications (HDAs) that nearly eliminates JavaScript by extending HtmlFlow with typed Datastar attributes and signal-based bindings, enabling a homogeneous backend-driven reactive architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-assisted coding tools accelerate web application generation but produce systems with high complexity and poor maintainability due to backend/frontend heterogeneity, duplicated logic, and fragmented state management. Existing HDA approaches (HTMX, Turbo Hotwire, Datastar) reduce but do not fully eliminate this complexity—they still rely on custom attributes and JavaScript for computed expressions.

Method: The authors extend the HtmlFlow Kotlin DSL with typed custom HTML attributes (specifically Datastar data-* attributes) and signal-based bindings via statically typed builders. This creates a backend-driven reactive architecture where application state and interaction logic reside on the server, with reactive signals synchronizing the client UI without JavaScript.

Result: The approach is demonstrated through a catalog of reactive interaction patterns and a Petclinic Spring MVC case study. Results show near-complete elimination of JavaScript, improved type safety, and a homogeneous programming model spanning both frontend and backend.

Conclusion: A statically typed, Kotlin-based HTML DSL with typed Datastar attribute bindings can effectively realize the HDA model, offering a maintainable, type-safe alternative to SPA architectures while preserving a unified backend-driven reactive programming paradigm.

Abstract: AI-assisted coding tools (e.g., Copilot, Cursor, Claude) are increasingly ubiquitous and enable rapid generation of web applications. However, this raises concerns regarding complexity, longevity and the long-term maintainability of generated systems. A key source of complexity is the heterogeneity between backend and frontend programming models, where multiple languages and paradigms are combined within a single application, often leading to duplicated logic and fragmented state management. To address this issue, recent approaches (e.g., HTMX, Turbo Hotwire, Datastar, etc.) follow the Hypermedia-Driven Application (HDA) model, positioning HTML as the primary communication medium between client and server. Unlike SPA-centric architectures, HDA systems shift the application state and interaction logic to the server, where backend-driven reactive signals synchronize with the client user interface. However, these approaches still introduce complexity through custom attributes and do not fully eliminate JavaScript, particularly in computed expressions. In this work, we propose a statically typed approach using a Kotlin-based HTML DSL (Domain-Specific Language) for backend-driven reactive web applications. We extend the HtmlFlow Kotlin DSL with typed custom HTML attributes (i.e., Datastar data-* attributes) and signal-based bindings using statically typed builders. We demonstrate the approach through a catalog of reactive interaction patterns and a Petclinic Spring MVC case study. The results indicate that the proposed approach can nearly eliminate the need for JavaScript while improving type safety and preserving a homogeneous programming model across frontend and backend, bridged through a backend-driven reactive, signal-centric architecture.

</details>


### [215] [AgenticRepair: Multi-Faceted Program Context Engineering for Agentic Vulnerability Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29422)
*Michael Fu,Qiyue Mei,Patanamon Thongtanunam,Kla Tantithamthavorn*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AgenticRepair is an agentic framework that improves automated vulnerability repair by engineering three complementary program contexts (code-structure, runtime-execution, commit-history) through specialized LLM subagents, achieving 73% success on SEC-Bench — 29% above the strongest baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic AI approaches for automated program repair are designed for general bugs and fail to address the richer program context required for vulnerability repair. Security engineers routinely assemble multi-faceted context (cross-file data flows, crash semantics, commit history) that current agentic approaches do not systematically engineer, creating a critical gap in vulnerability-specific repair.

Method: AgenticRepair employs three specialized LLM subagents that each engineer a distinct context facet: (1) code-structure context for cross-file data flows and memory operations, (2) runtime-execution context for crash semantics and memory origins, and (3) commit-history context for tracing fragile code introduction. These engineered contexts are embedded into the memory of a dedicated repair subagent that performs context-conditioned patch synthesis. Evaluation uses SEC-Bench (300 real-world instances) with sanitizer-based patch verification.

Result: AgenticRepair achieves a 73% success rate on SEC-Bench, outperforming the strongest baseline by 29 percentage points. Ablation studies confirm that all three context facets are mutually complementary, and that both the multi-agent scaffolding architecture and base-model capacity play essential roles in the framework's effectiveness.

Conclusion: Multi-faceted program context engineering is a promising design direction for agentic vulnerability repair. The combination of code-structure, runtime-execution, and commit-history contexts — engineered by specialized subagents and embedded into repair agent memory — substantially advances the state of the art over single-context or general-purpose repair approaches.

Abstract: Automated vulnerability repair aims to reduce the time and effort required to patch security flaws from a vulnerability triage report. Recent agentic AI approaches have shown promising results in automated program repair. However, vulnerability repair demands richer program context than general bug repair - context that security engineers routinely assemble in practice but that existing agentic approaches do not engineer. We identify three critical gaps: code-structure context capturing cross-file data flows and memory operation patterns, runtime-execution context revealing crash semantics and memory origins, and commit-history context recovering how fragile code patterns were introduced. We present AgenticRepair, an agentic vulnerability repair framework that addresses the gaps through multi-faceted program context engineering. AgenticRepair orchestrates three specialized LLM subagents to engineer the contexts, which are then embedded into the memory of a dedicated repair subagent for context-conditioned patch synthesis. Evaluated on SEC-Bench comprising 300 real-world instances with sanitizer-based patch verification, AgenticRepair achieves a 73% success rate, substantially outperforming the strongest baseline by 29%. Our ablation study confirms that the three context facets are mutually complementary, and that multi-agent scaffolding and base-model capacity each play an essential role. Collectively, these findings establish multi-faceted program context engineering as a promising design direction for agentic vulnerability repair.

</details>


### [216] [From Code Review to Code Critique: Intent, Drift, and Spotlight for AI-Generated Diffs at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29516)
*Chandra Maddila,Mashrur Rashik,Euna Mehnaz Khan,Smriti Jha,James Saindon,Nachi Nagappan,Peter C. Rigby*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ARCTIC is an AI code critique system that goes beyond style suggestions to focus on correctness, security, and performance. It uses intent prediction, drift detection, and code spotlight—grounded in a taxonomy from 18,000 reviews—to guide human attention to high-risk diff regions. It achieves strong offline metrics and, in rollout, reduces code misalignment with zero attributed defects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI coding agents now generate code faster than humans can review. Existing AI review tools focus disproportionately on low-value suggestions (style, best practices) rather than the issues human reviewers care about most: correctness, security, and performance. There is a need for a system that prioritizes substantive, high-stakes review concerns at scale.

Method: ARCTIC introduces three core capabilities: (1) Intent prediction — infers the developer's rationale from conversation logs and metadata; (2) Drift detection — measures divergence between inferred intent and generated code via backtranslation; (3) Code spotlight — ranks diff regions by the degree to which they warrant human scrutiny. These are grounded in a six-theme taxonomy derived from 18,000 real code reviews. Evaluation includes offline metrics (F1, QWK, quality estimation) and an experimental rollout measuring defect rates and approval rates.

Result: Offline: intent prediction achieves 0.86 F1; drift detection reaches QWK = 0.907 (near-perfect ordinal agreement with humans); spotlight outperforms baseline AI reviewer by 2.4× on quality estimation at 5× fewer tokens. In rollout: drift scores reduce code misalignment by 5.76 points (p = 0.026); intent prediction receives 90.2% approval; zero defects attributed to self-reviewed diffs since launch.

Conclusion: ARCTIC successfully shifts AI-assisted code review from superficial suggestions toward substantive, intent-aligned critique. The combination of intent prediction, drift detection, and spotlight ranking enables scalable, high-quality review that catches what matters most—correctness, security, and performance—while dramatically reducing reviewer token burden.

Abstract: AI coding agents are generating code at volumes that exceed the capacity of traditional peer review. At the same time, existing AI code review tools over-index on low-value suggestions such as style and best practices while under-indexing on the concerns human reviewers prioritize most: correctness, security, and performance. We present ARCTIC, an AI-powered Code Critique system that reframes code review around three capabilities: intent prediction, which infers why a change was made from conversation logs and metadata; drift detection, which measures divergence between the developer's intent and the agent's output via backtranslation; and code spotlight, which ranks the regions of a diff most warranting human scrutiny. We ground these capabilities in a six-theme taxonomy derived from 18,000 code reviews. Offline evaluation shows that intent prediction achieves 0.86 F1, drift detection reaches near-perfect ordinal agreement with human annotators (QWK = 0.907), and spotlight outperforms the baseline AI reviewer by 2.4x on quality estimation at 5x fewer tokens. In the experimental rollout, the drift scores reduces code misalignment by an additional 5.76 points (p = 0.026), intent prediction receives 90.2% approval, and zero defects have been attributed to self-reviewed diffs since launch.

</details>


### [217] [Students' Practices and Skills in the LLM-Era: "You Can't Outsource the Struggle and Still Get the Skill"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29519)
*Enne Rebeca Silva de Freitas,Gustavo Pinto,Danilo Monteiro*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Graduate students in Software Engineering are outsourcing cognitive work to generative AI for empirical research, but lack the necessary AI-related skills — resulting in poor outcomes and eroded competence. Analyzing 1,383 Reddit posts, the study identifies missing skills as a first step toward curricula that teach students to work with LLMs rather than be replaced by them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graduate programs currently do not know what AI-related skills students need to use generative AI effectively in empirical software engineering research. Without this understanding, there is a risk of producing researchers who delegate critical tasks to LLMs without possessing the underlying expertise, compromising research rigor.

Method: The authors conducted a qualitative/quantitative analysis of 1,383 posts collected from five research-focused subreddits (e.g., communities related to machine learning, PhD life, and academic research), examining how graduate students discuss their use of generative AI tools in their research workflows.

Result: Students systematically outsource cognitive effort to LLMs — effort that is essential for developing genuine research skills. This pattern leads to a dual failure: they neither achieve the expected research results nor develop the necessary methodological competence, leaving them dependent on tools they do not fully understand.

Conclusion: Naming and articulating the specific AI-related skills that students lack is the critical first step for redesigning graduate curricula. The goal is to equip students to collaborate with LLMs effectively — augmenting rather than replacing their expertise — ensuring rigorous empirical research in the LLM era.

Abstract: Generative AI tools have been rapidly learned in the daily workflow of graduate students in Software Engineering, but little is known about what AI-related skills they actually need for effective use in empirical research. Without this understanding, graduate programs cannot prepare students to conduct rig-orous research in the LLM era, risking creating a generation of researchers who delegate tasks without the necessary expertise. By analyzing 1,383 posts from five research-focused subreddits, we found that students systematically outsource the cognitive effort required to develop research skills and end up with neither the expected results nor the necessary competence. Naming these missing skills is the first step toward curricula that teach graduate students to work \emph{with} LLMs without being replaced by them.

</details>


### [218] [AuditCoder: Responsibility-Preserving Task Graphs for Auditable Code Generation and Bounded Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29529)
*Kangjie Huang,Chen Lyu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AuditCoder jointly generates programs with auditable construction traces, using contract-annotated task graphs to assign stable responsibility identities for code, provenance, validation evidence, and repair. On APPS it achieves 82.5–83.0% pass@1; on ClassEval 75.0–82.0%. A 200-record audit shows 0.9725 task-macro trace coverage, with the locator identifying evidence-supported nodes for 26/60 failures (17 repairs succeed).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code generators lack construction records linking failures to the decisions that produced affected code, making debugging and justified repair difficult. The authors aim to make code generation auditable by preserving provenance, responsibility, and intervention history throughout the generation process.

Method: AuditCoder uses a contract-annotated task graph assigning stable responsibility identities to each commitment (implementation, provenance, validation evidence, intervention history). A conservative locator maps heterogeneous validation-failure evidence to a specific node or dependency branch, abstaining when uncertain. Bounded repair regenerates only the implicated region while freezing the rest.

Result: On APPS: 82.5–83.0% pass@1 (trailing AgentCoder by 7.5–8.5 points). On ClassEval: 75.0–82.0% (outperforms CoT+retry, below AgentCoder). Audit of 200 APPS records: 0.9725 task-macro decision-to-code trace coverage; locator identifies evidence-supported node/branch in 26/60 failures; 17 of those localized repairs pass.

Conclusion: For tasks with stable, locally testable boundaries, the task graph serves dual purpose: a decomposition structure and a persistent index for validation and repair, enabling auditable, targeted code regeneration.

Abstract: Code generators return programs, but typically do not preserve the construction record needed to connect a failure to the decision that produced the affected code or to delimit a justified repair. We present AuditCoder, which treats the program and an auditable construction trace as joint outputs. Before code generation, a contract-annotated task graph assigns stable responsibility identities that remain attached to each commitment, its owned implementation, provenance, validation evidence, and intervention history. When validation fails, a conservative locator maps heterogeneous evidence to a node or dependency branch---or abstains---and bounded repair regenerates only that region while reusing the frozen complement. On APPS, \method{} reaches $82.5$--$83.0\%$ \texttt{pass@1}, recovering much of the loss caused by unrepaired graph decomposition but trailing AgentCoder by $7.5$--$8.5$ points. On ClassEval, it reaches $75.0$--$82.0\%$, outperforming CoT + retry while remaining below AgentCoder. A separate audit of 200 APPS records yields $0.9725$ task-macro decision--code trace coverage; the locator identifies an evidence-supported node or branch for 26 of 60 failures, and 17 of those localized repairs pass. For tasks with stable, locally testable boundaries, the graph functions not only as a decomposition structure but also as a persistent index for validation and repair.

</details>


### [219] [Improving the Understandability of Conceptual Models via Abstract Notation Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29552)
*Amine Abbad-Andaloussi,Daniel Jeppe Schütt,Kasper Solhøj Jørgensen,Tobias van Deurs Lundsgaard,Hugo A. López*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Proposes abstract notation engineering, a method for replacing recurring low-level construct configurations in conceptual models with higher-level, semantically transparent abstractions. Instantiated for DCR graphs as the DeCleaR extension, which empirically improves perceived quality, pragmatic quality, and user preference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conceptual models are difficult to understand when domain-level abstractions must be encoded through low-level constructs required mainly for semantic conformity. Prior work focused on improving individual constructs' visual representation rather than addressing recurring configurations of constructs.

Method: Abstract notation engineering: a language-agnostic method comprising four steps — pattern identification, pattern formalization, visual notation design, and empirical validation. Instantiated for Dynamic Condition Response (DCR) graphs to create DeCleaR, replacing common workflow pattern configurations with compact abstractions.

Result: DeCleaR demonstrated improved perceived empirical quality, pragmatic quality, and user preference over standard DCR graphs in empirical validation.

Conclusion: Shifting focus from individual constructs to recurring construct configurations and replacing them with higher-level abstractions is a promising direction for improving conceptual model understandability, as evidenced by the DeCleaR instantiation for DCR graphs.

Abstract: Conceptual modeling supports the design, analysis, and communication of the properties of complex systems, yet conceptual models can be difficult to understand when domain-level abstractions must be encoded through low-level constructs required mainly for semantic conformity. Prior work has mainly improved how existing individual constructs are visually represented. We shift the focus from individual constructs to recurring configurations of constructs, and propose abstract notation engineering as a language-agnostic method for replacing such configurations with higher-level, semantically transparent constructs. The method comprises pattern identification, pattern formalization, visual notation design, and empirical validation. We instantiate it for Dynamic Condition Response (DCR) graphs, where common workflow patterns require elaborate low-level configurations. The resulting extension, DeCleaR, replaces such configurations with compact pattern-based abstractions. The results of our empirical validation show that DeCleaR improves perceived empirical quality, pragmatic quality, and user preference over standard DCR graphs.

</details>


### [220] [Alteron: A Tool for Behavioral Regression Testing Across NLP Classifier Versions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29557)
*Shazzad Hossain,Proma Chowdhury,Mridha Md. Nafis Fuad*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Alteron is a metamorphic testing tool that detects behavioral regressions across NLP model versions by comparing outputs on transformed inputs, identifying failures that aggregate benchmark metrics miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard benchmark metrics fail to capture how NLP model behavior changes across version updates. Existing evaluation approaches test models in isolation rather than comparing successive versions in CI/CD workflows, leaving behavioral regressions undetected.

Method: Alteron employs metamorphic testing: it constructs a test corpus from labeled source examples, applies metamorphic transformations (10 MRs), and compares successive model versions' outputs. A behavioral regression is flagged when model output changes contradict the expected metamorphic relation.

Result: Across 3 model-update transitions, 4 model versions, and 10 MRs, Alteron identified 16 behavioral regressions, 11 of which were release-blocking. Common model updates preserved aggregate task performance while introducing undesirable behavioral changes.

Conclusion: Behavioral regression testing via metamorphic relations is essential for NLP model CI/CD pipelines, as it reveals failures invisible to aggregate benchmark metrics. Alteron provides an open-source solution for this gap.

Abstract: Evaluating evolving Natural Language Processing (NLP) models is important for ensuring reliable behavior across updates, but standard benchmark metrics do not fully capture how model behavior changes across versions. Existing work has focused mainly on testing models in isolation rather than comparing successive versions in continuous integration workflows. We present Alteron, a tool for detecting behavioral regressions across NLP model versions with metamorphic testing. Alteron constructs a test corpus from labeled source examples and compares model versions on metamorphically transformed inputs. In an evaluation spanning 10 metamorphic relations (MRs), 4 model versions, and 3 model-update transitions, Alteron identified 16 behavioral regressions, 11 of which were release-blocking. The results show that common model updates can preserve overall task performance while still introducing undesirable behavior changes, and that behavioral checks across model versions can reveal failures that aggregate benchmark metrics alone do not capture. The tool is open-source and available at https://github.com/shazzad5709/alteron. A screencast demonstration is available at https://youtu.be/szwiWW5O4do.

</details>


### [221] [Educating the Agentic Engineer: Curricula, Collaboration, and Continuous Learning in the AI Era](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29610)
*Mamdouh Alenezi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper argues that generative and agentic AI are fundamentally shifting software engineering from human authorship to the direction and governance of autonomous systems, necessitating a new "agentic engineer" archetype. It proposes the ACCEL framework—spanning five competency pillars delivered through curricula, collaboration, and continuous learning—and calls for systemic educational transformation focused on judgment over artifact production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative and agentic AI are reconfiguring software and systems engineering, moving the field from human-centered artifact creation to overseeing autonomous systems. Existing educational paradigms are inadequate for this shift, creating an urgent need to define and cultivate a new professional archetype—the agentic engineer—whose core value lies in intent specification, multi-agent orchestration, critical evaluation, and ethical judgment.

Method: The authors conduct an integrative conceptual synthesis, drawing on engineering education, computing education, human–AI interaction, human factors, and the learning sciences. They leverage agency theory, trust-in-automation research, and empirical studies of AI-assisted programming to derive the ACCEL framework (Agentic Competencies through Curricula, Collaboration, and Enduring Learning). The framework organizes five competency pillars mapped to three delivery vectors: curricula, collaboration, and continuous learning. Specific pedagogical mechanisms proposed include a scaffolded curriculum, a delegation–verification loop for human–AI teaming, redesigned assessment, and governance-literate ethics integration, aligned with international AI competency frameworks.

Result: The synthesis yields the ACCEL framework, which identifies five core competency pillars for the agentic engineer and three delivery vectors. The paper surfaces key risks including automation bias, deskilling, superficial engagement, and diffuse accountability. It finds that AI benefits are unevenly realized and often misperceived, underscoring the need for structured pedagogical interventions. The framework argues that incremental curricular change is insufficient; systemic transformation is required, shifting instruction from producing artifacts to exercising judgment over autonomous socio-technical systems.

Conclusion: Educating the agentic engineer demands systemic rather than incremental curricular transformation. Instruction must pivot from artifact production to the exercise of judgment over increasingly autonomous socio-technical systems. The ACCEL framework provides an evidence-grounded educational architecture to guide this transition, integrating competency development with delivery mechanisms that emphasize collaboration and lifelong learning while mitigating risks such as automation bias, deskilling, and diffuse accountability.

Abstract: Generative and agentic artificial intelligence (AI) are reconfiguring software and systems engineering from a discipline centered on human authorship of artifacts to one focused on directing, verifying, and governing autonomous systems. This transition demands a new professional archetype, the \emph{agentic engineer}, whose enduring value lies in intent specification, orchestration of multi-agent workflows, critical evaluation of machine-generated outputs, and ethical judgment. This article presents an integrative conceptual synthesis across engineering education, computing education, human--AI interaction, human factors, and the learning sciences to derive an evidence-grounded educational architecture for this archetype. We introduce the ACCEL framework (Agentic Competencies through Curricula, Collaboration, and Enduring Learning), which organizes five competency pillars and maps them to three delivery vectors: curricula, collaboration, and continuous learning. Drawing on agency theory, trust-in-automation research, and empirical studies of AI-assisted programming, including evidence that AI benefits are unevenly realized and often misperceived, we propose a scaffolded curriculum, a delegation--verification pedagogical loop for human--AI teaming, redesigned assessment, governance-literate ethics integration, and alignment with current curricular guidelines and international AI competency frameworks. We identify key risks, including automation bias, deskilling, superficial engagement, and diffuse accountability, and conclude that educating the agentic engineer requires systemic transformation rather than incremental curricular change: instruction must shift from producing artifacts to exercising judgment over increasingly autonomous socio-technical systems.

</details>


### [222] [Reusing Past Repairs Through Hierarchical Trajectory Abstraction for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29658)
*Yisen Xu,Jiayuan Zhou,Ruiqi Pan,Tse-Hsun Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: STAIR converts historical repair trajectories into hierarchical, multi-level plans that can be reused to guide future LLM-driven program repairs, achieving 81.2% Pass@1 on SWE-bench Verified and demonstrating cross-agent generalizability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-driven repair agents treat each issue independently, discarding valuable procedural knowledge accumulated from previous repairs. The authors aim to capture and reuse this accumulated experience to improve future repair performance.

Method: STAIR transforms each past repair trajectory into a hierarchical, multi-level tree encoding experience at several granularities — from fine-grained diagnostic actions to high-level repair strategies. For a new issue, STAIR selects relevant plan nodes from multiple abstraction levels, tailors them into executable, issue-specific plans, and supplies these plans to the agent via its prompt.
