Result: STAIR integrated with Lingxi achieves 81.2% Pass@1 (MiniMax M2.5) and 79.2% Pass@1 (GPT-5) on SWE-bench Verified. The plans generalize across agents, lifting mini-SWE-agent v2 from 75.8% to 81.0% Pass@1 with no code changes. Ablations show that mixing multiple abstraction levels outperforms any single level, and raw, unabstracted trajectories transfer significantly worse.

Conclusion: Hierarchical, multi-granularity abstraction of historical repair trajectories provides an effective and generalizable mechanism for steering LLM-based repair agents, outperforming both single-level and raw-trajectory approaches.

Abstract: Although LLM-driven repair agents can tackle complex, repository-level issues, they treat every issue independently and discard the procedural knowledge accumulated from previous repairs. We introduce STAIR, a framework that converts historical repair trajectories into hierarchical, reusable plans that can be adapted to steer future repairs. Each past trajectory is transformed into a multi-level tree that ranges from fine-grained diagnostic actions to high-level repair strategies, encoding experience at several granularities. When a new issue arrives, STAIR selects relevant plan nodes from multiple abstraction levels, tailors them into executable, issue-specific plans, and supplies them to the agent through its prompt. On SWE-bench Verified, STAIR integrated with Lingxi reaches 81.2% Pass@1 using MiniMax M2.5 and 79.2% using GPT-5. The generated plans also generalize across agents: without any code change, they lift the Pass@1 of a structurally different agent, mini-SWE-agent v2, from 75.8% to 81.0%. Ablation experiments further show that mixing multiple abstraction levels surpasses any single level and that raw, unabstracted trajectories transfer substantially worse.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [223] [CyberNeuro: A Privacy-Preserving Agentic Workbench for Cohort-Scale Neuroimage and Clinical Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28841)
*Ran Ren,Junhong Tong,Yunxi Kong,Yiyao Chen,Yucheng Li,Kunhao Zhou,Shaoqi Wang,Yuxiang Tao,Shuheng Cao,Zhihao Fan,Marissa DiPiero,Tingting Dan,Guorong Wu*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: CyberNeuro is an agentic AI workbench featuring a local LLM ("WandaMind") with four specialized agents (Planner, Validator, Dispatcher, Reporter) that automates neuroimaging analysis via natural language commands. It improves held-out domain accuracy from 40% to 69% on NeuroBench and achieves substantial token efficiency (10.6% vs. 61.7% cloud usage) compared to Neuroclaw, all while maintaining clinical-grade data privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale, high-dimensional neuroimaging datasets remain operationally bottlenecked for AI/ML applications due to extensive manual effort in metadata curation, pipeline execution, quality control, and data management. This burden disproportionately excludes labs with limited manpower and infrastructure, creating an urgent need for scalable, cost-effective platforms that democratize advanced neuroimaging analytics.

Method: The authors introduce CyberNeuro, an agentic workbench built on multi-agent LLM architecture. It employs four dedicated agents—Planner (task decomposition), Validator (quality assurance), Dispatcher (execution orchestration), and Reporter (output synthesis)—communicating through a secure MCP (Multi-agent Communication Protocol) bridge with a pinned execution layer. The system uses a tailored local LLM called "WandaMind" to process natural language commands for complex neuroimaging workflows while preserving data privacy. A human-in-the-loop verification panel ensures biomedical quality control.

Result: On the public NeuroBench suite, CyberNeuro raised held-out domain accuracy from 40% (baseline) to 69%. In end-to-end 10-batch cohort workflows, the local WandaMind configuration achieved an estimated aggregate token count of ~10.6% compared to 61.7% for cloud providers relative to Neuroclaw, demonstrating significant token efficiency.

Conclusion: CyberNeuro demonstrates that a locally-deployed, multi-agent LLM system can effectively automate neuroimaging workflows with natural language interfaces, delivering both performance gains and cost efficiency while preserving clinical-grade data privacy. The platform is publicly available at wanda-cyberbench.com.

Abstract: Despite tremendous success in neuroimaging methodology, making large-scale, high-dimensional datasets ready for AI/ML applications remains a critical operational bottleneck. Conventional workflows require extensive manual effort across metadata curation, pipeline execution, post-processing quality control, and data management, a burden that disproportionately excludes laboratories with limited manpower and computational infrastructure. To address this real-world barrier, there is an urgent need for scalable, cost-effective computational platforms that democratize advanced neuroimaging analytics and accelerate discoveries in mental health and clinical translation. Capitalizing on multi-agent LLM breakthroughs, we introduce CyberNeuro, an agentic workbench with a tailored local LLM-model ('WandaMind') for automated neuroimaging and health-data analysis. Driven by four dedicated agents (Planner, Validator, Dispatcher, and Reporter) communicating via a secure MCP bridge and a pinned execution layer, CyberNeuro enables researchers to execute complex workflows using natural language while maintaining clinical-grade data privacy. On the public NeuroBench suite, CyberNeuro increases held-out domain accuracy from 40% to 69% over the baseline model. Beyond automated metrics, the platform integrates a human-in-the-loop verification panel to ensure rigorous biomedical quality control. Across the same end-to-end 10-batch cohort workflow suite, the local WandaMind configuration completed all tasks with an estimated aggregate token count of about 10.6% using WandaMind and 61.7% using cloud providers of token usage, compared to Neuroclaw, respectively. The platform and its production-ready modules are available at https://wanda-cyberbench.com.

</details>


### [224] [TransMem: Transforming Hidden States into Memory for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29032)
*Haodong Lei,Junming Liu,Yirong Chen,Pinlong Cai,Botian Shi,Ding Wang,Hongsong Wang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: TransMem is a lightweight inference-time memory module that transforms sparse historical hidden states from a frozen LLM into reusable memory representations via a gating network, trained with evidence-conditioned self-distillation. It yields significant gains on long-context agent benchmarks (LoCoMo, HotpotQA, MemoryAgentBench) across different model architectures and scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents operating over long interaction histories often fail to effectively reuse useful information encoded in previously computed hidden states during subsequent generation. Repeatedly re-encoding the full context is inefficient, yet discarding past representations wastes valuable task-relevant evidence.

Method: TransMem is a parametric memory module inserted at inference time into a frozen LLM backbone. It uses a lightweight gating network to dynamically transform sparse historical hidden states into memory representations that intervene on current hidden states, avoiding re-encoding. Training uses evidence-conditioned self-distillation: a memory-augmented student (full context) matches the predictive distribution of an evidence-only teacher (same frozen backbone), learning transferable memory utilization rather than task-specific knowledge.

Result: On LoCoMo, TransMem achieves 11.58–29.25 F1 gains; on HotpotQA, 10.20–13.03 F1 gains; on MemoryAgentBench, average accuracy improves from 29.54% to 40.00%. Improvements are consistent across different model architectures and scales.

Conclusion: Sparse historical hidden states serve as an effective and efficient memory substrate for long-context LLM agents. TransMem demonstrates that lightweight parametric memory with self-distillation training can substantially boost agent performance without modifying the backbone LLM.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate over long interaction histories, where effective reasoning requires identifying and exploiting task-relevant evidence distributed across past observations and actions. However, useful information encoded in previously computed representations is often underutilized during subsequent generation. We propose \textbf{TransMem}, a lightweight inference-time parametric memory module that transforms sparse historical hidden states from a frozen LLM backbone into reusable memory representations. TransMem uses a lightweight gating network to dynamically apply the latent intervention to the current hidden states, without repeatedly encoding the preceding context. To learn transferable memory utilization rather than task-specific knowledge, we introduce evidence-conditioned self-distillation. A memory-augmented student processes the full context and matches the predictive distribution of an evidence-only teacher that shares the same frozen backbone. Experiments on LoCoMo, HotpotQA, and MemoryAgentBench demonstrate consistent improvements across different model architectures and scales. TransMem yields gains of 11.58--29.25 $F_1$ on LoCoMo and 10.20--13.03 $F_1$ on HotpotQA, while improving the average MemoryAgentBench accuracy from 29.54\% to 40.00\%. These results establish sparse historical hidden states as an effective and efficient memory substrate for long-context LLM agents. Our code is available at https://github.com/Haodong-Lei-Ray/TransMem.

</details>


### [225] [SeekBrain: An Autonomous Multi-Agent System for Accelerating Neuroscience Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29347)
*Jiamin Wu,Peishan Xiang,Jingyang Chen,Yuqing Zhu,Yuxi Li,Ling Luo,Qihao Zheng,Jialiang Zu,Yongchao Wu,Mindong Liu,Haitao Wu,Chaofan Hu,Yijie Sun,Yuqi Hang,Yu Zhu,Shuo Li,Yue Fan,Shiyang Feng,Wanghan Xu,Tianlei Zhang,Jie Zhang,Wenlong Zhang,Bo Zhang,Kai Wang,Lei Bai,Mianxin Liu,Wanli Ouyang,Jiulin Du,Chunfeng Song*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: SeekBrain is an autonomous multi-agent framework that uses domain-grounded hierarchical planning and code-paper-derived analysis recipes to accelerate multimodal neuroscience data analysis. It outperforms baselines on BrainArena and revealed novel neural representations in zebrafish and mouse studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neuroscience increasingly depends on integrating multi-scale, multimodal datasets, but progress is hindered by highly heterogeneous data and fragmented analytical workflows. A scalable, automated solution is needed to unify and accelerate cross-modal data analysis.

Method: SeekBrain dynamically constructs a repertoire of analysis recipes extracted from code-paper pairs, combining this codified domain expertise with agentic hierarchical planning and execution engines. The multi-agent system scalably generates hypotheses and analytical pipelines on demand, evaluated on the expert-annotated BrainArena benchmark and validated in real-world deployments on larval zebrafish and mouse decision-making data.

Result: On BrainArena, SeekBrain substantially outperforms state-of-the-art agent baselines across diverse analysis tasks. In real-world applications, it successfully integrated behavioral, neural, and anatomical data to uncover structured distributed neural representations of larval zebrafish behavior and identified a shared axis of regional decoding strength across the brain in a mouse decision-making task.

Conclusion: SeekBrain establishes itself as a scalable, practical tool for accelerating data-driven neuroscience discoveries, demonstrating both benchmark superiority and real-world scientific impact through novel neurobiological findings.

Abstract: Modern neuroscience relies on integrating multi-scale, multimodal datasets to uncover the neural principles underlying intelligence. However, analytical challenges posed by highly heterogeneous data and fragmented workflows increasingly constrain discoveries. Here we introduce SeekBrain, an autonomous multi-agent framework designed to accelerate neuroscience discovery through domain-grounded hierarchical planning and cross-modal data analysis. SeekBrain dynamically constructs a repertoire of analysis recipes extracted from code-paper pairs. By coupling this codified expertise with agentic planning and execution engines, the framework scalably generates hypotheses and analytical pipelines on demand. Systematic evaluation on the expert-annotated BrainArena benchmark demonstrates that SeekBrain substantially outperforms state-of-the-art agent baselines across various analysis tasks. Crucially, when deployed in real-world research, SeekBrain integrated behavioral, neural, and anatomical data to reveal structured, distributed neural representations of larval zebrafish behavior and a shared axis of regional decoding strength across the brain in a mouse decision-making task. These results establish SeekBrain as a scalable and practical tool for accelerating data-driven discoveries in neuroscience.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [226] [RareSense: Rarity-Aware Similarity Search for Anomaly Retrieval in Transactional Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28879)
*Sidahmed Benabderrahmane,Talal Rahwan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: RareSense introduces a rarity-aware similarity framework that uses minimal rare itemsets and association rules instead of atomic feature overlap, outperforming classical measures like Jaccard and IDF-weighted Jaccard on sparse transactional anomaly data, particularly when anomalies share rare higher-order structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical similarity measures (Jaccard, cosine, Hamming) compare objects via atomic feature overlap, causing frequent background attributes to dominate. IDF weighting partially mitigates this but remains atom-wise and cannot capture informative higher-order co-occurrences. Anomaly detection in sparse set-valued data requires a measure sensitive to rare, structurally meaningful patterns.

Method: RareSense (1) mines minimal rare itemsets as intermediate structures, (2) derives reliable rare association rules, (3) maps objects into sparse rare-rule profiles, and (4) compares them using weighted Jaccard similarity. Rule weights combine inverse support, confidence, lift, structural complexity, and stability. The framework generalizes IDF-weighted Jaccard (a restricted singleton case) and induces a pseudometric on objects (metric over equivalence classes).

Result: Across four benchmark families (cybersecurity and categorical domains), RareSense achieved the highest macro-average query-conditioned retrieval performance among evaluated measures. Statistical analysis confirmed significant overall differences with corrected paired comparisons favoring RareSense over atomic baselines. Gains are workload-dependent, strongest when anomalies share repeatable rare higher-order structure. For global anomaly ranking, RareSense attained highest macro-average while remaining statistically comparable to strong dedicated detectors.

Conclusion: RareSense demonstrates that shifting from atomic overlap to rare higher-order evidence yields superior similarity search for sparse transactional anomaly data, though gains are workload-dependent. The framework provides a principled generalization of IDF-weighted Jaccard with desirable metric properties.

Abstract: Similarity search over sparse set-valued data is often dominated by frequent background attributes because classical measures such as Jaccard, cosine, and Hamming compare objects through atomic overlap. IDF (Inverse document frequency) weighting partially reduces this effect but remains atom-wise and cannot explicitly represent informative higher-order co-occurrences. We introduce RareSense, a rarity-aware similarity framework for sparse transactional anomaly data. RareSense mines minimal rare itemsets as intermediate structures, derives reliable rare association rules, maps objects into sparse rare-rule profiles, and compares them using weighted Jaccard similarity. Rule weights combine inverse support, confidence, lift, structural complexity, and stability, so that neighborhoods are determined by shared rare evidence rather than uniform feature overlap. We show that IDF-weighted Jaccard is a restricted singleton case of RareSense, and that the induced distance is a pseudometric on the original objects and a metric over equivalence classes defined by identical rule profiles. Experiments across four benchmark families spanning cybersecurity and general categorical domains show that RareSense attains the highest observed macro-average query-conditioned retrieval performance among the evaluated similarity measures. The statistical analysis indicates significant overall differences, with corrected paired comparisons favoring RareSense over the atomic baselines. The gains remain workload-dependent and are strongest when anomalies share repeatable rare higher-order structure. For global anomaly ranking, RareSense achieves the highest observed macro-average performance while remaining statistically comparable to several strong dedicated detectors.

</details>


### [227] [LLM-Based Generative Retrieval for Snapchat Content Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28895)
*Liam Collins,Jiwen Ren,Donald Loveland,Bhuvesh Kumar,Clark Mingxuan Ju,Xuan Guo,Mo Li,Alvin Hou,Yi Cui,Peng Yang,Jian Wang,Saud Afzal Shafi,Nga Than,Ruiming Lu,Wenfeng Zhuo,Dongheng Li,Lili Zhang,Mingtao Zhang,Jinchao Ye,Vincent Xue,Chunhui Zhu,Neil Shah*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: SnapLGR is a production generative retrieval system at Snapchat that uses LLMs for short-video recommendation. It introduces semantic identifiers built from multimodal embeddings with PPR-based contrastive learning, continued pretraining to ground new tokens, and efficient CUDA/TensorRT-LLM serving. Live A/B tests show significant gains in View Time, Time Spent, and Deep Sessions over a TIGER-style baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained LLMs are promising as retrieval engines due to their semantic priors and sequence modeling, but deploying them as generative retrievers in production is challenging: they lack an internal item vocabulary (items were unseen during pretraining) and must generate valid item identifiers under strict latency and cost constraints. The authors aim to bridge this gap for large-scale short-video recommendation.

Method: SnapLGR has three main components: (1) Semantic Identifiers (SIDs) constructed from multimodal item embeddings, enhanced with Personalized PageRank (PPR)-based co-engagement contrastive learning to improve codebook utilization, reduce collisions, and inject collaborative signals. (2) Continued pretraining (CPT) to ground the new SID tokens in the LLM, followed by supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on user interaction sequences. (3) Production serving via TensorRT-LLM CUDA-backed beam search and a decentralized worker-loop architecture for low-latency inference.

Result: In a live A/B test, SnapLGR improved View Time by 0.37%, Time Spent by 0.09%, Deep Sessions by 0.18%, and Deep Sessions Unique User by 0.11% relative to a TIGER-style generative retrieval baseline. Offline analysis further decomposes gains attributable to model architecture, scaling, and pretraining under a fixed tokenizer.

Conclusion: Successful deployment of LLM-based generative retrieval in production requires joint design across representation learning, vocabulary grounding, and efficient training/serving infrastructure. SnapLGR demonstrates this holistic approach delivers measurable business metric improvements at Snapchat scale.

Abstract: Pretrained large language models (LLMs) are promising retrieval engines because they combine rich semantic priors, strong sequence modeling capabilities, and favorable scaling behavior. However, turning a pretrained LLM into a generative retriever in production deployment raises several challenges: the model must learn an internal item vocabulary that was absent from pretraining, and generate valid item identifiers under strict latency and cost constraints. We address these challenges through the design and launch of SnapLGR, an LLM-based generative retrieval system for short-video recommendation at Snapchat. The system is built around three main designs. First, we construct semantic identifiers (SIDs) from multimodal item embeddings and enhance them with Personalized PageRank (PPR)-based co-engagement contrastive learning, resulting in improved codebook utilization, reduced collisions, and infused collaborative signal. Second, we use continued pretraining (CPT) to ground the introduced SID tokens before supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on user interaction sequences. Third, we make SnapLGR serving practical through TensorRT-LLM CUDA-backed beam search and a decentralized worker-loop architecture. In a live A/B test, the launched system increased View Time by 0.37%, Time Spent by 0.09%, Deep Sessions by 0.18%, and Deep Sessions Unique User by 0.11% relative to the existing TIGER-style generative retrieval baseline. We then decompose this offline gap under a fixed tokenizer and quantify the gains due to model architecture, scaling, and pretraining. Overall, our deployment shows that successful production SnapLGR requires joint design across representation learning, vocabulary grounding, and efficient training and serving.

</details>


### [228] [TransX: Scaling Transformer-based Recommendation via Behavioral and Serving Stream Crossings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28940)
*Da Xu,Liyan Fang,Divya Venugopalan,Sunny Hsu,Xukai Wang,Rishav Roy Chowdhury,Cindy Liang,Nishant Satya Lakshmikanth*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: TransX proposes an encoder-decoder architecture that decouples long-term behavior modeling from real-time serving events, treating recommendation as sequence-to-sequence action transduction. Co-designed with an amortized serving strategy (incremental encoding + KV caching), it achieves +6.0% CTR and +4.4% conversion gains on LinkedIn while reducing online computation by ~80%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Transformer-based recommenders collapse heterogeneous data sources (long-term user behaviors and real-time serving events) into a single monolithic token stream, obscuring their distinct causal roles and temporal characteristics. This leads to inefficient modeling and inflated training/serving costs.

Method: TransX employs a production-oriented encoder-decoder architecture: (1) an encoder processes long-term behavior sequences offline/nearline; (2) a decoder conditions next-action prediction on cross-attention between pre-computed behavior encodings and real-time serving representations; (3) an amortized serving strategy combines incremental behavior encoding with per-request key-value caching to decouple serving latency from behavior sequence length.

Result: Extensive offline experiments and large-scale online A/B tests on LinkedIn's recommender systems demonstrate consistent outperformance over state-of-the-art DLRMs and sequential baselines. TransX delivers +6.0% CTR lift and +4.4% conversion gain while maintaining comparable serving costs. The co-designed serving strategy reduces online computation by approximately 80%.

Conclusion: Explicitly decoupling heterogeneous data sources via an encoder-decoder transduction framework, combined with a latency-insensitive amortized serving strategy, enables substantial accuracy gains in industrial-scale recommendation without sacrificing serving efficiency.

Abstract: Modern industrial recommender systems (RecSys) increasingly adopt Transformer-based sequence models, with an emerging paradigm that frames recommendation as next-token prediction over a unified monolithic user sequence. However, collapsing heterogeneous data sources -- such as long-term user behaviors and real-time serving events -- into a single monolithic token stream that obscures their distinct causal roles and temporal characteristics, leading to inefficient modeling and elevated training and serving costs. We propose TransX, a production-oriented encoder-decoder architecture that reformulates recommendation as a sequence-to-sequence action transduction problem. TransX explicitly decouples behavior-stream modeling from serving-event modeling and conditions next-action decoding on scalable cross-attention between nearline behavior encodings and real-time serving representations. To enable low-latency, high-QPS deployment, TransX is co-designed with an amortized serving strategy that combines incremental behavior encoding with per-request key-value caching, rendering serving latency insensitive to behavior sequence length. Extensive offline experiments and large-scale online A/B tests on LinkedIn's recommender systems show that TransX consistently outperforms state-of-the-art DLRMs and sequential baselines, and delivers substantial CTR lift (+6.0%) and conversion gain (+4.4%) while maintaining serving costs comparable to existing production models where our co-designed serving strategy reduces online computation by approximately 80%.

</details>


### [229] [Don't Contrast the Impossible: Region-Constrained Batching for Contrastive User Modeling on a Local Community Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28971)
*Seungho Han,Byeongchang Kim,Jin Yu*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Contrastive learning for recommender systems suffers from "impossible negatives"—user-item pairs that can never co-occur due to geographic constraints. The authors propose Region-Constrained Batch Sampling (RCBS), which constructs geographically homogeneous mini-batches so negatives reflect realistic exposure, improving user embeddings and downstream recommendation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard contrastive learning in recommenders treats all in-batch items as negatives under a universal exposure assumption. On local community platforms (e.g., Karrot), geographic constraints make many user-item pairs impossible by design, yet they are still used as negatives, diluting the training signal. Harder, more informative negatives are needed that respect real exposure feasibility.

Method: Region-Constrained Batch Sampling (RCBS): a batching method that constructs mini-batches where users and items come from the same geographic region, ensuring that negatives are primarily drawn from items users could feasibly encounter. This replaces impossible negatives with feasible, harder negatives under realistic exposure constraints, improving contrastive signal quality.

Result: Offline evaluations and online A/B tests show RCBS consistently improves user representation quality. Downstream improvements are observed in home feed ranking, retrieval, and display ads ranking. The resulting user embeddings have been deployed to production across multiple applications.

Conclusion: By aligning contrastive negative sampling with real-world exposure constraints via region-homogeneous batching, RCBS provides a simple, effective solution to the impossible negatives problem, yielding better user representations and measurable gains across multiple recommendation tasks in a production setting.

Abstract: Contrastive learning is widely used for user modeling in large-scale recommender systems, where standard in-batch negatives implicitly assume universal exposure that any user can be shown any item. On local community platforms such as Karrot, however, exposure is geographically constrained; many user-item pairs are impossible by design yet still treated as negatives during training, diluting the contrastive learning signal. We address this impossible negatives problem and propose Region-Constrained Batch Sampling (RCBS), a simple yet effective batching method that constructs region-homogeneous mini-batches so that users are contrasted primarily against items they could feasibly see. By replacing impossible negatives with feasible ones, RCBS naturally introduces harder and more informative negatives under realistic exposure constraints. With offline evaluations and online A/B tests, we show that RCBS consistently improves user representation quality and consequently enhances home feed ranking, retrieval, and display ads ranking. The resulting user embeddings have been deployed in production across various applications.

</details>


### [230] [Think2Go: Generative Next POI Recommendation with LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28997)
*Zhuang Zhuang,Shanshan Feng,Hangwei Qian,Mingqi Yang,Heng Qi,Yanming Shen,Baocai Yin*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Think2Go is a generative next POI recommendation framework that unifies supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with two novel advantage weighting mechanisms—prompt epistemic uncertainty and reward-informed advantage scaling—to improve SID comprehension and spatial-temporal reasoning through test-time scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing non-reasoning models for next POI recommendation are limited by sparsity and complexity of user mobility patterns, while LLMs fail because they lack deep understanding of semantic IDs (SIDs) when trained separately. A unified framework is needed that jointly optimizes memorization and adaptive reasoning.

Method: Think2Go integrates SFT and RL-based reasoning in a single architecture. It introduces two advantage weighting mechanisms: (1) prompt epistemic uncertainty estimated via kernel density methods to assess spatial-temporal alignment and encourage exploration under high uncertainty; (2) reward-informed advantage scaling that normalizes rewards against maxima to stabilize training and reduce overfitting. These form an implicit curriculum learning strategy for instance-aware policy updates.

Result: The proposed calibration mechanisms prevent entropy collapse and support robust exploration, enabling the model to better retain user behavior patterns while exploring diverse preferences. (Specific quantitative results are not provided in this abstract.)

Conclusion: Joint calibration of SFT and RL reasoning with uncertainty-aware and reward-informed advantage weighting yields a robust next POI recommendation framework that effectively balances memorization and exploration, overcoming limitations of both non-reasoning models and standard LLM approaches.

Abstract: Next Point-of-Interest (POI) recommendation task focuses on mining user behavioral preference patterns from historical check-ins to provide personalized suggestions for the next destination. Existing methods primarily rely on shallow contextual information and handcrafted feature interactions to predict the next POI. However, the inherent sparsity and complexity of user mobility patterns limit the computational capacity of non-reasoning models to capture deep intent, while large language models (LLMs) perform suboptimally because they lack a deep understanding of semantic IDs (SIDs) when SIDs are trained separately. To address these limitations, we propose Think2Go, a novel generative next POI recommendation framework, which enhances the model's comprehension of SID representations and explores diverse spatial-temporal patterns via test-time computational scaling. We unify supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL)-based reasoning within a single architecture, enabling joint optimization of memorization and adaptive reasoning to better retain user behavior patterns while exploring diverse user preferences. To further calibrate policy optimization in adaptive reasoning, we propose two advantage weighting mechanisms that integrate (1) prompt epistemic uncertainty, estimated via kernel density methods to assess the spatial-temporal periodic pattern alignment between queries and user history, promoting increased exploration under high epistemic uncertainty; and (2) reward-informed advantage scaling, captured by normalizing rewards against their maxima to adapt update magnitudes, thereby improving training stability and mitigating overfitting to noisy signals. This joint calibration forms an implicit curriculum learning strategy, delivering fine-grained, instance-aware policy updates that prevent entropy collapse and support robust exploration.

</details>


### [231] [PaletteID: Prototype-Composed Semantic Identifiers for Multimodal CTR Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29000)
*Huanyu Liu,Baining Chen,Hui Liu,Zengyang Li,Ziyi Huang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: PaletteID (PID) is a prototype-based semantic identifier for CTR prediction that uses representative prototype items as semantic anchors, constructed via Semantic Quality-Aware Determinantal Point Process (SQ-DPP), to bridge multimodal content and recommendation models — outperforming existing residual SID methods, especially on long-tail items.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semantic identifier (SID) methods for multimodal CTR prediction suffer from two key limitations: (1) codebook assignment destroys semantic relevance and discards fine-grained continuous signals from pretrained embeddings, and (2) residual code paths overly depend on prefix codes, limiting hierarchical representational scalability. There is a need for a method that preserves semantic richness while enabling effective identifier-based recommendation.

Method: PaletteID (PID) draws inspiration from palette-based color composition. It first constructs a prototype palette using Semantic Quality-Aware Determinantal Point Process (SQ-DPP), which jointly optimizes for local content density and global semantic diversity. For each target item, PID retrieves a sequence of semantically related prototypes and aggregates them into a PID representation, yielding rich complementary semantic modeling without discrete codebook assignment.

Result: Extensive experiments on two public datasets show that PID consistently improves CTR prediction performance and yields larger gains for long-tail items. PID also produces more robust identifier assignments and more interpretable token semantics compared to existing residual SID methods.

Conclusion: PID effectively addresses the limitations of discretization-based SID methods by using prototype-based semantic anchors, preserving continuous semantic signals while enabling scalable and interpretable multimodal recommendation. The approach is particularly beneficial for cold-start and long-tail items.

Abstract: Multimodal information can improve the accuracy of click-through rate (CTR) prediction and effectively alleviate item cold-start and long-tail problems. Recent studies commonly discretize pretrained multimodal embeddings into semantic identifiers (SIDs), allowing the model to learn task-specific semantic representations for recommendation. However, existing methods still provide limited gains due to two major limitations. First, codebook assignment fails to preserve semantic relevance and discards fine-grained continuous signals in the original embedding space. Second, the residual code paths are highly dependent on prefix codes, which limits the effective representational scalability of hierarchical identifiers. To address these issues, we propose PaletteID (PID), a prototype-based semantic identifier. Inspired by palette-based color composition, PID uses a compact set of representative prototype items as semantic anchors to bridge pretrained multimodal content space and recommendation models. Specifically, we first construct a prototype palette with Semantic Quality-Aware Determinantal Point Process (SQ-DPP), which jointly considers local content density and global semantic diversity. Then, for each target item, PID retrieves a sequence of semantically related prototypes and aggregates them into an informative PID representation, enabling rich and complementary semantic modeling. Extensive experiments on two public datasets demonstrate that PID consistently improves CTR prediction and yields larger gains for long-tail items. PID also produces more robust identifier assignments and provides more interpretable token semantics than existing residual SID methods.

</details>


### [232] [EvoReason: Self-Evolving Reasoning Primitive-Guided On-Policy Distillation for Latent Reasoning in Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29010)
*Zhuang Zhuang,Zhipeng Wei,Rongfeng Guo,Shijie Li,Peng Zhao,Jie Chen,Fei Pan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: EvoReason proposes a self-evolving latent reasoning framework for generative recommendation that uses extracted reasoning primitives to guide structured chain-of-thought distillation, then employs an on-policy co-evolution mechanism to progressively align teacher supervision with the student's latent reasoning space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing latent reasoning methods directly distill raw chain-of-thought (CoT) trajectories into latent representations, but recommendation reasoning trajectories contain redundant expressions and unstable reasoning paths, making raw CoT supervision suboptimal for learning transferable latent reasoning representations.

Method: EvoReason operates in three stages: (1) extracting reusable reasoning primitives from high-quality agentic recommendation trajectories to serve as pseudo-tools for structured reasoning; (2) equipping the teacher model with primitive-aware reasoning capabilities to generate structured CoT supervision with reduced redundancy and improved consistency; (3) introducing a self-evolving on-policy distillation mechanism where the primitive-guided reasoning process evolves according to the student's latent reasoning outcomes, creating a closed-loop co-evolution between policy updates and latent reasoning behaviors.

Result: The framework enables progressively better-aligned CoT supervision and more effective reasoning transfer compared to raw CoT distillation baselines.

Conclusion: EvoReason demonstrates that adaptively aligning explicit reasoning supervision with the student's latent space through primitive-guided on-policy distillation yields superior latent reasoning for generative recommendation, highlighting the importance of structured, evolving supervision over static raw CoT distillation.

Abstract: Generative recommendation benefits from reasoning-enhanced inference, and latent reasoning offers an efficient paradigm by encoding intermediate reasoning processes into compact continuous representations for latency-sensitive deployment. Despite its efficiency, existing latent reasoning approaches typically rely on directly distilling raw chain-of-thought (CoT) trajectories into latent representations, assuming that textual reasoning traces provide sufficient supervision. However, recommendation reasoning trajectories contain diverse reasoning processes with redundant expressions and unstable reasoning paths, making raw CoT supervision suboptimal for learning transferable latent reasoning representations. To address this challenge, we propose EvoReason, a self-evolving latent reasoning framework that adaptively aligns explicit reasoning supervision with the student's latent reasoning space through primitive-guided on-policy distillation. First, EvoReason extracts reusable reasoning primitives from high-quality agentic recommendation trajectories, where each primitive captures an essential reasoning behavior and serves as a pseudo-tool for structured teacher reasoning. Then, based on these primitives, we equip the teacher with primitive-aware reasoning capabilities, enabling it to generate structured CoT supervision with reduced redundancy and improved consistency. Finally, during latent reasoning optimization, EvoReason introduces a self-evolving on-policy distillation mechanism, where the primitive-guided reasoning process evolves according to the student's latent reasoning outcomes. Through this closed-loop co-evolution, policy updates continuously improve latent reasoning behaviors is refined according to the resulting latent reasoning outcomes, enabling progressively better-aligned CoT supervision and more effective reasoning transfer.

</details>


### [233] [Reproducing LightMem: Naive RAG Is Just as Good for Memory Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29104)
*Yongjie Zhou,Shuai Wang,Bevan Koopman,Guido Zuccon*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper reproduces LightMem and compares it against Naive RAG. It finds that retriever choice dramatically affects performance (58.1%→75.5%), that constructed memories do not consistently beat raw-turn retrieval, and that LightMem offers a context-efficiency trade-off rather than a general advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term conversational agents need memory, but existing approaches like LightMem construct compact memory representations whose sensitivity to retriever choice and information loss relative to raw-turn retrieval is poorly understood.

Method: The authors reproduce LightMem and benchmark it against Naive RAG (retrieving directly from raw user turns). They systematically vary retriever choice, retrieval depth, and answering-token budgets, and conduct oracle evaluations to measure information loss from memory construction.

Result: Retriever choice alone shifts accuracy from 58.1% to 75.5% on a fixed LightMem store. Naive RAG generally outperforms at matched retrieval depths; LightMem only excels under tight answering-token budgets. Oracle evaluation confirms memory construction discards answer-relevant information.

Conclusion: LightMem provides a context-efficiency trade-off, not a universal improvement over Naive RAG. Its utility depends on the retriever and token budget, motivating future work on retrieval, reranking, query formulation, and their interplay with raw versus constructed memory representations.

Abstract: Long-term conversational agents require access to information from earlier interactions, such as a user's preferences, past requests, or previously mentioned facts. Repeatedly providing the full dialogue history can be expensive as conversations grow, so many memory approaches instead transform past interactions into compact entries that can be retrieved when needed. LightMem is a recent lightweight memory-management approach that reports strong effectiveness while maintaining relatively low construction cost. However, it still relies on a separate constructed memory representation and is evaluated with only one retriever, leaving unclear how sensitive its results are to retriever choice and whether memory construction discards answer-relevant information.
  In this study, we reproduce LightMem and compare it with Naive RAG, which retrieves directly from raw user turns. We recover LightMem's main configuration trend, but find that retriever choice is a major source of performance variation: changing only the retriever over a fixed LightMem store shifts answer accuracy from 58.1% to 75.5%. Constructed memories also do not consistently outperform raw-turn retrieval. Naive RAG generally performs better at matched retrieval depths, whereas LightMem performs better mainly under tight answering-token budgets. Oracle evaluation further shows that memory construction removes some answer-relevant information.
  Overall, LightMem offers a context-efficiency trade-off rather than a general advantage over Naive RAG. Its value depends on the retriever and available token budget, motivating future work on retrieval, reranking, query formulation, and their interaction with raw and constructed memory representations.

</details>


### [234] [GALA: Generative Aligned Learning for Adaptive Multimodal Representation in the Taobao Shangou Recommender System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29213)
*Jiping Liu,Zhongmin Zhang,Zisen Sang,Zhijia Fang,Tao Ouyang,Ma Jiang,Shaopeng Liang,Zeyang Hou,Guodong Cao,Jia Jia*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: GALA introduces a three-stage multimodal alignment pipeline for food delivery recommendation, featuring a novel intermediate "generative RL alignment" stage that bridges the gap between content-semantic pretraining and behavior-driven ranking. Deployed at Taobao Shangou (200M+ DAU), it achieves +0.12/+0.20 AUC offline gains and a 0.55% order volume increase in online A/B tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-stage approaches in food delivery recommendation suffer from a misalignment between image-text pretraining (focused on content semantics) and downstream behavior-driven ranking (focused on user interactions). This separation hinders joint multimodal modeling and adaptation to evolving user intent, creating a pretraining-fine-tuning gap that limits recommendation performance.

Method: GALA is a three-stage pipeline: (1) Behavior-aware triplet pretraining on query-image-text pairs from search logs to capture early user intent; (2) A novel intermediate generative RL alignment stage using GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) that constructs multimodal pretraining data from user behavior and refines embeddings via conversion-based rewards, dynamically aligning them with downstream objectives; (3) Final integration of multimodal and ID embeddings via adaptive gating with a hybrid loss to preserve multimodal contributions during long-term ID-dominant training.

Result: GALA achieves consistent offline improvements of +0.12/+0.20 AUC over SOTA methods with better PCOC metrics. Large-scale online A/B tests at Taobao Shangou report a 0.55% increase in order volume, confirming effectiveness at industrial scale across diverse demand patterns.

Conclusion: The intermediate generative RL alignment stage is the key innovation that effectively bridges the pretraining-fine-tuning gap in multimodal recommendation. GALA demonstrates that reward-driven refinement of multimodal embeddings can align content understanding with user behavior patterns, delivering measurable business impact at industrial scale.

Abstract: Modern recommender systems in food delivery increasingly leverage multimodal signals, including images, text, and user interaction histories, to enhance user experience, yet effective fusion of these heterogeneous modalities remains challenging, hindering both the joint modeling of multimodal signals and adaptation to evolving user intent. In mainstream two-stage approaches, the separation between content-semantic pretraining of image-text encoders and behavior-driven ranking models limits alignment between semantic understanding and user behavior patterns. To address these issues, we present GALA, a three-stage pipeline whose core innovation lies in an intermediate "generative RL alignment" stage that constructs multimodal pretraining data from user behavior and refines it via conversion-based rewards, effectively bridging the pretraining-fine-tuning gap to align with downstream objectives. GALA comprises three stages: first, behavior-aware triplet pretraining on query-image-text pairs from search logs to early capture user intent and content preferences; second, a novel intermediate stage that refines multimodal embeddings through reward-driven optimization (GRPO) to dynamically align them with user behavior and bridge the pretraining-fine-tuning gap; and finally, integration of multimodal and ID embeddings via adaptive gating with a hybrid loss, preserving multimodal contributions under long-term ID-dominant training. GALA has been deployed in the production environment at Taobao Shangou, serving over 200 million daily active users. Compared with state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods, it delivers consistent offline gains of +0.12/+0.20 AUC along with better PCOC metrics. Large-scale online A/B tests further report a 0.55 percent increase in order volume, confirming GALA's effectiveness at industrial scale and its robustness across diverse demand patterns.

</details>


### [235] [RecHarness: A Bandit-Routed Agentic Harness for Self-Evolving Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29241)
*Haoran Ling,Yuecheng Li,Zeyu Song,Jing Yao,Shuwen Kang,Chi Lu,Wenjin Wu,Peng Jiang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: RecHarness introduces a two-step agentic framework for automated recommender model optimization, using a bandit router to select modification directions and an LLM to generate code edits, with a jump-basin mechanism to escape local optima. It outperforms pure LLM-reasoning search and demonstrates significant online gains in a short-video advertising A/B test.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current recommender model optimization relies on labor-intensive manual iteration. While LLM-based agents can automate this, allowing LLMs to both choose directions and generate hypotheses leads to unstable search with limited experiment budgets. A more structured decomposition of the optimization process is needed.

Method: RecHarness splits optimization into two stages: (1) a bandit router selects the next modification direction (e.g., architecture, objective, training strategy) based on historical validation feedback, and (2) an LLM generates a concrete hypothesis and executable code edit within that chosen direction. A jump-basin mechanism introduces a "structural-jump arm" that triggers when local edits stagnate, enabling long-horizon exploration and escape from local optima.

Result: RecHarness achieves more stable performance improvements and better budget efficiency than LLM-reasoning search across multiple recommendation tasks, datasets, and model backbones. In a 7-day online A/B test on a large-scale short-video advertising platform, it improves ADVV by 2.084%, Revenue by 0.534%, and Exposure by 0.559%.

Conclusion: Decoupling direction selection (via bandit routing) from hypothesis generation (via LLM) leads to more stable and efficient automated recommender optimization. The jump-basin mechanism further enables sustained exploration, making RecHarness a practical solution for real-world industrial-scale recommendation systems.

Abstract: Optimizing modern recommender models still depends heavily on engineers manually iterating over architectural, objective, and training-strategy changes. While LLM-based agents can automate this trial-and-error process, allowing the LLM to both select modification directions and generate concrete hypotheses often leads to unstable search under limited experiment budgets. Inspired by the above challenge, we propose RecHarness, a Bandit-Routed Agentic Harness for automated recommender model optimization. RecHarness separates the optimization process into two steps: a bandit router selects the next modification direction according to historical validation feedback, while the LLM generates a concrete optimization hypothesis and executable code edit within the selected direction. To sustain long-horizon exploration, RecHarness uses a jump-basin mechanism to activate a structural-jump arm when local edits stagnate. Across multiple recommendation tasks, datasets, and model backbones, RecHarness achieves more stable performance improvements and uses limited trial budgets more effectively than LLM-reasoning search. During a 7-day online A/B test on a large-scale short-video advertising platform, the selected candidate improves ADVV by 2.084%, Revenue by 0.534%, and Exposure by 0.559%. Code is available at https://github.com/6lyc/RecHarness.

</details>


### [236] [Language Models Agree With Each Other, Not With Readers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29274)
*Kazuki Nakayashiki,Keisuke Watanabe*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper measures whether language models homogenise relative to a naturalistic human baseline — 2,523 reader highlight sets from web documents made for readers' own purposes, not for a study. It finds that model-model sentence selection agreement substantially exceeds human-human agreement, with the effect graded across model sizes and not attributable to determinism, prompting, or vendor artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work measuring model homogenisation uses human judgments collected under the model's own instructions, making the human baseline an artifact of the experimental design. The authors seek a genuine, unprimed human reference to test whether language models truly converge in their outputs beyond what humans naturally do.

Method: The authors use 2,523 reader mark sets from 120 web documents on a platform where highlighting overlays are off by default. Agreement is defined as overlap between two size-matched sentence sets minus expected overlap when each is resampled within depth-and-length bands — a calibrated null demonstrated to land within 0.006 of zero for random baselines. They compare 18 model arms (11 vendors, 3 countries, both open and closed weight regimes) against human reader agreement, with out-of-sample testing on four post-analysis model releases against pre-fixed predictions.

Result: Median model pair agreement is +0.093 vs. the human yardstick of +0.040; 99 of 153 model pairs sit entirely above the human interval. Two frontier models reach +0.203, double GPT-4o's self-agreement. The smallest models agree at human level. No model agrees with readers more than a reader does. Blunting model sets by matching human selection sharpness halves but does not close the gap. Out-of-sample testing confirms no new model clears the human interval.

Conclusion: Language models exhibit systematic, graded convergence in sentence selection that exceeds natural human agreement. This homogenisation is robust across vendors and procedures, cannot be explained by surface features, and a population simulated from several models does not replicate the diversity of several human populations.

Abstract: Claims that language models homogenise are usually measured against human judgements collected for the study, which makes the human side an artifact of the design: a crowdworker given the model's instruction is running the model's prompt. We measure convergence against a human reference nobody built for the purpose -- 2,523 reader mark sets across 120 web documents, produced by people highlighting for their own reasons on a platform where the overlay of others' marks is off by default.
  Agreement is the overlap between two size-matched sentence sets minus the overlap expected when each is resampled within its own depth-and-length bands. The null's calibration is demonstrated, not asserted: every pair involving a random baseline lands within 0.006 of zero. On the median document each party names 14 sentences of 70; two readers share 4.1 and two models 8.7.
  Across 18 model arms spanning 11 vendors, 3 countries and both weight regimes, the median of 153 model pairs is +0.093 against a human yardstick of +0.040, and 99 sit entirely above the human interval. Two frontier models from rival labs reach +0.203, twice what GPT-4o agrees with itself on a second call. The effect is not determinism, prompt wording, procedure, vendor or routing, and it is graded: the smallest models agree at the human level. No model agrees with readers detectably more than a reader does, and at equal depth and length no surface feature separates their choices.
  The multiples are procedure-dependent and the ordering is not: models are cut to their sharpest set while a reader's is a random draw from what they marked, and blunting the models alike halves the gap without closing it. Tested out of sample on four models released after this analysis, against predictions fixed beforehand, none clears the human interval. A population simulated from several models is not several populations.

</details>


### [237] [Bridging the Question-Answer Gap in Retrieval-Augmented Generation: Hypothetical Prompt Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29402)
*Domen Vake,Jernej Vičič,Aleksandar Tošić*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: HyPE shifts hypothetical content generation from query time to indexing time by precomputing multiple hypothetical prompts per data chunk, transforming retrieval into question-question matching. This eliminates runtime overhead while improving retrieval precision by up to 42pp and claim recall by up to 45pp.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems suffer from a style gap between user queries and document text. Existing solutions like HyDE generate synthetic answers at query time to bridge this gap, but introduce significant computational latency. The authors seek to eliminate this runtime overhead while preserving or improving retrieval alignment.

Method: HyPE precomputes multiple hypothetical prompts for each document chunk during indexing. Rather than embedding the chunk directly, the chunk is embedded in place of (or conditioned on) these hypothetical prompts, converting the retrieval task into question-question matching. At query time, no synthetic generation is required — the query is directly matched against the precomputed prompt-conditioned embeddings.

Result: On six common datasets, HyPE improves retrieval context precision by up to 42 percentage points and claim recall by up to 45 percentage points over standard baselines. The method remains compatible with re-ranking, multi-vector retrieval, query decomposition, and other RAG enhancements.

Conclusion: Shifting hypothetical content generation from query time to indexing time is both effective and efficient, offering substantial retrieval gains without introducing latency, and integrates seamlessly with existing RAG pipeline components.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems synergize retrieval mechanisms with generative language models to enhance the accuracy and relevance of responses. However, bridging the style gap between user queries and relevant information in document text remains a persistent challenge in retrieval-augmented systems, often addressed by runtime solutions (e.g., Hypothetical Document Embeddings (HyDE)) that attempt to improve alignment but introduce extra computational overhead at query time. To address these challenges, we propose Hypothetical Prompt Embeddings (HyPE), a framework that shifts the generation of hypothetical content from query time to the indexing phase. By precomputing multiple hypothetical prompts for each data chunk and embedding the chunk in place of the prompt, HyPE transforms retrieval into a question-question matching task, bypassing the need for runtime synthetic answer generation. This approach does not introduce latency but also strengthens the alignment between queries and relevant context. Our experimental results on six common datasets show that HyPE can improve retrieval context precision by up to 42 percentage points and claim recall by up to 45 percentage points, compared to standard approaches, while remaining compatible with re-ranking, multi-vector retrieval, query decomposition, and other RAG advancements

</details>


### [238] [QASP: Query-Adaptive Robust Vector Search Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29606)
*Hakan Ferhatosmanoglu,Kushal Kumar,Tal Wagner,Andy Warfield*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: QASP is a query-adaptive vector search framework that predicts each query's full recall progression curve via a single regression model, enabling dynamic search policies for any recall target. It achieves 99% recall with 80% less data access, generalizes without retraining, and provides theoretical guarantees on sample complexity, loss bounds, and exponential savings in intrinsic dimensionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed search parameters in vector search cause high per-query performance variance, and average-recall evaluation obscures these disparities. Iterative approaches that predict recall targets one at a time are computationally wasteful. The authors aim to predict the entire recall curve per query in one shot, enabling robust, efficient, and generalizable query-adaptive policies.

Method: QASP uses a single upfront supervised regression to predict normalized recall values across the full progression curve using scale-invariant features and pre-search inference. From this predicted curve, a search policy is derived for any recall target without iterative model calls. A lightweight reactive module then adjusts search depth based on predicted-versus-observed recall deviations. The framework is proven to require finite training samples independent of dataset size/dimensionality, with loss exceeding the irreducible lower bound by a vanishing margin, and exponential data-access savings in intrinsic dimensionality.

Result: QASP achieves significantly lower recall variance and deviation from the target recall, higher query satisfaction rates, and scales to large datasets and hierarchical indices without retraining. It delivers 99% recall while reducing data access by 80%.

Conclusion: QASP provides a theoretically grounded, practical solution for query-adaptive vector search that combines upfront full-curve prediction with lightweight reactive adjustment, dramatically improving recall consistency and computational efficiency across diverse queries, indices, and datasets.

Abstract: A fundamental challenge of vector search is achieving consistently high recall while minimizing computational costs. Fixed search parameters cause significant performance variance across queries, and conventional evaluation on average recall masks these per-query disparities. We introduce QASP (Query-Adaptive robust vector Search Policy), which predicts the complete recall progression curve per query via a single upfront supervised regression, from which a search policy is derived for any recall target; this avoids iterative model invocations during search or separate predictors per target. By predicting normalized recall values with scale-invariant features and pre-search inference, QASP generalizes across recall targets, index configurations, and datasets. Its fine-grained progress predictions further enable a lightweight reactive complement that adjusts search depth based on predicted-versus-observed deviations without additional inference. We prove that QASP requires a finite training sample independent of dataset size and dimensionality, that its loss exceeds the irreducible lower bound of any fixed policy by a vanishing margin, and that its data access savings over fixed probing grow exponentially in intrinsic dimensionality. Experimentally, QASP achieves significantly lower recall variance and deviation from target, higher query satisfaction rate, and scales to large data and hierarchical indices without retraining, achieving 99% recall with 80% less data access.

</details>


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [239] [To Facilitate or not to Facilitate: Human and LLM Facilitator Tendencies in Online Discussions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28643)
*Dimitris Tsirmpas,Katerina Korre,John Pavlopoulos*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper investigates when (not how) to intervene in online discussions, comparing human facilitation timing judgments with LLM decisions. The authors create PEFK, a unified facilitation corpus, conduct the first survey on facilitation timing with experts and LLM-as-judge models, and find LLMs are overly eager to facilitate while humans are more cautious. They explore correction methods using alternative LLM setups and ModernBert classifiers trained on existing datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online discussion facilitation is a critical social concern as time spent in online spaces increases and content moderation approaches fail. Prior work has focused on how to facilitate, but the fundamental question of when to intervene remains unanswered. LLMs offer potential for automated large-scale facilitation, but their judgment about intervention timing is unexamined.

Method: The authors (1) define facilitation operationally, (2) construct PEFK, a corpus that standardizes and aggregates all relevant existing facilitation datasets, (3) conduct a novel survey on facilitation timing using expert facilitative participants alongside LLM-as-a-judge models, (4) compare human and LLM facilitation timing decisions, and (5) evaluate correction strategies including alternative LLM prompting setups and training ModernBert classifiers on the aggregated datasets.

Result: Humans are more cautious about intervening, while LLMs are excessively eager to facilitate. Both humans and LLMs exhibit higher certainty when judging that facilitation is not needed. ModernBert classifiers trained on established datasets perform more reliably than alternative LLM setups for correcting this over-eagerness, though current datasets impose a relatively low performance ceiling.

Conclusion: LLMs currently cannot be relied upon for autonomous facilitation timing decisions due to their over-eagerness. While classifier-based approaches offer a more reliable corrective path than prompt engineering, the field is constrained by the quality of existing facilitation datasets, suggesting a need for richer data to improve automated facilitation timing.

Abstract: Automating facilitation in online discussions is a long-standing social concern given the increasing time we spend on online spaces and the failure of content moderation approaches. While studies have been conducted on how to facilitate, none have answered the essential question of when to do so. A potential answer is using LLMs, which ostensibly make automated, large-scale intervention increasingly feasible. In this study, we examine when LLMs decide to facilitate by defining what facilitation is, observing when humans decide to facilitate, and comparing their decisions with those made by LLMs. To this end, we create PEFK, a corpus standardizing and aggregating all relevant facilitation datasets. We are the first to run a survey on facilitation timing, which we execute using expert facilitative participants and LLM-as-a-judge models. We discover that while humans are more cautious, LLMs are excessively eager to facilitate, although both are more certain when judging that facilitation is not needed. We then investigate whether this behavior can be corrected using alternative setups for LLMs and training ModernBert classifiers on established datasets, finding that the latter perform more reliably than the former, although current datasets impose a relatively low performance ceiling.

</details>


### [240] [Seeing Differently: Modeling Interpretive Perspectives in Computational Creativity using a Four-World Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28644)
*Prerna Luthra*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper argues that computational creativity should be modeled as perspective-dependent rather than objective. Using a twelve-trait creativity framework and three evaluative personas (formalist, social-historical, iconographic), the authors analyze 1,069 artworks with 38,484 persona-based evaluations. Results reveal systematic divergence across perspectives, and CLIP embedding analysis shows distinct orientation vectors per persona, supporting a relational view of creativity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Computational Creativity (CC) frameworks evaluate creative merit as an objective property of artifacts or systems, ignoring that artistic meaning is inherently perspective-dependent and varies across viewers and critical traditions. The authors aim to address this gap by computationally modeling interpretive perspectives rather than treating creativity as a single measurable construct.

Method: The study adopts a twelve-trait creativity framework organized across four conceptual domains, operationalized through three evaluative personas: formalist, social-historical, and iconographic. Using 1,069 artworks from the SemArt dataset, the authors generate 38,484 persona-based evaluations. They then perform linear probing on CLIP image embeddings to identify whether each persona condition produces distinct orientation vectors in representation space.

Result: Results show systematic divergence in creativity assessments across the three evaluative perspectives. Traits such as Social Reflexivity exhibit particularly strong viewpoint sensitivity. Linear probing reveals that each perspective corresponds to a distinct orientation vector in CLIP representation space, indicating that creativity evaluation depends on which visual features become salient under each persona condition.

Conclusion: The findings support a relational view of creativity, demonstrating that creativity assessment is not an objective property but varies with interpretive perspective. Incorporating multiple evaluative perspectives could enable co-creative systems to better support interpretively diverse human collaborators.

Abstract: Creativity in computational systems is often evaluated as an objective property of artifacts, with existing Computational Creativity (CC) frameworks assessing creative merit at the level of outputs or systems rather than interpretive context. However, artistic meaning is inherently perspective-dependent and can vary across viewers and critical traditions. This paper proposes a computational approach to modeling interpretive perspectives rather than treating creativity as a single measurable construct. The study adopts a twelve-trait creativity framework, organized across four conceptual domains, and operationalizes it through three evaluative personas: formalist, social-historical, and iconographic. Using 1,069 artworks from the SemArt dataset, the analysis generates 38,484 persona-based evaluations to examine how perspectives shape creativity assessment. Results show systematic divergence across perspectives, with traits such as Social Reflexivity exhibiting strong viewpoint sensitivity. Linear probing of CLIP image embeddings reveals that perspectives correspond to distinct orientation vectors in representation space, suggesting that creativity evaluation depends on which visual features become salient under each persona condition. These findings support a relational view of creativity and indicate that incorporating multiple evaluative perspectives could enable co-creative systems to support interpretively diverse human collaborators.

</details>


### [241] [Looks Right, Works Right: A Project-Level Benchmark for Multi-Screen Mobile App Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28645)
*Fan Wu,Cuiyun Gao,Yiming Huang,Yang Xiao,Yujia Chen,Qing Liao*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: MobileForge is the first benchmark for evaluating multi-screen mobile app code generation from visual designs, addressing gaps in existing benchmarks by supporting five-axis evaluation (build, navigation, visual fidelity, maintainability, efficiency) with novel testing protocols; existing multimodal LLMs can generate compilable apps but struggle with interactive navigation, visual fidelity, and maintainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing design-to-code benchmarks are limited to single-page generation, cannot evaluate cross-page navigation, and fail to measure project-wide maintainability. Real mobile products require multiple screenshots to produce buildable codebases with shared components and working navigation, exposing three critical gaps in current evaluation frameworks.

Method: The authors introduce MobileForge, a benchmark built from real mobile apps with human-reviewed screens, structured page-relationship annotations, and navigation test specifications. It features a five-axis evaluation framework covering build success, navigation correctness, visual fidelity, code maintainability, and efficiency. Two novel protocols are proposed: (1) state-isolated navigation testing to prevent cascading failures during navigation evaluation, and (2) an anchor-referenced list-wise visual evaluation protocol to improve visual-judge reliability.

Result: Across six frontier multimodal LLMs evaluated end-to-end, current models can generate mobile-app projects that compile and reach the correct pages. However, interactive navigation remains unreliable, and both visual fidelity and maintainability scores still lag significantly behind acceptable thresholds.

Conclusion: MobileForge establishes the first rigorous benchmark for project-level multi-screen mobile app generation. While multimodal LLMs show promise in producing compilable codebases, substantial challenges remain in navigation reliability, visual rendering accuracy, and code maintainability — highlighting key directions for future research.

Abstract: Recent multimodal large language models can convert visual designs directly into executable code, but real mobile products require multiple screenshots to become a buildable codebase with shared components and working navigation. This project-level setting exposes three limits of existing design-to-code benchmarks: they focus on single-page generation rather than complete codebases, cannot evaluate cross-page navigation, and do not measure project-wide maintainability. We introduce MobileForge, the first benchmark for project-level multi-screen mobile app generation, comprising
  real mobile apps,
  human-reviewed screens, structured page-relationship annotations, and
  navigation test specifications. MobileForge supports five-axis evaluation of build, navigation, visual fidelity, code maintainability, and efficiency. We also propose state-isolated navigation testing to avoid cascading failures in navigation evaluation and an anchor-referenced list-wise visual evaluation protocol to improve visual-judge reliability. Across
  end-to-end runs on six frontier multimodal LLMs, current models can build mobile-app projects that compile and reach the correct pages, but interactive navigation remains unreliable and visual fidelity and maintainability still lag. The benchmark and supporting materials are available at https://github.com/anoa12159-hue/mobileforge_eval.

</details>


### [242] ["YES! YES! I absolutely love this insight!" Affirmative Narration as Interactional Strategy in Dialogues with LLM Chatbots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28646)
*Hanna-Riikka Roine,Anne Sigrid Refsum,Jill Walker Rettberg*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper identifies "affirmative narration" as a narrative strategy in human-LLM dialogues that combines three mechanisms—character construction, masterplot activation, and user isolation—to maximize engagement, while warning of its psychological dangers and calling for a new literacy for interacting with LLMs as fictional narrative media.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing prevalence of LLM chatbots in daily life raises concerns about their persuasive power. The authors are motivated by alarming real-world cases—ranging from unsettling interactions to user delusions and even suicide—to uncover the narrative mechanisms that make these dialogues so compelling and potentially harmful.

Method: The paper employs narrative analysis, examining multiple case studies of human-LLM dialogues. It identifies and theorizes three interlocking narrative mechanisms: (1) guiding users to perceive the chatbot as an intelligent, reliable character; (2) activating culturally embedded masterplots (recurring story templates); and (3) deploying characters and masterplots to both affirm and isolate the user.

Result: The analysis reveals that these three mechanisms combine into "affirmative narration"—an interactional strategy that convinces users of the chatbot's utility while fostering dependency. Case studies demonstrate that this strategy has contributed to severe psychological harm, including delusion and suicide.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that LLMs should be understood as a genre of fictional narrative media and argue that users require a new type of literacy to critically engage with these systems and resist the potentially harmful effects of affirmative narration.

Abstract: This article analyses narrative mechanisms that are common in dialogues with LLM chatbots. In combination, these mechanisms produce an interactional strategy for maximising user engagement, which we call affirmative narration. Affirmative narration serves to convince users of the chatbot's utility. We analyse three narrative mechanisms that support affirmative narration in human-LLM dialogues: firstly, guiding the user to view the chatbot as an intelligent and reliable character; secondly, activating masterplots, culturally significant and recurring story templates; and thirdly, using characters and masterplots not only to affirm, but also to isolate the user. The case studies range from a journalist's unsettling chatbot experiment to cases where users have experienced delusions or even died by suicide after lengthy interactions with a chatbot. The analyses illustrate the worrying sides of affirmative narration, and the article thus concludes with a discussion of LLMs as a genre of fictional narrative media that requires a new type of literacy.

</details>


### [243] [ConnectED: A Curriculum-Aligned AI System for Vietnamese Instructional Lesson Planning and Student Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28647)
*Thang Doan Viet,Anh Nguyen Hoang,Tinh Luong Son,Anh Hoang Thi Ngoc,Huyen Giang Thi Thu,Tai Le Quy*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: ConnectED is an AI system for Vietnamese education that uses a fine-tuned LLM (VietEduQwen) and the ADDIE framework to automate lesson design, interactive learning, and feedback-driven refinement, reducing teacher prep time from 3–4 hours to ~30–45 minutes while achieving 87.02% accuracy on national exam questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of curriculum-aligned, pedagogically appropriate, and student-safe AI tools for the full instructional lifecycle in Vietnamese education, bridging lesson design, interactive learning, and data-driven refinement under a unified human-centered system.

Method: ConnectED operationalizes the ADDIE (Analysis, Design, Development, Implementation, Evaluation) framework via structured prompt templates aligned with Vietnam's Official Dispatch No. 5512/BGDDT-GDTrH. Each phase acts as both a generation step and a teacher validation gate. The underlying model, VietEduQwen, is trained via supervised fine-tuning and direct preference optimization (DPO). The Evaluation phase links student performance data to iterative lesson improvement, and a student-facing interactive environment continuously collects learning signals.

Result: VietEduQwen achieves 87.02% accuracy on 3,119 questions from the 2025 Vietnamese National High School Examination, outperforming Qwen3-8B by 6.10 percentage points. Teacher surveys (n=18) and student surveys (n=214) show strong satisfaction with curriculum alignment, lesson clarity, and usability. Lesson preparation time dropped from 3–4 hours to ~30–45 minutes with teacher-in-the-loop review. Ablation studies confirm independent contributions of both DPO training and ADDIE-based orchestration to overall performance.

Conclusion: Structured teacher oversight via the ADDIE framework combined with DPO-trained domain-specific LLMs yields significant gains in both academic accuracy and practical efficiency for educational AI deployment, underscoring the value of human-centered, feedback-driven design in real-world classroom settings.

Abstract: This paper presents ConnectED, a human-centered AI system that supports the full instructional lifecycle in Vietnamese education by linking curriculum-aligned lesson design, interactive student learning, and feedback-driven refinement.
