Built on VietEduQwen, a Vietnamese educational large language model trained via supervised fine-tuning and direct preference optimization, the system ensures academically accurate, pedagogically appropriate, and student-safe interactions. ConnectED operationalizes the ADDIE framework through structured prompt templates aligned with Official Dispatch No. 5512/BGDDT-GDTrH, where each phase serves as both a generation step and a teacher validation gate. The Evaluation phase further closes the loop by connecting student performance data with iterative lesson improvement. Beyond lesson generation, the system integrates a student-facing interactive environment, enabling continuous collection of learning signals to support teacher decision-making. Evaluation on 3,119 questions from the 2025 Vietnamese National High School Examination shows that VietEduQwen achieves 87.02% accuracy, outperforming Qwen3-8B by 6.10 percentage points. Surveys of teachers (n=18) and students (n=214) demonstrate strong satisfaction with curriculum alignment, lesson clarity, and usability. In practice, lesson preparation time is reduced from 3--4 hours to approximately 30--45 minutes with teacher-in-the-loop review. Ablation studies confirm that both DPO training and ADDIE-based orchestration contribute independently to system performance, highlighting the importance of structured teacher oversight for practical deployment.

</details>


### [244] [Why It Hurts: Identifying the Drivers of Negative Thoughts in Emotional Support Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28648)
*Hainiu Xu,Zhaoyue Sun,Hanqi Yan,Jinhua Du,Caroline Catmur,Yulan He*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLMs can infer contextually salient cognitive appraisal dimensions in emotional support conversations, introducing the AppraiSal benchmark and the PRISM probabilistic framework to improve performance on this task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based frameworks for emotional support tasks like negative thought reframing exhaustively evaluate all possible cognitive appraisal dimensions, ignoring the fact that different dimensions vary in saliency across contexts. The authors identify a gap: no existing work examines whether LLMs can infer which appraisal dimensions are most salient in a given conversation.

Method: The authors introduce (1) the AppraiSal benchmark with 996 emotional support conversations annotated for mental states and salient appraisal dimensions, and (2) PRISM, a multi-agent probabilistic framework grounded in Bayesian Inverse Planning that models how LLMs can identify context-specific appraisal dimensions.

Result: PRISM consistently improves LLM performance across various model sizes, with particularly strong gains in identifying the most salient cognitive appraisal dimensions compared to baseline approaches.

Conclusion: LLMs, when augmented with the PRISM framework, can effectively infer salient appraisal dimensions from emotional support conversations, paving the way for more context-aware cognitive reframing systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for emotional support tasks, such as negative thought reframing. This task relies on modifying cognitive appraisals, the subjective interpretation of events that elicit negative emotions, which is typically conceptualized along multiple discrete dimensions. Current LLM-based frameworks model cognitive appraisal by exhaustively evaluating all possible dimensions, but they fail to account for the varying saliency of these dimensions across different contexts. In this work, we investigate a vital yet overlooked question: "Can LLMs infer the salient appraisal dimensions from emotional support conversations?" To address this question, we introduce the AppraiSal benchmark, containing 996 emotional support conversations with human-annotated mental states, including salient cognitive appraisal dimensions. Furthermore, we propose PRISM, a multi-agent probabilistic framework grounded in Bayesian Inverse Planning, designed to improve LLMs' ability to identify context-specific appraisal dimensions. Experimental results show that PRISM brings improvements to LLMs across various sizes, particularly in identifying the most salient appraisal dimensions.

</details>


### [245] [COSI-Lab: Conference Living Lab for Modeling Multi-Perspective Multimodal Social Intention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28649)
*Zonghuan Li,Litian Li,Arthur Mercier,Gara Dorta,Balint Dioszegi,Jose Morales-Vargas,Chenxu Hao,Ivan Kondyurin,Vanessa Begemann,Nale Lehmann-Willenbrock,Bernd Dudzik,Saunaq Chakrabarty,Sotiris Vacanas,Laura Cabrera-Quirós,Anne L. J. ter Wal,Vitaliy Popov,Jorge Castro-Godínez,Chirag Raman,Stephanie Tan,Hayley Hung*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: COSI-Lab introduces a multimodal dataset of 32 academics in a semi-structured mingling workshop, focusing on Apparent Intent Inference (AII) from observer perspectives. It provides novel annotation methods that treat subjective perceptual differences as explainable perspective-driven reasoning rather than label noise, along with benchmarks, high-quality audio, and participant self-report coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current intelligent systems often treat subjective perceptual variations as label noise. The authors argue that modeling the multiplicity of subjective perceptions as an explainable, perspective-driven reasoning process would enable better handling of real-world social understanding. They address the gap in ecologically valid, multimodal datasets that capture genuine social interactions with real professional and social stakes.

Method: The authors designed a weakly scripted, ecologically valid experimental paradigm: 32 academics at an international conference participated in two 30-minute mingling sessions with real professional and social consequences. They developed a novel annotation process for Apparent Intent Inference (AII) that explicitly accounts for each perceiver's interpretative tendencies. Intentions are conceptualized as independent of manifest future outcomes and are determined by ex-situ observers. The dataset includes speech-quality audio, privacy-preserving multimodal data, and couples self-reported participant goals (spanning 30 min to 3 hours) with second-level AII annotations.

Result: The paper contributes: (1) a novel AII annotation process capturing perceiver-specific interpretative tendencies; (2) quantitative and qualitative analyses of intent narratives regarding diversity, grounding, and plausibility; (3) benchmark tasks for AII and related contextual factors like social involvement; (4) high-quality speech audio and privacy-preserving multimodal data enabling lexical and nonverbal behavior analysis; and (5) coupling of participant self-reported goals with annotated AII at the second-level granularity.

Conclusion: The COSI-Lab dataset provides a foundation for developing intelligent systems that model subjective social perception as perspective-driven reasoning. By decoupling intentions from outcomes and accounting for observer-specific interpretations, the work advances the study of apparent intent inference in realistic, consequential social settings.

Abstract: COSI-Lab presents a multimodal, multi-sensor dataset of an interdisciplinary scientific workshop containing 32 academics at an international conference. It captures ecologically valid social interactions in a weakly scripted setting consisting of two 30-minute mingling sessions with real professional and social consequences for the participants involved. We argue that future intelligent systems could be better equipped to handle subjective perceptions by modeling their multiplicity not as label noise but as a explainable perspective-driven reasoning process. We focus on the Apparent Intent Inference (AII) problem as determined by ex-situ observers and conceptualize intentions to be independent of manifest future outcomes. We contribute 1. a novel annotation process for AII that accounts for a perceiver's own interpretative tendencies, 2. quantitative and qualitative analyses of intent narratives with respect to diversity, grounding, and plausibility; 3. benchmark tasks for AII and surrounding relevant contextual factors such as social involvement; 4. speech quality audio for all participants as well as privacy preserving multi-modal data, enabling lexical and nonverbal behavior analysis; and 5. coupling of self-reported goals of each participant (30 minute to 3 hour) with annotated AII (seconds).

</details>


### [246] [Unanticipated Effects of Generative AI on Expertise Pathways and Performance Perception in System Administration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28650)
*Rana Abou Khamis,Hala Assal,Ashraf Matrawy*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Interviews with 14 IT professionals reveal that GenAI adoption in system administration compresses traditional expertise pathways (shortcutting hands-on learning) and shifts performance perceptions (creating productivity guilt and two-speed team cultures), raising concerns about long-term expertise development and professional value assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industry discourse frames GenAI narrowly as an automation tool for productivity gains, overlooking deeper, poorly understood shifts in professional practice. This study aims to surface the lived, unanticipated socio-technical consequences of embedding GenAI into system administration work.

Method: The authors conducted 14 semi-structured interviews with IT professionals and applied inductive thematic analysis to examine how GenAI is integrated into daily routines of troubleshooting, scripting, and system verification.

Result: Two key findings emerged: (1) "Compression of traditional expertise pathways" — GenAI acts as both a mentor-like tutor and a "ladder-shortening" tool, enabling faster cross-domain performance but potentially depriving practitioners of foundational trial-and-error learning experiences. (2) "Performance perception shift" — AI-assisted speed resets productivity expectations, fostering a "two-speed culture" and "productivity guilt," where necessary manual verification is reframed as inefficiency.

Conclusion: The findings raise critical questions about how GenAI may reshape expertise development, redefine professional value in high-stakes environments, and challenge the role of human judgment, cautioning against a purely efficiency-driven integration of AI in technical work.

Abstract: While industry discourse often emphasizes immediate productivity gains and frames GenAI primarily as a tool for automation, the integration of GenAI into system administration may involve deeper shifts in professional practice that are not yet fully understood. Drawing on 14 semi-structured interviews with IT professionals, this paper explores the lived reality of embedding GenAI into daily routines of troubleshooting, scripting, and system verification. Through inductive thematic analysis, we uncover two unanticipated socio-technical findings. First, we describe a "compression of traditional expertise pathways" where GenAI appears to function as both a mentor-like tutor and a "ladder-shortening" tool. While the tool can support faster task performance in unfamiliar domains, our findings suggest it may also reduce a practitioner's exposure to the foundational, hands-on cycles of building, failing, and debugging that historically served as the training ground for technical expertise. Second, we describe a "performance perception shift," where the speed of AI-assisted work begins to reset organizational and self-expectations for productivity. This shift may create a "two-speed culture" within teams and introduce "productivity guilt," as necessary manual work, even when required for safety or validation, is increasingly perceived as slow or a failure of efficiency. Our results raise broader questions about how GenAI may influence expertise development, how professional value is assessed in high-stakes technical environments, and the role of human judgment in complex technical environments.

</details>


### [247] [Measuring Cognitive Engagement in Collaborative Discourse with an Extended ICAP Framework: Comparing Human Annotation, In-Context Learning, and Reflective LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28651)
*Lan Anh Do,Hanling Jiang,Shuchin Aeron,Ayanna K. Thomas*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This study extends the ICAP framework to a 7-point scale for coding cognitive engagement in collaborative dialogue, comparing human annotators with LLM-based labeling approaches. Human annotators achieved high agreement (κ = 0.906–0.998), substantially surpassing ICL-based LLMs (κ = 0.541–0.609). Human framework refinement improved human agreement more than LLM agreement, while agent-refined frameworks improved cross-model but not human-level agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collaborative learning depends on the quality of cognitive engagement during discourse, yet reliably characterizing that engagement remains challenging. The authors seek to determine whether LLM-based annotation methods can match human reliability in applying an extended ICAP coding framework, and whether iterative framework refinement (human- or agent-driven) can close the gap between human and machine performance.

Method: The ICAP framework (Interactive, Constructive, Active, Passive) was extended to a 7-point ordinal scale to capture finer-grained variation in cognitive engagement. Trained human annotators coded collaborative dialogue transcripts, and their interrater reliability was assessed via Cohen's kappa. Three LLM-based annotation approaches were evaluated: in-context learning (ICL), zero-shot prompting, and self-reflective agents. Framework refinement was conducted both by humans and by LLM-based agents, with agreement metrics compared across conditions.

Result: Human interrater reliability was robust across all refinement stages (κ = 0.906–0.998). ICL-based LLM agreement was only moderate (κ = 0.541–0.609). Human-driven framework refinement yielded a substantial gain for humans (Δκ ≈ 0.10) but only modest improvement for ICL-based LLMs (Δκ < 0.04). Agent-refined frameworks improved cross-model agreement but still fell short of the human-refined framework.

Conclusion: LLM-based annotation shows promise but does not yet approach human-level reliability for coding cognitive engagement. Iterative interaction between theory-guided human refinement and LLM-based methods — including agent-driven approaches — represents a productive direction for future work.

Abstract: Collaboration supports learning and problem-solving, but its effectiveness depends on cognitive engagement during discourse. This study applies an extended 7-point ICAP framework based on the Interactive, Constructive, Active, and Passive modes to characterize variation in cognitive engagement during collaborative dialogue. Engagement was coded by trained human annotators and compared with large language model (LLM)-based labeling approaches, including in-context learning (ICL), zero-shot prompting, and self-reflective agents. Interrater reliability among human annotators was robust across framework refinement stages (kappa = 0.906-0.998), higher than the moderate agreement observed for ICL-based annotation (kappa = 0.541-0.609). The human-refined framework improved agreement among human annotators (Delta kappa = 0.10), but produced only modest gains for ICL-based LLMs (Delta kappa less than 0.04). Agent-refined frameworks improved cross-model agreement but remained below the human-refined framework. These findings highlight the promise of agent-based approaches and the importance of continued interaction between theory-guided human annotation and LLM-based methods in future work.

</details>


### [248] [Guided Exploration of Iterative Schedule Modifications: A Design Study on Railway Traction Unit Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28694)
*Andreas Zajic,Vera Hechtl,Cornelia Geischläger,Maximilian Kunovjanek-Bachler,Thomas Hulka,Anna-Lena Penk,Belma Turan,Nadine Schwab,Maximilian Viehauser,Helwig Hauser,Krešimir Matković*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper presents an interactive visual exploration system for optimizing traction unit circulation plans in large railway networks. It tightly integrates schedule visualization, simulation-based evaluation, and a three-level guidance mechanism to help planners systematically identify, evaluate, and apply crossing operations that reduce delay propagation. A provenance-based history mechanism supports non-linear exploration of alternative modification paths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimizing traction unit schedules in large railway networks is challenging: multi-objective optimization produces ideal plans, but simulation is needed to assess robustness under realistic conditions. A key refinement mechanism involves crossing operations where co-located traction units exchange schedules to reduce delays. However, the space of possible crossing sequences grows exponentially, and existing tools offer limited support for identifying promising crossing candidates, evaluating their impact, and managing the exploration process.

Method: The authors propose an interactive visual exploration approach with three main components: (1) a domain-familiar schedule visualization that integrates simulation results to expose delay propagation within the planning context; (2) a three-level guidance framework that spatially aggregates crossing candidates, ranks them by estimated KPI impact at an overview level, and provides detailed per-candidate evaluation at a detail level; and (3) a provenance-based history mechanism that, upon applying a crossing change, triggers automatic schedule recomputation and re-simulation, enabling non-linear exploration of alternative modification paths.

Result: The approach was demonstrated through real-world use case scenarios and showed substantial reductions in the time and effort required to identify and evaluate promising schedule modifications compared to the current workflow.

Conclusion: The tightly coupled visual-interactive approach effectively bridges the gap between optimization and simulation for traction unit scheduling. By combining domain-specific visualization, guided candidate exploration, and provenance tracking, the system significantly streamlines the iterative refinement of circulation plans under realistic operating conditions.

Abstract: Traction unit scheduling in large railway networks involves complex operational constraints: multi-objective optimization produces feasible circulation plans under ideal assumptions, while simulation is required to assess their robustness under realistic operating conditions. A critical refinement mechanism relies on crossing operations, in which co-located traction units exchange their remaining schedules to reduce delay propagation. The space of possible crossing sequences, however, grows exponentially. Existing tools provide limited support for identifying promising candidates, evaluating their impact, and managing the resulting exploration. We present an interactive visual exploration approach that tightly couples schedule visualization, simulation-based evaluation, and a three-level guidance mechanism to support the systematic exploration and interactive optimization of traction unit circulation plans. The system renders the circulation plan in its domain-familiar form and integrates simulation results to expose delay propagation directly within the planning context. A three-level guidance framework aggregates crossing candidates spatially and ranks them by estimated impact on key performance indicators (KPIs) at an overview level, while exposing detailed per-candidate evaluation at a detail level to support informed decision-making. Applying a crossing change triggers an automatic schedule recomputation and re-simulation, with a provenance-based history mechanism enabling the non-linear exploration of alternative modification paths. We demonstrate the approach through real-world use case scenarios and report substantial reductions in the time and effort required to identify and evaluate promising schedule modifications compared to the current workflow.

</details>


### [249] [Optimizing Monetization Strategies for Generative AI Firms: Implications for Search Engagement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28780)
*Veronica Rosendo-Rios,Paurav Shukla*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Introducing one ad-supported tier to GenAI platforms creates a compromise effect that drives free users to upgrade but causes paid users to downgrade; offering two ad-supported tiers mitigates paid-user downgrading while still attracting free users. Affective and cognitive evaluations serially mediate these preferences, with temporal (not visual) intrusiveness as a key moderator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GenAI platforms face mounting operational costs and need alternative monetization strategies beyond subscriptions, yet little is known about how advertising-supported models affect user search query engagement and upgrading/downgrading behavior.

Method: Four experiments (N=1063) drawing on compromise effect and affective primacy theories, testing the introduction of advertising-supported monetization options and their influence on user decisions, with serial mediation and moderation analyses.

Result: A single ad-supported option enhances the compromise effect: free users upgrade but paid subscribers downgrade. Two ad-supported options mitigate this, retaining subscribers while still motivating free-user upgrades. Affective and cognitive evaluations serially mediate ad-supported model preference. Temporal intrusiveness moderates these effects, while visual intrusiveness does not.

Conclusion: GenAI firms can optimize revenue by strategically structuring the number of ad-supported tiers—offering two rather than one helps balance monetization with user engagement retention, providing actionable guidance for platform revenue strategy.

Abstract: As Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) platforms, such as ChatGPT, have transformed digital search querying behavior, mounting operational costs challenge firms to explore alternative monetization strategies beyond traditional subscription models. However, little is known about how alternative advertising-supported monetization models can help GenAI firms recover costs while maintaining search query engagement. Drawing on the compromise effect and affective primacy theories, we develop a framework wherein the introduction of advertising-supported monetization models influences user upgrading and downgrading decisions, contingent on the number of available monetization options. Across four experiments (N=1063), findings reveal that introducing a single advertising-supported option enhances the compromise effect, encouraging free users to upgrade, but leading paid subscribers to downgrade. However, offering two advertising-supported models mitigates the effect, maintaining subscriber retention while still motivating free users to upgrade. We show that affective and cognitive evaluations serially mediate preference for advertising-supported models, with temporal intrusiveness, but not visual, moderating these effects. We provide actionable insights for GenAI firms on potentially optimizing revenue strategies while balancing user engagement with search queries on their platform.

</details>


### [250] [Spatial Visual Analytics for Multi-Document Summary Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28853)
*Jiahao Xu,Wei Liu,Yang Liu,Eric Krokos,Kirsten Whitley,Xuan Wang,Rebecca Faust,Chris North*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: SVS is a visual analytics system for verifying multi-document summaries using spatial document layouts (summary-guided and source-guided) with coordinated provenance visualization, demonstrating improved accuracy and reduced workload over linear baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Verifying whether summary statements in multi-document summarization (MDS) are grounded in source materials is challenging because evidence is distributed across many documents, potentially incomplete, conflicting, or missing. Existing tools lack scalable ways to trace claims back to sources.

Method: The authors built SVS featuring two 2D canvas layouts: a SUMMARY-GUIDED layout organizing documents by alignment with summary sentences, and a SOURCE-GUIDED layout arranging documents by semantic similarity. Both use coordinated provenance visualization to make relationships among summary content, source documents, and evidence explicit. A comparative study evaluated both layouts against a linear baseline.

Result: Both spatial layouts improved aggregate accuracy and reduced workload compared to the linear baseline, with strongest gains on relevance tasks. The SUMMARY-GUIDED layout provided the best overall balance of accuracy, efficiency, confidence, and workload.

Conclusion: Spatial document organization with provenance visualization effectively supports scalable multi-document summary verification, with the summary-guided layout offering optimal performance across multiple metrics.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly generate summaries from collections of documents to support sensemaking and reporting, but verifying whether summary statements are grounded in source materials remains difficult. In multi-document summarization (MDS), evidence is distributed across many source documents and may be incomplete, conflicting, or missing. We present Summary Verification Space (SVS), a visual analytics system for verifying multi-document summaries through spatial document organization and coordinated provenance visualization. To support scalable verification, we investigate two alternative 2D canvas layouts: a SUMMARY-GUIDED layout that organizes documents by alignment with summary sentences, and a SOURCE-GUIDED layout that arranges documents by semantic similarity. Coordinated provenance visualization then makes relationships among summary content, source documents, and supporting evidence explicit, enabling users to trace support, contradiction, and missing evidence during verification. A task-driven usage scenario illustrates an auditing workflow in which users use the layouts to locate relevant documents and coverage gaps, then inspect linked claims and source evidence to make their own grounding judgments. In a comparative study with provenance held constant, both spatial layouts improved aggregate accuracy and reduced workload relative to a linear baseline, with the clearest gains on relevance tasks. The SUMMARY-GUIDED layout provides the strongest overall balance of accuracy, efficiency, confidence, and workload.

</details>


### [251] [Human-LLM Collaborative Inductive Coding for Conceptualizing K-12 Educator AI Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28889)
*Alex Liu,Min Sun,Lief Esbenshade,Michael Xiao,Victor Tian,Zachary Zhang,Kevin He*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper presents a detailed methodological account of a human-LLM collaborative pipeline for developing a hierarchical codebook from 45,000 K-12 educator–AI platform messages, using adapted open/axial/selective coding across three phases, with humans retaining interpretive authority. The resulting 72-code instrument was validated via systematic human coding on 2,560 messages with set-valued agreement measures, yielding a replicable template for qualitative researchers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Qualitative researchers face interaction corpora too large for manual coding alone. While LLMs are proposed as analytic assistants, there is no clear procedural guidance on to what extent, in what phases, and under what safeguards LLMs can participate in qualitative codebook development without ceding human interpretive authority.

Method: A three-phase human-LLM collaborative pipeline: (1) LLMs generated candidate labels and structured annotations at scale on 45,000 K-12 educator–AI platform messages; (2) human researchers exercised conceptual authority over category definitions, merging decisions, and interpretive frameworks; (3) three trained coders applied the codebook to an independent sample of 2,560 messages, establishing reliability through iterative calibration using set-valued agreement measures for multi-label annotation, and extended the instrument with five human-surfaced codes absent from LLM phases.

Result: The final codebook comprises 72 items within 19 categories and six domains. Five additional codes emerged solely from human coding that LLM-assisted phases had not surfaced. Set-valued intercoder agreement measures proved appropriate for multi-label reliability assessment.

Conclusion: LLMs can serve effectively as labeling instruments—not interpretive agents—in codebook development at scale, provided human researchers maintain conceptual authority over definitions, merging, and interpretive frameworks. The paper offers an auditable, replicable template for qualitative researchers considering LLM assistance, with specific attention to units of analysis, agreement measurement, and conditions where human domain expertise remains decisive.

Abstract: Qualitative researchers increasingly encounter interaction corpora whose scale exceeds what manual coding alone can address, and large language models (LLMs) are frequently proposed as analytic assistants. The open questions are not whether LLMs can participate in qualitative analysis but to what extent, in what phases, and under what safeguards. This article provides a detailed procedural account of a multi-phase human-LLM collaborative pipeline that adapted open, axial, and selective coding to develop a hierarchical codebook from 45,000 messages exchanged between K-12 educators and a generative AI platform. Across three phases, LLMs generated candidate labels and structured annotations at scale, while human researchers retained conceptual authority over category definitions, merging decisions, and interpretive frameworks. The resulting instrument was then tested through systematic human coding, in which three trained coders with educational domain expertise applied the codebook to an independent sample of 2,560 messages, established reliability through iterative calibration using set-valued agreement measures appropriate for multi-label annotation, and extended the instrument with five codes that the LLM-assisted phases had not surfaced. The final codebook comprises 72 items within 19 categories and six domains. We reflect on the methodological decisions the pipeline required, including the choice of a conversational unit of analysis, the treatment of the LLM as a labeling instrument rather than an interpretive agent, the measurement of intercoder agreement under multi-label coding, and the conditions under which human domain expertise remained decisive. The account is offered as an auditable template for qualitative researchers considering LLM assistance in codebook development while preserving human interpretive authority.

</details>


### [252] [Agreement Is Not Quality: Blind Expert Verification of Human and LLM Qualitative Coding When Human Consensus Is Not Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28890)
*Alex Liu,Lief Esbenshade,Michael Xiao,Victor Tian,Zachary Zhang,Kevin He,Min Sun*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This study challenges the standard practice of evaluating LLM-assisted qualitative coding via human-coder agreement. Using a blind expert verification protocol, it finds that despite lower human-LLM agreement, LLM coding is preferred at rates indistinguishable from human coding, with some LLMs even ranked above human coders — demonstrating that agreement metrics alone are insufficient for automation decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prevailing practice in evaluating LLM-assisted qualitative coding measures model performance solely as agreement with human coders, presuming human coding as the gold standard. The authors argue this presumption can fail in ways that agreement metrics cannot detect, necessitating empirical investigation into whether human-LLM agreement validly reflects coding quality.

Method: Five LLM systems and three trained human coders independently applied a 72-item hierarchical codebook to 2,560 educator messages from a K-12 AI platform. Beyond conventional inter-rater agreement analysis (Jaccard similarity), an independent domain expert judged 855 pairwise code-set comparisons blind to source, treating human and machine outputs symmetrically. Results were analyzed using preference rates, significance tests, and a Bradley-Terry ranking model.

Result: Human-LLM agreement (mean Jaccard 0.30) was substantially lower than human-human agreement (0.52), which under standard practice would indicate inferior LLM coding. However, the blind verifier preferred human and LLM coding at statistically indistinguishable rates (51.5% vs. 48.5%, p = 0.537), and a Bradley-Terry ranking placed two LLMs above two of the three human coders. For several substantive codes, human consensus reflected shared bias that the verifier rejected in favor of LLM interpretations.

Conclusion: Agreement-based evaluation is insufficient for making automation decisions about LLM-assisted qualitative coding. The authors contribute a transferable blind verification protocol and a code-level division-of-labor framework to more robustly assess coding quality across human and machine sources.

Abstract: Evaluations of LLM-assisted qualitative coding almost universally measure model performance as agreement with human coders, a practice that presumes human coding is the standard to approximate. This study provides empirical evidence that the presumption fails in ways agreement metrics cannot detect. Five LLM systems and three trained human coders independently applied a 72-item hierarchical codebook to 2,560 educator messages from a K-12 AI platform. Beyond conventional agreement analysis, an independent domain expert judged 855 pairwise comparisons of code sets blind to source, treating human and machine sources symmetrically. The two evaluation approaches diverge in both directions. Human-LLM agreement (mean Jaccard 0.30) falls well below human-human agreement (0.52), which standard practice would read as inferior LLM coding, yet the blind verifier preferred human and LLM coding at indistinguishable rates (51.5% vs. 48.5%, p = 0.537), and a Bradley-Terry ranking placed two LLMs above two of three human coders. For several substantive codes, human consensus encoded shared bias that the verifier rejected in favor of the LLM interpretation. Agreement-based evaluation is therefore insufficient for automation decisions, and the study demonstrates a transferable verification protocol and a code-level division-of-labor framework.

</details>


### [253] [Design Concept: Scaffolding Geopolitical Reflection Among Tech Workers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28904)
*Sydney Reis*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Proposes an AI-enabled interactive narrative system to foster geopolitical reflexivity among tech workers, arguing that speculative narrative approaches can introduce geopolitical awareness into responsible technology initiatives without being prescriptive or moralizing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Technology firms and their workers are increasingly recognized as geopolitical actors whose decisions shape international dynamics, yet existing Responsible Innovation and Responsible AI frameworks rarely address the geopolitical narratives and imaginaries driving AI development. There is a gap in tools for encouraging tech workers to critically examine their geopolitical positionality.

Method: A speculative HCI design proposal for an AI-enabled interactive narrative system combining: (1) narrative interaction with a speculative scenario about technology, power, and geopolitics; (2) archetype assignment to position users within the narrative; and (3) socially scaffolded workshop reflection sessions. The approach draws on RI scholarship on reflexivity, reflective HCI, and creative HCI work on computational narratives.

Result: This is a design proposal paper; no empirical results are reported. The paper contributes a theoretically grounded speculative system design and argues for the productive potential of narrative-based approaches to geopolitical reflexivity.

Conclusion: Speculative narrative systems offer a promising, non-prescriptive avenue for integrating geopolitical reflexivity into responsible technology initiatives, addressing a recognized blind spot in current RI and Responsible AI practices.

Abstract: This paper presents a speculative Human-Computer Interaction design proposal for encouraging geopolitical reflexivity amongst tech workers at geopolitically relevant technology companies. Recent scholarship in International Relations and Science and Technology Studies increasingly recognizes technology firms and their workers as geopolitical actors whose decisions shape international dynamics. However, existing Responsible Innovation and Responsible AI approaches rarely engage with the geopolitical narratives and imaginaries that underpin contemporary AI development. Building upon RI scholarship on reflexivity, reflective HCI, and creative HCI work on computational narratives, this paper proposes an AI-enabled interactive narrative system in which users engage with a speculative scenario centred on technology, power, and geopolitics. Through narrative interaction, archetype assignment, and socially scaffolded workshop reflection, the system aims to encourage target users to critically examine their assumptions, values, and positionality within broader sociotechnical systems. We argue that speculative narrative systems may offer a productive avenue for introducing geopolitical reflexivity into responsible technology initiatives without relying on prescriptive or moralising approaches.

</details>


### [254] [Designing a digital word-learning intervention with neurodiverse children: Experiences and ideas from children with developmental language disorder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29113)
*Rafiah Ansari,Rina R. Wehbe,Lizbeth Olivia Escobedo*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A participatory design study involving four children with Developmental Language Disorder (DLD) explored their emotional responses and design preferences to inform a word-learning intervention. Children responded best to familiar characters, videogames, and direct contribution opportunities, while high cognitive demand reduced engagement. Key design requirements included multimodal, personalised learning with progress markers and alignment between digital and real-world support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DLD affects 7% of children and shares features with ASD, yet it has received minimal attention in participatory design research. The study aimed to fill this gap by engaging children with DLD directly in the co-design of a word-learning intervention, capturing both their emotional responses and design contributions.

Method: A bespoke participatory design approach integrated learner-centred, cooperative, and accessible co-design principles, tailored for four children with DLD. Sessions were iteratively refined through ongoing monitoring. Data sources included design artefacts (drawings), structured feedback via sentence-starter prompts, researcher field notes, and pre-/post-session emotion scales. Reflexive thematic analysis was applied.

Result: Children responded positively to activities featuring familiar story characters and videogames and those enabling direct idea contribution. Higher cognitive demand reduced interaction. Key design requirements identified were multimodal, personalised learning, use of progress markers, and alignment of digital with real-world learning (e.g., in-person adult support).

Conclusion: A carefully planned, bespoke participatory design approach was both meaningful and rewarding for children with DLD and yielded valuable, actionable insights for developing the word-learning intervention.

Abstract: Introduction - Developmental language disorder (DLD) is a neurodevelopmental condition often characterised by word-learning difficulties that can lead to significant social and academic challenges. The disorder shares some features with other neurodevelopmental conditions such as autism spectrum disorder (ASD). Despite affecting 7 percent of children, the condition has received little coverage in participatory design research. This paper addresses this by reporting on the emotional responses and design outputs of children with DLD following participatory sessions to inform a word-learning intervention.
  Method - Principles from learner-centred, cooperative, and accessible co-design approaches were integrated to tailor activities for four children with DLD. Design sessions were refined through ongoing monitoring of the children's experiences. The data that informed the findings included design artefacts such as children's drawings, structured feedback obtained using sentence-starter prompts, and researcher field notes. Reflexive thematic analysis was applied to the data, complemented by findings from an emotion scale completed before and after sessions.
  Results - Children responded positively to design activities that incorporated familiar story characters and videogames, and that allowed a direct contribution of design ideas. Activities with higher cognitive demand led to reduced interactions. Key design requirements for the word-learning intervention included multimodal, personalised learning opportunities and the use of progress markers. Aligning digital and real-world learning, such as providing in-person adult support, was also important.
  Conclusions - Through careful planning and refinement, a bespoke participatory design approach proved meaningful and rewarding for children with DLD, whilst also yielding valuable insights for the development of the intervention.

</details>


### [255] [The persuasive power of large language models does not depend on their perceived national origin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29334)
*Ningzhi Liu,Yannic Hinrichs,Jonas R. Kunst*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A randomized experiment found that labeling an AI chatbot as Chinese vs. American did not affect its persuasive power on U.S. citizens, despite lower initial trust in the Chinese-labeled bot. Political topics slowed persuasion, and collective narcissism reduced attitude change regardless of the AI's origin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study addresses a gap in knowledge about whether perceived national origin of conversational AI influences its persuasive impact on citizens, with implications for democratic discourse, information sovereignty, and foreign influence operations.

Method: A preregistered randomized experiment with 403 nationally representative U.S. adults who debated a GPT-4o chatbot labeled as either American ("DiscoveryAI") or Chinese ("ZhengheAI") across political and non-political topics. Measurements included pre/post self-reports of attitudes, trust, and collective narcissism, combined with computational analyses of 1,209 conversation turns using LLM-coded stance, argumentative conduct, stance-sensitive embeddings, and keyword-masked emotion/toxicity classifiers.

Result: Substantial attitude change occurred across all conditions. The nationality label had no reliable effect on self-reported attitude change, expressed stance, concessions, counterarguing, or affect — confirmed by equivalence tests and Bayes factors. The only significant label effect was lower pre-conversation human-like trust in the Chinese-labeled bot; functionality trust was unaffected. Political topics slowed stance movement toward the AI's position, and collective narcissism predicted less attitude change regardless of origin, acting as a general barrier rather than an out-group filter.

Conclusion: Origin labeling and transparency requirements alone may offer weak protection against foreign influence operations conducted through conversational AI, as users initially withhold social trust from a rival's AI but still assimilate its arguments.

Abstract: Conversational AI developed by geopolitical rivals reaches citizens worldwide, raising concerns that it could sway public opinion or be rejected as foreign propaganda, with consequences for democratic discourse and information sovereignty. Yet, whether an AI's perceived national origin shapes its persuasive power is unknown. In a preregistered randomized experiment, 403 adults from a nationally representative United States sample held a three-round debate with a chatbot introduced as either American ("DiscoveryAI") or Chinese ("ZhengheAI"), discussing a political or non-political topic. In all conditions, participants actually conversed with the same model (GPT-4o), instructed to argue against their initial position. We combined pre- and post-conversation self-reports of attitudes, trust, and collective narcissism with computational analyses of 1,209 participant turns, including LLM-coded stance and argumentative conduct, stance-sensitive embeddings, and keyword-masked emotion and toxicity classifiers. The conversations produced substantial attitude changes in every condition. Critically, the nationality label affected neither self-reported attitude change nor expressed stance, concessions, counterarguing, or affect, and equivalence tests and Bayes factors largely supported these null effects. The label's only reliable footprint was lower pre-conversation human-like trust in the Chinese model, whereas functionality trust was unaffected. Political topics slowed stance movement toward the AI's position, and collective narcissism predicted less attitude change regardless of origin, acting as a general barrier rather than an out-group filter. Users thus initially withhold social trust from a rival's AI yet still assimilate its arguments; origin labeling and transparency requirements alone may offer weak protection against foreign influence operations conducted through conversational AI.

</details>


### [256] [An Algorithmic Perspective on Information Visualization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29360)
*Wouter Meulemans*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper argues that Munzner's visualization design model conflates design and algorithmic concerns, undermining quality assessment and trustworthiness. The authors propose complementing it with a formal algorithmic perspective model that enforces a clearer separation of concerns, enabling better comparison, evaluation, and discovery in visualization research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In information visualization, design considerations often overshadow algorithmic rigor. Munzner's model, while valuable, treats algorithms as secondary, causing design and algorithmic choices to become entangled. This entanglement leads to undefined quality metrics, reliance on ad-hoc heuristics, reduced trustworthiness, and potentially erroneous conclusions. The authors seek to address this gap by formalizing the algorithmic perspective as a distinct, complementary model.

Method: The paper explores the consequences of design-algorithm entanglement in layout algorithms, examining how quality remains unmeasured and heuristics prevail. It then proposes complementing Munzner's design-centric model with a formal model of the algorithmic perspective. The core strategy is a clearer separation of concerns: explicitly translating design choices into formal specifications suitable for algorithmic study, rather than treating algorithms as an afterthought.

Result: The authors argue that their proposed separation yields several benefits: more meaningful comparison between different visualization approaches, a more fine-grained understanding of strengths and weaknesses, and the uncovering of new research opportunities—ultimately leading to a better overall understanding of visualization.

Conclusion: A formal, clearly separated algorithmic model complementing Munzner's design model is essential for advancing visualization research. This separation paradoxically enables deeper integration between design and algorithm by making their interplay measurable and well-defined, fostering rigor, trustworthiness, and innovation.

Abstract: Information visualization is inherently a field that brings together various research domains. Roughly speaking, we may identify two perspectives: the design perspective, revolving around how to ensure that a human can work effectively with the visual representations of data and the tools that offer them, and the algorithmic perspective, focusing on how to automatically create such visual representations. Munzner's model for visualization design places design choices before algorithmic considerations. It offers predominantly a design perspective; as a consequence, applications of this model may consider the algorithmic perspective as an afterthought, bypassing a step that translates the design into the formalism necessary for algorithmic study. As a result, the design may be entangled with the algorithms used to compute a visualization. Focusing on layout algorithms, we explore the ramifications of this entanglement: quality often goes undefined and unmeasured, and ad-hoc heuristics tend to be applied, reducing trustworthiness and potentially leading to incorrect conclusions. We look at how we may complement Munzner's model---the design perspective---with a clear model of the algorithmic perspective, to obtain a formal, measured understanding of the interplay between visualizations and the algorithms used to create them. Paradoxically, the solution lies in a clearer separation of concerns between design and algorithm. We argue that this model leads to better comparison between approaches, a more fine-grained understanding of their strengths and weaknesses, and allows for uncovering new opportunities, as to eventually lead to a better understanding of visualization.

</details>


### [257] [SAVVY: Student Attention Visualization for Video-based Learning Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29413)
*Shixian Zhou,Minghuan Shen,Xiaolin Wen,Zijun Qiu,Yongliang Jiang,Xiangyang Wu,Fei Wu,Yong Wang,Zhiguang Zhou*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: SAVVY is a visual analytics system that uses multimodal brain signals to track student attention during Video-Based Learning, helping teachers identify attention fluctuations and optimize instructional videos through coordinated visualizations of attention trajectories, content structure, and information density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing attention quantification algorithms for VBL are noise-sensitive and degrade in real-world settings. Furthermore, even with usable attention data, teachers face substantial empirical revision effort, limiting practical feasibility for instructional video improvement.

Method: The authors propose (1) a multimodal brain-signal-based attention modeling framework for stable attention tracking, and (2) SAVVY, an interactive visual analytics system with three coordinated modules covering course structure, audiovisual information density, attentional resource allocation, and multi-temporal-resolution attention trajectories for individual students.

Result: Quantitative experiments, two case studies, and expert interviews demonstrate SAVVY's effectiveness and usability in intuitively identifying student attention variations and supporting instructional video optimization.

Conclusion: SAVVY successfully enables top-down exploration of student attention variations by integrating visual and auditory attention data, allowing teachers to comprehensively analyze attention fluctuation causes and inform video improvement decisions.

Abstract: Video-Based Learning (VBL) has become a popular delivery medium of education in the past decade, ranging from online education to hybrid learning. Students' rising expectations for video quality have motivated teachers to enhance the design of instructional videos before releasing them. Analyzing the attention of pilot cohorts in advance has become a conventional optimization strategy to guide course improvement. However, existing attention quantification algorithms are highly susceptible to noise in real-world environments, degrading estimation accuracy. Moreover, even when attention data are available, teachers must still invest substantial effort in empirical revision attempts, limiting practical feasibility. To address these challenges, we first propose a novel attention modeling framework based on multimodal brain signals that enables stable tracking of student attention levels. We then develop SAVVY, a novel interactive visual analytics system that integrates visual and auditory attention to support top-down exploration of student attention variations. SAVVY comprises three coordinated visualization modules. These modules incorporate multi-level information, including course content structure, audiovisual information density, and attentional resource allocation, and provide multi-temporal-resolution attention trajectories of individual students, enabling teachers to comprehensively analyze the underlying causes of attention fluctuations and inform their subsequent instructional video improvement. We evaluate SAVVY through quantitative experiments, two case studies, and expert interviews. The results demonstrate the effectiveness and usability of SAVVY in intuitively identifying student attention variations and supporting instructional video optimization.

</details>


### [258] [Exploratory Integration of EEG Spectral Features and Gaze Variability for Mild Cognitive Impairment Discrimination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29493)
*Takeru Mukunoki,Mamoru Hiroe,Minoru Nakayama,Yujia Zheng,Yuma Sonoda,Hisatomo Kowa,Takashi Nagamatsu*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Combining EEG spectral features (LASSO-selected) with gaze variability improved MCI discrimination from AUC 0.52 (full EEG) to 0.78, suggesting neural-behavioral complementarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG and eye-tracking have been studied separately as MCI biomarkers, but their combined discriminative value remains underexplored. This study addresses the gap by testing whether integrating neural (EEG spectral power) and behavioral (gaze variability) measures yields complementary information for early MCI detection.

Method: EEG was recorded via the 10–20 system and spectral power features were extracted. Three models were compared: (a) full high-dimensional EEG features, (b) L1-regularized (LASSO) feature selection on EEG features, and (c) LASSO-selected EEG features integrated with gaze variability. Performance was assessed by leave-one-out cross-validation with AUC.

Result: Model (a) performed near chance (AUC=0.52). LASSO feature selection improved AUC to 0.64. Adding gaze variability further boosted AUC to 0.78, indicating synergistic complementarity between EEG and eye-tracking features.

Conclusion: Preliminary evidence supports complementarity between neural (EEG) and behavioral (gaze) variability measures for MCI discrimination. Multimodal integration may enhance early detection beyond unimodal approaches, warranting further validation.

Abstract: Early detection of mild cognitive impairment (MCI) is an important challenge in aging societies. Electroencephalography (EEG) and eye-tracking have independently been explored as potential biomarkers; however, their integrative effects remain insufficiently examined. This exploratory study investigated whether combining EEG spectral features with gaze variability may provide complementary information for MCI discrimination. EEG signals were recorded using the 10--20 system, and spectral power features were extracted. We compared three models: (a) high-dimensional EEG features, (b) L1-regularized feature selection (LASSO), and (c) integration of the selected EEG features with gaze variability. Performance was evaluated using leave-one-out cross-validation and area under the ROC curve (AUC). Model (a) yielded limited discrimination (AUC = 0.52). Feature selection increased AUC (0.64), and additional integration of gaze variability further increased AUC (0.78). These preliminary findings suggest potential complementarity between neural and behavioral variability measures.

</details>


### [259] [Data Visualization Style Guides in Practice: Why They Emerge, How They Work, and When They Bend](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29645)
*Alvitta Ottley,Jonathan Schwabish*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Visualization style guides are under-scrutinized socio-technical systems that coordinate visual work within organizations. Through interviews with nine authors of twenty-six style guides across journalism, government, industry, and the public sector, the authors reveal tensions between standardization and flexibility, and propose PRISM — a framework characterizing style guides by Purpose, Rules & Mechanisms, Institutional Enforcers, and Situated Flexibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visualization style guides significantly shape data interpretation and trust but receive little critical scrutiny. Existing perspectives focus narrowly on the specific rules they prescribe, neglecting how they function organizationally to coordinate visual work, manage trade-offs, and support judgment under real-world constraints.

Method: Semi-structured interviews with nine authors who collectively created twenty-six visualization style guides, spanning four sectors: journalism, government, industry, and the public sector. Thematic analysis of interview data was conducted to surface organizational challenges and socio-technical dynamics.

Result: The study identifies that visualization style guides address organizational needs including consistency, training, governance, and accountability. Key tensions emerge between standardization vs. flexibility, guidance vs. discretion, and automation vs. human oversight. Publicly available style guides reveal only a subset of the full socio-technical system. The PRISM framework (Purpose, Rules & Mechanisms, Institutional Enforcers, Situated Flexibility) is proposed to characterize these systems holistically.

Conclusion: Viewing visualization style guides as socio-technical systems enriches design guidance research and provides practical insights for creators and users in critical environments. The PRISM framework offers a lens for understanding the full organizational and institutional context in which these guides operate.

Abstract: Visualization style guides play a crucial role in shaping how data is interpreted and trusted, yet they often receive little scrutiny in their creation and use. Understanding their impact requires looking beyond the specific rules that style guides prescribe and examining how they function within organizations to coordinate visual work, manage trade-offs, and support judgment under real constraints. Analyzing interviews with nine authors of twenty-six style guides across journalism, government, industry, and the public sector, we reveal how these guides reflect the specific challenges of their organizations, including consistency, training, governance, and accountability. Our study highlights the tensions between standardization and flexibility, guidance and discretion, and automation and human oversight. We propose PRISM, a socio-technical framework that characterizes visualization style guides by their Purpose, Rules & Mechanisms, Institutional Enforcers, and Situated Flexibility. We show that publicly available style guides expose only a subset of this system. By viewing style guides as socio-technical systems, we enrich the research on design guidance and offer practical insights for those who create and rely on these guides in critical environments.

</details>
